Part: Tools (122)

1. Introduction to the laboratory (29)

orl-f-001 · level facile · notes
In the notation used throughout the lab notes, how is the value of the variable $x_j$ in an *optimal* solution of a model written?
  1. With a bar, $\bar x_j$
  2. With a tilde, $\tilde x_j$
  3. With an asterisk, $x_j^*$
  4. With a hat, $\hat x_j$
Explanation. In the notes the bar marks the values of a feasible solution and the tilde those of an optimal one ($\tilde x_j$, $\tilde z$); the asterisk is not used for the solutions of the models.
orl-f-002 · level facile · notes
The lab classifies its models as LP, QP or NLP. Which class has a quadratic objective function and only linear constraints?
  1. LP
  2. NLP with conic constraints
  3. QP
  4. Stochastic LP
Explanation. QP (*Quadratic Programming*): quadratic objective, linear constraints. In an LP everything is linear; in an NLP the objective or the constraints are general nonlinear functions.
orl-f-003 · level facile · notes
In the lab notes an optimization problem is called *convex* when:
  1. every local minimum is also a global minimum
  2. all its constraints are linear functions
  3. its optimal solution is unique
  4. its objective is differentiable everywhere
Explanation. This is the working definition of the notes: for LPs it always holds, for QPs and NLPs it depends on the functions. Linearity, uniqueness and differentiability are different properties.
orl-f-004 · level facile · notes
All the models of the lab use only continuous variables (no binary or integer ones). What is the main reason given in the notes?
  1. Gurobi, called from Python, does not accept integer or binary variables
  2. Models with continuous variables are always convex, hence easy to solve
  3. The pip licence of `gurobipy` is limited to models without binary variables
  4. With continuous variables duality, shadow prices and KKT give an economic reading
Explanation. The choice is didactic: with continuous variables the solution is explained through duality, shadow prices and KKT conditions. Gurobi handles integer variables, and continuous models are not always convex (e.g. pricing).
orl-f-005 · level facile · notes
In the notation of the lab notes, which symbols denote, in this order, the dual variable of constraint $i$, the reduced cost of variable $x_j$ and the slack of constraint $i$ (in a feasible solution)?
  1. $\bar\pi_i$, $\bar c_j$, $\bar s_i$
  2. $\bar y_i$, $\bar r_j$, $\bar s_i$
  3. $\bar\lambda_i$, $\bar\pi_j$, $\bar u_i$
  4. $\bar y_i$, $\bar c_j$, $\bar\xi_i$
Explanation. Dual variables are always $\pi_i$ (never $y$), reduced costs $\bar c_j$ and slacks $\bar s_i$, with the bar for a feasible solution and the tilde for an optimal one.
orl-f-163 · level facile · notes
Every application chapter of the lab notes follows the same eight-step outline. In which step is a minimal instance solved by hand, before turning on the solver?
  1. Guided construction of the model (step 2)
  2. Case study with reproducible CSV data (step 4)
  3. Worked numerical example (step 3)
  4. Sensitivity analysis (step 7)
Explanation. The outline is: motivation, guided construction, worked example by hand, case study, Python/Gurobi code, results, sensitivity, exercises.
orl-f-164 · level facile · notes
The notes give three reasons for using only continuous variables: didactic (duality, shadow prices, KKT), coverage (many real decisions are continuous), progression (from LP to QP and NLP with the same tool). Which of these is **not** one of them?
  1. Continuous models are always solved in polynomial time
  2. Quantities, flows, shares, prices and powers are naturally continuous
  3. The same application can grow from LP to QP and NLP
  4. Duality and KKT turn a solution into an economic explanation
Explanation. Non-convex continuous models (e.g. pricing with bilinear terms) are not solved in polynomial time in general; the other three are the reasons given in the notes.
orl-f-165 · level facile · notes
The lab classifies models as LP (everything linear), QP (quadratic objective, linear constraints) and NLP (general nonlinear objective or constraints). A model with a linear objective and the constraint $x_1^2 + x_2^2 \le d^2$ is:
  1. an LP, because the objective is linear
  2. a QP, because it contains quadratic terms
  3. an NLP, because a constraint is nonlinear
  4. not classifiable in these three classes
Explanation. A QP requires linear constraints; a quadratic constraint makes the model an NLP (here a convex one, like the conic constraints of the location chapter).
orl-f-169 · level facile · notes
The lab turns managerial problems into continuous optimization models and solves them with Gurobi. According to the notes, what is the right question at the end of every lab session?
  1. What is the optimal value of the model?
  2. Which solver finds the optimum fastest?
  3. Which decision do we recommend, and how robust is it?
  4. How many variables and constraints does the model have?
Explanation. The value of a model lies in the quality of the recommendation it supports: the optimum is the starting point, not the answer.
orl-f-170 · level facile · notes
In the lab a solved model is not only read but *interrogated*. Which of these is an example of interrogating a production plan?
  1. How much is one more hour of capacity worth?
  2. How many simplex iterations did Gurobi need?
  3. Which Python version ran the script?
  4. How many decimal digits does the solver print?
Explanation. Interrogating means asking economic questions of the solution: the value of a resource (shadow prices), the robustness to data changes, the trade-offs to recommend.
orl-f-171 · level facile · notes
The map of the models in the notes assigns each application chapter to a class (LP, QP, convex NLP, NLP, stochastic LP). Which application is a QP?
  1. The advertising budget with logarithmic responses
  2. Data Envelopment Analysis
  3. Pricing with demand $d(p)$ and capacity
  4. The Markowitz portfolio (minimum variance)
Explanation. The variance is a quadratic form and the constraints are linear: QP. The budget is a convex NLP, DEA an LP, pricing a (non-convex) NLP.
orl-f-172 · level facile · notes
The map of the models in the notes assigns each application chapter to a class (LP, QP, convex NLP, NLP, stochastic LP). To which class does the newsvendor with demand scenarios belong?
  1. QP, because the expected cost is quadratic
  2. Stochastic LP, with one pair of variables per scenario
  3. Non-convex NLP, because of the positive parts
  4. Convex NLP solved with function constraints
Explanation. With scenarios the expected cost is a weighted sum of positive parts, linearized with the variables $o_s$ and $u_s$: a stochastic LP.

Every application chapter of the notes follows an eight-step outline. In the step “Results”, what happens to the output of the solver?
  1. It is used to build a second, larger model
  2. It is translated into managerial language
  3. It is copied unchanged into the report
  4. It is compared with the output of another solver
Explanation. The steps are: motivation, guided construction, worked example by hand, case study, Python/Gurobi code, results (the output in managerial language), sensitivity, exercises.

  4. Switch to the academic licence of Gurobi
Explanation. The scripts regenerate data, results and figures: changed data mean regenerated numbers, never hand-edited ones.

In the third lab session of the essential path the student works on pricing *or* on the advertising budget. What are its learning objectives?

orl-f-246 · level facile · notes
The lab uses mathematical programming as a *decision* tool. According to the notes, where does the value of a model lie?
  1. In the precision of the optimal value it computes
  2. In the speed of the solver that solves it
  3. In the quality of the recommendation it makes possible
  4. In the number of variables and constraints it handles

orl-f-247 · level facile · notes
After solving a lab model we *interrogate* it with three kinds of questions: marginal value, robustness, compromise. Which question is about **robustness**?
  1. Does the solution hold if the data change by 5%?
  2. How much is one more hour of capacity worth?
  3. Which compromise would we advise the decision maker?
  4. What is the optimal value of the objective?
Explanation. Robustness asks whether the recommendation survives a perturbation of the data. One more hour of capacity is a marginal value (a shadow price), the compromise is a trade-off, the optimal value is not an interrogation at all.
orl-f-248 · level facile · notes
The lab classifies its models as LP (all linear), QP (quadratic objective, linear constraints) and NLP (general nonlinear functions). For which class do the notes say that convexity (every local minimum is global) **always** holds?
  1. QP
  2. NLP
  3. None: it must always be checked
  4. LP
Explanation. For an LP convexity always holds; for QPs and NLPs it depends on the functions (for a QP, on the matrix $\mathbf Q$), and the notes point it out chapter by chapter.
orl-f-249 · level facile · notes
In the notation of the lab notes, how is it written that the matrix $\mathbf Q$ is positive semidefinite?
  1. $\mathbf Q \ge 0$
  2. $\mathbf Q \succeq 0$
  3. $\mathbf Q > 0$
  4. $\det \mathbf Q \ge 0$
Explanation. Matrices are bold capital letters and $\mathbf Q \succeq 0$ means positive semidefinite; $\mathbf Q \ge 0$ would read as nonnegative entries, which is a different property.
orl-f-250 · level facile · notes
In the lab notes the objects of a model (products, channels, assets, scenarios) are numbered. How are the index sets written?
  1. Enumerated explicitly, e.g. $i \in \{1, 2, \dots, n\}$
  2. As abstract sets with no numbering, e.g. $i \in I$
  3. Only with the counter $n$, never with a set
  4. As sets of names (strings) read from the data
Explanation. Indices run over explicitly enumerated sets, so the text already uses the notation of the built model; the only non-enumerated sets are those of a graph $G = (N, A)$.
orl-f-251 · level facile · notes
Every model of the lab notes is written with the same layout: variables introduced first, numbered objective and constraint families, a description family by family. What closes each formulation?
  1. The objective function, written last
  2. The list of the data with their units
  3. The sign constraints, which define the variables
  4. The value of the optimal solution, if known
Explanation. By convention the nonnegativity constraints close the model, since they define the variables; after the model a list describes objective and constraints, with the number of constraints of each family.

The lab uses only continuous variables; one of the three reasons is *coverage*: many real decisions are naturally continuous. Which list contains only decisions of this kind?

  1. The Markowitz portfolio

  4. Pricing and revenue management
Explanation. DEA solves one LP per unit (central concept: relative efficiency). Markowitz is a QP, the budget a convex NLP, pricing an NLP.

  1. Pricing, because of the price × quantity term
  2. Continuous location of a service (Weber)
  3. Service capacity and waiting times
  4. Advertising budget with concave responses
Explanation. Pricing has the bilinear term $p \cdot q$ and is nonconvex; location, queues and budget are convex NLPs, with a certifiable global optimum.

2. Background: LP, QP, NLP and sensitivity analysis (37)

orl-f-006 · level facile · notes
Duality rules of LP. In a maximization primal, the equality constraint $\sum_{j} a_{ij} x_j = b_i$ generates in the dual a variable $\pi_i$ that is:
  1. non-negative, $\pi_i \ge 0$
  2. non-positive, $\pi_i \le 0$
  3. free, $\pi_i \gtreqless 0$
  4. fixed at $\pi_i = 0$
Explanation. By the duality rules, equality constraints of the primal give free dual variables (and free primal variables give dual equality constraints).
orl-f-007 · level facile · notes
Primal $\max \sum_j c_jx_j$ subject to $\sum_j a_{ij}x_j \le b_i$ ($i = 1, \dots, m$), with dual variables $\pi_i \ge 0$. Which dual constraint does a variable $x_j \ge 0$ generate?
  1. $\sum_{i} a_{ij}\pi_i \ge c_j$
  2. $\sum_{i} a_{ij}\pi_i \le c_j$
  3. $\sum_{i} a_{ij}\pi_i = c_j$
  4. $\sum_{i} a_{ij}\pi_i \ge b_i$
Explanation. The dual constraint of $x_j$ has as coefficients the column of $x_j$ and as right-hand side $c_j$; a non-negative variable of a maximization gives a $\ge$ constraint in the (minimization) dual.
orl-f-008 · level facile · notes
In a **minimization** LP with optimal value $\tilde z$, the shadow price of constraint $i$ is $\tilde\pi_i = \partial\tilde z/\partial b_i$. What is its sign for an active requirement $\sum_j a_{ij}x_j \ge b_i$?
  1. $\tilde\pi_i \le 0$
  2. $\tilde\pi_i \ge 0$
  3. Any sign, depending on the data
  4. Always $\tilde\pi_i = 0$
Explanation. Increasing $b_i$ shrinks the feasible region, and a narrower region cannot lower the minimum: $\tilde\pi_i \ge 0$, the marginal cost of the requirement.
orl-f-009 · level facile · notes
At the optimum of an LP, constraint $i$ has slack $\bar s_i > 0$ (it is not active). What is its shadow price $\tilde\pi_i$ (rate of change of the optimal value with $b_i$)?
  1. $\tilde\pi_i = \bar s_i$
  2. $\tilde\pi_i = -\bar s_i$
  3. It depends on the sign of $b_i$
  4. $\tilde\pi_i = 0$
Explanation. By complementary slackness $\tilde\pi_i\,\bar s_i = 0$: a resource left over is worth nothing at the margin.
orl-f-010 · level facile · notes
LP $\max \sum_j c_jx_j$ subject to $\sum_j a_{ij}x_j \le b_i$, $x \ge 0$, and dual values $\bar\pi_i$. How is the reduced cost $\bar c_j$ of the variable $x_j$ defined?
  1. $\bar c_j = c_j - \sum_{i} a_{ij}\,\bar\pi_i$
  2. $\bar c_j = \sum_{i} a_{ij}\,\bar\pi_i - b_i$
  3. $\bar c_j = c_j - \sum_{i} a_{ij}\,\bar x_j$
  4. $\bar c_j = c_j - b_i\,\bar\pi_i$
Explanation. The reduced cost is the slack of the dual constraint of $x_j$: the coefficient $c_j$ net of the value, at the dual prices, of the resources consumed by one unit of $x_j$.
orl-f-011 · level facile · notes
At the optimum of an LP the variable $x_j$ is at zero, $\tilde x_j = 0$, and its reduced cost is also zero, $\bar c_j = 0$. What can be concluded?
  1. $x_j$ is positive in every other optimal solution
  2. The LP is infeasible or unbounded
  3. Nothing certain: under degeneracy this can happen
  4. Its objective coefficient $c_j$ is zero
Explanation. Only the implication $\bar c_j \ne 0 \Rightarrow \tilde x_j$ on a bound holds, not the converse: a null reduced cost does not say that the variable is used (degeneracy, alternative optima).
orl-f-012 · level facile · notes
The LP $\max\,30x_1 + 50x_2$ (two constraints) has optimal value $\tilde z = 1900$. Constraint 1 has right-hand side $b_1 = 90$, shadow price $\tilde\pi_1 = 14$ and validity range $[40, 240]$ (the values of $b_1$ for which $\tilde\pi_1$ stays the same). What is the optimal value if $b_1 = 100$?
  1. $1914$
  2. $2040$
  3. $1900$
  4. $3300$
Explanation. $100$ lies inside the validity range, so the shadow price is exact: $1900 + (100 - 90) \cdot 14 = 2040$.
orl-f-013 · level teorema · notes
Primal (P) $\max \sum_j c_jx_j$ subject to $\sum_j a_{ij}x_j \le b_i$, $x \ge 0$; dual (D) $\min \sum_i b_i\pi_i$ subject to $\sum_i a_{ij}\pi_i \ge c_j$, $\pi \ge 0$. Which statement is the weak duality theorem?
  1. For all feasible $\bar x$ of (P) and $\bar\pi$ of (D): $\sum_{j} c_j\bar x_j \le \sum_{i} b_i\bar\pi_i$
  2. For all feasible $\bar x$ of (P) and $\bar\pi$ of (D): $\sum_{j} c_j\bar x_j \ge \sum_{i} b_i\bar\pi_i$
  3. For all feasible $\bar x$ of (P) and $\bar\pi$ of (D): $\sum_{j} c_j\bar x_j = \sum_{i} b_i\bar\pi_i$
  4. If (P) has a feasible solution, then (D) also has a feasible solution
Explanation. Every feasible solution of the dual gives an upper bound on the value of the maximization primal. Equality holds only for optimal pairs (strong duality).
orl-f-014 · level teorema · notes
Let (P) be a maximization LP and (D) its minimization dual. Which statement is the strong duality theorem?
  1. If (P) and (D) are both feasible, every pair of feasible solutions has the same value
  2. If (P) has an optimal solution, it is unique, and so is the optimal solution of (D)
  3. (P) has an optimal solution if and only if (D) has one, and then the optimal values coincide
  4. If both have optimal solutions, the optimal value of (D) is strictly larger than that of (P)
Explanation. Strong duality: an optimum of one problem exists exactly when an optimum of the other exists, and the gap closes. Existence does not mean uniqueness.
orl-f-015 · level teorema · notes
Primal $\max\{\sum_j c_jx_j : \sum_j a_{ij}x_j \le b_i,\ x \ge 0\}$; for feasible $\bar x$ and dual-feasible $\bar\pi$ let $\bar s_i = b_i - \sum_j a_{ij}\bar x_j$ (slacks) and $\bar c_j = c_j - \sum_i a_{ij}\bar\pi_i$ (reduced costs). The pair is optimal if and only if:
  1. $\bar\pi_i + \bar s_i = 0$ for every $i$ and $\bar x_j + \bar c_j = 0$ for every $j$
  2. $\bar s_i = 0$ for every $i$ and $\bar c_j = 0$ for every $j$
  3. $\bar s_i > 0 \Leftrightarrow \bar\pi_i = 0$ for every $i$ and $\bar x_j > 0 \Leftrightarrow \bar c_j = 0$ for every $j$
  4. $\bar\pi_i\,\bar s_i = 0$ for every $i$ and $\bar x_j\,\bar c_j = 0$ for every $j$
Explanation. These are the complementary slackness conditions. They give implications (slack constraint $\Rightarrow$ null dual), not equivalences: degeneracy is allowed, so the third option is too strong.
orl-f-016 · level teorema · notes
Problem $\min f(x)$ subject to $g_i(x) \le 0$ and $h_k(x) = 0$, with differentiable functions. Which statement about the KKT conditions is correct?
  1. Every point satisfying them is a global optimum, whatever $f$, $g_i$ and $h_k$
  2. They hold at every regular local optimum, and under convexity they are also sufficient
  3. They are necessary and sufficient as soon as all the constraints are linear
  4. They guarantee global optimality whenever all the multipliers are strictly positive
Explanation. The KKT conditions are necessary at regular optima and become sufficient when the problem is convex. Linear constraints do not make a non-convex $f$ harmless. For an LP they reduce to complementary slackness.
orl-f-017 · level teorema · notes
A QP has objective $\tfrac12 \sum_{i}\sum_{j} q_{ij} x_i x_j + \sum_j c_j x_j$ (to be minimized) and linear constraints. It is convex:
  1. if and only if the matrix $Q = (q_{ij})$ is positive semidefinite
  2. always, because its constraints are linear
  3. if and only if all the entries $q_{ij}$ are non-negative
  4. if and only if $Q$ is diagonal with positive entries
Explanation. The feasible set is a polyhedron (convex); the quadratic objective is convex exactly when $Q \succeq 0$. Non-negative entries are neither necessary nor sufficient, and diagonal is only a special case.
orl-f-018 · level ipotesi · notes
To prove weak duality for (P) $\max\{\sum_j c_jx_j : \sum_j a_{ij}x_j \le b_i,\ x \ge 0\}$ and its dual (D), with $\bar x$ feasible for (P) and $\bar\pi$ for (D), one uses the chain $\sum_j c_j\bar x_j \le \sum_j\sum_i a_{ij}\bar\pi_i\bar x_j \le \sum_i b_i\bar\pi_i$. Which facts are needed?
  1. Complementary slackness at $(\bar x, \bar\pi)$ and strong duality
  2. Optimality of $\bar x$ for (P) and uniqueness of $\bar\pi$
  3. First: $\sum_i a_{ij}\bar\pi_i \ge c_j$, $\bar x \ge 0$; second: $\sum_j a_{ij}\bar x_j \le b_i$, $\bar\pi \ge 0$
  4. Existence of an optimal solution of both (P) and (D)
Explanation. Only feasibility of the two solutions is used: dual feasibility multiplied by $\bar x \ge 0$, then primal feasibility multiplied by $\bar\pi \ge 0$. No optimality or strong duality is involved.
orl-f-019 · level ipotesi · notes
A point satisfies the KKT conditions of $\min f(x)$ subject to $g_i(x) \le 0$, $h_k(x) = 0$ (multipliers $\lambda_i \ge 0$ for the $g_i$). What is needed to conclude that it is a **global** optimum?
  1. The problem must be convex: $f$ and the $g_i$ convex, the $h_k$ affine
  2. All the inequality constraints $g_i$ must be active at the point
  3. All the multipliers $\lambda_i$ must be strictly positive
  4. It suffices that $f$ be differentiable at the point
Explanation. Convexity makes the KKT conditions sufficient. Active constraints or positive multipliers do not guarantee globality in a non-convex problem.
orl-f-020 · level ipotesi · notes
In a **minimization** LP with optimal value $\tilde z$, the shadow price $\tilde\pi_i = \partial\tilde z/\partial b_i$ of a capacity constraint $\sum_j a_{ij}x_j \le b_i$ satisfies $\tilde\pi_i \le 0$. Which facts give this sign?
  1. The constraint is active at the optimum and its slack is zero
  2. Raising $b_i$ enlarges the region, and a larger region never raises the minimum
  3. Strong duality and the non-negativity of all the variables
  4. The reduced costs of the basic variables are zero at the optimum
Explanation. The sign of $\tilde\pi_i$ always follows from two questions: does raising $b_i$ enlarge or shrink the feasible region? A wider region never worsens the optimum.
orl-f-021 · level facile · notes
Every chapter of the lab ends with the same six-step sensitivity protocol. What does step 3, devoted to shadow prices and reduced costs, prescribe?
  1. Build three scenarios (pessimistic, central, optimistic) and compare the plans
  2. Vary a weight in the objective and trace a cost-service frontier
  3. Check each dual against a re-optimization after a small perturbation, and read the reduced costs
  4. Flag the abrupt changes of the optimal mix as the data vary
Explanation. Step 3 verifies the duals by perturbation and reads the reduced cost of every variable at zero; scenarios are step 4, trade-offs step 5, stability step 6.
orl-f-177 · level facile · notes
Convex QP: $\min x_1^2 + 2x_2^2$ subject to $x_1 + x_2 \ge 6$, $x_1, x_2 \ge 0$. At the optimum the constraint is active and the partial derivatives equal its multiplier $\lambda$: $2x_1 = 4x_2 = \lambda$. What are $\tilde x$, the optimal value and $\lambda$?
  1. $\tilde x = (3, 3)$, value $27$, $\lambda = 6$
  2. $\tilde x = (4, 2)$, value $24$, $\lambda = 8$
  3. $\tilde x = (2, 4)$, value $36$, $\lambda = 16$
  4. $\tilde x = (6, 0)$, value $36$, $\lambda = 12$
Explanation. $x_1 = 2x_2$ and $x_1 + x_2 = 6$ give $(4, 2)$, value $16 + 8 = 24$, $\lambda = 8$. By perturbation, with right-hand side 7 the value becomes $32.67 \approx 24 + 8$.
orl-f-178 · level facile · notes
Every chapter of the lab ends with the same six-step sensitivity protocol. What does the last step, “stability”, check?
  1. That the solver status is optimal and the active constraints are the expected ones
  2. How the optimum moves when one key parameter varies over a grid
  3. How cost and service trade off along a frontier
  4. Whether the recommendation still holds when the data change by $\pm 5\%$
Explanation. Steps: base scenario, one-at-a-time, shadow prices and reduced costs, scenarios, trade-off, stability (data $\pm 5\%$).
orl-f-259 · level facile · notes
Duality rules for LPs. What is the dual of $\max\ 30x_1 + 50x_2$ subject to $x_1 + 3x_2 \le 90$, $2x_1 + x_2 \le 80$, $x_1, x_2 \ge 0$ (one dual variable $\pi_i$ per constraint)?
  1. $\min\ 90\pi_1 + 80\pi_2$, $\pi_1 + 3\pi_2 \ge 30$, $2\pi_1 + \pi_2 \ge 50$, $\pi \ge 0$
  2. $\min\ 90\pi_1 + 80\pi_2$, $\pi_1 + 2\pi_2 \ge 30$, $3\pi_1 + \pi_2 \ge 50$, $\pi \ge 0$
  3. $\max\ 90\pi_1 + 80\pi_2$, $\pi_1 + 2\pi_2 \le 30$, $3\pi_1 + \pi_2 \le 50$, $\pi \ge 0$
  4. $\min\ 30\pi_1 + 50\pi_2$, $\pi_1 + 2\pi_2 \ge 90$, $3\pi_1 + \pi_2 \ge 80$, $\pi \ge 0$
Explanation. Two $\le$ constraints in a maximum give $\pi_1, \pi_2 \ge 0$; each variable $x_j \ge 0$ gives a $\ge c_j$ constraint whose coefficients are the *columns* of the primal; the right-hand sides $(90, 80)$ become the costs of a minimum. Using the rows is the typical mistake.
orl-f-260 · level facile · notes
Duality rules for LPs, read for a **minimization** primal (the table is read from right to left). Which sign has the dual variable $\pi_i$ of a constraint $\sum_j a_{ij}x_j \le b_i$?
  1. $\pi_i \ge 0$
  2. $\pi_i$ free
  3. $\pi_i \le 0$
  4. It depends on the sign of $b_i$
Explanation. In a minimum a $\ge$ constraint gives $\pi_i \ge 0$, an equality a free $\pi_i$, a $\le$ constraint $\pi_i \le 0$ (as in the «all cases» LP of the notes, where $\tilde\pi_3 = -1$).
orl-f-261 · level teorema · notes
Let (P) be a maximization LP and (D) its minimization dual. (P) is feasible and **unbounded** (its objective grows without limit). What does weak duality imply for (D)?
  1. (D) is infeasible
  2. (D) is unbounded too
  3. (D) has an optimal value $+\infty$ attained at a vertex
  4. Nothing: weak duality concerns only optimal solutions
Explanation. Weak duality says that every feasible $\bar\pi$ of (D) gives an upper bound $\sum_i b_i\bar\pi_i$ on every primal value: if the primal values are unbounded, no such bound exists, so (D) has no feasible solution.
orl-f-262 · level ipotesi · notes
The strong duality theorem says: (P) has an optimal solution if and only if (D) has one, and then the optimal values coincide. Its hypothesis is that (P) **has an optimum**. If instead (P) is infeasible, what can be said of (D)?
  1. (D) has an optimum, with value 0
  2. (D) is always unbounded, never infeasible
  3. (D) is always infeasible as well
  4. (D) is infeasible or unbounded
Explanation. Without an optimum of (P) the theorem says only that (D) has no optimum either: (D) can be unbounded or itself infeasible (there are pairs of LPs that are both infeasible).
orl-f-263 · level facile · notes
Complementary slackness for an LP: $\bar\pi_i\bar s_i = 0$ (dual value times slack of constraint $i$) and $\bar x_j\bar c_j = 0$ (variable times reduced cost). At the optimum, which implication **always** holds?
  1. Slack constraint ($\bar s_i \ne 0$) $\Rightarrow$ $\bar\pi_i = 0$
  2. $\bar\pi_i = 0$ $\Rightarrow$ the constraint is slack
  3. Variable at zero ($\bar x_j = 0$) $\Rightarrow$ $\bar c_j \ne 0$
  4. Active constraint ($\bar s_i = 0$) $\Rightarrow$ $\bar\pi_i \ne 0$
Explanation. The conditions are implications, not equivalences: a slack constraint has zero dual and a nonzero variable has zero reduced cost. Because of degeneracy an active constraint can have a zero dual, and a variable at zero a zero reduced cost.
orl-f-264 · level ipotesi · notes
For an LP, $\bar x$ and $\bar\pi$ satisfy $\bar\pi_i\bar s_i = 0$ for every constraint $i$ and $\bar x_j\bar c_j = 0$ for every variable $j$ ($\bar s_i$ slacks, $\bar c_j$ reduced costs). What else is needed to conclude that both are optimal?
  1. Nothing else: complementary slackness alone suffices
  2. That the primal has a unique optimal solution
  3. That $\bar x$ is feasible for (P) and $\bar\pi$ for (D)
  4. That every constraint of the primal is active
Explanation. The theorem is about a pair of **feasible** solutions: feasibility plus complementary slackness gives equal objective values, hence optimality by weak duality. An infeasible pair can satisfy the products and be far from optimal.
orl-f-265 · level teorema · notes
(P) is a maximization LP and (D) its dual. A feasible $\bar x$ of (P) and a feasible $\bar\pi$ of (D) have the **same** objective value, $\sum_j c_j\bar x_j = \sum_i b_i\bar\pi_i$. What follows?
  1. Nothing, unless strong duality is checked first
  2. Both are optimal, by weak duality
  3. Only $\bar x$ is optimal; $\bar\pi$ may not be
  4. The two problems have unique optimal solutions
Explanation. By weak duality every primal value is $\le \sum_i b_i\bar\pi_i$, which $\bar x$ attains: $\bar x$ is optimal, and symmetrically $\bar\pi$. Equal values certify optimality; uniqueness is not implied.
orl-f-266 · level facile · notes
LP sensitivity analysis: what does the validity interval $[c_j^{\min}, c_j^{\max}]$ of the objective coefficient $c_j$ (Gurobi attributes `SAObjLow`–`SAObjUp`) mean?
  1. Varying only $c_j$ in it, the optimal value does not change
  2. Varying only $c_j$ in it, the shadow prices do not change
  3. Varying only $c_j$ in it, the optimal solution does not change
  4. Varying only $c_j$ in it, $x_j$ stays strictly positive
Explanation. It is the largest interval in which, changing only $c_j$, the optimal solution stays the same; the optimal value does change (by $\tilde x_j$ per unit of $c_j$). For a variable at zero one endpoint is the threshold at which it becomes worth using.
orl-f-267 · level facile · notes
LP $\max\ 30x_1 + 50x_2$ ($x_1 + 3x_2 \le 90$, $2x_1 + x_2 \le 80$) with a third variable $x_3$ ($c_3 = 20$, consumptions $1$ and $1$): at the optimum $x_3 = 0$, reduced cost $\bar c_3 = -2$, threshold $c_3^{\max} = 22$. With $c_3 = 23$ the new optimum is $(0, 5, 75)$. What does this show?
  1. The reduced cost was computed wrongly
  2. The reduced cost says *when* $x_3$ enters, not *how* the solution changes
  3. Above the threshold $x_3$ enters with one unit only
  4. Above the threshold the old solution stays optimal
Explanation. Past $c_3^{\max} = 22$ the variable becomes worth using, but the new solution must be recomputed: here it changes completely, from $(30, 20, 0)$ to $(0, 5, 75)$ with value $1975$.
orl-f-268 · level media · notes
For which values of the parameter $\gamma$ is $f(x_1, x_2) = x_1^2 + \gamma x_1x_2 + x_2^2$ convex? (A quadratic function $\tfrac12\mathbf x'\mathbf Q\mathbf x$ is convex if and only if $\mathbf Q \succeq 0$.)
  1. For every $\gamma \ge 0$
  2. For $|\gamma| \le 1$
  3. For $|\gamma| \le 2$
  4. For every real $\gamma$
Explanation. Here $\mathbf Q = \begin{pmatrix} 2 & \gamma \\ \gamma & 2 \end{pmatrix}$: diagonal entries positive and $\det\mathbf Q = 4 - \gamma^2 \ge 0$, i.e. $|\gamma| \le 2$. Forgetting the factor $\tfrac12$ gives $|\gamma| \le 1$.
orl-f-269 · level facile · notes
Convex QP $\min\ x_1^2 + 2x_2^2$ subject to $x_1 + x_2 \ge d$, $x \ge 0$: with $d = 6$ the optimal value is $24$ and the multiplier $\lambda = 8$. With $d = 7$ the optimal value is $32.67$, not $24 + 8 = 32$. Why?
  1. The multiplier is exact only at the margin (curvature)
  2. The solver uses too loose a feasibility tolerance
  3. With $d = 7$ the constraint is no longer active
  4. The multiplier of a QP is never a marginal value
Explanation. Here $\tilde f(d) = \tfrac23 d^2$, so $\tilde f(6 + \varepsilon) = 24 + 8\varepsilon + \tfrac23\varepsilon^2$: the multiplier gives the first-order term, and the quadratic term of the curvature adds $0.67$ for $\varepsilon = 1$.
orl-f-270 · level facile · notes
KKT conditions for $\min f(\mathbf x)$ subject to $g_i(\mathbf x) \le 0$ ($i = 1, \dots, m$) and $h_j(\mathbf x) = 0$ ($j = 1, \dots, q$). How is the Lagrangian written in the lab notes?
  1. $f - \sum_i\lambda_ig_i - \sum_j\nu_jh_j$, with $\lambda_i \le 0$
  2. $f + \sum_i\lambda_ig_i + \sum_j\nu_jh_j$, with $\nu_j \ge 0$ and $\lambda_i$ free
  3. $\sum_i\lambda_ig_i + \sum_j\nu_jh_j$, with $\lambda_i \ge 0$
  4. $f + \sum_i\lambda_ig_i + \sum_j\nu_jh_j$, with $\lambda_i \ge 0$
Explanation. $L = f + \sum_i\lambda_ig_i + \sum_j\nu_jh_j$: the multipliers of the inequalities $g_i \le 0$ are nonnegative, those of the equalities free. Stationarity asks $\partial L/\partial x_k = 0$, complementarity $\lambda_ig_i = 0$.
orl-f-271 · level teorema · notes
What do the KKT conditions become when applied to an LP (written as $\min$ with constraints $g_i \le 0$)?
  1. They lose meaning, because an LP has no derivatives
  2. Exactly complementary slackness, with dual feasibility
  3. Only the primal feasibility conditions of the LP
  4. Weaker conditions, necessary but never sufficient
Explanation. For an LP the multipliers $\lambda_i$ are the duals $\pi_i$, those of the sign constraints are the reduced costs; stationarity is dual feasibility and complementarity is complementary slackness. And since an LP is convex, they are also sufficient.
orl-f-272 · level media · notes
Solve with the KKT conditions $\min\ (x - 3)^2 + (y - 1)^2$ subject to $x + 2y \le 2$, $x, y \ge 0$. Which is the optimum, with its active constraints?
  1. $(2; 0)$: active $x + 2y \le 2$ and $y \ge 0$, both multipliers $= 2$
  2. $(2.4; -0.2)$: active $x + 2y \le 2$ only, multiplier $1.2$
  3. $(3; 1)$: no active constraint, all multipliers zero
  4. $(0; 1)$: active $x + 2y \le 2$ and $x \ge 0$, multiplier $0$
Explanation. The free minimum $(3, 1)$ violates $x + 2y \le 2$; projecting on the line gives $(2.4, -0.2)$, which violates $y \ge 0$. With $y = 0$ and $x = 2$: $2(x - 3) + \lambda = 0$ gives $\lambda = 2$, and $2(y - 1) + 2\lambda - \mu = 0$ gives $\mu = 2 \ge 0$. The problem is convex, so the KKT point is the global optimum (value $2$).
orl-f-273 · level ipotesi · notes
The KKT theorem of the notes holds «at a **regular** optimal point». What can happen at an optimum where the regularity hypothesis fails?
  1. The point cannot be a local minimum at all
  2. The multipliers exist but are all equal to zero
  3. KKT multipliers may fail to exist
  4. The conditions become sufficient without convexity
Explanation. Regularity (a constraint qualification) is what guarantees the existence of the multipliers: without it an optimum may fail to satisfy stationarity for any choice of $\lambda_i \ge 0$, $\nu_j$.
orl-f-274 · level facile · notes
Every lab session ends with the same six-step sensitivity protocol. What does step 2, «one-at-a-time», prescribe?
  1. Change all parameters together by $\pm 5\%$
  2. Vary one key parameter on a grid and plot value and decisions
  3. Solve the model once per scenario of a pessimistic–optimistic set
  4. Remove one constraint at a time and solve again
Explanation. One-at-a-time: one key parameter moves on a grid, the others stay fixed, and the optimal value and decisions are plotted. Scenarios are step 4, stability step 6.
orl-f-275 · level facile · notes
Six-step sensitivity protocol of the lab. In which step are frontiers such as cost–service, risk–return or cost–emissions traced, by varying a weight or a threshold?
  1. Step 1, base scenario
  2. Step 3, shadow prices and reduced costs
  3. Step 4, scenarios
  4. Step 5, trade-off
Explanation. Step 5 (trade-off) varies a weight or a threshold to trace a frontier; step 4 builds at least three coherent scenarios (pessimistic, central, optimistic).
orl-f-518 · level teorema · notes
LP $\max\ 30x_1 + 50x_2$ subject to $x_1 + 3x_2 \le 90$, $2x_1 + x_2 \le 80$, $x \ge 0$, and its dual $\min\ 90\pi_1 + 80\pi_2$ subject to $\pi_1 + 2\pi_2 \ge 30$, $3\pi_1 + \pi_2 \ge 50$, $\pi \ge 0$. $\bar x = (30, 20)$ and $\bar\pi = (14, 8)$ are feasible and both objectives equal $1900$. What does this certify, and by which theorem?
  1. Only that $\bar x$ is feasible
  2. That the dual is unbounded
  3. Both optimal: strong duality
  4. That the optimum of (P) is unique
Explanation. $90\cdot 14 + 80\cdot 8 = 1260 + 640 = 1900 = 30\cdot 30 + 50\cdot 20$: a feasible primal–dual pair with equal values is optimal (weak duality makes equality a certificate; strong duality guarantees that such a pair exists).
orl-f-519 · level teorema · notes
Strong duality for LPs: (P) has an optimal solution if and only if (D) has one, and then the optimal values coincide. What does it **not** say?
  1. That the optimal solutions are unique
  2. That the optimal values are equal
  3. That an optimum of (D) exists when (P) has one
  4. That the duality gap closes at the optimum
Explanation. Existence is not uniqueness: there can be several optimal solutions (e.g. with degeneracy or parallel objective), but all share the same optimal value.

3. The solver: building, solving, interpreting (41)

orl-f-022 · level facile · notes
In gurobipy, `m` is a `Model`. What does `m.addVar(name='b')` create, with no other argument?
  1. A free continuous variable
  2. A continuous variable with `lb=0` and `ub=+inf`
  3. A binary variable
  4. A continuous variable in $[0, 1]$
Explanation. The default is continuous with lower bound $0$: a free variable (SVM intercept, CVaR threshold) needs `lb=-GRB.INFINITY`. Forgetting it is the most frequent mistake in the lab.
orl-f-023 · level facile · notes
After solving an LP with gurobipy, which attributes of a constraint give its shadow price and the validity range of its right-hand side?
  1. `X` and `SAObjLow`/`SAObjUp`
  2. `RC` and `SARHSLow`/`SARHSUp`
  3. `Pi` and `SARHSLow`/`SARHSUp`
  4. `Slack` and `SAObjLow`/`SAObjUp`
Explanation. `Pi` on the constraint is $\tilde\pi_i$ and `SARHSLow`–`SARHSUp` is the range $[b_i^{\min}, b_i^{\max}]$; `X`, `RC` and `SAObjLow`/`SAObjUp` belong to the variables.
orl-f-024 · level facile · notes
In gurobipy, after `m.optimize()`, what must be checked before reading the values `x.X` of the variables or `c.Pi` of the constraints?
  1. That `m.Status == GRB.OPTIMAL`
  2. That `m.ObjVal` is positive
  3. That all the `Slack` values are zero
  4. That `m.Params.OutputFlag == 1`
Explanation. Values can be read only if the status is optimal (code 2); otherwise one diagnoses infeasibility (code 3) or unboundedness (code 5).
orl-f-025 · level facile · notes
After `m.optimize()` the status of a gurobipy model is `GRB.INFEASIBLE`. What is the recommended diagnosis?
  1. Set `m.Params.NonConvex = 2` and re-optimize
  2. Raise `m.Params.TimeLimit` and re-optimize
  3. Read the reduced costs `RC` of the variables at zero
  4. Call `m.computeIIS()` and read the `.ilp` file it writes
Explanation. The IIS is a minimal subset of mutually incompatible constraints, e.g. total demand larger than total capacity without a shortage variable.
orl-f-026 · level facile · notes
In gurobipy the constraint `constr` is written `quicksum(x[v,t] for v in V) + base[t] <= C`, where `base[t]` is a constant (the base load of hour `t`) and `C` is the meter capacity. To set the capacity to `nuovaC` in a sensitivity analysis one writes:
  1. `constr.RHS = nuovaC`
  2. `constr.RHS = nuovaC - base[t]`
  3. `constr.RHS = nuovaC + base[t]`
  4. `constr.Pi = nuovaC`
Explanation. Gurobi moves the constant to the right-hand side: the stored RHS is `C - base[t]`. Writing `RHS = nuovaC` would relax the constraint by `base[t]` (the bug of the EV case study).
orl-f-027 · level facile · notes
A gurobipy model contains the product $p \cdot q$ of two decision variables (price times quantity, a bilinear term). Which parameter must be set for Gurobi to solve it?
  1. `m.Params.NonConvex = 2`
  2. `m.Params.FuncNonlinear = 1`
  3. `m.Params.QCPDual = 1`
  4. None: bilinear terms are convex
Explanation. Bilinear (non-convex) terms require `NonConvex = 2`; `FuncNonlinear = 1` is for the general functions introduced with `addGenConstrLog/Exp/Pow`.
orl-f-028 · level facile · notes
The objective of a gurobipy model must contain $\log(1 + k x)$, with $x$ a decision variable and $k > 0$ a datum. How is this term introduced?
  1. Directly in `setObjective`, writing `gp.log(1 + k*x)`
  2. With a piecewise-linear approximation of the log written by hand
  3. With auxiliary variables $g = 1 + kx$, $z$ and the constraint `m.addGenConstrLog(g, z)`
  4. It cannot: Gurobi accepts only linear and quadratic expressions
Explanation. Nonlinear functions enter as function constraints on auxiliary variables; with `FuncNonlinear = 1` they are handled exactly and the optimum is certified global.
orl-f-029 · level facile · notes
For a gurobipy model with function constraints (e.g. `addGenConstrLog`) the solver returns no duals. How is the multiplier of a constraint estimated?
  1. It is taken as zero, since such constraints have no dual
  2. Raise the right-hand side by $\varepsilon$, re-optimize, take the incremental ratio
  3. From the reduced costs `RC` of the variables in the constraint
  4. From the attributes `SARHSLow` and `SARHSUp` of the constraint
Explanation. Perturbation is the general tool of the lab: new optimum $\approx$ old optimum $+$ multiplier $\cdot\,\varepsilon$.
orl-f-030 · level facile · notes
The Gurobi log of a model reports a range of the matrix coefficients from $10^{-6}$ to $10^{9}$. What is the first thing to do?
  1. Increase the time limit, since badly scaled models are slow
  2. Switch to a local solver, less sensitive to large coefficients
  3. Set `NonConvex = 2`, since large ranges signal non-convexity
  4. Change the units (thousands of €, MWh, ...): the model is badly scaled
Explanation. Very different orders of magnitude signal bad scaling: rescale the data before looking for other errors.
orl-f-031 · level facile · notes
A gurobipy model meant to maximize profit, with non-negative variables, returns optimum $0$ with all variables at zero. What is the most likely cause?
  1. The objective is missing or has the wrong sense (e.g. `GRB.MINIMIZE`)
  2. The model is infeasible and Gurobi returns the origin
  3. The size limit of the pip licence has been exceeded
  4. A variable was declared free with `lb=-GRB.INFINITY`
Explanation. With non-negative variables, minimizing a profit gives $x = 0$: check `setObjective(..., GRB.MAXIMIZE)`. An infeasible model returns no solution at all.
orl-f-032 · level facile · notes
In gurobipy, after `x = m.addVars(I, T, name='x')` (a `tupledict` indexed by pairs `(i, t)`), how is the sum of `x[i,t]` over all `t`, for a fixed `i`, written?
  1. `x.sum('*', i)`
  2. `sum(x)`
  3. `x.sum(i, '*')`
  4. `gp.quicksum(x[i])`
Explanation. In a `tupledict` the wildcard `'*'` runs over one index: `x.sum(i, '*')` sums over the second index; `gp.quicksum(x[i, t] for t in T)` is the alternative.
orl-f-033 · level facile · notes
The lab uses a single solver, Gurobi, for all its models, including the non-convex ones (pricing). Why?
  1. Because local solvers are slower on convex problems, the most common ones
  2. Because it certifies a global optimum also for non-convex models, with one syntax
  3. Because Gurobi reformulates every model as an equivalent LP
  4. Because Gurobi returns the duals for every class of model
Explanation. With a local solver every result would carry the doubt “is it only a local optimum?”. Duals are not available with function or bilinear constraints.
orl-f-179 · level facile · notes
In gurobipy, which parameter, set before `m.optimize()`, stops the solver from printing its log on screen?
  1. `m.Params.TimeLimit = 0`
  2. `m.Params.NonConvex = 0`
  3. `m.Params.OutputFlag = 0`
  4. `m.Params.FuncNonlinear = 0`
Explanation. `OutputFlag` is 1 by default (log on screen) and 0 for a silent run; `TimeLimit` bounds the seconds, `NonConvex` and `FuncNonlinear` concern non-convex terms and functions.
orl-f-180 · level facile · notes
After solving an LP with gurobipy, which attribute of a constraint tells whether it is active (binding) at the optimum?
  1. `c.Pi`, which is non-zero exactly when the constraint is active
  2. `c.Slack`, which is $0$ when the constraint is active
  3. `c.SARHSUp`, which equals the right-hand side when it is active
  4. `c.RC`, which is positive when the constraint is active
Explanation. An active constraint has zero slack. A null `Pi` does not imply a slack constraint (degeneracy), and `RC` belongs to variables, not constraints.
orl-f-181 · level facile · notes
A gurobipy script created `x = m.addVars(I, T)` with `I` a list of strings. Reading `x[1, t]` raises a `KeyError`. What is the most likely cause?
  1. The model has not been optimized yet
  2. The variables were declared with the default bounds
  3. The licence limit has been exceeded
  4. The indices used differ from those of `addVars` (int instead of str)
Explanation. A `tupledict` is indexed exactly by the keys given to `addVars`: the integer `1` is not the string `'1'`. Check the types of the indices.
orl-f-182 · level facile · notes
Every gurobipy script of the lab follows the same skeleton: data, model, solution, results, sensitivity. Which instruction comes right after `m.optimize()` in the “solution” block?
  1. `assert m.Status == GRB.OPTIMAL`, to stop on an unexpected status
  2. `m.setObjective(...)`, to fix the objective
  3. `m.addConstrs(...)`, to add the missing constraints
  4. `m.computeIIS()`, to be run in every script
Explanation. The skeleton checks the status before reading any value; objective and constraints belong to the model block, `computeIIS()` only to the diagnosis of an infeasible model.
orl-f-183 · level facile · notes
The standard gurobipy skeleton of the lab has five blocks: data, model, solution, results, sensitivity. What does the sensitivity block do?
  1. It reads the CSV files and defines the parameters
  2. It creates variables, constraints and objective
  3. It loops over a key parameter, re-optimizes and saves figures
  4. It prints `.X`, `.Pi` and `.RC` in a readable report
Explanation. Data come first, then the model, the solution (with the status check), the report of `.X`, `.Pi`, `.RC`, and finally the loop over a parameter.
orl-f-184 · level facile · notes
A convex QP (e.g. Markowitz) is solved with gurobipy, and the attribute `Pi` of its linear constraints is not available. What does the lab suggest?
  1. Declare the model with `NonConvex = 2`
  2. Read `SARHSLow`/`SARHSUp` instead of `Pi`
  3. Set `m.Params.QCPDual = 1`, or check the multiplier by perturbation
  4. Assume all multipliers are zero
Explanation. For QPs the LP duals are not returned by default: `QCPDual = 1` asks for them, and perturbing the right-hand side by $\varepsilon$ checks them.
orl-f-185 · level facile · notes
Gurobi handles nonlinear models through special parameters: `NonConvex = 2` for bilinear terms, `FuncNonlinear = 1` for functions such as $\log$. Which lab model needs neither, being purely conic?
  1. Pricing, with the term $p \cdot q$
  2. The advertising budget, with $\log(1 + k_ix_i)$
  3. The M/M/1 queue, with the constraint $w(\mu - \lambda) = 1$
  4. Weber location, with $d_x^2 + d_y^2 \le d_i^2$
Explanation. Convex quadratic or conic structures (a convex QCP) need nothing special; bilinear terms need `NonConvex = 2`, function constraints `FuncNonlinear = 1`.
orl-f-242 · level facile · notes
A gurobipy model minimizes a total cost and, after `m.optimize()`, its status is `GRB.UNBOUNDED`. What is the most likely cause?
  1. A missing constraint or bound lets the cost decrease without limit
  2. Total demand exceeds total capacity and no shortage variable exists
  3. The coefficients span too many orders of magnitude (bad scaling)
  4. The time limit `TimeLimit` stopped the solver before the optimum
Explanation. Unbounded means the objective improves indefinitely: almost always a constraint or a bound is missing. Demand above capacity gives `INFEASIBLE`, a time limit gives `TIME_LIMIT`.
orl-f-243 · level facile · notes
Before solving, the Gurobi log prints the line `Optimize a model with 24 rows, 36 columns and 72 nonzeros`. What are the 24 rows and the 36 columns?
  1. 24 variables and 36 constraints
  2. 24 iterations and 36 seconds
  3. 24 constraints and 36 variables
  4. 24 data rows and 36 data columns of the CSV
Explanation. Rows are constraints, columns are variables, nonzeros the non-null coefficients of the matrix: the first check is that the model has the expected size.
orl-f-276 · level facile · notes
`python3 -m pip install gurobipy` installs Gurobi together with a demo licence. What are its limits?
  1. Only LPs: QPs and NLPs are excluded
  2. Up to 2000 variables and 2000 constraints
  3. Up to 30 days of use, then it expires
  4. Up to 200 variables and no limit on constraints
Explanation. The pip licence covers models with up to 2000 variables and 2000 constraints, enough for almost all the models of the notes; beyond, the free academic licence is needed.
orl-f-277 · level facile · notes
How is the full (free) academic Gurobi licence obtained, according to the lab notes?
  1. It is already included in `pip install gurobipy`
  2. By buying it from the Gurobi website with any email
  3. By setting `m.Params.License = 'academic'`
  4. University email on the Gurobi portal, then `grbgetkey`
Explanation. One registers on portal.gurobi.com with the institutional email, requests a Named-User Academic License and runs the `grbgetkey` command given by the portal, from the university network or VPN.
orl-f-278 · level facile · notes
A gurobipy script stops with the error `Model too large for size-limited license`. What is the remedy?
  1. Activate the full academic licence
  2. Set `m.Params.OutputFlag = 0` to save memory
  3. Split the model into two smaller models solved separately
  4. Declare all variables with `lb=-GRB.INFINITY`
Explanation. The pip licence stops at 2000 variables and 2000 constraints; the full academic licence removes the limit. Splitting the model would change the problem.
orl-f-279 · level facile · notes
In gurobipy the lab writes `v1 = m.addConstr(x1 + 3*x2 <= 90, name='vincolo1')`, keeping the returned object in `v1`. Why?
  1. Otherwise the constraint is not added to the model
  2. To be able to delete it before `m.optimize()`
  3. To read later its attributes, such as the dual `v1.Pi`
  4. Because `addConstr` requires an assignment
Explanation. `addConstr` adds the constraint in any case; keeping the object is what allows reading `Pi`, `Slack`, `SARHSLow/Up` after the solve, or changing its `RHS` in a sensitivity loop.
orl-f-280 · level facile · notes
Step 5 of building a gurobipy model in the lab is `m.write('lp_2x2.lp')`. What is it for?
  1. It saves the optimal solution to a file
  2. It writes the model in readable form, for checking
  3. It is required before calling `m.optimize()`
  4. It exports the log of the solver to a file
Explanation. It is a debugging step (optional): the `.lp` file shows objective and constraints as Gurobi received them, the quickest way to catch a sign or index error.
orl-f-281 · level facile · notes
A gurobipy script sets `m.Params.TimeLimit = 60` before `m.optimize()`. What happens if the solver has not finished after 60 seconds?
  1. The solve restarts with looser tolerances
  2. The best solution found is reported with status `OPTIMAL`
  3. Python raises an exception and the model is lost
  4. It stops, with status `TIME_LIMIT` (code 9)
Explanation. The limit is in seconds; when it is reached `m.Status` is `TIME_LIMIT` (9), not `OPTIMAL`: this is why the status is always checked before reading any number.
orl-f-282 · level facile · notes
In gurobipy, after `m.optimize()`, `m.Status` is an integer code. Which code corresponds to `GRB.OPTIMAL`?
  1. 0
  2. 1
  3. 2
  4. 3
Explanation. The table of the notes: `OPTIMAL` = 2, `INFEASIBLE` = 3, `UNBOUNDED` = 5, `TIME_LIMIT` = 9. In code one compares with the named constant, `m.Status == GRB.OPTIMAL`.
orl-f-283 · level facile · notes
After solving an LP with gurobipy, which attributes of a **variable** give the validity interval of its objective coefficient (the range in which the optimal solution does not change)?
  1. `SAObjLow` and `SAObjUp`
  2. `SARHSLow` and `SARHSUp`
  3. `LB` and `UB` of the variable
  4. `RC` and `Slack`
Explanation. `SAObjLow`–`SAObjUp` is $[c_j^{\min}, c_j^{\max}]$; `SARHSLow`–`SARHSUp` is the interval of a constraint's right-hand side; `LB`/`UB` are the bounds of the variable.
orl-f-284 · level facile · notes
The interpretation checklist of the lab has seven points, from «is the status `OPTIMAL`?» onwards. What is the last one?
  1. Print all the variables with six decimals
  2. Save the model and the solution to an `.lp` file
  3. Solve again with a different solver
  4. Write a three-line managerial recommendation
Explanation. The checklist ends with the recommendation: status, order of magnitude, active constraints, shadow prices, reduced costs, stability under 5% perturbations, then three lines for the decision maker.
orl-f-285 · level facile · notes
In the interpretation checklist of the lab, what should be read for each variable that is at zero in the optimal solution of an LP?
  1. Its `SARHSLow`/`SARHSUp` validity interval
  2. Its reduced cost `RC`
  3. Its `Slack`, which must be positive
  4. Nothing: a variable at zero tells nothing
Explanation. The reduced cost of a variable at zero is the improvement of $c_j$ needed for it to enter; `Slack` and `SARHS` are attributes of constraints, not of variables.
orl-f-286 · level facile · notes
gurobipy output of the LP $\max\ 30x_1 + 50x_2$: `vincolo1: Slack = 0, Pi = 14.0, SARHS = [40, 240]`, `vincolo2: Slack = 0, Pi = 8.0, SARHS = [30, 180]`. Only one right-hand side can be raised by a few units, at the same cost per unit. Which one, and why?
  1. $b_2$: its validity interval $[30, 180]$ is narrower
  2. Either one: both constraints are active at the optimum
  3. $b_1$: one unit is worth $14$ against $8$
  4. Neither: active constraints cannot be relaxed
Explanation. Shadow prices compare resources: one unit of $b_1$ raises the optimum by $14$, one of $b_2$ by $8$, valid while $b_1$ stays in $[40, 240]$.
orl-f-287 · level facile · notes
gurobipy output of a **minimization** LP: the constraint `vincolo3`, $x_1 - 2x_2 \le -20$, has `Slack = 0`, `Pi = -1.0`, `SARHS = [-200, inf]`. How is it read?
  1. Raising $b_3$ by 1 lowers the optimal cost by 1
  2. Raising $b_3$ by 1 raises the optimal cost by 1
  3. The constraint is not active, so it can be removed
  4. A negative `Pi` signals an error in the model
Explanation. $\texttt{Pi} = \partial\tilde z/\partial b_3 = -1$: a $\le$ constraint in a minimum has $\texttt{Pi} \le 0$, and loosening it saves $1$ per unit (check: $b_3 = -19$ gives $\tilde z = 619$ instead of $620$).
orl-f-288 · level facile · notes
gurobipy output of a **minimization** LP: the variable $x_3 \le 0$ (declared with `ub=0`) has `X = 0`, `RC = -3.0`, `SAObj = [-inf, -6.0]`; its objective coefficient is $c_3 = -9$. How is it read?
  1. $x_3$ is basic and could move away from zero for free
  2. At its upper bound; it enters only if $c_3 \ge -6$
  3. $x_3$ is at its upper bound and lowering $c_3$ makes it enter
  4. The negative `RC` means the solution is not optimal
Explanation. Zero is the upper bound of a variable $\le 0$; forcing $x_3 = -1$ would worsen the cost by $3$, and the variable becomes worth using only when $c_3$ reaches the endpoint `SAObjUp` $= -6$.
orl-f-289 · level facile · notes
In a minimum-cost flow LP solved with gurobipy, an arc is saturated (its flow equals its capacity $u$, a variable at its upper bound) and has `RC` $= -4$. How is it read?
  1. The arc is too expensive and should carry less flow
  2. The solution is degenerate and `RC` has no meaning
  3. Raising its cost by 4 would make it worth using
  4. One more unit of capacity on that arc would save 4
Explanation. For a variable at its upper bound `RC` is the shadow price of the bound: in a minimum, a saturated arc with $\texttt{RC} < 0$ saves $|\texttt{RC}|$ per extra unit of capacity.
orl-f-290 · level facile · notes
A lab model contains the mean time in an M/M/1 queue, $w = 1/(\mu - \lambda)$, with capacity $\mu$ a decision variable. How is it written for Gurobi?
  1. Directly as `w == 1/(mu - lam)` in `addConstr`
  2. With `addGenConstrLog` applied to $\mu - \lambda$
  3. With the bilinear constraint $w(\mu - \lambda) = 1$
  4. By fixing $\mu$ and solving one LP per value of $\mu$
Explanation. Ratios are reformulated with an auxiliary variable and a bilinear constraint, `m.addQConstr(w * (mu - lam) == 1)`, which requires `NonConvex = 2`; gurobipy does not accept a division by a variable.
orl-f-291 · level facile · notes
A lab script estimates differences between two close optimal values (a marginal analysis). Which Gurobi parameters do the notes tighten to $10^{-9}$?
  1. `TimeLimit` and `OutputFlag`
  2. `FeasibilityTol` and `OptimalityTol`
  3. `NonConvex` and `FuncNonlinear`
  4. `Threads` and `Seed` of the solver
Explanation. For accurate marginal analyses the tolerances are tightened: `MIPGap`, `FeasibilityTol` and `OptimalityTol` at $10^{-9}$. The other parameters control time, log and nonlinear features.
orl-f-292 · level facile · notes
A pricing model with constant-elasticity demand needs the constraint $q \le A\,p^{-\varepsilon}$ ($p$ price, $q$ quantity, $\varepsilon > 1$), where $p^{-\varepsilon}$ is tiny. How do the notes rewrite it?
  1. As $q\cdot r \le A$ with an auxiliary $r = p^{\varepsilon}$
  2. As $\log q \le \log A + \varepsilon\log p$
  3. As $q \le A$, dropping the price term
  4. As $q\,p \le A$, a bilinear constraint
Explanation. Scaling: with $r = p^{\varepsilon}$ (a power constraint) the condition becomes $q\,r \le A$, which keeps the numbers in a healthy range. The logarithmic form has the wrong sign of the exponent.
orl-f-293 · level facile · notes
A multiplier is estimated by perturbation: the right-hand side is raised by $\varepsilon$, the model is solved again and the difference of the two optimal values is divided by $\varepsilon$. Why do the lab scripts tighten the solver tolerances to $10^{-9}$ for this?
  1. Because otherwise Gurobi refuses to solve twice
  2. Because the multiplier must be an integer
  3. To make the two solves faster
  4. Otherwise numerical error dominates the difference
Explanation. The incremental ratio divides a small difference by a small $\varepsilon$: with default tolerances (around $10^{-6}$) the numerical noise of each optimum can be comparable to the difference itself.
orl-f-294 · level facile · notes
A model with a functional constraint (`addGenConstrLog`) gives no duals. The budget constraint is raised from $100$ to $100.1$ and the optimal value goes from $500$ to $501.2$. What is the perturbation estimate of its multiplier?
  1. $1.2$
  2. $12$
  3. $0.12$
  4. $120$
Explanation. Incremental ratio: $(501.2 - 500)/0.1 = 12$ per unit of right-hand side. Forgetting to divide by $\varepsilon = 0.1$ gives $1.2$.
orl-f-520 · level media · notes
A gurobipy production LP returns `INFEASIBLE`. `m.computeIIS()` writes a conflict made of the capacity constraints of months 1–4 and the balances of the same months. What is the most sensible diagnosis?
  1. A sign error in the objective
  2. Early demand exceeds early capacity
  3. A variable declared free by mistake
  4. The model is badly scaled overall
Explanation. An irreducible inconsistent set (IIS) is a minimal set of constraints that cannot hold together: here capacity and balances of the first four months, i.e. their demand cannot be served in time. Remedies: more capacity, or lost demand with a penalty.

18. Organization of the lab (15)

orl-f-158 · level facile · notes
The lab notes close with a checklist of the most frequent mistakes made with gurobipy and the models. Which of these is in that checklist?
  1. Checking `m.Status` before reading the solution
  2. Quoting each shadow price with its validity range
  3. Reading `.X` or `.Pi` without checking `m.Status`
  4. Writing the model to a `.lp` file for debugging
Explanation. The other three are good practices recommended by the notes.
orl-f-159 · level facile · notes
How must the hyperparameters of the lab models be chosen ($C$ and $\gamma$ of the SVM, the weight $\lambda$ of a risk or regularization term)?
  1. On validation data, never on the test set or the decision scenarios
  2. By maximizing the accuracy on the test set
  3. Always equal to $1$, the neutral value
  4. On the same scenarios later used to decide
Explanation. Choosing them on the test set (or on the decision scenarios) gives optimistic results that do not transfer to new data.
orl-f-160 · level facile · notes
In an LP, a shadow price $\tilde\pi_i$ is used to evaluate a change of the right-hand side $b_i$ far beyond `SARHSUp` (the upper end of its validity range). Why is it a mistake?
  1. Outside the range shadow prices are always zero
  2. `SARHSUp` is the optimal value, not a bound on $b_i$
  3. The shadow price always changes sign at `SARHSUp`
  4. $\tilde\pi_i$ is exact only within its range: beyond, re-solve
Explanation. Interpreting a shadow price outside `SARHSLow`–`SARHSUp` is in the checklist of the most frequent mistakes: the optimal value is piecewise linear in $b_i$.

Explanation. Lab 1 production (duals, slacks, ranges, a shadow price checked by perturbation), Lab 2 Markowitz, Lab 3 pricing or budget, Lab 4 a project.
orl-f-162 · level facile · notes
A parameter estimated from data (e.g. an expected return) changes at the second decimal digit when the sample changes slightly. How many digits should a lab report show?

  3. None: estimates should not be reported
  4. As many as the solver prints
Explanation. Spurious precision is in the checklist of mistakes: the digits shown must reflect the reliability of the estimate.
orl-f-166 · level facile · notes

orl-f-168 · level facile · notes
In a **minimization** LP solved with gurobipy, what sign does the attribute `Pi` (shadow price $\partial\tilde z/\partial b_i$) of an active constraint $\sum_j a_{ij}x_j \le b_i$ have? Getting it wrong is in the lab checklist of mistakes.
  1. $\texttt{Pi} \le 0$: more capacity can only lower the cost
  2. $\texttt{Pi} \ge 0$: capacity always has a positive price
  3. $\texttt{Pi} = 0$: active constraints have no price
  4. Any sign, depending on the solver's conventions
Explanation. Raising $b_i$ enlarges the feasible region and a larger region never raises the minimum, so $\texttt{Pi} \le 0$; in a maximization the same constraint has $\texttt{Pi} \ge 0$.

orl-f-241 · level facile · notes
One entry of the lab checklist of mistakes is “optimizing a single objective when the problem has two”. Which lab example illustrates it?

Part: Deterministic models (251)

4. Multi-period production and inventory (32)

orl-f-034 · level facile · notes
Minimum-cost production-inventory LP (products $i$, months $t$; $x_{it}$ = units produced, $s_{it}$ = inventory at the end of month $t$, $d_{it}$ = demand). What is the role of the balance constraints $s_{i,t-1} + x_{it} = d_{it} + s_{it}$?
  1. They bound the machine hours used in each month $t$
  2. They make the objective convex, hence the LP solvable
  3. They link consecutive months and force demand to be served
  4. They force the inventory $s_{it}$ to zero in every month
Explanation. The balance is the intertemporal link: what comes in (previous inventory plus production) equals what goes out (demand plus next inventory).
orl-f-035 · level facile · notes
The production-inventory LP with $n$ products and $m$ months has balance constraints $s_{i,t-1} + x_{it} = d_{it} + s_{it}$ (for every product $i$ and month $t$) and machine-capacity constraints $\sum_i a_i x_{it} \le b_t$. How many constraints does it have, besides non-negativity?
  1. $n\,m + m$
  2. $2\,n\,m$
  3. $n + m$
  4. $n\,m$
Explanation. $n\,m$ inventory balances (one per product and month) and $m$ capacity constraints (one per month, shared by all products).
orl-f-036 · level facile · notes
Minimum-cost production-inventory LP (products $i$, months $t$; $x_{it}$ = units produced, $s_{it}$ = inventory at the end of month $t$, $d_{it}$ = demand). $h_i$ is the monthly holding cost and $a_i$ the machine hours per unit of product $i$. In the case study the shadow prices of the monthly capacity constraints differ by exactly $h_3/a_3 = 0.762$ € per hour between consecutive months. Why?
  1. Because $0.762$ €/hour is the production cost of product 3
  2. Because the solver rounds the duals to the last product's data
  3. Because each hour costs the same in every month of the horizon
  4. Because one more hour lets $1/a_3$ units of product 3 be made a month later
Explanation. Making $1/a_3$ units of product 3 one month later saves one month of holding, $h_3/a_3$ per hour: the economic argument and the solver give the same number.
orl-f-037 · level facile · notes
Minimum-cost production-inventory LP (products $i$, months $t$; $x_{it}$ = units produced, $s_{it}$ = inventory at the end of month $t$, $d_{it}$ = demand). What happens if the balance constraints $s_{i,t-1} + x_{it} = d_{it} + s_{it}$ are removed, keeping capacity and non-negativity?
  1. The model becomes infeasible, since capacity alone cannot be met
  2. The optimum produces nothing: no constraint forces demand to be served
  3. The model becomes unbounded, since the cost can decrease without limit
  4. Nothing changes: the capacity constraints already link the months
Explanation. Without the balance the minimum cost is reached by producing nothing. With the balances, if total demand exceeds cumulated capacity, the model is infeasible.
orl-f-038 · level facile · notes
Minimum-cost production-inventory LP (products $i$, months $t$; $x_{it}$ = units produced, $s_{it}$ = inventory at the end of month $t$, $d_{it}$ = demand). The term $\gamma \sum_{t=2}^{m} (v_t - v_{t-1})^2$, with $\gamma > 0$ and $v_t = \sum_i x_{it}$ the total production of month $t$, is added to the minimized cost. What is it for?
  1. To penalize the inventory held at the end of each month
  2. To keep the problem an LP while adding a cost
  3. To smooth the plan, penalizing changes of pace between months
  4. To reduce the demand to be served in the peak months
Explanation. It is the QP variant: in the case study, with $\gamma = 0.5$ the profile becomes almost flat at a cost of only $0.35\%$.
orl-f-039 · level facile · notes
Minimum-cost production-inventory LP (products $i$, months $t$; $x_{it}$ = units produced, $s_{it}$ = inventory at the end of month $t$, $d_{it}$ = demand). Capacity is tight in the peak months, so some production must be anticipated. Holding costs $h_i$ (€/unit/month) and machine hours $a_i$ (per unit) differ by product. Which product is it cheapest to produce in advance and stock?
  1. The one with the smallest $h_i/a_i$, the holding cost per machine hour anticipated
  2. The one with the smallest $h_i$, whatever its machine hours $a_i$
  3. The one with the smallest $a_i$, which uses the least capacity
  4. All products in proportion to their demand in the peak months
Explanation. Anticipating one machine hour by one month costs $h_i/a_i$. In the case study product 3 ($h_3/a_3 = 0.762$, the smallest) is produced in advance, up to 107 units in stock.
orl-f-040 · level teorema · notes
Consider a minimization LP and its optimal value $\tilde z(b_i)$ as a function of a single right-hand side $b_i$, on the values where the LP is feasible. Which statement is correct?
  1. $\tilde z(b_i)$ is concave and piecewise linear
  2. $\tilde z(b_i)$ is convex and piecewise linear, with slope $\tilde\pi_i$
  3. $\tilde z(b_i)$ is linear on its whole domain
  4. $\tilde z(b_i)$ is differentiable on its whole domain
Explanation. The slope is the shadow price, which changes at the endpoints of the validity ranges: this is why the capacity value curve of the production case study is convex and decreasing, with kinks.
orl-f-041 · level ipotesi · notes
Minimum-cost production-inventory LP (products $i$, months $t$; $x_{it}$ = units produced, $s_{it}$ = inventory at the end of month $t$, $d_{it}$ = demand). Production costs $c_{it}$ and holding costs $h_{it}$ are the data of the objective. Which facts prove that at the optimum the inventory of the last month $m$ is zero, $\tilde s_{im} = 0$?
  1. Only that the capacity constraints are active in month $m$
  2. Strong duality between the LP and its dual
  3. That demand is the same in every month
  4. $h_{im} > 0$ and $c_{it} > 0$: producing the leftover less keeps all balances and lowers the cost
Explanation. A positive terminal inventory is money wasted: removing it (producing less in the last month in which it was produced) keeps every balance feasible and strictly lowers the cost.
orl-f-186 · level facile · notes
Minimum-cost production-inventory LP (products $i$, months $t$; $x_{it}$ = units produced, $s_{it}$ = inventory at the end of month $t$, $d_{it}$ = demand). In the case study (3 products, 6 months) the problem becomes infeasible when the monthly capacities $b_t$ are cut below $97\%$ of their values. Why?
  1. Because the holding costs become negative
  2. Because the objective becomes non-convex
  3. Because cumulated capacity can no longer cover cumulated demand
  4. Because Gurobi needs at least $97\%$ of capacity
Explanation. With balances that force demand to be served, the production of months $1..t$ must cover the demand of months $1..t$ (net of initial stocks): below that capacity no plan exists.
orl-f-187 · level facile · notes
Minimum-cost production-inventory LP (products $i$, months $t$; $x_{it}$ = units produced, $s_{it}$ = inventory at the end of month $t$, $d_{it}$ = demand). One product, two months: demand $d = (60, 100)$, capacity $80$ units per month, production cost $10$ €/unit, holding cost $1$ €/unit/month, no initial inventory. What is the optimal cost?
  1. $1620$ €
  2. $1600$ €
  3. $1640$ €
  4. $1700$ €
Explanation. Month 2 can make only 80 of its 100 units, so 20 are made in month 1 and held: $x = (80, 80)$, $s_1 = 20$, cost $10 \cdot 160 + 1 \cdot 20 = 1620$ €.

Minimum-cost production-inventory LP (products $i$, months $t$; $x_{it}$ = units produced, $s_{it}$ = inventory at the end of month $t$, $d_{it}$ = demand). What does this check show?
  1. The shadow price is confirmed: the change, $-3.81$, matches it
  2. The shadow price is wrong, since the cost decreased
  3. The extra hour is outside the validity range
  4. Capacity in month 6 is not binding
Explanation. This is step 3 of the protocol: the re-optimization reproduces the marginal prediction of the dual, $\Delta\tilde z = \tilde\pi_6 \cdot 1$.
orl-f-189 · level facile · notes
Minimum-cost production-inventory LP (products $i$, months $t$; $x_{it}$ = units produced, $s_{it}$ = inventory at the end of month $t$, $d_{it}$ = demand). The smoothing variant adds $\gamma\sum_{t=2}^m (v_t - v_{t-1})^2$, with $v_t = \sum_i x_{it}$ and $\gamma > 0$, to the linear cost. To which class does the resulting model belong?
  1. A non-convex QP, because of the squares
  2. An LP, because the constraints are linear
  3. A non-convex NLP that needs `NonConvex = 2`
  4. A convex QP: a sum of squares with $\gamma > 0$ plus linear terms
Explanation. Each $(v_t - v_{t-1})^2$ is the square of a linear function, hence convex; with $\gamma > 0$ and linear constraints the model is a convex QP and its optimum is global.
orl-f-244 · level facile · notes
Minimum-cost production-inventory LP (products $i$, months $t$; $x_{it}$ = units produced, $s_{it}$ = inventory at the end of month $t$, $d_{it}$ = demand). Case study: 3 products, 6 months, demand peaking in months 4–5, the same unit costs in every month. Product 3 is produced in $89.5$ units in month 1 but only $29.0$ in month 5. Why?
  1. Its demand is much higher in month 1 than in month 5
  2. Pre-build: it is made early to free capacity in the peak months
  3. Its production cost is lower in month 1 than in month 5
  4. A numerical error: an optimal plan never builds inventory
Explanation. With equal costs, inventory only costs money; the solver anticipates product 3 (the one with the smallest holding cost per machine hour) to free machine hours in the peak months, and the stock returns to zero in month 6.
orl-f-392 · level facile · notes
According to the lab notes, what is the core difficulty of multi-period production planning with shared resources?
  1. The demand of the future months is unknown
  2. Today's decision changes tomorrow's constraints
  3. The products need integer quantities
  4. The production costs are nonlinear
Explanation. Producing early or not changes the inventory available later, hence what the later months must produce within their capacity: the balance constraints tie the months together.

Where does the multi-period production-inventory problem of the lab appear in practice?
  1. Only in the scheduling of a single machine
  2. Only in the choice of the location of a plant
  3. Only in the pricing of seasonal products
  4. In master production schedules and monthly contracts
Explanation. Whenever production is planned over several periods with shared resources: master production schedules (MPS), aggregate planning, supply contracts with monthly profiles.

  3. It has a closed-form solution
  4. It needs no data from the company

One product, two months: demand $(60, 100)$, capacity $80$ per month, $c = 10$, $h = 1$; optimal plan $x = (80, 80)$, $s_1 = 20$. With one more unit of capacity in month 2 the cost drops by 1 €.   2. $-10$: one unit less is produced
  3. $+1$: extra capacity costs money
  4. $0$: the constraint is not active
Explanation. With $b_2 = 81$: $x_2 = 81$, $s_1 = 19$, $x_1 = 79$; the total production is the same, but one unit no longer waits a month in stock, saving $h = 1$. In a minimum a $\le$ capacity has dual $\le 0$.

One product, two months, demand $(60, 100)$, capacity $80$ per month: both capacity constraints are active, yet one more unit of capacity in month 1 changes nothing (dual $0$). How is this possible?

  2. Because month 1 has a higher cost
  3. Because the holding cost is paid in month 2
  4. Degeneracy: active, yet zero dual
Explanation. Month 1 already produces what is needed (60 for itself, 20 for month 2); extra capacity there is useless. Complementary slackness gives only «slack ⇒ zero dual», not the converse.

orl-f-405 · level facile · notes
Minimum-cost production-inventory LP (products $i$, months $t$; $x_{it}$ = units produced, $s_{it}$ = inventory at the end of month $t$, $d_{it}$ = demand, $c_{it}$ and $h_{it}$ = unit production and holding costs, $a_i$ = machine hours per unit, $b_t$ = hours available): balances $s_{i,t-1} + x_{it} = d_{it} + s_{it}$, capacities $\sum_ia_ix_{it} \le b_t$. The balances are written with `==` in the script. What does the equality impose?
  1. That production equals demand in every month
  2. That the inventories stay constant
  3. That all demand is served on time
  4. That the capacity is fully used
Explanation. In every month incoming stock plus production equals demand plus outgoing stock: nothing is lost and no backlog is allowed. Allowing lost demand would need an extra variable with a penalty.

orl-f-409 · level facile · notes
Minimum-cost production-inventory LP (products $i$, months $t$; $x_{it}$ = units produced, $s_{it}$ = inventory at the end of month $t$, $d_{it}$ = demand, $c_{it}$ and $h_{it}$ = unit production and holding costs, $a_i$ = machine hours per unit, $b_t$ = hours available): balances $s_{i,t-1} + x_{it} = d_{it} + s_{it}$, capacities $\sum_ia_ix_{it} \le b_t$. Case study: 3 products, 6 months; $(c_i, h_i, a_i, s_{i0})$ = $(12, 0.8, 0.9, 30)$, $(18, 1.2, 1.4, 20)$, $(25, 1.6, 2.1, 10)$; hours $b_t$ = 420, 420, 460, 460, 460, 420; demand peaking in months 4–5.   1. Month 1 is the bottleneck of the plan
  2. Extra hours in month 1 are worth $0.762$ €
  3. Slack constraint: extra hours are worthless
  4. The model is degenerate in month 1
