orl-f-521 · level ipotesi · notes
Production LP with one product, initial stock 0, demands $d_t$, capacities $b_t$ (units per month), balances $s_{t-1} + x_t = d_t + s_t$, $0 \le x_t \le b_t$, $s_t \ge 0$. Which condition on the data is necessary and sufficient for feasibility?
  1. Total capacity at least total demand
  2. $b_t \ge d_t$ in every month
  3. Capacity of the last month at least its demand
  4. Cumulated capacity $\ge$ cumulated demand, every $t$
Explanation. Stock can only carry production forward: demand up to month $t$ must be produced in months $1, \dots, t$. Comparing only the totals is not enough (a late surplus cannot serve an early peak); $b_t \ge d_t$ is sufficient but not necessary.

5. Supply chain with congestion and sustainability (27)

orl-f-042 · level facile · notes
Minimum-cost flow LP of the supply chain on a network $G = (N, A)$: flows $x_{ij}$ on the arcs, unit costs $c_{ij}$, capacities $u_{ij}$. In the flow conservation constraint $\sum_{j:(i,j)\in A} x_{ij} - \sum_{j:(j,i)\in A} x_{ji} = b_i$ (outgoing minus incoming flow), a market, i.e. a demand node, has:
  1. $b_i > 0$
  2. $b_i < 0$
  3. $b_i = 0$
  4. $b_i = \sum_{j:(j,i)\in A} u_{ji}$
Explanation. Out minus in equals the balance: positive at plants (supply), negative at markets (demand), zero at hubs (transit).
orl-f-043 · level facile · notes
Minimum-cost flow LP of the supply chain on a network $G = (N, A)$: flows $x_{ij}$ on the arcs, unit costs $c_{ij}$, capacities $u_{ij}$. The congestion term $\alpha\,c_{ij}\,x_{ij}^2/u_{ij}$, with $\alpha > 0$, is added to the cost of every arc. What is its effect on the optimal flows?
  1. It saturates the cheapest routes even more
  2. It makes the model non-convex and harder to solve
  3. It spreads the flow over several routes instead of saturating one
  4. It reduces the total demand that must be served
Explanation. The term is convex and grows with the utilization $x_{ij}/u_{ij}$: in the case study no arc stays saturated, as a prudent planner would choose.
orl-f-044 · level facile · notes
Minimum-cost flow LP of the supply chain on a network $G = (N, A)$: flows $x_{ij}$ on the arcs, unit costs $c_{ij}$, capacities $u_{ij}$. An internal CO$_2$ price $\tau$ (€/kg) is added to the arc costs, $c_{ij} + \tau e_{ij}$ ($e_{ij}$ = emissions per unit). As $\tau$ grows, the cost-emissions frontier of the LP proceeds by jumps. Why?
  1. LP optima lie at vertices: the optimum stays on a vertex, then jumps to another
  2. The emissions data $e_{ij}$ are rounded to whole kilograms
  3. The solver stops before the optimum for some values of $\tau$
  4. The capacities $u_{ij}$ change with $\tau$ in steps
Explanation. In the case study nothing changes up to $\tau = 1$ €/kg and the main jump happens at $\tau = 1.25$. A strictly convex model (with congestion) would move continuously.
orl-f-045 · level facile · notes
Minimum-cost flow LP of the supply chain on a network $G = (N, A)$: flows $x_{ij}$ on the arcs, unit costs $c_{ij}$, capacities $u_{ij}$. At the optimum an arc with cost $c_{ij} = 7$ €/unit carries no flow and has reduced cost $+2$ (Gurobi attribute `RC`). Below which cost would it start carrying flow?
  1. Above $9$ €/unit
  2. Below $7$ €/unit
  3. It never would: positive `RC` means useless
  4. Below $5$ €/unit
Explanation. In a minimization the reduced cost of a variable at zero is the improvement it needs: threshold $7 - 2 = 5$ €/unit (`SAObjLow`); above it the arc stays unused.
orl-f-046 · level facile · notes
Minimum-cost flow LP of the supply chain on a network $G = (N, A)$: flows $x_{ij}$ on the arcs, unit costs $c_{ij}$, capacities $u_{ij}$. $e_{ij}$ are the kg of CO$_2$ emitted per unit sent on arc $(i,j)$. How is an internal CO$_2$ price $\tau$ (€/kg) introduced in the model?
  1. With a binary variable for each polluting arc
  2. Replacing $c_{ij}$ with $c_{ij} + \tau\,e_{ij}$ in the objective
  3. Adding the constraint $\sum e_{ij}x_{ij} = \tau$
  4. Multiplying every capacity $u_{ij}$ by $\tau$
Explanation. This is the “weight” approach, with no new constraints; the alternative imposes $\sum e_{ij}x_{ij} \le \bar e$ and reads the dual of that constraint.
orl-f-047 · level facile · notes
Minimum-cost flow LP of the supply chain on a network $G = (N, A)$: flows $x_{ij}$ on the arcs, unit costs $c_{ij}$, capacities $u_{ij}$. The “pure” minimax utilization model minimizes $z$ subject to $x_{ij}/u_{ij} \le z$ on every arc and the flow constraints. Why is it not used alone?
  1. Any routing with utilization at most $\tilde z$ is optimal, even a very expensive one
  2. It is non-convex because of the ratios $x_{ij}/u_{ij}$
  3. It is always infeasible when some arc is saturated
  4. Gurobi cannot solve problems with a minimax objective
Explanation. The pure minimax is degenerate in the cost; in practice one minimizes cost $+\,\rho\, z$ or bounds $z$ and minimizes the cost.
orl-f-048 · level facile · notes
Minimum-cost flow LP of the supply chain on a network $G = (N, A)$: flows $x_{ij}$ on the arcs, unit costs $c_{ij}$, capacities $u_{ij}$. The demand of each market is a flow conservation constraint. In the case study the shadow prices of these constraints range between $8$ and $10.50$ €/unit. What do they represent?
  1. The selling prices charged in the markets
  2. The residual capacities of the arcs entering the markets
  3. The marginal cost of serving one more unit in each market
  4. The CO$_2$ emitted per unit delivered to each market
Explanation. They are the marginal costs of service: the number to compare with the commercial margin before accepting new orders.
orl-f-049 · level ipotesi · notes
Minimum-cost flow LP of the supply chain on a network $G = (N, A)$: flows $x_{ij}$ on the arcs, unit costs $c_{ij}$, capacities $u_{ij}$. With the congestion term $\alpha c_{ij}x_{ij}^2/u_{ij}$ ($\alpha > 0$) the optimal flows move continuously as the data change, while in the LP they can jump. Which property is responsible?
  1. The linearity of the flow conservation constraints
  2. The strict convexity of the objective, which gives a unique optimum
  3. The non-negativity of the flows $x_{ij}$
  4. The integrality of the capacities $u_{ij}$
Explanation. With a strictly convex objective the optimum is unique and varies continuously with the data; an LP can have its optimum jump between vertices.
orl-f-190 · level facile · notes
Supply chain with congestion: 100 units go on two routes; route 1 costs $2x_1 + x_1^2/40$ (cost $c_1 = 2$, capacity $u_1 = 80$, $\alpha = 1$), route 2 costs $5$ €/unit with ample capacity. At the optimum the marginal costs are equal. What is the split $(x_1, x_2)$?
  1. $(80, 20)$
  2. $(100, 0)$
  3. $(60, 40)$
  4. $(50, 50)$
Explanation. Marginal cost of route 1: $2 + x_1/20$; setting it equal to $5$ gives $x_1 = 60$, $x_2 = 40$. The LP without congestion would fill route 1 up to its capacity, $(80, 20)$.
orl-f-191 · level facile · notes
Minimum-cost flow LP of the supply chain on a network $G = (N, A)$: flows $x_{ij}$ on the arcs, unit costs $c_{ij}$, capacities $u_{ij}$. Node balances $b_i$ are positive at plants (supply), negative at markets (demand), zero at hubs. Which condition on the balances is necessary for the model to be feasible?
  1. $\sum_{i \in N} b_i \ge 0$: total supply covers total demand
  2. $b_i \le u_{ij}$ for every arc leaving $i$
  3. $\sum_{i \in N} b_i < 0$: demand exceeds supply
  4. All $b_i$ must be integers
Explanation. If total demand exceeds total supply no flow can satisfy all conservation constraints; capacities can make the model infeasible even when the balance condition holds.
orl-f-192 · level facile · notes
Minimum-cost flow LP of the supply chain on a network $G = (N, A)$: flows $x_{ij}$ on the arcs, unit costs $c_{ij}$, capacities $u_{ij}$. $e_{ij}$ = kg of CO$_2$ per unit on arc $(i,j)$. Instead of an internal price, the company imposes a cap $\sum e_{ij}x_{ij} \le \bar e$ on total emissions. What does the shadow price of this constraint represent?
  1. The emissions saved per euro spent
  2. The total cost of the network divided by $\bar e$
  3. The emissions of the cheapest route
  4. The implicit CO$_2$ price: cost change per kg of extra allowance
Explanation. The dual of the cap is the marginal cost of abatement: it is the internal price $\tau$ that would produce the same routing with the “weight” approach.
orl-f-193 · level ipotesi · notes
Minimum-cost flow LP of the supply chain on a network $G = (N, A)$: flows $x_{ij}$ on the arcs, unit costs $c_{ij}$, capacities $u_{ij}$. Congestion version: each arc costs $c_{ij}x_{ij} + \alpha c_{ij}x_{ij}^2/u_{ij}$. Which facts make this model convex?
  1. The flows are integers and the capacities are positive
  2. $\alpha \ge 0$, $c_{ij} \ge 0$, $u_{ij} > 0$: convex quadratic terms, linear constraints
  3. The network has no cycles
  4. Every arc is used at the optimum
Explanation. $x_{ij}^2$ is convex and is multiplied by the non-negative coefficient $\alpha c_{ij}/u_{ij}$; the conservation and capacity constraints are linear, so the feasible set is a polyhedron.

  3. How much should each arc be taxed by the state?
  4. Which CO$_2$ price makes clean routes pay?
Explanation. The three questions: how to route flows at minimum cost, what changes when congestion is penalized, and how high an internal CO$_2$ price must be for the «clean» routes to become convenient.

  4. It needs no capacities on the arcs

  4. Uses only route 1, cost $200$ €

  4. Demand $5$, capacity $-3$
Explanation. The 101st unit would travel on route 2, costing 5; one more unit of capacity on route 1 moves a unit from route 2 to route 1, saving $5 - 2 = 3$ (dual $-3$ for a $\le$ capacity in a minimum).

  3. $\tau$ has no effect at all on an LP
  4. Below it nothing changes; above, a jump

orl-f-522 · level teorema · notes
Minimum-cost flow LP with costs $c_{ij} + \tau e_{ij}$ ($\tau$ = internal CO$_2$ price, $e_{ij}$ = kg per unit): as $\tau$ grows, emissions stay constant on intervals and then drop in jumps. Which LP property explains it?
  1. The LP has a unique solution for every $\tau$
  2. An optimal vertex always exists
  3. The objective is convex in the flows
  4. The flows are always integer numbers
Explanation. For each $\tau$ an optimal vertex exists; it stays optimal on an interval of $\tau$ and is then replaced by an adjacent vertex. A strictly convex model (congestion) would move continuously instead.

6. The Markowitz portfolio (26)

orl-f-050 · level facile · notes
Markowitz model: shares $x_i$ of capital in assets $i$, expected returns $\mu_i$, covariances $q_{ij}$; minimize the variance $\sum_i\sum_j q_{ij}x_ix_j$ with $\sum_i x_i = 1$ and expected return at least $\bar r$. Where does diversification come from?
  1. From the covariances $q_{ij}$ in the variance, which reward weakly correlated assets
  2. From the return constraint, which forces several assets into the portfolio
  3. From the caps $x_i \le u_i$, which forbid investing everything in one asset
  4. From the budget constraint $\sum_i x_i = 1$, which spreads the capital
Explanation. The variance contains the covariances: combining weakly correlated assets lowers the risk below that of each component, even without caps.
orl-f-051 · level facile · notes
Markowitz model: shares $x_i$ of capital in assets $i$, expected returns $\mu_i$, covariances $q_{ij}$; minimize the variance $\sum_i\sum_j q_{ij}x_ix_j$ with $\sum_i x_i = 1$ and expected return at least $\bar r$. Without the return constraint, the minimum-variance portfolio already has expected return $11.06\%$. What is the multiplier of the constraint “expected return at least $\bar r = 8\%$”?
  1. Positive, because every point of return costs variance
  2. Negative, because the constraint is a lower bound
  3. Zero, because the constraint is not active
  4. Undefined, because the model is a QP
Explanation. The constraint does not bite up to $11.06\%$, so by complementarity its multiplier is zero; only beyond that threshold every extra point of return is paid in variance.
orl-f-052 · level facile · notes
Markowitz model: shares $x_i$ of capital in assets $i$, expected returns $\mu_i$, covariances $q_{ij}$; minimize the variance $\sum_i\sum_j q_{ij}x_ix_j$ with $\sum_i x_i = 1$ and expected return at least $\bar r$. The inputs $\mu_i$ and $q_{ij}$ are estimated from 60 months of returns. What is the main practical lesson of the case study about these estimates?
  1. Covariances are much harder to estimate than expected returns
  2. Expected returns are far less stable than covariances, and the optimum chases their errors
  3. Sixty months of data give estimates accurate enough to trust the optimum
  4. The caps $x_i \le u_i$ remove the estimation errors from the inputs
Explanation. An asset with a true expected return of $11\%$ shows an estimated $1.6\%$ over 60 months. Remedies: caps, shrinkage, or the minimum-variance portfolio only.
orl-f-053 · level facile · notes
Markowitz model: shares $x_i$ of capital in assets $i$, expected returns $\mu_i$, covariances $q_{ij}$; minimize the variance $\sum_i\sum_j q_{ij}x_ix_j$ with $\sum_i x_i = 1$ and expected return at least $\bar r$. The efficient frontier collects the portfolios of minimum variance for each $\bar r$. In the case study, how does the equally weighted portfolio $x_i = 1/n$ compare with it?
  1. It lies exactly on the efficient frontier
  2. It coincides with the minimum-variance portfolio
  3. It lies above the frontier, dominating it
  4. It is dominated: similar return, almost twice the volatility
Explanation. The “I know nothing” strategy is clearly dominated; the minimum-variance portfolio has volatility $6\%$ and return $11\%$.
orl-f-054 · level facile · notes
Markowitz model: shares $x_i$ of capital in assets $i$, expected returns $\mu_i$, covariances $q_{ij}$; minimize the variance $\sum_i\sum_j q_{ij}x_ix_j$ with $\sum_i x_i = 1$ and expected return at least $\bar r$. Varying $\bar r$ traces the efficient frontier. Which formulation does **not** trace the same frontier?
  1. $\max \sum_i\mu_ix_i - \lambda\sum_i\sum_j q_{ij}x_ix_j$, varying $\lambda \ge 0$
  2. $\max \sum_i \mu_i x_i$, with no risk term and no bound on the variance
  3. $\max \sum_i\mu_ix_i$ with variance at most $\bar\sigma^2$, varying $\bar\sigma$
  4. $\min$ variance with expected return exactly $\bar r$ on the upper branch
Explanation. Threshold on the return, mean-variance and bound on the variance trace the same frontier; maximizing the return alone gives only one extreme point.
orl-f-055 · level facile · notes
Markowitz model: shares $x_i$ of capital in assets $i$, expected returns $\mu_i$, covariances $q_{ij}$; minimize the variance $\sum_i\sum_j q_{ij}x_ix_j$ with $\sum_i x_i = 1$ and expected return at least $\bar r$. A mandate adds the cap $x_i \le 30\%$ for every asset. What is the effect on the efficient frontier?
  1. It cuts the upper part: the highest returns become unreachable
  2. It shifts the whole frontier upwards, improving every portfolio
  3. It has no effect, since optimal portfolios are already diversified
  4. It lowers the minimum attainable variance
Explanation. Mandate constraints restrict the feasible set, so they can only worsen the frontier; their cost is the distance between the two frontiers, largest at high returns.
orl-f-056 · level teorema · notes
Markowitz model: shares $x_i$ of capital in assets $i$, expected returns $\mu_i$, covariances $q_{ij}$; minimize the variance $\sum_i\sum_j q_{ij}x_ix_j$ with $\sum_i x_i = 1$ and expected return at least $\bar r$. Which statement about this QP is correct?
  1. It is convex only if short selling is forbidden ($x_i \ge 0$)
  2. It is convex only if the assets are uncorrelated ($q_{ij} = 0$ for $i \ne j$)
  3. Every covariance matrix is positive semidefinite, so the QP is convex and its optimum global
  4. Every covariance matrix is positive definite, so the optimal portfolio is unique
Explanation. Covariance matrices are $\succeq 0$, not necessarily $\succ 0$: convexity holds with any linear constraints, uniqueness is not guaranteed.
orl-f-057 · level ipotesi · notes
Markowitz model with two uncorrelated assets of variances $\sigma_1^2$, $\sigma_2^2$, no return constraint: minimize $\sigma_1^2x_1^2 + \sigma_2^2x_2^2$ with $x_1 + x_2 = 1$. Which steps prove that $x_1 = \sigma_2^2/(\sigma_1^2 + \sigma_2^2)$ is optimal?
  1. The return constraint must be active at the optimum
  2. Strong LP duality between the QP and its dual
  3. The caps $x_i \le u_i$ must be inactive at the optimum
  4. Substitute $x_2 = 1 - x_1$: a convex parabola, whose stationary point is the minimum
Explanation. $f(x_1) = \sigma_1^2x_1^2 + \sigma_2^2(1-x_1)^2$ is convex, so $f'(x_1) = 0$ gives the global minimum; here it falls in $[0, 1]$.
orl-f-194 · level facile · notes
Markowitz model: shares $x_i$ of capital in assets $i$, expected returns $\mu_i$, covariances $q_{ij}$; minimize the variance $\sum_i\sum_j q_{ij}x_ix_j$ with $\sum_i x_i = 1$ and expected return at least $\bar r$.   4. That short selling is forbidden
Explanation. A negative share would be a short sale; with $\ell_i = 0$ only non-negative shares are allowed, while $\ell_i > 0$ would force a minimum holding.

Markowitz model: shares $x_i$ of capital in assets $i$, expected returns $\mu_i$, covariances $q_{ij}$; minimize the variance $\sum_i\sum_j q_{ij}x_ix_j$ with $\sum_i x_i = 1$ and expected return at least $\bar r$.   4. Raising $\bar r$ by one unit raises the minimum variance by about $0.022$
Explanation. The multiplier is $d(\text{variance})/d\bar r$: beyond $11.06\%$ every extra point of required return is paid in variance, and the price grows along the frontier.

orl-f-428 · level facile · notes
Markowitz model: shares $x_i$ of capital in assets $i$, expected returns $\mu_i$, covariances $q_{ij}$; minimize the variance $\sum_i\sum_jq_{ij}x_ix_j$ with $\sum_ix_i = 1$, expected return at least $\bar r$ and bounds $\ell_i \le x_i \le u_i$. Two uncorrelated assets with volatilities $\sigma_1 = 20\%$, $\sigma_2 = 30\%$, no return constraint. What is the minimum-variance share $x_1$?
  1. $x_1 = 2/5 = 40\%$
  2. $x_1 = 9/13 \approx 69.2\%$
  3. $x_1 = 1/2$
  4. $x_1 = 3/5 = 60\%$
Explanation. Minimizing $0.04x_1^2 + 0.09(1 - x_1)^2$: $x_1 = 0.09/(0.04 + 0.09) = 9/13$. Weights are inversely proportional to the variances (not to the volatilities, which would give $60\%$).
orl-f-429 · level facile · notes
Markowitz model: shares $x_i$ of capital in assets $i$, expected returns $\mu_i$, covariances $q_{ij}$; minimize the variance $\sum_i\sum_jq_{ij}x_ix_j$ with $\sum_ix_i = 1$, expected return at least $\bar r$ and bounds $\ell_i \le x_i \le u_i$. Two uncorrelated assets, $\sigma_1 = 20\%$, $\sigma_2 = 30\%$: the minimum-variance portfolio has variance $0.0277$. What is its volatility, compared with the two assets?
  1. $25\%$, between the two
  2. $20\%$, as the safer asset
  3. $30\%$, as the riskier asset
  4. $16.6\%$, below both
Explanation. $\sqrt{0.0277} \approx 16.6\% < 20\%$: the portfolio is less risky than either asset, diversification in its purest form.
orl-f-430 · level facile · notes
Markowitz model: shares $x_i$ of capital in assets $i$, expected returns $\mu_i$, covariances $q_{ij}$; minimize the variance $\sum_i\sum_jq_{ij}x_ix_j$ with $\sum_ix_i = 1$, expected return at least $\bar r$ and bounds $\ell_i \le x_i \le u_i$. In the two-asset example ($\sigma_1 = 20\%$, $\sigma_2 = 30\%$, uncorrelated, $x_2 = 1 - x_1$), how is the optimal share found by hand?
  1. Zero derivative of the variance in $x_1$
  2. Taking the asset with the higher return
  3. Splitting the capital in equal parts
  4. Solving an LP on the two shares
Explanation. After substituting the budget, the variance is a convex parabola in $x_1$: $0.08x_1 - 0.18(1 - x_1) = 0$ gives $x_1 = 9/13$.

orl-f-432 · level facile · notes
Markowitz model: shares $x_i$ of capital in assets $i$, expected returns $\mu_i$, covariances $q_{ij}$; minimize the variance $\sum_i\sum_jq_{ij}x_ix_j$ with $\sum_ix_i = 1$, expected return at least $\bar r$ and bounds $\ell_i \le x_i \le u_i$. In the gurobipy code the shares are created with `x = m.addVars(n, ub=u)`. Which constraint of the model does `ub=u` implement?

  3. The minimum return $\sum_i\mu_ix_i \ge \bar r$

Markowitz model: shares $x_i$ of capital in assets $i$, expected returns $\mu_i$, covariances $q_{ij}$; minimize the variance $\sum_i\sum_jq_{ij}x_ix_j$ with $\sum_ix_i = 1$, expected return at least $\bar r$ and bounds $\ell_i \le x_i \le u_i$.   4. UTL, with volatility $7.53\%$

orl-f-434 · level facile · notes
Markowitz model: shares $x_i$ of capital in assets $i$, expected returns $\mu_i$, covariances $q_{ij}$; minimize the variance $\sum_i\sum_jq_{ij}x_ix_j$ with $\sum_ix_i = 1$, expected return at least $\bar r$ and bounds $\ell_i \le x_i \le u_i$. In the gurobipy code the objective is `quicksum(Q[i, j]*x[i]*x[j] for i in range(n) for j in range(n))`. What kind of model does Gurobi receive?
  1. An LP, since the constraints are linear
  2. A convex QP
  3. A nonconvex QP needing `NonConvex = 2`
  4. An NLP with functional constraints only
Explanation. A covariance matrix is positive semidefinite, so the quadratic objective is convex and Gurobi certifies a global optimum without special parameters.
orl-f-435 · level facile · notes
Markowitz model: shares $x_i$ of capital in assets $i$, expected returns $\mu_i$, covariances $q_{ij}$; minimize the variance $\sum_i\sum_jq_{ij}x_ix_j$ with $\sum_ix_i = 1$, expected return at least $\bar r$ and bounds $\ell_i \le x_i \le u_i$. Case study: up to $\bar r = 11\%$ the volatility stays $6.03\%$; at $\bar r = 12\%$ it becomes $6.10\%$ with multiplier about $0.022$. What does the multiplier measure?
  1. The return gained per unit of variance
  2. The share invested in the best asset
  3. Variance paid per point of return
  4. The volatility of the min-variance portfolio
Explanation. It is $\partial(\text{variance})/\partial\bar r$ for the active return constraint; below $11.06\%$ the constraint is inactive and the multiplier is zero.
orl-f-523 · level teorema · notes
Markowitz with two uncorrelated assets of variances $\sigma_1^2$ and $\sigma_2^2$, budget $x_1 + x_2 = 1$, no return constraint. Which statement about the minimum-variance shares is correct?
  1. $x_1 = \sigma_2^2/(\sigma_1^2 + \sigma_2^2)$
  2. $x_i$ is proportional to $\sigma_i^2$
  3. $x_i$ is inversely proportional to $\sigma_i$
  4. $x_1 = x_2 = 1/2$, whatever the variances
Explanation. Setting the derivative of $\sigma_1^2x_1^2 + \sigma_2^2(1 - x_1)^2$ to zero gives $x_1\sigma_1^2 = x_2\sigma_2^2$: weights inversely proportional to the variances, and portfolio variance below both.
orl-f-524 · level ipotesi · notes
The Markowitz QP minimizes $\sum_i\sum_jq_{ij}x_ix_j$; the notes call it convex because $\mathbf Q = (q_{ij})$ is a covariance matrix. If $\mathbf Q$ were patched together from estimates on different samples and lost positive semidefiniteness, what would fail?
  1. Nothing: any symmetric matrix gives a convex QP
  2. Only the budget constraint of the model
  3. Convexity of the QP
  4. The linearity of the return constraint
Explanation. The QP is convex if and only if $\mathbf Q \succeq 0$; a genuine covariance matrix always is, but an inconsistent patchwork may not, and then the objective can be nonconvex (even unbounded below along some direction).

7. Pricing and revenue management (22)

orl-f-058 · level facile · notes
Pricing model: choose price $p$ and quantity sold $q$ to maximize $p\,q - c\,q$ subject to $q \le d(p)$ (demand at price $p$) and $q \le k$ (capacity). Where does the non-convexity of this model come from?
  1. From the capacity constraint $q \le k$
  2. From the bilinear term $p \cdot q$ in the objective
  3. From the non-negativity of the price $p$
  4. From the demand constraint, even when $d$ is linear
Explanation. The product of two variables is non-convex. With linear demand, substituting $q = a - bp$, the reduced problem is a concave parabola in $p$.
orl-f-059 · level facile · notes
Pricing model: choose price $p$ and quantity sold $q$ to maximize $p\,q - c\,q$ subject to $q \le d(p)$ (demand at price $p$) and $q \le k$ (capacity). With linear demand $d(p) = a - b\,p$ and no capacity limit, what is the optimal price $p^\circ$?
  1. $p^\circ = (a/b + c)/2$
  2. $p^\circ = a/b$
  3. $p^\circ = c$
  4. $p^\circ = (a - c)/b$
Explanation. The profit $(p - c)(a - bp)$ is a concave parabola: setting its derivative to zero gives $p^\circ = (a/b + c)/2$, halfway between $c$ and the choke price $a/b$.
orl-f-060 · level facile · notes
Pricing model: choose price $p$ and quantity sold $q$ to maximize $p\,q - c\,q$ subject to $q \le d(p)$ (demand at price $p$) and $q \le k$ (capacity). Concert example: $d(p) = 1200 - 5p$, $c = 20$ €, $k = 400$ seats, optimal price $\tilde p = 160$ €. The value of one more seat, $d\Pi/dk$ with $\Pi(k)$ the optimal profit, is $60$ €, not the margin $\tilde p - c = 140$ €. Why?
  1. Because the marginal cost $c$ grows with the capacity $k$
  2. Because the extra seat would stay empty at the price $\tilde p$
  3. Because the capacity constraint becomes inactive at $k = 401$
  4. Because the price falls for everybody: $d\Pi/dk = (\tilde p - c) + k\,d\tilde p/dk = 140 - 80$
Explanation. With $\tilde p = (a - k)/b$ the price falls by $1/b$ per extra seat, on all $k$ tickets. Ignoring it is a classic mistake in evaluating capacity investments.
orl-f-061 · level facile · notes
Pricing model: choose price $p$ and quantity sold $q$ to maximize $p\,q - c\,q$ subject to $q \le d(p)$ (demand at price $p$) and $q \le k$ (capacity). With constant-elasticity demand $d(p) = \theta p^{-\varepsilon}$ ($\varepsilon > 1$) and binding capacity, the optimum is $\tilde p = (\theta/k)^{1/\varepsilon}$, where $d(\tilde p) = k$. What kind of optimum is it?
  1. A stationary point of the profit $(p - c)\,d(p)$
  2. A local minimum of the profit
  3. A corner point, where no derivative of the profit vanishes
  4. An unbounded solution cut off by the capacity
Explanation. Below $\tilde p$ the capacity sells out anyway (better raise the price), above it the profit falls: with constraints the optimum must not be sought among stationary points only.
orl-f-062 · level facile · notes
Pricing model: choose price $p$ and quantity sold $q$ to maximize $p\,q - c\,q$ subject to $q \le d(p)$ (demand at price $p$) and $q \le k$ (capacity). Concert example $d(p) = 1200 - 5p$, $c = 20$ €: without capacity the optimal quantity is $q^\circ = 550$ seats. What happens to the value of capacity $d\Pi/dk$ for $k > 550$?
  1. It keeps growing linearly with $k$
  2. It is zero: capacity is no longer binding
  3. It becomes negative: empty seats cost money
  4. It equals the full margin $p^\circ - c$
Explanation. Beyond the unconstrained optimal quantity the optimal profit curve $\Pi(k)$ is flat: additional capacity is worth nothing.
orl-f-063 · level facile · notes
Pricing model with two seat categories (stalls and balcony) and substitution: the demand of each category depends on both prices $p_1, p_2$. Why must the two prices be decided jointly?
  1. Because each demand depends on both prices: one at a time loses profit
  2. Because the two categories have the same marginal cost
  3. Because the two categories have the same capacity
  4. Because with two products the model becomes an LP
Explanation. Raising the stalls price pushes part of the demand towards the balcony: the joint model keeps the stalls expensive to exploit the substitution.
orl-f-064 · level facile · notes
Pricing model: choose price $p$ and quantity sold $q$ to maximize $p\,q - c\,q$ subject to $q \le d(p)$ (demand at price $p$) and $q \le k$ (capacity). With a general demand function $d(p)$ (e.g. logistic) the profit may not be concave. What does the lab recommend before trusting the solver's answer?
  1. Replacing $d(p)$ with a linear demand fitted to the data
  2. Removing the capacity constraint, a source of corner points
  3. Setting the price equal to the marginal cost $c$
  4. Studying domain and derivatives and plotting the profit
Explanation. Gurobi certifies the global optimum, but understanding the shape of the profit (domain, derivatives, plot over the relevant interval, several starting points) remains part of the work.
orl-f-065 · level ipotesi · notes
Pricing model: choose price $p$ and quantity sold $q$ to maximize $p\,q - c\,q$ subject to $q \le d(p)$ (demand at price $p$) and $q \le k$ (capacity). The model writes $q \le d(p)$ rather than $q = d(p)$. Which assumption guarantees that, at the optimum, $\tilde q = \min\{d(\tilde p), k\}$?
  1. $k > d(p)$ for every price $p$
  2. The demand $d(p)$ is linear in $p$
  3. $\tilde p > c$: every extra unit sold adds profit
  4. $\tilde p = c$: the margin is zero
Explanation. With a positive unit margin each extra unit sold increases the profit, so $q$ is pushed up to the smaller of demand and capacity.
orl-f-198 · level facile · notes
Two-category pricing (stalls 1, balcony 2) with demands $d_1 = 500 - 2p_1 + 0.6\,p_2$ and $d_2 = 900 + 0.8\,p_1 - 4p_2$. What does the positive coefficient $0.6$ of $p_2$ in $d_1$ express?

  4. Substitution: a dearer balcony moves demand to the stalls
Explanation. A positive cross coefficient means substitute products; this is why the two prices must be chosen jointly.
orl-f-199 · level facile · notes
Two-category pricing: maximize $\sum_i (p_i - c_i)q_i$ with $q_i \le d_i(p_1, p_2)$ (linear demands) and $q_i \le k_i$. Which Gurobi setting does this model need?
  1. None: with linear demands it is an LP
  2. `NonConvex = 2`, because of the bilinear terms $p_i q_i$
  3. `FuncNonlinear = 1`, because of the demand functions
  4. `QCPDual = 1`, to make it convex
Explanation. The objective contains the products of two variables $p_iq_i$, as in the single-product case: bilinear, non-convex, solved globally with `NonConvex = 2`.
orl-f-200 · level facile · notes
Pricing model: choose price $p$ and quantity sold $q$ to maximize $p\,q - c\,q$ subject to $q \le d(p)$ (demand at price $p$) and $q \le k$ (capacity). Why is the optimum with capacity $k$ instead $\tilde p = 79.11$ €?

  2. Because the formula $c\varepsilon/(\varepsilon - 1)$ holds only for $\varepsilon < 1$
  3. Because the solver stops at a local optimum
  4. Because at $36.67$ € the margin $p - c$ is negative

orl-f-201 · level ipotesi · notes
Pricing model: choose price $p$ and quantity sold $q$ to maximize $p\,q - c\,q$ subject to $q \le d(p)$ (demand at price $p$) and $q \le k$ (capacity). With linear demand $d(p) = a - bp$ ($b > 0$) and no capacity, which facts make $p^\circ = (a/b + c)/2$ the global optimum?
  1. With $q = a - bp$ the profit $(p - c)(a - bp)$ is concave in $p$: stationary means global
  2. The bilinear term $p\,q$ is convex, so every stationary point is a global optimum
  3. The demand constraint is inactive at the optimum, so the model reduces to an LP
  4. Strong duality between the pricing model and its dual, with equal optimal values
Explanation. The coefficient of $p^2$ is $-b < 0$: the reduced profit is concave in $p$ and $\Pi'(p) = 0$ gives its global maximum.
orl-f-436 · level facile · notes
What is new in the pricing chapter of the lab compared with the previous models?
  1. The demand is a fixed datum of the model
  2. The price is a decision variable
  3. The model has integer variables
  4. The capacity is assumed unlimited
Explanation. With the price as a decision, the quantity sold depends on it and the profit $p\cdot q$ contains a bilinear term: the lab meets nonconvexity for the first time.
orl-f-437 · level facile · notes
Revenue management, born in the airlines, adds a capacity constraint to pricing. What makes capacity special in this setting?
  1. It can be stored in a warehouse
  2. It is always unlimited in practice
  3. It costs nothing to expand
  4. Unsold seats are lost
Explanation. Seats, rooms and rental slots perish: what is not sold by the deadline is lost, so price and capacity must be decided together.
orl-f-438 · level facile · notes
Why do the lab notes call the price «perhaps the business decision with the highest impact on margin»?
  1. Price moves the margin of every unit
  2. Because prices never change
  3. Because costs do not depend on volume
  4. Because demand is insensitive to price
Explanation. A price change hits the margin of every unit sold, while a volume change affects only the extra units; hence the attention to pricing models.
orl-f-439 · level facile · notes
One learning goal of the pricing chapter is to recognize «corner optima». What are they?
  1. Optima of linear programs only
  2. Optima found by rounding a solution
  3. Optima where no derivative vanishes
  4. Local optima of nonconvex functions
Explanation. With capacity binding, the optimum can sit where $d(p) = k$: the profit has no stationary point there, so searching only among stationary points would miss it.
orl-f-440 · level facile · notes
Pricing model: choose price $p$ and quantity sold $q$ to maximize $p\,q - c\,q$ subject to $q \le d(p)$ (demand at price $p$) and $q \le k$ (capacity). Linear demand $d(p) = a - bp$. Substituting $q = a - bp$, what does the profit $(p - c)(a - bp)$ become as a function of $p$?
  1. A convex parabola: the problem is hard
  2. A linear function: the optimum is at a bound
  3. A concave parabola: the reduced problem is easy
  4. A cubic with two local maxima
Explanation. The reduced profit is a concave quadratic in $p$, maximized at $p^\circ = (a/b + c)/2$ without capacity; the bilinear version is still solved globally by Gurobi with `NonConvex = 2`.
orl-f-441 · level facile · notes
Pricing model: choose price $p$ and quantity sold $q$ to maximize $p\,q - c\,q$ subject to $q \le d(p)$ (demand at price $p$) and $q \le k$ (capacity). How do the notes treat the general demand functions (constant elasticity $\theta p^{-\varepsilon}$, logistic) in Gurobi?

  3. With a local NLP solver from a guess
  4. By replacing them with linear demand
Explanation. Gurobi's functional constraints (`addGenConstrPow`, `addGenConstrExp`) handle them in the same solver, still with a global optimum.
orl-f-442 · level facile · notes
Pricing model: choose price $p$ and quantity sold $q$ to maximize $p\,q - c\,q$ subject to $q \le d(p)$ (demand at price $p$) and $q \le k$ (capacity). Concert case ($d(p) = 1200 - 5p$, $c = 20$, $k = 400$): Gurobi gives $p^* = 160$, profit $56{,}000$ €; resolving with $k = 401$ estimates the value of one more seat at $59.80$ € (theory: $60$).   1. Because the LP duals are too slow to read
  2. Because the capacity constraint is inactive
  3. Because the result must be an integer
  4. Nonconvex QPs give no duals: perturbation is used
Explanation. With the bilinear objective (`NonConvex = 2`) Gurobi returns no `Pi`; the script sets `v_cap.RHS = K + 1`, re-optimizes and takes the difference.
orl-f-443 · level facile · notes
Pricing model: choose price $p$ and quantity sold $q$ to maximize $p\,q - c\,q$ subject to $q \le d(p)$ (demand at price $p$) and $q \le k$ (capacity). What do the notes recommend before trusting such a result?
  1. Nothing: Gurobi is always right

  3. Round the price to the nearest euro first

orl-f-525 · level teorema · notes
Pricing with linear demand $d(p) = a - bp$, cost $c$, capacity $k$ binding: $p^* = (a - k)/b$ and profit $\Pi(k) = (p^* - c)k$. Why is one more seat worth less than the full margin $p^* - c$?
  1. Because the cost $c$ grows with $k$
  2. Because demand becomes infinite
  3. Because the capacity constraint becomes inactive
  4. Filling it lowers the price for all
Explanation. $dp^*/dk = -1/b$, so $d\Pi/dk = (p^* - c) + k\,dp^*/dk$; in the concert ($a = 1200$, $b = 5$, $c = 20$, $k = 400$) it is $140 - 80 = 60$ €, not 140.
orl-f-526 · level teorema · notes
Pricing with constant-elasticity demand $d(p) = \theta p^{-\varepsilon}$ ($\varepsilon > 1$), cost $c$, capacity $k$; the unconstrained optimum $c\varepsilon/(\varepsilon - 1)$ would exceed capacity. Why is the optimum at $p^* = (\theta/k)^{1/\varepsilon}$, where $d(p) = k$?
  1. It is the only stationary point of the profit
  2. Below it profit rises, above it falls
  3. Because the profit is linear in $p$
  4. Because the elasticity equals 1 there
Explanation. For $p < p^*$ demand exceeds $k$, sales stay at $k$ and $(p - c)k$ grows; for $p > p^*$ the profit $(p - c)\theta p^{-\varepsilon}$ decreases (we are past its unconstrained peak). The optimum is a corner where no derivative vanishes.

8. Advertising budget allocation (26)

orl-f-066 · level facile · notes
Advertising budget model: spend $x_i$ on channel $i$, concave response $r_i(x_i)$, maximize $\sum_i r_i(x_i)$ subject to $\sum_i x_i \le b$ (budget, multiplier $\lambda \ge 0$) and $0 \le x_i \le u_i$ (caps). What holds at the optimum for every channel with $0 < \tilde x_i < u_i$?
  1. $r_i'(\tilde x_i) = \lambda$: the last euro yields the same in all such channels
  2. $\tilde x_i = b/n$: the budget is split evenly among the channels
  3. $r_i(\tilde x_i) = \lambda$: every channel yields the same total response
  4. $r_i'(\tilde x_i) = 0$: every channel is pushed to its saturation point
Explanation. If the marginal returns differed, moving one euro from the low one to the high one would improve the total response.
orl-f-067 · level facile · notes
Advertising budget model: spend $x_i$ on channel $i$, concave response $r_i(x_i)$, maximize $\sum_i r_i(x_i)$ subject to $\sum_i x_i \le b$ (budget, multiplier $\lambda \ge 0$) and $0 \le x_i \le u_i$ (caps). A channel is left at zero in the optimal allocation, $\tilde x_i = 0$. What can be said about it?
  1. Its marginal return $r_i'(0)$ equals $\lambda$
  2. Its cap $u_i$ is too small to be useful
  3. Its initial marginal return $r_i'(0)$ is at most $\lambda$
  4. The budget constraint is not active
Explanation. A channel at zero is not worth even the first euro: $r_i'(0) \le \lambda$. Channels at the cap may instead have marginal return $\ge \lambda$.
orl-f-068 · level facile · notes
Advertising budget model: spend $x_i$ on channel $i$, concave response $r_i(x_i)$, maximize $\sum_i r_i(x_i)$ subject to $\sum_i x_i \le b$ (budget, multiplier $\lambda \ge 0$) and $0 \le x_i \le u_i$ (caps). In the case study the caps are $u = (60, 80, 120, 35)$ thousand € and $\lambda$ drops to zero when the budget reaches $b = 300$. Why?
  1. Because the responses $r_i$ become linear for large spends
  2. Because the solver fails for large budgets
  3. Because the budget constraint becomes an equality
  4. Because all channels are at their caps: $60 + 80 + 120 + 35 = 295 < 300$
Explanation. With every channel at its cap the budget constraint is no longer active, so its multiplier is zero. The budget value curve is concave: $\lambda$ falls as $b$ grows.
orl-f-069 · level facile · notes
Advertising budget model: spend $x_i$ on channel $i$, concave response $r_i(x_i)$, maximize $\sum_i r_i(x_i)$ subject to $\sum_i x_i \le b$ (budget, multiplier $\lambda \ge 0$) and $0 \le x_i \le u_i$ (caps). The responses are $r_i(x) = a_i\log(1 + k_ix)$. In the case study TV gets less than social media although its cap $u_i$ is higher. What explains it?
  1. The cap of TV is active at the optimum
  2. The saturation speed $k_i$ of each channel, not its size
  3. A numerical error of the solver
  4. The scale coefficient $a_i$ alone
Explanation. What matters is how fast the marginal return $a_ik_i/(1 + k_ix)$ decreases, governed by $k_i$: a large channel that saturates quickly gets less.
orl-f-070 · level facile · notes
Advertising budget model: spend $x_i$ on channel $i$, concave response $r_i(x_i)$, maximize $\sum_i r_i(x_i)$ subject to $\sum_i x_i \le b$ (budget, multiplier $\lambda \ge 0$) and $0 \le x_i \le u_i$ (caps). With $r_i(x) = a_i\log(1 + k_ix)$, how is the objective $\sum_i a_i\log(1 + k_ix_i)$ handled in Gurobi?
  1. Auxiliary $g_i = 1 + k_ix_i$, $z_i = \log g_i$ (function constraints), objective $\sum_i a_iz_i$
  2. By replacing each logarithm with its tangent line at $x_i = 0$
  3. By setting `NonConvex = 2` and writing the logarithms directly
  4. By solving the KKT conditions by hand and fixing the $x_i$
Explanation. The function constraints make the objective linear in the $z_i$; the optimum is certified global and the KKT conditions are then checked on the numbers.
orl-f-071 · level facile · notes
Advertising budget model: spend $x_i$ on channel $i$, concave response $r_i(x_i)$, maximize $\sum_i r_i(x_i)$ subject to $\sum_i x_i \le b$ (budget, multiplier $\lambda \ge 0$) and $0 \le x_i \le u_i$ (caps). What does the multiplier $\lambda$ of the budget constraint tell a CFO?
  1. The total response of the campaign at the optimum
  2. The cap of the channel with the best response
  3. The extra response bought by one more euro of budget
  4. The average response per euro spent
Explanation. $\lambda$ is the marginal value of the budget: it is worth adding budget as long as $\lambda$ exceeds the value of a euro invested elsewhere, and $\lambda$ falls as the budget grows.
orl-f-072 · level teorema · notes
Advertising budget model: spend $x_i$ on channel $i$, concave response $r_i(x_i)$, maximize $\sum_i r_i(x_i)$ subject to $\sum_i x_i \le b$ (budget, multiplier $\lambda \ge 0$) and $0 \le x_i \le u_i$ (caps). Which statement justifies that this model has a certifiable global optimum?
  1. Every NLP with linear constraints has a unique optimum
  2. A sum of concave functions is maximized over a polyhedron: local maxima are global
  3. Every separable function is linear on a polyhedron
  4. The objective is increasing, so the budget is active
Explanation. The objective is separable and a sum of concave functions, the constraints are linear: the problem is convex and every local maximum is global.
orl-f-073 · level ipotesi · notes
Advertising budget model: spend $x_i$ on channel $i$, concave response $r_i(x_i)$, maximize $\sum_i r_i(x_i)$ subject to $\sum_i x_i \le b$ (budget, multiplier $\lambda \ge 0$) and $0 \le x_i \le u_i$ (caps). Which ingredients yield the condition $r_i'(\tilde x_i) = \lambda$ for a channel at the optimum?
  1. Only the activity of the budget constraint
  2. The linearity of the response functions $r_i$
  3. Strong LP duality applied to the budget constraint
  4. KKT stationarity, $r_i$ differentiable, and $0 < \tilde x_i < u_i$ (null bound multipliers)
Explanation. For channels on a bound, the multipliers of $x_i \ge 0$ or $x_i \le u_i$ enter the stationarity condition and the equality with $\lambda$ no longer holds.
orl-f-202 · level facile · notes
Advertising budget model: spend $x_i$ on channel $i$, concave response $r_i(x_i)$, maximize $\sum_i r_i(x_i)$ subject to $\sum_i x_i \le b$ (budget, multiplier $\lambda \ge 0$) and $0 \le x_i \le u_i$ (caps). The responses $r_i$ are increasing. What would happen without the budget constraint $\sum_i x_i \le b$?
  1. The model would become infeasible
  2. Every channel would get the same spend
  3. The model would become non-convex
  4. Every channel would go to its cap $u_i$
Explanation. The budget is the only constraint linking the channels: with increasing responses and no budget, each channel is pushed independently to its cap.
orl-f-203 · level facile · notes
Advertising budget model: spend $x_i$ on channel $i$, concave response $r_i(x_i)$, maximize $\sum_i r_i(x_i)$ subject to $\sum_i x_i \le b$ (budget, multiplier $\lambda \ge 0$) and $0 \le x_i \le u_i$ (caps). In the case study, with $b = 100$ thousand €, all four channels lie strictly between $0$ and their caps and their marginal returns $r_i'(\tilde x_i)$ all equal $8.2693$. What does this confirm?

  2. That the budget constraint is not active
  3. The KKT condition $r_i'(\tilde x_i) = \lambda$, with $\lambda = 8.2693$
  4. That the four responses are identical
Explanation. For interior channels stationarity gives $r_i'(\tilde x_i) = \lambda$: the last euro yields the same everywhere, and the common value is the multiplier of the budget.
orl-f-204 · level facile · notes
Advertising budget model: spend $x_i$ on channel $i$, concave response $r_i(x_i)$, maximize $\sum_i r_i(x_i)$ subject to $\sum_i x_i \le b$ (budget, multiplier $\lambda \ge 0$) and $0 \le x_i \le u_i$ (caps). In the case study $\lambda = 8.2693$; adding $1$ (thousand €) to the budget and re-solving, the response grows by $8.244$. Why slightly less than $\lambda$?

  2. Because one channel reaches its cap
  3. Because the multiplier is wrong by about $0.3\%$
  4. Concavity: the marginal value falls as the budget grows
Explanation. The optimal response $V(b)$ is concave: the finite increment over $[b, b + 1]$ is below the slope $\lambda$ at $b$. The check still confirms the reading of the multiplier.
orl-f-205 · level teorema · notes
Advertising budget model: spend $x_i$ on channel $i$, concave response $r_i(x_i)$, maximize $\sum_i r_i(x_i)$ subject to $\sum_i x_i \le b$ (budget, multiplier $\lambda \ge 0$) and $0 \le x_i \le u_i$ (caps). Let $V(b)$ be the optimal total response as a function of the budget $b$. Which statement is correct?
  1. $V(b)$ is concave, with slope $\lambda(b)$ non-increasing in $b$
  2. $V(b)$ is convex, so $\lambda(b)$ grows with the budget
  3. $V(b)$ is linear, so $\lambda$ is the same for every budget
  4. $V(b)$ is decreasing once all channels are active
Explanation. Maximizing a concave function with the budget as right-hand side gives a concave value function: in the case study $\lambda$ falls from $19$ ($b = 20$) to $4$ ($b = 260$).
orl-f-444 · level facile · notes
Why is the response of an advertising channel modelled as a concave function of the spend in the lab?
  1. Because concave functions are linear
  2. Because spend must be an integer
  3. Because every channel saturates at once
  4. The first euros yield much, the next ones less
Explanation. Diminishing marginal returns: each extra euro on a channel adds less response than the previous one, which is exactly concavity.

  4. Sequencing jobs on a machine
Explanation. Marketing channels, funds among projects, time among activities, fertilizer among fields: the same model and the same optimality rule apply.
orl-f-446 · level facile · notes

  1. Marginal returns are equal at the optimum
  2. All channels get the same spend
  3. The largest channel gets everything
  4. The spend is proportional to the caps
Explanation. At the optimum the marginal returns of the active channels coincide, and the solver returns exactly that, digit by digit (the case study shows $8.2693$ for all four channels).
orl-f-447 · level facile · notes

  2. To choose the response functions
  3. To negotiate the size of the budget
  4. To round the spends
Explanation. $\lambda$ is the extra response per extra euro of budget; the curve of $\lambda$ against the budget is the quantitative argument for deciding how much to give to marketing.
orl-f-448 · level media · notes
Advertising budget model: spend $x_i$ on channel $i$, concave response $r_i(x_i)$, maximize $\sum_ir_i(x_i)$ subject to $\sum_ix_i \le b$ (budget, multiplier $\lambda \ge 0$) and $0 \le x_i \le u_i$ (caps). Two channels, $r_1(x) = 100\log(1 + 0.2x)$, $r_2(x) = 60\log(1 + 0.1x)$, budget $b = 50$ (thousand €), no caps. What is the optimal split?
  1. $x_1 = 25$, $x_2 = 25$
  2. $x_1 = 35.6$, $x_2 = 14.4$
  3. $x_1 = 50$, $x_2 = 0$
  4. $x_1 = 31.25$, $x_2 = 18.75$
Explanation. Equal marginals: $20/(1 + 0.2x_1) = 6/(1 + 0.1x_2)$ with $x_2 = 50 - x_1$ gives $120 - 2x_1 = 6 + 1.2x_1$, so $x_1 = 114/3.2 = 35.6$, $x_2 = 14.4$.
orl-f-449 · level facile · notes
Advertising budget model: spend $x_i$ on channel $i$, concave response $r_i(x_i)$, maximize $\sum_ir_i(x_i)$ subject to $\sum_ix_i \le b$ (budget, multiplier $\lambda \ge 0$) and $0 \le x_i \le u_i$ (caps). Two channels, $r_1(x) = 100\log(1 + 0.2x)$, $r_2(x) = 60\log(1 + 0.1x)$, $b = 50$: the optimum is $x_1 = 35.6$, $x_2 = 14.4$. What is the shadow price of the budget?

  2. $\lambda = 6.0$
  3. $\lambda = 0.0$
  4. $\lambda = 2.46$
Explanation. $\lambda = r_1'(35.6) = 20/(1 + 0.2\cdot 35.6) = 2.46$, the same as $r_2'(14.4) = 6/2.44$: one more euro of budget yields about $2.46$ units, whichever channel it enters.

orl-f-452 · level facile · notes
Advertising budget model: spend $x_i$ on channel $i$, concave response $r_i(x_i)$, maximize $\sum_ir_i(x_i)$ subject to $\sum_ix_i \le b$ (budget, multiplier $\lambda \ge 0$) and $0 \le x_i \le u_i$ (caps). In the gurobipy code, with $r_i(x) = a_i\log(1 + k_ix)$, the script creates `g = m.addVars(4, lb=1.0)` and `z = m.addVars(4, lb=-GRB.INFINITY)`, with `g[i] == 1 + k[i]*x[i]` and `addGenConstrLog(g[i], z[i])`. Why is `z` free?

  3. Because a logarithm can be negative in general
  4. Because $z_i$ is a binary variable
Explanation. `addGenConstrLog` imposes $z = \log g$; the default bound $z \ge 0$ would cut values where $g < 1$. Here $g \ge 1$ so $z \ge 0$ anyway, but declaring it free avoids a hidden restriction.

orl-f-454 · level facile · notes

Advertising budget model: spend $x_i$ on channel $i$, concave response $r_i(x_i)$, maximize $\sum_ir_i(x_i)$ subject to $\sum_ix_i \le b$ (budget, multiplier $\lambda \ge 0$) and $0 \le x_i \le u_i$ (caps). orl-f-527 · level ipotesi · notes
Advertising budget model: maximize $\sum_ir_i(x_i)$ (concave) with $\sum_ix_i \le b$ (multiplier $\lambda$) and $0 \le x_i \le u_i$. The rule $r_i'(\tilde x_i) = \lambda$ holds for channels strictly inside $(0, u_i)$. What happens for a channel at its cap $u_i$?
  1. $r_i'(u_i) \ge \lambda$
  2. $r_i'(u_i) = \lambda$ in any case
  3. $r_i'(u_i) \le \lambda$
  4. $r_i'(u_i) = 0$ at the cap
Explanation. Stationarity reads $r_i'(\tilde x_i) = \lambda + \mu_i$ with $\mu_i \ge 0$ the multiplier of $x_i \le u_i$: a capped channel would deserve more money but cannot take it. Dropping the hypothesis «interior» changes the condition.

9. Continuous location of a service (26)

orl-f-074 · level facile · notes
Continuous location of one facility at $(x, y)$ serving districts $i$ at $(a_i, b_i)$ with weights $w_i$ (population). Which objective has a closed-form optimum?
  1. Weber: weighted sum of the distances
  2. Minimax: maximum distance
  3. Weighted sum of the squared distances
  4. None of the three objectives
Explanation. Setting the gradient of $\sum_i w_i[(x - a_i)^2 + (y - b_i)^2]$ to zero gives the weighted centroid $\tilde x = \sum_i w_ia_i/\sum_i w_i$ (and similarly for $y$).
orl-f-075 · level facile · notes
Continuous location of one facility at $(x, y)$ serving districts $i$ at $(a_i, b_i)$ with weights $w_i$ (population). Which property characterizes the optimum of the minimax model (minimize the distance of the farthest district)?
  1. It is the centre of the smallest circle containing all the districts
  2. It is the weighted centroid of the districts
  3. It is the weighted median of the districts, coordinate by coordinate
  4. It always coincides with the Weber point
Explanation. It depends only on the extreme districts and ignores the weights: this is why the minimax protects the outskirts, regardless of their population.
orl-f-076 · level facile · notes
Continuous location of one facility at $(x, y)$ serving districts $i$ at $(a_i, b_i)$ with weights $w_i$ (population). Weber model: minimize $\sum_i w_i\,d_i$, with $d_i$ the Euclidean distance from district $i$. Which condition holds at the optimum, when it is not a district point?
  1. The facility is equidistant from all the districts
  2. The gradient of the squared distances vanishes
  3. The maximum distance $\max_i d_i$ is minimal
  4. $\sum_i w_i\,\mathbf u_i = \mathbf 0$, with $\mathbf u_i$ the unit vector towards district $i$
Explanation. The weighted unit “pulls” balance; the function is not differentiable at the district points and has no closed form.
orl-f-077 · level facile · notes
Continuous location of one facility at $(x, y)$ serving districts $i$ at $(a_i, b_i)$ with weights $w_i$ (population). How are the Euclidean distances written in the Gurobi model of the case study?
  1. With a piecewise-linear approximation of the square root
  2. With a variable $d_i$ and the convex conic constraint $(x - a_i)^2 + (y - b_i)^2 \le d_i^2$
  3. With `NonConvex = 2` and the squared distances in the objective
  4. With binary variables that select the nearest district
Explanation. Weber minimizes $\sum_i w_id_i$, the minimax a variable $z$ with $d_i \le z$: purely conic (convex) structures need no special parameter in Gurobi.
orl-f-078 · level facile · notes
Continuous location of one facility at $(x, y)$ serving districts $i$ at $(a_i, b_i)$ with weights $w_i$ (population). The efficiency-equity frontier plots the weighted mean distance against the maximum distance. In the case study it is almost flat. What does it mean?
  1. Efficiency and equity are in strong conflict
  2. The model with both objectives is infeasible
  3. Lowering the maximum distance costs very little mean distance
  4. The Weber and minimax points coincide
Explanation. Guaranteeing $0.63$ km less to the farthest district costs only $47$ metres of mean distance: a strong argument in a public discussion.
orl-f-079 · level facile · notes
Continuous location of one facility at $(x, y)$ serving districts $i$ at $(a_i, b_i)$ with weights $w_i$ (population). How is the efficiency-equity frontier (mean distance versus maximum distance) built with the $\varepsilon$-constraint method?
  1. Minimize the mean distance with a cap $D$ on every distance, varying $D$
  2. Minimize the sum of the mean distance and of the maximum distance
  3. Solve Weber and minimax separately and average the two locations
  4. Vary the weights $w_i$ of the districts and re-solve Weber
Explanation. Each value of $D$ gives one point of the frontier; the extremes are the minimax solution (smallest feasible $D$) and the Weber point (large $D$).
orl-f-080 · level teorema · notes
Location on a line: customers at positions $a_i$ with weights $w_i \ge 0$. Which statement about $\min_x \sum_i w_i|x - a_i|$ (Weber in one dimension) is correct?
  1. The optimum is the weighted mean $\sum_i w_ia_i/\sum_i w_i$
  2. An optimum is the weighted median: at least half of the weight on each side
  3. The optimum is the midpoint of the two extreme customers
  4. The optimum is the unweighted median of the positions
Explanation. Moving away from the weighted median increases the cost on the heavier side faster than it decreases on the other. The weighted mean solves the squared distances, the midpoint the minimax.
orl-f-081 · level ipotesi · notes
Continuous location of one facility at $(x, y)$ serving districts $i$ at $(a_i, b_i)$ with weights $w_i$ (population). Which facts make the Weber objective $\sum_i w_i\sqrt{(x - a_i)^2 + (y - b_i)^2}$ convex in $(x, y)$?
  1. It is differentiable everywhere in the plane
  2. The squared distance is convex and the square root is increasing
  3. The districts are finitely many and numbered
  4. Norm of an affine map is convex, $w_i \ge 0$, sums of convex functions are convex
Explanation. Each term is the Euclidean norm of an affine function of $(x, y)$; the weights are non-negative. Differentiability fails at the district points, but convexity holds. Note: increasing $\circ$ convex is convex only if the outer function is also convex, and the square root is concave.
orl-f-206 · level facile · notes
Continuous location of one facility at $(x, y)$ serving districts $i$ at $(a_i, b_i)$ with weights $w_i$ (population). The facility must lie within distance $r$ of an electrical substation at $(a_0, b_0)$. How is this constraint written, and is it convex?
  1. $|x - a_0| + |y - b_0| \ge r$, non-convex
  2. $(x - a_0)^2 + (y - b_0)^2 \le r^2$, convex
  3. $(x - a_0)^2 + (y - b_0)^2 \ge r^2$, convex
  4. $x \le a_0 + r$ only, linear
Explanation. A disc is a convex set; adding it to any convex location objective keeps the problem convex. The $\ge$ version would describe the outside of a disc, which is not convex.
orl-f-207 · level facile · notes
Continuous location of one facility at $(x, y)$ serving districts $i$ at $(a_i, b_i)$ with weights $w_i$ (population). In the case study the facility must be within 2 km of a substation; the constrained Weber optimum lies on the boundary of that disc and costs only $+0.2\%$. What is the managerial lesson?
  1. The constraint is inactive and could be removed
  2. The Weber model is not suited to constrained problems
  3. Equity and efficiency are in strong conflict
  4. A feared constraint may turn out almost free: measure it
Explanation. The constraint is active (optimum on the boundary) but its cost is tiny: discovering it is a result as important as the optimum itself.
orl-f-208 · level facile · notes
Continuous location of one facility at $(x, y)$ serving districts $i$ at $(a_i, b_i)$ with weights $w_i$ (population). In the case study (12 districts) the Weber point $(4.886, 5.310)$ almost coincides with the weighted centroid $(4.897, 5.397)$, while the minimax point moves half a kilometre away. Why are Weber and centroid so close?
  1. Because the weights are fairly evenly distributed around the centre
  2. Because Weber and centroid always coincide
  3. Because the minimax ignores the weights
  4. Because the substation constraint is active
Explanation. Weber (sum of distances) and centroid (sum of squared distances) differ when a few heavy or far districts pull differently; with balanced weights they are close, while the minimax follows the extreme districts.
orl-f-209 · level facile · notes
Continuous location of one facility at $(x, y)$ serving districts $i$ at $(a_i, b_i)$ with weights $w_i$ (population). The efficiency-equity frontier plots the minimum mean distance against a cap $D$ on the maximum distance. What would a **steep** frontier mean?
  1. That equity is almost free
  2. That the model is badly scaled
  3. That Weber and minimax coincide
  4. A real conflict: each km less for the farthest costs much mean distance
Explanation. In the case study the frontier is almost flat (equity almost free); a steep one would signal that protecting the outskirts has a high efficiency cost.
orl-f-457 · level facile · notes
Continuous location of one facility at $(x, y)$ serving districts $i$ at $(a_i, b_i)$ with weights $w_i$ (population). The chapter compares three meanings of «close to the demand». Which objective expresses **equity** toward the worst-off user?
  1. The weighted mean distance (Weber)
  2. The weighted squared distance
  3. The maximum distance (minimax)
  4. The number of districts served
Explanation. Mean distance measures efficiency, the squared distance leads to the barycenter, and the maximum distance protects the farthest district.
orl-f-458 · level facile · notes
Why do the lab notes stress that location decisions deserve a careful model?
  1. They are costly to correct
  2. They can be changed every week
  3. They never involve trade-offs
  4. They always have closed-form answers
Explanation. A hub in the wrong place stays there for years; the continuous version of the problem makes the efficiency–equity conflict visible.
orl-f-459 · level facile · notes
Which facilities does the lab cite as examples for the continuous location model?
  1. Warehouses chosen from a fixed list
  2. Machines on a production line
  3. Bank branches to close down
  4. A charging station or a micro-hub
Explanation. In the continuous version the coordinates are free on the plane: a charging station, a logistic micro-hub or a health post placed «close» to the demand.
orl-f-460 · level facile · notes
How is the efficiency–equity frontier of the location chapter built?
  1. By averaging Weber and minimax points
  2. With a cap on the max distance
  3. By solving the quadratic model only
  4. By enumerating all the districts
Explanation. Minimize the mean distance subject to a cap $D$ on the maximum distance, and vary $D$ between the minimax and the Weber values.
orl-f-461 · level facile · notes
Continuous location of one facility at $(x, y)$ serving districts $i$ at $(a_i, b_i)$ with weights $w_i$ (population). Three customers on a road at km $0$, $4$ and $10$, weights $1$, $1$ and $3$. What is the barycenter (weighted squared distance)?
  1. $x = 4.67$, the plain mean
  2. $x = 6.8$
  3. $x = 10$
  4. $x = 5$
Explanation. $(1\cdot 0 + 1\cdot 4 + 3\cdot 10)/5 = 34/5 = 6.8$: unlike the median it depends on how far the points are, not only on which side.
orl-f-462 · level facile · notes
Continuous location of one facility at $(x, y)$ serving districts $i$ at $(a_i, b_i)$ with weights $w_i$ (population). Three customers on a road at km $0$, $4$ and $10$, weights $1$, $1$ and $3$. Where is the minimax location (smallest maximum distance)?
  1. $x = 10$
  2. $x = 6.8$
  3. $x = 4$
  4. $x = 5$
Explanation. The midpoint of the extreme customers, $(0 + 10)/2 = 5$, whatever the weights: three reasonable objectives give $10$, $6.8$ and $5$.
orl-f-463 · level facile · notes
Continuous location of one facility at $(x, y)$ serving districts $i$ at $(a_i, b_i)$ with weights $w_i$ (population). Three customers on a road at km $0$, $4$, $10$, weights $1$, $1$, $3$: Weber (sum of weighted distances) gives $x = 10$. Why does moving left from $10$ by $\delta$ make things worse?
  1. The first two save $2\delta$, the third pays $3\delta$
  2. All three customers pay more
  3. The cost function is constant there
  4. The road ends at km 10
Explanation. Moving left lowers the distances of the two customers on the left (weight 2 in all) and raises that of the customer with weight 3: net $+\delta$. The optimum is the weighted median.
orl-f-464 · level facile · notes
Continuous location of one facility at $(x, y)$ serving districts $i$ at $(a_i, b_i)$ with weights $w_i$ (population). Case study: in the gurobipy code each distance is a variable $d_k$ with `dx[k]**2 + dy[k]**2 <= d[k]*d[k]`. What kind of constraint is it?
  1. A linear constraint in $d_k$
  2. A nonconvex bilinear constraint
  3. A convex conic constraint
  4. A functional constraint on a logarithm
Explanation. $\sqrt{d_x^2 + d_y^2} \le d_k$ is a second-order cone: convex, handled by Gurobi without `NonConvex`; minimizing $\sum_kw_kd_k$ pushes each $d_k$ down to the true distance.
orl-f-465 · level facile · notes
Continuous location of one facility at $(x, y)$ serving districts $i$ at $(a_i, b_i)$ with weights $w_i$ (population). Case study: twelve districts, and the station must stay within $r = 2$ km of the electrical substation at $(7, 6)$. How is this written?
  1. $(x - 7)^2 + (y - 6)^2 \le 4$
  2. $|x - 7| + |y - 6| = 2$
  3. $x \le 7$ and $y \le 6$, a box
  4. As a penalty in the objective
Explanation. A disc around the substation is a convex region, so the constrained Weber problem stays convex; the constraint is a quadratic inequality.
orl-f-466 · level facile · notes
Continuous location of one facility at $(x, y)$ serving districts $i$ at $(a_i, b_i)$ with weights $w_i$ (population). Case study: how does the minimax model use the distance variables $d_k$?
  1. It minimizes $\sum_kw_kd_k$
  2. It minimizes $\sum_kd_k^2$
  3. It fixes every $d_k$ to the same value
  4. It minimizes $z$, with $d_k \le z$
Explanation. Same conic distances as Weber, but the objective is an auxiliary $z$ bounded below by every distance: the largest distance is minimized.
orl-f-467 · level facile · notes
Continuous location of one facility at $(x, y)$ serving districts $i$ at $(a_i, b_i)$ with weights $w_i$ (population). Case results: Weber $(4.886, 5.310)$ with mean $3.344$ km and max $6.314$ km; minimax $(5.496, 5.029)$ with mean $3.391$ km and max $5.680$ km. How is the move of the minimax point read?
  1. An error: the two should coincide
  2. It moves toward the peripheral districts
  3. It moves toward the most populated district
  4. It moves to the substation
Explanation. The minimax point shifts over half a km south-east to protect the outlying districts, cutting the maximum distance by $0.63$ km at a small cost in mean distance.
orl-f-468 · level facile · notes
Continuous location of one facility at $(x, y)$ serving districts $i$ at $(a_i, b_i)$ with weights $w_i$ (population). Case results: Weber constrained to stay within 2 km of the substation lies on the boundary of the disc and costs $+0.2\%$ of total distance. What is the managerial lesson?
  1. The constraint should be removed
  2. The substation is badly placed
  3. The constrained model is infeasible
  4. A feared constraint may be cheap
Explanation. The constraint is active (optimum on the boundary) but costs very little: discovering that is a result as important as the optimum itself.
orl-f-469 · level facile · notes
Continuous location of one facility at $(x, y)$ serving districts $i$ at $(a_i, b_i)$ with weights $w_i$ (population). Efficiency–equity frontier of the case: cap $D = 5.680$ km gives mean $3.391$, $D = 5.997$ gives $3.351$, $D = 6.314$ gives $3.344$. What does a frontier this flat mean?
  1. Equity costs very little in efficiency
  2. Efficiency and equity are in strong conflict
  3. The minimax solution is infeasible
  4. The mean distance is independent of the cap
Explanation. Guaranteeing $0.63$ km less to the farthest district costs only $47$ m of mean distance: the fair solution is «almost free»; a steep frontier would signal a real conflict.
orl-f-528 · level teorema · notes
Continuous location with the weighted squared distance $\sum_iw_i[(x - a_i)^2 + (y - b_i)^2]$ ($w_i > 0$ weights, $(a_i, b_i)$ district coordinates). Which statement is correct?
  1. The optimum is the weighted median
  2. The optimum has no closed form
  3. The weighted barycenter
  4. The optimum is the center of the smallest enclosing circle
Explanation. The objective is a strictly convex quadratic; setting its gradient to zero, $\sum_iw_i(x - a_i) = 0$, gives the barycenter (and similarly for $y$): the only one of the three objectives with a closed form.

10. Smart charging of electric vehicles (26)

orl-f-082 · level facile · notes
EV charging LP: power $x_{vt}$ to vehicle $v$ in hour $t$ (1 h), energy price $\pi_t$, availability datum $a_{vt} \in \{0,1\}$, charger power $\bar p_v$, required energy $e_v$, efficiency $\eta$, base load $b_t$, meter limit $k$; minimize $\sum_{v,t}\pi_tx_{vt}$. Why does the model need no binary variables?
  1. Availability $a_{vt}$ is an observed datum and the decision (how much power) is continuous
  2. Gurobi cannot handle binary variables in models of this size
  3. All the vehicles are plugged in during every hour of the night
  4. The energy prices $\pi_t$ are the same in every hour
Explanation. The constraint $x_{vt} \le a_{vt}\,\bar p_v$ sets the power to zero in the hours of absence: no on/off decision is needed.
orl-f-083 · level facile · notes
EV charging LP: power $x_{vt}$ to vehicle $v$ in hour $t$ (1 h), energy price $\pi_t$, availability datum $a_{vt} \in \{0,1\}$, charger power $\bar p_v$, required energy $e_v$, efficiency $\eta$, base load $b_t$, meter limit $k$; minimize $\sum_{v,t}\pi_tx_{vt}$. The energy constraint of vehicle $v$ is $\eta\sum_t x_{vt} \ge e_v$. Why does it hold with equality at the optimum?
  1. Because $\eta < 1$ forces extra energy to be drawn
  2. Because the meter constraint is always saturated
  3. Because prices are positive: extra energy only adds cost
  4. Because the charging windows are disjoint
Explanation. Charging more than necessary is allowed but never worthwhile: with $\pi_t > 0$ any extra kWh only increases the cost, so the requirement is met exactly.
orl-f-084 · level facile · notes
EV charging LP: power $x_{vt}$ to vehicle $v$ in hour $t$ (1 h), energy price $\pi_t$, availability datum $a_{vt} \in \{0,1\}$, charger power $\bar p_v$, required energy $e_v$, efficiency $\eta$, base load $b_t$, meter limit $k$; minimize $\sum_{v,t}\pi_tx_{vt}$. In the case study the shadow price of the energy constraint of one vehicle is $0.0947 = 0.09/\eta$ €/kWh. What does it represent?
  1. The average energy price of the night
  2. The price of the vehicle's marginal hour, divided by $\eta$
  3. The energy price of the evening peak
  4. The hourly cost of the meter capacity $k$
Explanation. The marginal kWh is bought in the cheapest hour still free in the vehicle's window; one more kWh in the battery needs $1/\eta$ kWh from the grid at that hour's price.
orl-f-085 · level facile · notes
EV charging LP: power $x_{vt}$ to vehicle $v$ in hour $t$ (1 h), energy price $\pi_t$, availability datum $a_{vt} \in \{0,1\}$, charger power $\bar p_v$, required energy $e_v$, efficiency $\eta$, base load $b_t$, meter limit $k$; minimize $\sum_{v,t}\pi_tx_{vt}$. How is the peak-shaving variant (minimize the highest hourly draw from the grid) formulated?
  1. Adding a binary variable for each peak hour
  2. Minimizing $\sum_v\sum_t x_{vt}^2$
  3. Fixing the power of every vehicle to $e_v/(\eta\, m)$
  4. Minimizing $z$ subject to $\sum_v x_{vt} + b_t \le z$ for every hour $t$
Explanation. The auxiliary variable $z$ is the peak draw; the multi-objective variant minimizes cost $+\,\rho\, z$.
orl-f-086 · level facile · notes
EV charging LP: power $x_{vt}$ to vehicle $v$ in hour $t$ (1 h), energy price $\pi_t$, availability datum $a_{vt} \in \{0,1\}$, charger power $\bar p_v$, required energy $e_v$, efficiency $\eta$, base load $b_t$, meter limit $k$; minimize $\sum_{v,t}\pi_tx_{vt}$. Peak shaving minimizes the peak draw $z$. In the case study the pure minimax (peak 68 kW) spends $27.79$ €, while minimizing cost $+\,0.2\,z$ reaches the same peak for $22.15$ €. What is the lesson?
  1. With two objectives, optimizing one alone wastes the other
  2. The pure minimax is always the best choice for the meter
  3. Peak shaving is never convenient for the operator
  4. The energy cost does not depend on the peak
Explanation. The pure minimax is degenerate in the cost: among the solutions with minimum peak it returns an arbitrary one. The weighted objective picks the cheapest.

A sensitivity analysis on the meter limit $k$ gives the same cost for every value of $k$, even very small ones. What should be suspected first?

orl-f-088 · level facile · notes

Which constraints couple the vehicles to one another?

  3. When the meter constraint is active in every hour $t$
  4. When in hour $t$ it is plugged in and no power limit is saturated
Explanation. The marginal kWh can then be bought exactly in hour $t$; if that hour were saturated, the dual would also include the value of the binding limit.
orl-f-210 · level facile · notes
EV charging, one vehicle, two hours: it needs $e = 10$ kWh ($\eta = 1$), prices $\pi_1 = 0.12$ and $\pi_2 = 0.20$ €/kWh, charger power $\bar p = 8$ kW, so it charges 8 kWh in hour 1 and 2 in hour 2 (hours of 1 h, so kW and kWh coincide). How much would one more kW of charger power in hour 1 save?
  1. $0.20$ €, the price of hour 2
  2. $0.12$ €, the price of hour 1
  3. $0.08$ €, the price difference $\pi_2 - \pi_1$
  4. Nothing, since the requirement is already met
Explanation. One more kW in hour 1 moves one kWh from the expensive to the cheap hour: saving $0.20 - 0.12 = 0.08$ €. That is the (absolute) value of the dual of the power limit.

orl-f-245 · level ipotesi · notes
EV charging LP: power $x_{vt}$ to vehicle $v$ in hour $t$ (1 h), energy price $\pi_t$, availability datum $a_{vt} \in \{0,1\}$, charger power $\bar p_v$, required energy $e_v$, efficiency $\eta$, base load $b_t$, meter limit $k$; minimize $\sum_{v,t}\pi_tx_{vt}$. Which condition on the data is necessary for the LP to be feasible, whatever the meter limit?
  1. $e_v \le \bar p_v$ for every vehicle $v$: one hour of charging is enough
  2. $\sum_t \pi_t \ge e_v$ for every vehicle $v$
  3. $\eta\,\bar p_v \sum_t a_{vt} \ge e_v$ for every vehicle $v$

Explanation. Vehicle $v$ can receive at most $\eta\,\bar p_v$ kWh in each hour in which it is plugged in: if $\eta\,\bar p_v\sum_t a_{vt} < e_v$ its energy constraint cannot hold. The meter can make the model infeasible even when this holds.
orl-f-470 · level facile · notes
A company fleet of electric vans charges at night when energy is cheap. What risk does the smart-charging chapter of the lab point out?
  1. Vans may not be available in the morning
  2. If all charge together, the peak load explodes
  3. Energy prices are fixed by contract
  4. Chargers cannot modulate power
Explanation. Charging everything in the cheap hours concentrates the demand: power costs grow and the meter limit can be exceeded; the model spreads the power over vehicles and hours.
orl-f-471 · level facile · notes
The smart-charging problem «seems» to need on/off variables but is a pure LP. Why?
  1. Because Gurobi cannot handle binaries
  2. Because vehicles never stop charging
  3. Because prices are constant
  4. The true decision is continuous
Explanation. The model decides the power given to each vehicle in each hour, a continuous quantity between 0 and the charger power: no on/off variable is needed.

11. Service capacity and waiting times (22)

orl-f-090 · level facile · notes
M/M/1 capacity model: arrival rate $\lambda$, capacity (service rate) $\mu > \lambda$, cost $c$ per unit of capacity, value $h$ of one hour of a customer in the system; minimize $C(\mu) = c\,\mu + h\lambda/(\mu - \lambda)$. What is the optimal utilization $\rho = \lambda/\tilde\mu$?
  1. $100\%$, because unused capacity is waste
  2. Below $100\%$: spare capacity keeps the queue short
  3. Exactly $50\%$, whatever the data
  4. Undefined, because the cost diverges
Explanation. In the worked example $\lambda = 8$, $c = 2$, $h = 4$: $\tilde\mu = 12$, $\rho = 66.7\%$; with $\mu = 9$ ($\rho = 89\%$) the cost rises from $32$ to $50$ €/h.
orl-f-091 · level facile · notes
M/M/1 capacity model: arrival rate $\lambda$, capacity (service rate) $\mu > \lambda$, cost $c$ per unit of capacity, value $h$ of one hour of a customer in the system; minimize $C(\mu) = c\,\mu + h\lambda/(\mu - \lambda)$. The optimum has safety margin $\tilde\mu - \lambda = \sqrt{h\lambda/c}$. How does it grow with the demand $\lambda$?
  1. Like $\sqrt{\lambda}$: large systems need proportionally less buffer
  2. Linearly in $\lambda$: the buffer is a fixed share of demand
  3. Like $\lambda^2$: queues explode with demand
  4. It does not depend on $\lambda$
Explanation. Capacity = demand + safety margin, and the margin grows less than proportionally to demand: economies of scale in the buffer (pooling).
orl-f-092 · level facile · notes
M/M/1 queue (Poisson arrivals at rate $\lambda$, exponential service at rate $\mu > \lambda$, one server). Which formulas give the mean time in the system $w$ and the mean number of customers in the system $l$?
  1. $w = 1/\mu$, $l = \lambda/\mu$
  2. $w = \lambda/(\mu - \lambda)$, $l = 1/(\mu - \lambda)$
  3. $w = 1/(\mu - \lambda)$, $l = \lambda/(\mu - \lambda)$
  4. $w = \mu/\lambda$, $l = \mu - \lambda$
Explanation. $w = 1/(\mu - \lambda)$ and, by Little's law, $l = \lambda\,w$; $1/\mu$ is only the mean service time.
orl-f-093 · level facile · notes
M/M/1 capacity model: arrival rate $\lambda$, capacity (service rate) $\mu > \lambda$, cost $c$ per unit of capacity, value $h$ of one hour of a customer in the system; minimize $C(\mu) = c\,\mu + h\lambda/(\mu - \lambda)$. Marketing promises a mean time in the system $w = 1/(\mu - \lambda)$ of at most $w_{\max}$. Which constraint on the capacity follows?
  1. $\mu \le \lambda + 1/w_{\max}$
  2. $\mu \ge \lambda\,w_{\max}$
  3. $\mu \ge 1/w_{\max}$
  4. $\mu \ge \lambda + 1/w_{\max}$
Explanation. From $1/(\mu - \lambda) \le w_{\max}$; the constraint matters only when it is tighter than the economic optimum $\tilde\mu$.
orl-f-094 · level facile · notes
M/M/1 capacity model: arrival rate $\lambda$, capacity (service rate) $\mu > \lambda$, cost $c$ per unit of capacity, value $h$ of one hour of a customer in the system; minimize $C(\mu) = c\,\mu + h\lambda/(\mu - \lambda)$. In the case study $\lambda$ is uncertain in $[36, 48]$ and the optimum computed for the nominal demand is $\tilde\mu = 46.58$. Why is sizing the system on it dangerous?
  1. Because $C(\mu)$ is not convex for uncertain $\lambda$
  2. Because with $\lambda = 48 > \tilde\mu$ the queue is unstable
  3. Because $\tilde\mu$ is larger than any likely demand
  4. Because the formula of $\tilde\mu$ holds only for $\lambda < 36$
Explanation. With $\mu \le \lambda$ the queue grows without bound and the cost diverges. Minimizing the worst-case cost gives $\mu = 52.90$: an insurance premium of about $13\%$.
orl-f-095 · level media · notes
M/M/1 capacity model: arrival rate $\lambda$, capacity (service rate) $\mu > \lambda$, cost $c$ per unit of capacity, value $h$ of one hour of a customer in the system; minimize $C(\mu) = c\,\mu + h\lambda/(\mu - \lambda)$. When the promise $w \le w_{\max}$ is active, $\mu = \lambda + 1/w_{\max}$ and the cost is $C(w_{\max}) = c\lambda + c/w_{\max} + h\lambda\,w_{\max}$. With $c = 3$, $h = 1.5$, $\lambda = 42$ and $w_{\max} = 0.1$ h, what is $dC/dw_{\max}$?

  3. $-300$ €/h per hour of promise
  4. $-30$ €/h per hour of promise
Explanation. $dC/dw_{\max} = -c/w_{\max}^2 + h\lambda = -3/0.01 + 63 = -237$: relaxing the promise saves money, the number to give marketing before signing the SLA.
orl-f-096 · level facile · notes
M/M/1 queue with utilization $\rho = \lambda/\mu$ and mean time in the system $w = 1/(\mu - \lambda)$. The “utilization wall” plot shows $w$ as $\rho$ grows, for a fixed $\mu$. What does it show?
  1. The total cost decreases as $\rho$ grows
  2. The waiting time does not depend on $\rho$
  3. From $\rho = 90\%$ to $\rho = 99\%$ the time $w$ is multiplied by ten
  4. The optimal utilization is $99\%$
Explanation. $w = 1/(\mu(1 - \rho))$ explodes like a hyperbola as $\rho \to 1$: saturating resources and giving good service are in mathematical conflict.
orl-f-097 · level teorema · notes
M/M/1 capacity model: arrival rate $\lambda$, capacity (service rate) $\mu > \lambda$, cost $c$ per unit of capacity, value $h$ of one hour of a customer in the system; minimize $C(\mu) = c\,\mu + h\lambda/(\mu - \lambda)$. Which statement about $C(\mu)$ on $\mu > \lambda$ is correct?
  1. It is concave, so its stationary point is a maximum
  2. It is linear in $\mu$, so the optimum is at a bound
  3. It has no minimum on $(\lambda, +\infty)$
  4. It is convex there, so $\tilde\mu = \lambda + \sqrt{h\lambda/c}$ is the global minimum
Explanation. $C''(\mu) = 2h\lambda/(\mu - \lambda)^3 > 0$; the cost tends to $+\infty$ both as $\mu \to \lambda^+$ and as $\mu \to +\infty$.
orl-f-098 · level ipotesi · notes
M/M/1 capacity model: arrival rate $\lambda$, capacity (service rate) $\mu > \lambda$, cost $c$ per unit of capacity, value $h$ of one hour of a customer in the system; minimize $C(\mu) = c\,\mu + h\lambda/(\mu - \lambda)$. To claim that $\tilde\mu = \lambda + \sqrt{h\lambda/c}$ is the optimal capacity, which facts are used?
  1. Strong LP duality
  2. $C'(\tilde\mu) = 0$, convexity of $C$ on $(\lambda, +\infty)$ and $\tilde\mu > \lambda$
  3. That the arrival rate $\lambda$ is an integer
  4. That the utilization at the optimum is $100\%$
Explanation. The derivative vanishes at $\tilde\mu$, which satisfies the stability requirement; convexity turns the stationary point into the global minimum.
orl-f-214 · level facile · notes
M/M/1 capacity model: arrival rate $\lambda$, capacity (service rate) $\mu > \lambda$, cost $c$ per unit of capacity, value $h$ of one hour of a customer in the system; minimize $C(\mu) = c\,\mu + h\lambda/(\mu - \lambda)$.   3. It forces $\mu = \lambda + 4 = 46$, saving money
  4. It is infeasible at this demand
