orl-f-215 · level facile · notes
M/M/1 capacity model: arrival rate $\lambda$, capacity (service rate) $\mu > \lambda$, cost $c$ per unit of capacity, value $h$ of one hour of a customer in the system; minimize $C(\mu) = c\,\mu + h\lambda/(\mu - \lambda)$. In the Gurobi model of the case study the mean time $w$ is a variable linked to $\mu$ by the constraint $w(\mu - \lambda) = 1$. Which parameter does this require?

  2. `QCPDual = 1`, to get the multipliers
  3. No parameter: the constraint is linear
  4. `NonConvex = 2`, because $w\mu$ is a bilinear term

orl-f-216 · level facile · notes
M/M/1 capacity model: arrival rate $\lambda$, capacity (service rate) $\mu > \lambda$, cost $c$ per unit of capacity, value $h$ of one hour of a customer in the system; minimize $C(\mu) = c\,\mu + h\lambda/(\mu - \lambda)$. In the case study, tightening the promise on the mean time in the system from 12 to 9 minutes costs $1.85$ €/h, while going from 3 to 2 minutes costs $28.95$ €/h. What does this show?

  2. That the cost of the promise is linear in $w_{\max}$
  3. That the price of the promise grows explosively as $w_{\max}$ shrinks
  4. That the promise is never binding
Explanation. With the promise active, $C(w_{\max}) = c\lambda + c/w_{\max} + h\lambda w_{\max}$: the term $c/w_{\max}$ makes the marginal cost explode for short promises.
orl-f-482 · level facile · notes
What is the central, counter-intuitive message of the lab chapter on service capacity and waiting times?
  1. Capacity should always be minimized
  2. Waiting times do not depend on capacity
  3. The optimal utilization is not 100%
  4. Queues disappear at 100% utilization
Explanation. Capacity costs money but so does waiting; the optimum leaves part of the capacity idle, which is what keeps queues short.

  3. Only manufacturing assembly lines
  4. Services with deterministic arrivals
Explanation. Any service with random arrivals: capacity costs, waiting costs too; queueing theory gives the exact relation between the two.

  4. Capacity $\mu$ becomes a variable
Explanation. The M/M/1 formulas are standard; the chapter treats the service rate as a decision and chooses it by minimizing capacity cost plus waiting cost.

  4. A Markov chain with a single state only

M/M/1 capacity model (Poisson arrivals, exponential service, one server): arrival rate $\lambda$, capacity (service rate) $\mu > \lambda$, cost $c$ per unit of capacity, value $h$ of one hour of a customer in the system; minimize $C(\mu) = c\mu + h\lambda/(\mu - \lambda)$, whose optimum is $\tilde\mu = \lambda + \sqrt{h\lambda/c}$. 12. Data Envelopment Analysis (44)

orl-f-099 · level facile · notes
DEA: $n$ units (DMUs) $j$ use inputs $x_{ij}$ to produce outputs $y_{rj}$; DMU $o$ is evaluated. With weights $u_r, v_i \ge 0$, the efficiency ratio of DMU $j$ is $\sum_r u_ry_{rj}/\sum_i v_ix_{ij}$. How is the relative efficiency of DMU $o$ defined?
  1. As the ratio between its total output and the average output of all DMUs
  2. As its efficiency ratio computed with weights fixed by management
  3. As the maximum of its ratio over weights giving no DMU a ratio above $1$
  4. As the minimum of its ratio over all non-negative weights
Explanation. Each DMU chooses the weights that show it in the best light: if even then it stays below $1$, the inefficiency does not depend on an arbitrary choice of prices.
orl-f-100 · level facile · notes
DEA: $n$ units (DMUs) $j$ use inputs $x_{ij}$ to produce outputs $y_{rj}$; DMU $o$ is evaluated. The fractional CCR model maximizes the ratio $\sum_r u_ry_{ro}/\sum_i v_ix_{io}$ subject to ratio $\le 1$ for every DMU. What allows the Charnes–Cooper transformation to turn it into an LP?
  1. Scaling all weights by $t > 0$ changes no ratio, so one may impose $\sum_i v_ix_{io} = 1$
  2. All the ratios are smaller than $1$, so the denominators can be dropped
  3. The optimal weights are integers, so the ratios can be enumerated
  4. The outputs are the same for all DMUs, so only the inputs matter
Explanation. With the denominator of the objective normalized to $1$ the objective is the numerator, and each ratio constraint multiplied by its positive denominator becomes linear.
orl-f-101 · level facile · notes
DEA: $n$ units (DMUs) $j$ use inputs $x_{ij}$ to produce outputs $y_{rj}$; DMU $o$ is evaluated. Input-oriented CCR envelopment form: $\min\theta$ subject to $\sum_j\lambda_jx_{ij} \le \theta x_{io}$, $\sum_j\lambda_jy_{rj} \ge y_{ro}$, $\lambda_j \ge 0$, $\theta$ free. What is the reference set (the peers) of DMU $o$?
  1. All the DMUs with efficiency $\tilde\theta = 1$
  2. The DMUs $j$ with $\tilde\lambda_j > 0$ in the optimal solution
  3. All the DMUs with larger outputs than $o$
  4. The DMUs that use the same inputs as $o$
Explanation. The peers are the efficient DMUs combined into the virtual DMU of $o$: the units $o$ should learn from.
orl-f-102 · level facile · notes
DEA: $n$ units (DMUs) $j$ use inputs $x_{ij}$ to produce outputs $y_{rj}$; DMU $o$ is evaluated. Input-oriented CCR envelopment form: $\min\theta$ subject to $\sum_j\lambda_jx_{ij} \le \theta x_{io}$, $\sum_j\lambda_jy_{rj} \ge y_{ro}$, $\lambda_j \ge 0$, $\theta$ free. In the case study the CCR optimum of branch 6 has $\sum_j \tilde\lambda_j = 1.924$ (and is unique). What does it suggest about its scale?
  1. Too small (increasing returns): consider merging it
  2. Efficient at every scale: nothing to change
  3. Wrong data: the sum must be at most $1$
  4. Too large (decreasing returns): consider splitting it
Explanation. $\sum_j\tilde\lambda_j > 1$ signals decreasing returns, $< 1$ increasing returns; branch 6 is in fact efficient in the BCC model, so its CCR inefficiency is due to size.
orl-f-103 · level facile · notes
DEA: $n$ units (DMUs) $j$ use inputs $x_{ij}$ to produce outputs $y_{rj}$; DMU $o$ is evaluated. Input-oriented CCR envelopment form: $\min\theta$ subject to $\sum_j\lambda_jx_{ij} \le \theta x_{io}$, $\sum_j\lambda_jy_{rj} \ge y_{ro}$, $\lambda_j \ge 0$, $\theta$ free. A second phase maximizes the input and output slacks with $\theta$ fixed at $\tilde\theta$. When is DMU $o$ weakly but not strongly efficient?
  1. When $\tilde\theta < 1$
  2. When $\tilde\theta = 1$ and all the slacks are zero
  3. When $\tilde\theta = 1$ but some optimal slack of the second phase is positive
  4. When it is not a peer of any other DMU
Explanation. It cannot contract all inputs in the same proportion, but it can reduce at least one input (or expand an output): another combination of DMUs does the same with less.
orl-f-104 · level facile · notes
DEA: $n$ units (DMUs) $j$ use inputs $x_{ij}$ to produce outputs $y_{rj}$; DMU $o$ is evaluated. Input-oriented CCR envelopment form: $\min\theta$ subject to $\sum_j\lambda_jx_{ij} \le \theta x_{io}$, $\sum_j\lambda_jy_{rj} \ge y_{ro}$, $\lambda_j \ge 0$, $\theta$ free. What sign do the shadow prices of the output constraints $\sum_j\lambda_jy_{rj} \ge y_{ro}$ have, and what are they?
  1. $\ge 0$; they are the optimal output weights $\tilde u_r$ of the multiplier form
  2. $\le 0$; they are the output slacks of the second phase
  3. Any sign; they are the optimal intensities $\tilde\lambda_j$
  4. $\ge 0$; they are the optimal input weights $\tilde v_i$
Explanation. Raising the right-hand side of a $\ge$ constraint shrinks the region, so the minimum $\tilde\theta$ cannot decrease; by duality these duals are the weights $\tilde u_r$.
orl-f-105 · level facile · notes
DEA: $n$ units (DMUs) $j$ use inputs $x_{ij}$ to produce outputs $y_{rj}$; DMU $o$ is evaluated. In the case study of 12 bank branches, branch 7 is a peer (reference unit) of five inefficient branches. What does the lab recommend before acting on the targets?
  1. Nothing: the targets computed by the LP are exact
  2. Check the data of the most cited peers and repeat the analysis without them
  3. Remove all the inefficient branches from the analysis
  4. Switch to the BCC model, which has no peers
Explanation. DEA is relative: a single DMU on the frontier can change the verdict on many others, and a recording error would make it “too good”.
orl-f-106 · level teorema · notes
DEA: $n$ units (DMUs) $j$ use inputs $x_{ij}$ to produce outputs $y_{rj}$; DMU $o$ is evaluated. The BCC model adds $\sum_j\lambda_j = 1$ to the input-oriented CCR envelopment form. Which relation between the two efficiencies of the same DMU always holds?
  1. $\tilde\theta^{\rm BCC} \le \tilde\theta^{\rm CCR}$
  2. $\tilde\theta^{\rm BCC} = \tilde\theta^{\rm CCR}$
  3. $\tilde\theta^{\rm BCC} \ge \tilde\theta^{\rm CCR}$
  4. No general relation holds
Explanation. Hence the scale efficiency $\tilde\theta^{\rm CCR}/\tilde\theta^{\rm BCC}$ lies in $(0, 1]$. The extra constraint shrinks the feasible region of a minimization, so $\tilde\theta$ cannot decrease; the difference between the two measures is due to size.
orl-f-107 · level teorema · notes
DEA: $n$ units (DMUs) $j$ use inputs $x_{ij}$ to produce outputs $y_{rj}$; DMU $o$ is evaluated. Input-oriented CCR envelopment form: $\min\theta$ subject to $\sum_j\lambda_jx_{ij} \le \theta x_{io}$, $\sum_j\lambda_jy_{rj} \ge y_{ro}$, $\lambda_j \ge 0$, $\theta$ free. Let $\tilde h_o$ be the optimal value of the CCR multiplier form (the relative efficiency of $o$). Which statement links it to the optimal $\tilde\theta$ of the envelopment form?
  1. $\tilde\theta = \tilde h_o$, with $0 < \tilde h_o \le 1$
  2. $\tilde\theta = 1/\tilde h_o$
  3. $\tilde\theta \ge 1 \ge \tilde h_o$
  4. $\tilde\theta + \tilde h_o = 1$
Explanation. The envelopment form is the dual of the multiplier form: by strong duality the optimal values coincide, so the optimal radial contraction of the inputs is the relative efficiency.
orl-f-108 · level ipotesi · notes
DEA: $n$ units (DMUs) $j$ use inputs $x_{ij}$ to produce outputs $y_{rj}$; DMU $o$ is evaluated. Input-oriented CCR envelopment form: $\min\theta$ subject to $\sum_j\lambda_jx_{ij} \le \theta x_{io}$, $\sum_j\lambda_jy_{rj} \ge y_{ro}$, $\lambda_j \ge 0$, $\theta$ free. Which argument proves $\tilde\theta \le 1$?
  1. The input weights $v_i$ of the multiplier form are non-negative
  2. Weak duality between the envelopment and the multiplier form
  3. All the outputs $y_{rj}$ are positive
  4. $\lambda_o = 1$, $\lambda_j = 0$ ($j \ne o$), $\theta = 1$ is feasible and $\theta$ is minimized
Explanation. DMU $o$ itself is a feasible virtual DMU with $\theta = 1$: the minimum cannot be larger.
orl-f-109 · level ipotesi · notes
DEA: $n$ units (DMUs) $j$ use inputs $x_{ij}$ to produce outputs $y_{rj}$; DMU $o$ is evaluated. The BCC model is the input-oriented CCR envelopment form plus the constraint $\sum_j\lambda_j = 1$. Which facts prove $\tilde\theta^{\rm BCC} \ge \tilde\theta^{\rm CCR}$?
  1. Strong duality between the BCC and the CCR models, which have the same dual
  2. The extra constraint shrinks the region, and a minimum over a smaller set is never lower
  3. Variable returns to scale always make every DMU look worse than constant returns
  4. The slacks of the second phase are non-negative in both models
Explanation. Same objective and the same constraints plus one: the feasible set of BCC is contained in that of CCR.
orl-f-110 · level ipotesi · notes
DEA: $n$ units (DMUs) $j$ use inputs $x_{ij}$ to produce outputs $y_{rj}$; DMU $o$ is evaluated. CCR multiplier form: $\max\sum_r u_ry_{ro}$ subject to $\sum_i v_ix_{io} = 1$ and $\sum_r u_ry_{rj} - \sum_i v_ix_{ij} \le 0$ for every $j$, $u, v \ge 0$. In its dual (the envelopment form), why is $\theta$ free and each $\lambda_j \ge 0$?
  1. $\theta$ is an efficiency, which can have any sign; the $\lambda_j$ are weights, hence $\ge 0$
  2. The outputs $y_{rj}$ are positive, so both the dual variables must be $\ge 0$ except $\theta$
  3. The normalization is an equality (free dual); the ratio constraints are $\le$ in a max ($\ge 0$)
  4. The inputs $x_{ij}$ are positive, so the duals of the ratio constraints are $\ge 0$
Explanation. These are the duality conversion rules: equality $\to$ free variable, $\le$ constraint in a maximization $\to$ variable $\ge 0$.
orl-f-217 · level facile · notes
DEA: $n$ units (DMUs) $j$ use inputs $x_{ij}$ to produce outputs $y_{rj}$; DMU $o$ is evaluated. Why does DEA let each DMU choose its own weights $u_r, v_i$, instead of comparing outputs and inputs at fixed prices?
  1. No common prices exist: an inefficiency must survive the most favourable weights
  2. Fixed weights would make the LP infeasible for the most efficient DMUs
  3. The weights must be integers, and fixed prices are rarely integers
  4. Fixed prices would make every DMU efficient, so no ranking would remain
Explanation. If a DMU stays below $1$ even with the weights most favourable to it, its inefficiency does not depend on an arbitrary choice of prices.
orl-f-218 · level facile · notes
DEA: $n$ units (DMUs) $j$ use inputs $x_{ij}$ to produce outputs $y_{rj}$; DMU $o$ is evaluated. In the CCR multiplier form, which quantities are the decision variables of the LP solved for DMU $o$?
  1. The inputs $x_{ij}$ and outputs $y_{rj}$ of all DMUs
  2. The efficiencies of all the DMUs
  3. The intensities $\lambda_j$ of the virtual DMU
  4. The output weights $u_r$ and input weights $v_i$
Explanation. In DEA $x$ and $y$ are data; the multiplier form chooses the weights, while the intensities $\lambda_j$ are the variables of the dual (envelopment) form.
orl-f-219 · level facile · notes
DEA: $n$ units (DMUs) $j$ use inputs $x_{ij}$ to produce outputs $y_{rj}$; DMU $o$ is evaluated. One input, one output. DMU 1 uses $x = 2$ to produce $y = 2$; DMU 5 uses $x = 2$ to produce $y = 1$. DMU 5 has BCC efficiency $\tilde\theta = 1$ (its input cannot be contracted radially). Is it strongly efficient?
  1. Yes: $\tilde\theta = 1$ means strongly efficient
  2. No: it is weakly efficient, with output slack $1$
  3. No: its efficiency is $0.5$
  4. Yes, because it uses the smallest input
Explanation. DMU 1 does more with the same input: the second phase finds output slack $\tilde s^+ = 1$. With $\tilde\theta = 1$ but a positive slack, DMU 5 is only weakly efficient.
orl-f-220 · level facile · notes
DEA: $n$ units (DMUs) $j$ use inputs $x_{ij}$ to produce outputs $y_{rj}$; DMU $o$ is evaluated. Case study of 12 bank branches: branch 1 has CCR efficiency $0.806$, BCC efficiency $0.900$ and, in the CCR optimum, $\sum_j\tilde\lambda_j = 0.671$. What is the diagnosis?
  1. Too small (increasing returns) and also inefficient once scale is removed
  2. Too large: it should be split
  3. Efficient: the CCR score is an artefact
  4. Efficient in the BCC model, so only a scale problem
Explanation. $\sum\tilde\lambda_j < 1$ signals increasing returns (too small); BCC $0.90 < 1$ means a technical inefficiency remains even comparing with branches of its size.
orl-f-221 · level facile · notes
DEA: $n$ units (DMUs) $j$ use inputs $x_{ij}$ to produce outputs $y_{rj}$; DMU $o$ is evaluated. Case study: CCR-efficient branches are 2, 7, 9, 10; BCC-efficient branches are 2, 3, 6, 7, 8, 9, 10, 12. What do branches 3, 6, 8, 12 have in common?
  1. They are inefficient in every model
  2. They are peers of all the other branches
  3. Their CCR inefficiency is due only to scale
  4. They have data errors
Explanation. BCC efficiency $1$ with CCR below $1$ means scale efficiency $\tilde\theta^{\rm CCR}/\tilde\theta^{\rm BCC} < 1$: they work well for their size (here all too large, $\sum\tilde\lambda_j > 1$).
orl-f-491 · level facile · notes
A bank compares its twelve branches with Data Envelopment Analysis (DEA). Why is a simple comparison of spending not enough?
  1. Because branches have the same size
  2. Because the data are confidential
  3. Because DEA needs only one output
  4. Outputs have no common price
Explanation. Loans and deposits have no agreed relative value, and a large branch is not inefficient just because it spends more: DEA compares each branch with the best observed practices.
orl-f-492 · level facile · notes
How does DEA compute the efficiency index of the units it compares?
  1. By solving one LP for each unit
  2. By one regression on all units
  3. By ranking units by output
  4. By a single LP for all units together
Explanation. Each unit gets its own LP (same constraints, different objective and normalization); the index compares it with the best observed practices.
orl-f-493 · level facile · notes
Where is DEA used, according to the lab notes?
  1. Only for bank branches
  2. Only for single-input processes
  3. Hospitals, schools, offices, plants
  4. Only where prices of outputs are known
Explanation. Wherever there are comparable units transforming several inputs into several outputs without a common price for the outputs.
orl-f-494 · level facile · notes
Besides an efficiency index, what does the DEA chapter of the lab learn to read in the dual (envelopment) form?
  1. The prices of the outputs on the market
  2. Reference units and targets
  3. The number of employees to hire next year
  4. The demand of each unit
Explanation. The envelopment form names the peers ($\tilde\lambda_j > 0$) and, through $\tilde\theta$ and the slacks, how much each input should shrink; BCC also separates technical and scale inefficiency.
orl-f-495 · level facile · notes
DEA: $n$ units (DMUs) $j$ use inputs $x_{ij}$ to produce outputs $y_{rj}$; DMU $o$ is evaluated. What is a DMU in DEA?
  1. A decision variable of the LP
  2. A data matrix of the model
  3. A unit compared with similar units
  4. The best unit of the sample
Explanation. Decision Making Unit: branches, hospitals, schools... each uses the same kinds of inputs to produce the same kinds of outputs, so they can be compared.
orl-f-496 · level facile · notes
DEA: $n$ units (DMUs) $j$ use inputs $x_{ij}$ to produce outputs $y_{rj}$; DMU $o$ is evaluated. Relative efficiency is the weighted ratio $\sum_ru_ry_{ro}/\sum_iv_ix_{io}$ with weights chosen by the DMU itself. What value means «efficient»?
  1. $1$, the maximum allowed
  2. $0$, the minimum allowed
  3. Any value above $1$
  4. The mean of all the ratios
Explanation. The weights must keep every DMU's ratio at most $1$; a DMU is efficient when, with its most favourable weights, it reaches $1$.
orl-f-497 · level teorema · notes
DEA: $n$ units (DMUs) $j$ use inputs $x_{ij}$ to produce outputs $y_{rj}$; DMU $o$ is evaluated. The CCR fractional model maximizes $\sum_ru_ry_{ro}/\sum_iv_ix_{io}$ with every ratio $\le 1$. Why can it be turned into an LP (Charnes–Cooper)?
  1. Because the ratios are always equal to 1
  2. Scaling all weights changes no ratio
  3. Because the inputs are positive integers
  4. Because the outputs are fixed
Explanation. Ratios are invariant to a common scaling of the weights, so one can impose $\sum_iv_ix_{io} = 1$: the objective becomes the numerator and each ratio constraint, multiplied by its positive denominator, becomes linear.
orl-f-498 · level teorema · notes
DEA: $n$ units (DMUs) $j$ use inputs $x_{ij}$ to produce outputs $y_{rj}$; DMU $o$ is evaluated. Input-oriented CCR envelopment form: $\min\theta$ subject to $\sum_j\lambda_jx_{ij} \le \theta x_{io}$, $\sum_j\lambda_jy_{rj} \ge y_{ro}$, $\lambda_j \ge 0$. Why is $\tilde\theta \le 1$ always?
  1. Because the outputs are positive
  2. Because every $\lambda_j \le 1$ by construction
  3. The DMU alone is feasible
  4. Because of the normalization of the inputs
Explanation. The DMU itself, taken alone, is a feasible virtual unit with $\theta = 1$; minimizing can only do as well or better, so $\tilde\theta \le 1$.
orl-f-499 · level ipotesi · notes
DEA: $n$ units (DMUs) $j$ use inputs $x_{ij}$ to produce outputs $y_{rj}$; DMU $o$ is evaluated. Input-oriented CCR envelopment form: $\min\theta$ subject to $\sum_j\lambda_jx_{ij} \le \theta x_{io}$, $\sum_j\lambda_jy_{rj} \ge y_{ro}$, $\lambda_j \ge 0$. In the CCR envelopment form, $\sum_j\tilde\lambda_j < 1$ suggests increasing returns (the DMU is too small) and $> 1$ decreasing returns. Under which condition is this reading valid?
  1. When the optimum is unique
  2. Always, whatever the optimal solution
  3. Only when $\tilde\theta = 1$
  4. Only with a single output
Explanation. With several optimal $\lambda$'s their sum can differ from one solution to another, and the direction of the scale inefficiency would be ambiguous.
orl-f-500 · level facile · notes
DEA: $n$ units (DMUs) $j$ use inputs $x_{ij}$ to produce outputs $y_{rj}$; DMU $o$ is evaluated. Input-oriented CCR envelopment form: $\min\theta$ subject to $\sum_j\lambda_jx_{ij} \le \theta x_{io}$, $\sum_j\lambda_jy_{rj} \ge y_{ro}$, $\lambda_j \ge 0$. After the radial contraction, a second phase computes the slacks $s_i^-$, $s_r^+$ at fixed $\tilde\theta$. What are the targets of DMU $o$?
  1. $\hat x_{io} = x_{io}$, $\hat y_{ro} = y_{ro}$
  2. $\hat x_{io} = x_{io} - s_i^-$, outputs unchanged
  3. Only $\hat x_{io} = \tilde\theta x_{io}$, outputs unchanged
  4. $\tilde\theta x_{io} - s_i^-$ and $y_{ro} + s_r^+$
Explanation. First the radial contraction by $\tilde\theta$, then the residual slack of each input is removed and each output slack added.
orl-f-501 · level facile · notes
DEA: $n$ units (DMUs) $j$ use inputs $x_{ij}$ to produce outputs $y_{rj}$; DMU $o$ is evaluated. Input-oriented CCR envelopment form: $\min\theta$ subject to $\sum_j\lambda_jx_{ij} \le \theta x_{io}$, $\sum_j\lambda_jy_{rj} \ge y_{ro}$, $\lambda_j \ge 0$. When is a DMU **strongly** efficient?
  1. When $\tilde\theta = 1$, whatever the slacks
  2. $\tilde\theta = 1$ and no slack
  3. When it is a peer of another DMU of the sample
  4. When its slacks are positive
Explanation. With $\tilde\theta = 1$ but a positive slack the DMU is only weakly efficient: it cannot shrink all inputs proportionally, but another unit does the same with less of some input.
orl-f-502 · level facile · notes
DEA: $n$ units (DMUs) $j$ use inputs $x_{ij}$ to produce outputs $y_{rj}$; DMU $o$ is evaluated. Five DMUs with one input and one output: $x = (2, 4, 6, 5, 2)$, $y = (2, 6, 7, 3, 1)$. What is the CCR efficiency of DMU 1, with one input and one output?
  1. $0.67$
  2. $1.00$
  3. $0.40$
  4. $0.50$
Explanation. With one input and one output the CCR efficiency is $y/x$ divided by the best ratio, $6/4 = 1.5$ (DMU 2): $(2/2)/1.5 = 0.667$.
orl-f-503 · level facile · notes
DEA: $n$ units (DMUs) $j$ use inputs $x_{ij}$ to produce outputs $y_{rj}$; DMU $o$ is evaluated. Five DMUs with one input and one output: $x = (2, 4, 6, 5, 2)$, $y = (2, 6, 7, 3, 1)$. In the CCR envelopment form for DMU 1, the only peer is DMU 2. What is $\tilde\lambda_2$?
  1. $1$
  2. $2/3$
  3. $1/3$
  4. $1/2$
Explanation. The virtual unit $\lambda_2(4, 6)$ must produce DMU 1's output: $6\lambda_2 = 2$, so $\lambda_2 = 1/3$, and it uses $4/3 = 0.667\cdot 2$ of input, i.e. $\tilde\theta = 0.667$.
orl-f-504 · level media · notes
DEA: $n$ units (DMUs) $j$ use inputs $x_{ij}$ to produce outputs $y_{rj}$; DMU $o$ is evaluated. Five DMUs with one input and one output: $x = (2, 4, 6, 5, 2)$, $y = (2, 6, 7, 3, 1)$. BCC efficiencies: $(1;\ 1;\ 1;\ 0.5;\ 1)$; CCR: $(0.667;\ 1;\ 0.778;\ 0.4;\ 0.333)$. What is the scale efficiency $\mathrm{SE} = \tilde\theta^{\rm CCR}/\tilde\theta^{\rm BCC}$ of DMU 4?
  1. $0.5$
  2. $0.4$
  3. $1.25$
  4. $0.8$
Explanation. $0.4/0.5 = 0.8$: part of DMU 4's inefficiency is technical (BCC $0.5$), part is due to scale.
orl-f-505 · level facile · notes
DEA: $n$ units (DMUs) $j$ use inputs $x_{ij}$ to produce outputs $y_{rj}$; DMU $o$ is evaluated. Five DMUs with one input and one output: $x = (2, 4, 6, 5, 2)$, $y = (2, 6, 7, 3, 1)$. DMU 5 ($x = 2$, $y = 1$) has $\tilde\theta^{\rm BCC} = 1$ but a second-phase output slack $\tilde s^+ = 1$. Why?
  1. It is the most efficient DMU
  2. DMU 1 does better with it
  3. Its input could be cut in half
  4. It is only a numerical error
Explanation. No convex combination uses less than 2 units of input, so $\tilde\theta = 1$; but DMU 1 does better with the same input: DMU 5 is only weakly efficient.
orl-f-506 · level facile · notes

orl-f-507 · level facile · notes

orl-f-508 · level facile · notes

orl-f-509 · level facile · notes
DEA: $n$ units (DMUs) $j$ use inputs $x_{ij}$ to produce outputs $y_{rj}$; DMU $o$ is evaluated. Bank case: 12 branches, inputs staff (FTE) and operating costs, outputs loans and deposits. Branch 7 is the smallest (9 FTE, 640 k€ of costs) and is a peer of five inefficient branches. What does «peer» mean?

  2. A unit with $\tilde\lambda_j > 0$ in the virtual unit
  3. A branch of the same size
  4. The branch with the lowest costs
Explanation. The peers are the efficient units that make up the virtual unit of DMU $o$ in the envelopment form: they are its benchmarks.
orl-f-510 · level facile · notes
DEA: $n$ units (DMUs) $j$ use inputs $x_{ij}$ to produce outputs $y_{rj}$; DMU $o$ is evaluated. Input-oriented CCR envelopment form: $\min\theta$ subject to $\sum_j\lambda_jx_{ij} \le \theta x_{io}$, $\sum_j\lambda_jy_{rj} \ge y_{ro}$, $\lambda_j \ge 0$. In the gurobipy function `inviluppo` the variable `theta` is created with `lb=-GRB.INFINITY`. Why?
  1. Because $\theta$ must be negative
  2. Because Gurobi needs a free objective
  3. $\theta$ is free in the model
  4. To allow $\theta > 1$
Explanation. In the envelopment form $\theta$ is a free variable; the constraints and $\lambda_o = 1$ give $0 < \tilde\theta \le 1$ anyway. Forgetting `lb` would just impose a harmless $\theta \ge 0$ here, but the model declares what it states.
orl-f-511 · level facile · notes
DEA: $n$ units (DMUs) $j$ use inputs $x_{ij}$ to produce outputs $y_{rj}$; DMU $o$ is evaluated. Input-oriented CCR envelopment form: $\min\theta$ subject to $\sum_j\lambda_jx_{ij} \le \theta x_{io}$, $\sum_j\lambda_jy_{rj} \ge y_{ro}$, $\lambda_j \ge 0$. The function `inviluppo(X, Y, o, vrs=False)` adds `lam.sum() == 1` when `vrs=True`. Which model does it then solve?
  1. BCC, with variable returns to scale
  2. CCR, with constant returns
  3. The multiplier form
  4. The second phase of the slacks
Explanation. The convexity constraint $\sum_j\lambda_j = 1$ makes the virtual unit a convex combination of observed units: the BCC model.
orl-f-512 · level facile · notes
DEA: $n$ units (DMUs) $j$ use inputs $x_{ij}$ to produce outputs $y_{rj}$; DMU $o$ is evaluated. Input-oriented CCR envelopment form: $\min\theta$ subject to $\sum_j\lambda_jx_{ij} \le \theta x_{io}$, $\sum_j\lambda_jy_{rj} \ge y_{ro}$, $\lambda_j \ge 0$. For each branch the script solves both the envelopment form and the multiplier form, with an `assert` that the two optimal values coincide. What does this check?
  1. That the data are positive
  2. That every branch is efficient
  3. That the solver is fast enough
  4. Strong duality between the two forms
Explanation. The two forms are a primal–dual pair: equal optimal values (and the shadow price of the normalization equal to $\tilde\theta$) verify strong duality numerically.
orl-f-513 · level facile · notes
DEA: $n$ units (DMUs) $j$ use inputs $x_{ij}$ to produce outputs $y_{rj}$; DMU $o$ is evaluated. Input-oriented CCR envelopment form: $\min\theta$ subject to $\sum_j\lambda_jx_{ij} \le \theta x_{io}$, $\sum_j\lambda_jy_{rj} \ge y_{ro}$, $\lambda_j \ge 0$. In the script one envelopment LP per DMU is built and solved. What changes from one DMU $o$ to the next?
  1. The whole set of constraints
  2. Only the terms of DMU $o$
  3. The number of variables
  4. The sense of the objective
Explanation. The constraint matrix of the observed units is the same; only the terms with $x_{io}$ and $y_{ro}$ change, so one function called with different `o` builds all twelve LPs.
orl-f-514 · level facile · notes
DEA: $n$ units (DMUs) $j$ use inputs $x_{ij}$ to produce outputs $y_{rj}$; DMU $o$ is evaluated. Bank case: 12 branches, inputs staff (FTE) and operating costs, outputs loans and deposits.   2. Close the branch down
  3. Consider splitting it in two
  4. Nothing: it is efficient
Explanation. BCC efficiency 1 means no technical inefficiency; the CCR gap is all scale, and a sum of $\lambda$'s above 1 signals decreasing returns: the branch is too big.
orl-f-515 · level facile · notes
DEA: $n$ units (DMUs) $j$ use inputs $x_{ij}$ to produce outputs $y_{rj}$; DMU $o$ is evaluated. Bank case: 12 branches, inputs staff (FTE) and operating costs, outputs loans and deposits. Branch 1: CCR $0.8055$, BCC $0.90$, sum of $\tilde\lambda_j = 0.671$; after the second phase its cost target is $463.2$ k€ against $720$ actual. What is its situation?

  2. Too large, but technically efficient
  3. Efficient in both models
  4. Inefficient only in the outputs
Explanation. A sum of $\lambda$'s below 1 means increasing returns (too small), and BCC $< 1$ means technical inefficiency too; a residual cost slack of $116.8$ k€ remains after the radial contraction.
orl-f-516 · level facile · notes
DEA: $n$ units (DMUs) $j$ use inputs $x_{ij}$ to produce outputs $y_{rj}$; DMU $o$ is evaluated. Input-oriented CCR envelopment form: $\min\theta$ subject to $\sum_j\lambda_jx_{ij} \le \theta x_{io}$, $\sum_j\lambda_jy_{rj} \ge y_{ro}$, $\lambda_j \ge 0$. Branch 6 ($\tilde\theta = 0.76$): the shadow price of the deposits constraint is $0.0104$, equal to the optimal weight $\tilde u_2$ of the multiplier form. Why are these prices nonnegative?
  1. Because outputs are positive numbers
  2. Because $\theta \le 1$
  3. Because the LP is degenerate
  4. A $\ge$ in a minimum cannot lower $\tilde\theta$
Explanation. Raising the required output shrinks the feasible region, and in a minimum the optimum cannot improve: $\tilde\theta$ rises by $0.0104$ per million of deposits (checked by perturbation).
orl-f-517 · level facile · notes
DEA: $n$ units (DMUs) $j$ use inputs $x_{ij}$ to produce outputs $y_{rj}$; DMU $o$ is evaluated. Input-oriented CCR envelopment form: $\min\theta$ subject to $\sum_j\lambda_jx_{ij} \le \theta x_{io}$, $\sum_j\lambda_jy_{rj} \ge y_{ro}$, $\lambda_j \ge 0$. Removing branch 7 (peer of five branches) and recomputing CCR, five branches change efficiency and branches 3 and 8 become efficient. What do the notes recommend?

  2. Check the most cited peers' data
  3. Ignore the result: DEA is stable
  4. Use only the BCC model from now on
Explanation. DEA measures efficiency relative to the observed units: one frontier unit can change many judgements, so a recording error there would make it «too good».

Part: Decisions under uncertainty (91)

13. The Newsvendor and its variants (34)

orl-f-111 · level facile · notes
Newsvendor: a product is bought at cost $c$, sold at price $p$, and unsold units are salvaged at $v < c$. What are the underage cost $c_u$ (per unit of unmet demand) and the overage cost $c_o$ (per unsold unit)?
  1. $c_u = p$, $c_o = c$
  2. $c_u = p - c$, $c_o = c - v$
  3. $c_u = c - v$, $c_o = p - c$
  4. $c_u = p - v$, $c_o = c$
Explanation. Underage = lost margin $p - c$; overage = cost of an unsold unit net of its salvage value, $c - v$.
orl-f-112 · level facile · notes
Newsvendor: order quantity $q$ before observing the random demand $D$ (distribution function $F$); unit underage cost $c_u$ (demand left unmet) and overage cost $c_o$ (unit left unsold); minimize $c_o\mathbb E[(q - D)^+] + c_u\mathbb E[(D - q)^+]$. If running short costs much more than running long ($c_u \gg c_o$), the optimal quantity $\tilde q$ is:
  1. the mean demand $\mathbb E[D]$
  2. a low quantile of the demand
  3. zero
  4. a high quantile of the demand
Explanation. $\alpha = c_u/(c_u + c_o)$ is close to $1$ and $\tilde q = F^{-1}(\alpha)$. With perishable goods (high $c_o$) one orders little.
orl-f-113 · level facile · notes
Newsvendor: order quantity $q$ before observing the random demand $D$ (distribution function $F$); unit underage cost $c_u$ (demand left unmet) and overage cost $c_o$ (unit left unsold); minimize $c_o\mathbb E[(q - D)^+] + c_u\mathbb E[(D - q)^+]$. Scenario LP: demand $d_s$ with probability $\pi_s$; the overage is a variable $o_s$ with $o_s \ge q - d_s$, $o_s \ge 0$ and term $\pi_sc_oo_s$ in the minimized cost. Why does $o_s = (q - d_s)^+$ hold at the optimum without binary variables?
  1. Its positive cost pushes $o_s$ down to the larger of its two lower bounds
  2. Because $q$ is larger than every demand $d_s$ at the optimum
  3. Because Gurobi linearizes the $\max$ automatically
  4. Because the scenarios are equally likely
Explanation. This positive-part trick returns unchanged in the CVaR (the excesses $\xi_s$), in the SVM (the slacks) and in quantile regression.
orl-f-114 · level facile · notes
Newsvendor: order quantity $q$ before observing the random demand $D$ (distribution function $F$); unit underage cost $c_u$ (demand left unmet) and overage cost $c_o$ (unit left unsold); minimize $c_o\mathbb E[(q - D)^+] + c_u\mathbb E[(D - q)^+]$. What does the value of the stochastic solution (VSS) measure?
  1. The expected value of a perfect demand forecast
  2. The expected cost of the optimal order $\tilde q$
  3. Expected cost of ordering $\mathbb E[D]$ minus expected cost of $\tilde q$
  4. The variance of the cost at the optimal order
Explanation. VSS is the gain from modelling the uncertainty instead of plugging in the mean: in the case study $11.17$ € per cycle, $11\%$ of the cost.
orl-f-115 · level facile · notes
Newsvendor: order quantity $q$ before observing the random demand $D$ (distribution function $F$); unit underage cost $c_u$ (demand left unmet) and overage cost $c_o$ (unit left unsold); minimize $c_o\mathbb E[(q - D)^+] + c_u\mathbb E[(D - q)^+]$. In the case study, at $\tilde q = 110$ the probability of no stock-out, $\mathbb P(D \le \tilde q)$, is $69\%$, while the fill rate (expected share of demand served) is $96\%$. What does this show?
  1. That the newsvendor model is wrong
  2. That cycle service level and fill rate are different measures
  3. That the fill rate is always the smaller of the two
  4. That $\tilde q$ should equal the mean demand
Explanation. The two are often confused in contracts: promising $95\%$ probability of full coverage costs $45.59$ € per cycle more, promising a $95\%$ fill rate is almost free.
orl-f-116 · level facile · notes
Newsvendor: order quantity $q$ before observing the random demand $D$ (distribution function $F$); unit underage cost $c_u$ (demand left unmet) and overage cost $c_o$ (unit left unsold); minimize $c_o\mathbb E[(q - D)^+] + c_u\mathbb E[(D - q)^+]$. Three equally likely demand scenarios $D \in \{80, 100, 120\}$, $c_u = 9$, $c_o = 4$. What is the optimal quantity?
  1. $100$, the mean demand
  2. $80$, the smallest scenario
  3. $110$, between the two largest
  4. $120$, the largest scenario
Explanation. $\alpha = 9/13 = 0.6923$ and $F(100) = 2/3 < \alpha$: the smallest $q$ with $F(q) \ge \alpha$ is $120$. Check: $C(120) = 80$ against $C(100) = 86.7$.
orl-f-117 · level teorema · notes
Newsvendor: order quantity $q$ before observing the random demand $D$ (distribution function $F$); unit underage cost $c_u$ (demand left unmet) and overage cost $c_o$ (unit left unsold); minimize $c_o\mathbb E[(q - D)^+] + c_u\mathbb E[(D - q)^+]$. $F$ is continuous. Which statement is the quantile rule for the optimal quantity $\tilde q$?
  1. $F(\tilde q) = c_o/(c_u + c_o)$
  2. $F(\tilde q) = 1/2$: order the median
  3. $F(\tilde q) = c_u/(c_u + c_o)$, i.e. $\tilde q = F^{-1}(c_u/(c_u + c_o))$
  4. $\tilde q = \mathbb{E}[D]$: order the mean
Explanation. The critical ratio weighs underage against the total cost of an error; swapping $c_u$ and $c_o$ is the typical mistake.
orl-f-118 · level ipotesi · notes
Newsvendor: order quantity $q$ before observing the random demand $D$ (distribution function $F$); unit underage cost $c_u$ (demand left unmet) and overage cost $c_o$ (unit left unsold); minimize $c_o\mathbb E[(q - D)^+] + c_u\mathbb E[(D - q)^+]$. Which argument and assumptions give the quantile rule $F(\tilde q) = c_u/(c_u + c_o)$?
  1. Marginal unit: gains $c_u$ w.p. $1 - F(q)$, costs $c_o$ w.p. $F(q)$; $F$ continuous
  2. Strong LP duality applied to the scenario model; $F$ discrete
  3. Setting the derivative of the expected cost to zero; $D$ normal
  4. Marginal unit: gains $c_u$ w.p. $F(q)$, costs $c_o$ w.p. $1 - F(q)$
Explanation. It pays to order one more unit while $c_u(1 - F(q)) \ge c_oF(q)$; continuity of $F$ makes the equality attainable. No normality is needed; with discrete demand one takes the smallest $q$ with $F(q) \ge \alpha$.
orl-f-119 · level ipotesi · notes

  4. That the problem be a maximization
Explanation. If $\pi_sc_o > 0$, any $o_s$ above $\max\{q - d_s, 0\}$ could be lowered, reducing the cost. With a zero or negative coefficient the trick fails.

orl-f-226 · level facile · notes

  1. higher, to use the whole budget
  2. identical, since the products are independent
  3. zero for all but the most profitable product
  4. lower: the budget multiplier acts like an extra unit cost
Explanation. With multiplier $\lambda$ the optimality condition of each product includes the term $\lambda c_i$, as if the purchase cost were higher: every quantity shrinks (in the case study from $111.1$ to $99.3$ for the first cake).
orl-f-341 · level facile · notes
The newsvendor model describes a very common decision. Which one?
  1. Choosing a price after observing the demand
  2. Splitting a budget among channels with known returns
  3. Routing flows on a network with known demands
  4. Choosing a quantity before observing the demand
Explanation. Order too much and units are left unsold; too little and sales are lost: fashion, fresh food, drugs, hotel capacity, spare parts. It is the entry point to stochastic optimization: decide first, discover later.
orl-f-342 · level facile · notes
According to the lab notes, what is the «clear-cut result» that makes the newsvendor didactically strong?
  1. The optimal quantity is not the mean demand
  2. The optimal quantity is always the mean demand
  3. Ordering the largest possible demand is always optimal
  4. Uncertainty can be replaced by its expected value
Explanation. The optimum is a quantile of demand fixed by the ratio of the two costs, generally different from the mean; replacing the uncertainty with its mean has a measurable cost (the VSS).

  3. Scenarios, VSS and risk measures
  4. Shadow prices of capacity constraints only
Explanation. The chapter turns the stochastic problem into a scenario LP, measures the value of modelling uncertainty (VSS) and adds service levels and risk aversion (mean–CVaR).

14. VaR and CVaR (27)

orl-f-120 · level facile · notes
For a random loss $L$ and a level $\alpha \in (0, 1)$, how is $\mathrm{VaR}_\alpha(L)$ defined?
  1. $\inf\{\eta : \mathbb{P}(L \le \eta) \ge \alpha\}$
  2. The average loss in the worst tail of mass $1 - \alpha$
  3. The expected loss $\mathbb{E}[L]$
  4. $\sup\{\eta : \mathbb{P}(L \ge \eta) \ge \alpha\}$
Explanation. VaR is the $\alpha$-quantile of the loss; the average of the tail is the CVaR.
orl-f-121 · level facile · notes
Six equally likely losses $\{2, 4, 5, 7, 12, 20\}$, $\alpha = 0.80$. $\mathrm{VaR}_\alpha$ is the smallest loss $\eta$ with $\mathbb P(L \le \eta) \ge \alpha$, and $\mathrm{CVaR}_\alpha = \mathrm{VaR}_\alpha + \frac{1}{1-\alpha}\mathbb E[(L - \mathrm{VaR}_\alpha)^+]$. What are VaR and CVaR?
  1. VaR $= 12$, CVaR $= 16$
  2. VaR $= 20$, CVaR $= 20$
  3. VaR $= 12$, CVaR $= 18.67$
  4. VaR $= 7$, CVaR $= 12$
Explanation. $F$ reaches $0.80$ at the fifth loss: VaR $= 12$. CVaR $= 12 + \frac{1}{0.2}\cdot\frac16(20 - 12) = 18.67$: part of the tail mass sits on the point 12. The plain mean of $\{12, 20\}$, $16$, is the typical mistake.
orl-f-122 · level facile · notes
Rockafellar–Uryasev LP for a loss with scenarios $s$ of probability $\pi_s$ and loss $\ell_s(\mathbf x)$: $\min\ \eta + \frac{1}{1-\alpha}\sum_s\pi_s\xi_s$ subject to $\xi_s \ge \ell_s(\mathbf x) - \eta$, $\xi_s \ge 0$, $\mathbf x \in X$. How must the threshold variable $\eta$ be declared in gurobipy?
  1. With the default bounds of `addVar`
  2. As a binary variable
  3. With `ub=0`
  4. Free: `m.addVar(lb=-GRB.INFINITY)`
Explanation. $\eta \gtreqless 0$ in the model; the default `lb=0` would impose $\eta \ge 0$, a frequent mistake in the lab.
orl-f-123 · level facile · notes
Rockafellar–Uryasev LP for a loss with scenarios $s$ of probability $\pi_s$ and loss $\ell_s(\mathbf x)$: $\min\ \eta + \frac{1}{1-\alpha}\sum_s\pi_s\xi_s$ subject to $\xi_s \ge \ell_s(\mathbf x) - \eta$, $\xi_s \ge 0$, $\mathbf x \in X$. With $\alpha = 0.99$ and only $k = 220$ equally likely scenarios, why is the estimated CVaR unreliable?
  1. Because the LP becomes non-convex for $\alpha$ close to $1$
  2. Because the tail contains only 2–3 scenarios
  3. Because the VaR is then always unique
  4. Because the CVaR is not defined for $\alpha > 0.95$
Explanation. The tail of mass $0.01$ holds $2.2$ scenarios: the estimate is almost noise. Rule of thumb: at least a few dozen scenarios beyond the quantile.
orl-f-124 · level facile · notes
Two-stage supply chain with CVaR: capacities $x_a$ are booked with suppliers $a$ before the demand scenario $s$ is known; then purchases $f_{as}$ and shortages $u_s$ are decided in each scenario. Which variables are first-stage?
  1. The purchases $f_{as}$ in each scenario
  2. The shortages $u_s$ in each scenario
  3. The booked capacities $x_a$, the same in all scenarios
  4. The CVaR excesses $\xi_s$
Explanation. First-stage decisions are taken before observing the scenario and cannot depend on it; purchases and shortages are recourse decisions.
orl-f-125 · level facile · notes
Two-stage supply chain: in scenario $s$ the demand $d_s$ is covered by purchases $f_{1s} + f_{2s}$ and a shortage variable $u_s \ge 0$, with $f_{1s} + f_{2s} + u_s \ge d_s$; each unit of $u_s$ costs a penalty $g$. Why is the model never infeasible?
  1. Uncovered demand becomes shortage, paid with the penalty $g$
  2. Because the booked capacities are unlimited
  3. Because the demand is deterministic
  4. Because the risk weight is zero
Explanation. The model “buys” infeasibility at the price of the penalty: the demand constraint can always be satisfied with $u_s$ large enough.
orl-f-126 · level teorema · notes
Rockafellar–Uryasev LP for a loss with scenarios $s$ of probability $\pi_s$ and loss $\ell_s(\mathbf x)$: $\min\ \eta + \frac{1}{1-\alpha}\sum_s\pi_s\xi_s$ subject to $\xi_s \ge \ell_s(\mathbf x) - \eta$, $\xi_s \ge 0$, $\mathbf x \in X$. Which statement is correct?
  1. At the optimum $\eta$ is the expected loss and the value is the VaR
  2. At the optimum $\eta$ is a VaR and the optimal value is the CVaR
  3. Binary variables are needed to select the tail scenarios
  4. The optimal value is the maximum loss over the scenarios
Explanation. Minimizing over $\eta$ balances threshold and excesses: the balance point is the $\alpha$-quantile. With discrete distributions the VaR may not be unique ($\tilde\eta$ is a VaR).
orl-f-127 · level teorema · notes
VaR$_\alpha$ is the $\alpha$-quantile of a loss, CVaR$_\alpha$ the average loss in the worst tail of mass $1 - \alpha$. Which comparison is correct?
  1. VaR is convex in the decisions and CVaR is not
  2. Both always reward diversification
  3. VaR has a scenario-based LP formulation, CVaR does not
  4. CVaR is convex and subadditive; VaR in general is neither
Explanation. CVaR is the coherent measure of the pair: convex, subadditive (it rewards diversification) and optimizable with an LP; VaR may violate subadditivity.
orl-f-128 · level ipotesi · notes
Rockafellar–Uryasev LP for a loss with scenarios $s$ of probability $\pi_s$ and loss $\ell_s(\mathbf x)$: $\min\ \eta + \frac{1}{1-\alpha}\sum_s\pi_s\xi_s$ subject to $\xi_s \ge \ell_s(\mathbf x) - \eta$, $\xi_s \ge 0$, $\mathbf x \in X$. Under which assumptions is the whole model an LP?
  1. Equally likely scenarios and $\alpha = 0.9$
  2. Normally distributed scenario losses
  3. $X$ is a polyhedron and each loss $\ell_s(\mathbf x)$ is linear in $\mathbf x$
  4. At most 2000 scenarios
Explanation. With polyhedral $X$ and linear losses all constraints and the objective are linear; the threshold $\eta$ is free and the excesses non-negative. 2000 is only the Colab licence limit.
orl-f-129 · level ipotesi · notes
Rockafellar–Uryasev LP for a loss with scenarios $s$ of probability $\pi_s$ and loss $\ell_s(\mathbf x)$: $\min\ \eta + \frac{1}{1-\alpha}\sum_s\pi_s\xi_s$ subject to $\xi_s \ge \ell_s(\mathbf x) - \eta$, $\xi_s \ge 0$, $\mathbf x \in X$. Why does $\xi_s = (\ell_s - \eta)^+$ hold at the optimum, for every scenario with $\pi_s > 0$?
  1. $\xi_s$ has cost $\pi_s/(1-\alpha) > 0$ and lower bounds $\ell_s - \eta$ and $0$
  2. Because the threshold $\eta$ is a free variable
  3. Because $\alpha < 1$ makes the problem bounded
  4. Because the losses $\ell_s$ are positive
Explanation. Same positive-part trick as in the newsvendor: the objective pushes each $\xi_s$ down to the larger of its two lower bounds.
orl-f-227 · level facile · notes
Mean-CVaR portfolio: shares $x_i$, scenario returns $r_{is}$, loss $\ell_s(\mathbf x) = -\sum_i r_{is}x_i$; minimize $\mathrm{CVaR}_{0.90}$ of the loss with expected return at least $8\%$. In the case study it is compared with Markowitz (same return, same mean loss): CVaR $4.89\%$ against $5.31\%$. Why does mean-CVaR do better on the tail?
  1. Because its expected return is higher
  2. Because CVaR ignores the covariances
  3. Variance penalizes deviations on both sides, CVaR only the bad tail
  4. Because Markowitz uses fewer scenarios
Explanation. Variance treats gains and losses symmetrically; CVaR looks only where it hurts, so it cuts the high-beta assets that drive the worst scenarios.
orl-f-228 · level facile · notes
Mean-CVaR portfolio: the loss of scenario $s$ is $\ell_s(\mathbf x) = -\sum_i r_{is}x_i$, where $r_{is}$ is the return of asset $i$ in scenario $s$ and $x_i$ its share. Why can the whole model be solved as an LP?
  1. $r_{is}$ are data, so each loss is linear in $\mathbf x$, and the shares form a polyhedron
  2. The returns are normally distributed, so the CVaR has a closed linear form
  3. The scenarios are equally likely, so the CVaR is a plain average of losses
  4. The losses are always positive, so the positive parts can be dropped
Explanation. Linear losses and polyhedral constraints ($\sum_ix_i = 1$, return threshold, $x \ge 0$) keep the Rockafellar–Uryasev formulation linear, whatever the distribution of the returns (here fat tails).
orl-f-229 · level facile · notes
Rockafellar–Uryasev LP for a loss with scenarios $s$ of probability $\pi_s$ and loss $\ell_s(\mathbf x)$: $\min\ \eta + \frac{1}{1-\alpha}\sum_s\pi_s\xi_s$ subject to $\xi_s \ge \ell_s(\mathbf x) - \eta$, $\xi_s \ge 0$, $\mathbf x \in X$. How can a stress test be built into this scenario model?
  1. By raising $\alpha$ until the LP becomes infeasible
  2. By declaring $\eta$ non-negative
  3. By removing the scenarios with the largest losses
  4. By adding extreme scenarios or raising their probabilities $\pi_s$
Explanation. The probabilities $\pi_s$ are data: reweighting scenarios or adding extreme ones shows how the optimal decision and the CVaR react to harsher tails.
orl-f-361 · level facile · notes
Many lab models optimize a mean value. Why do VaR and CVaR look at the **tail** of the loss distribution instead?
  1. Because the mean cannot be computed with scenarios
  2. The mean can hide rare large losses
  3. Because the tail is always normal
  4. Because the tail makes the model integer
Explanation. A mean can look reassuring while a few scenarios carry very large losses; VaR gives a high threshold of loss, CVaR the average loss beyond it.
orl-f-362 · level facile · notes
VaR and CVaR of a loss at level $\alpha$: which question does each answer?
  1. VaR: the mean loss; CVaR: the largest loss
  2. VaR: the probability of a loss; CVaR: its variance
  3. Both: the loss in the single worst scenario
  4. VaR: a threshold; CVaR: the mean beyond it
Explanation. VaR is the $\alpha$-quantile of the loss (a threshold exceeded with probability at most $1 - \alpha$); CVaR is the average loss in the worst tail of mass $1 - \alpha$, so it also says how bad things get.
orl-f-363 · level facile · notes
According to the lab notes, what is the didactic «surprise» of the VaR and CVaR chapter?
  1. The CVaR is optimized with a simple LP
  2. The VaR is always convex and easy to optimize
  3. Tail risk needs integer variables
  4. The CVaR coincides with the variance of the loss
Explanation. With scenarios, the Rockafellar–Uryasev formulation makes the CVaR an LP; the VaR has no such formulation and is not convex in general.

  1. Only academic statisticians
  2. Only weather forecasters
  3. Banks, insurers, supply chain managers
  4. Only portfolio managers with normal returns
Explanation. After the financial crises «how much do we lose in the worst scenarios?» became a regulatory question too; the same measures are used for logistics plans, as in the two-stage supply chain of the chapter.
orl-f-365 · level facile · notes
Six equiprobable losses $\{2, 4, 5, 7, 12, 20\}$, $\alpha = 0.80$: mean $8.33$, $\mathrm{VaR}_{0.80} = 12$, $\mathrm{CVaR}_{0.80} = 18.67$. Why is the CVaR not simply $20$, the mean of the scenarios strictly above the VaR?
  1. Because the CVaR must equal the VaR plus the mean
  2. Because of a rounding error
  3. Because part of the tail mass sits on $12$
  4. Because the losses are not equiprobable
Explanation. The scenario at 20 weighs $1/6 \approx 0.167$, less than $0.20$: the remaining $0.033$ of the tail sits on the quantile 12. Hence $\mathrm{CVaR} = 12 + \frac{1}{0.20}\cdot\frac16(20 - 12) = 18.67$.
orl-f-366 · level facile · notes
Rockafellar–Uryasev LP for a loss with scenarios $s$ of probability $\pi_s$ and loss $\ell_s(\mathbf x)$, level $\alpha \in (0, 1)$: $\min\ \eta + \frac{1}{1-\alpha}\sum_s\pi_s\xi_s$ subject to $\xi_s \ge \ell_s(\mathbf x) - \eta$, $\xi_s \ge 0$, $\mathbf x \in X$, $\eta$ free. Six equiprobable losses $\{2, 4, 5, 7, 12, 20\}$ (no decision $\mathbf x$), $\alpha = 0.80$: the LP returns $\eta^* = 12$. What are the excesses $\xi_s$ and the optimal value?
  1. $\xi = (0, 0, 0, 0, 0, 20)$, value $28.67$
  2. $\xi = (0, 0, 0, 0, 0, 8)$, value $18.67$
  3. $\xi = (0, 0, 0, 0, 0, 8)$, value $13.33$
  4. $\xi = (10, 8, 7, 5, 0, 8)$, value $50.5$
Explanation. $\xi_s = (\ell_s - 12)^+$: only the loss 20 exceeds the threshold, by 8. Value $12 + \frac{1}{0.2}\cdot\frac16\cdot 8 = 18.67$: one LP gives VaR and CVaR, with the mass «straddling» the quantile treated correctly.
orl-f-367 · level facile · notes

  2. The VaR is the most pessimistic of the three
  3. Only the mean matters for decisions
  4. Three numbers that tell different stories
Explanation. Three numbers, three stories: the CVaR is always at least the VaR, which in turn is far above the mean when the tail is heavy.
orl-f-368 · level facile · notes

  4. To reduce the number of scenarios needed

orl-f-372 · level teorema · notes
Rockafellar–Uryasev LP for a loss with scenarios $s$ of probability $\pi_s$ and loss $\ell_s(\mathbf x)$, level $\alpha \in (0, 1)$: $\min\ \eta + \frac{1}{1-\alpha}\sum_s\pi_s\xi_s$ subject to $\xi_s \ge \ell_s(\mathbf x) - \eta$, $\xi_s \ge 0$, $\mathbf x \in X$, $\eta$ free. With discrete scenarios the LP returns some optimal $\eta^*$. What can be said of it?
  1. It is always the mean loss
  2. It is always unique and equal to the largest loss
  3. It is a VaR, not necessarily unique
  4. It has no meaning, only the objective does
Explanation. At the optimum $\eta^*$ is an $\alpha$-quantile of the loss; with discrete distributions the set of minimizers in $\eta$ can be an interval, so $\eta^*$ is *a* VaR. The optimal value, the CVaR, is unique.

Rockafellar–Uryasev LP for a loss with scenarios $s$ of probability $\pi_s$ and loss $\ell_s(\mathbf x)$, level $\alpha \in (0, 1)$: $\min\ \eta + \frac{1}{1-\alpha}\sum_s\pi_s\xi_s$ subject to $\xi_s \ge \ell_s(\mathbf x) - \eta$, $\xi_s \ge 0$, $\mathbf x \in X$, $\eta$ free. Sensitivity analysis of the lab: what is the expected effect of raising the confidence level $\alpha$?
  1. Fewer scenarios in the tail: noisier estimate
  2. A smaller CVaR
  3. No effect at all on the stability of the estimate
  4. The LP becomes infeasible for large $\alpha$
Explanation. A higher $\alpha$ looks at a thinner tail: with a fixed number of scenarios fewer of them lie beyond the quantile and the estimate is noisier; the CVaR itself can only grow.
orl-f-531 · level ipotesi · notes
Portfolio choice with scenarios: minimizing the CVaR of the loss and minimizing the variance (Markowitz), at the same required return. Under which hypothesis on the returns do the two give essentially the same portfolio?
  1. Normally distributed returns
  2. Returns with fat tails
  3. Returns with skewed losses
  4. Only with a single asset
Explanation. For normal returns the CVaR of a portfolio is a fixed multiple of its standard deviation plus a function of the mean, so at fixed mean both criteria rank portfolios alike; with fat tails (the lab case) they differ.

15. Arbitrage and pricing (30)

orl-f-130 · level facile · notes
One-period market: security $i$ costs $s^0_i$ today and pays $s^1_{ij}$ tomorrow in state $j$; security 0 is risk-free (costs 1, pays $R$). Arbitrage LP: $\min\sum_i s^0_ix_i$ subject to $\sum_i s^1_{ij}x_i \ge 0$ for every state $j$, positions $x_i$ free. The LP turns out to be unbounded. What does it mean?
  1. No arbitrage exists in this market
  2. A type A arbitrage exists: cash today with no risk, scalable at will
  3. The price data are inconsistent and the LP is infeasible
  4. Only a type B arbitrage exists
Explanation. A strategy of negative cost and non-negative payoffs exists; the constraints are homogeneous, so it can be scaled. Adding $\sum_i s^0_ix_i \ge -1$ gives optimum $-1$ and a concrete strategy.
orl-f-131 · level facile · notes
One-period market: security $i$ costs $s^0_i$ today and pays $s^1_{ij}$ tomorrow in state $j$; security 0 is risk-free (costs 1, pays $R$). Arbitrage LP: $\min\sum_i s^0_ix_i$ subject to $\sum_i s^1_{ij}x_i \ge 0$ for every state $j$, positions $x_i$ free. What is a type B arbitrage?
  1. A strategy that collects cash today and never pays tomorrow
  2. A strategy with negative cost and negative payoffs
  3. A strategy costing nothing today, never losing, gaining in some state
  4. A portfolio made only of risk-free securities
Explanation. In the LP: optimum $0$, with some state constraint slack in every optimal solution.
orl-f-132 · level facile · notes
One-period market: security $i$ costs $s^0_i$ today and pays $s^1_{ij}$ tomorrow in state $j$; security 0 is risk-free (costs 1, pays $R$). Arbitrage LP: $\min\sum_i s^0_ix_i$ subject to $\sum_i s^1_{ij}x_i \ge 0$ for every state $j$, positions $x_i$ free. The dual has a variable $p_j \ge 0$ per state and constraints $\sum_j s^1_{ij}p_j = s^0_i$; from security 0, $\sum_j R\,p_j = 1$. What are the numbers $q_j = R\,p_j$?
  1. Risk-neutral probabilities: prices are discounted expected payoffs under $q$
  2. The real-world probabilities of the states
  3. The optimal positions of the portfolio
  4. The payoffs of the risk-free security
Explanation. They are non-negative and sum to $1$, and $s^0_i = \frac1R\sum_j q_j\,s^1_{ij}$ for every security.
orl-f-133 · level facile · notes
One-period market: security $i$ costs $s^0_i$ today and pays $s^1_{ij}$ tomorrow in state $j$; security 0 is risk-free (costs 1, pays $R$). Arbitrage LP: $\min\sum_i s^0_ix_i$ subject to $\sum_i s^1_{ij}x_i \ge 0$ for every state $j$, positions $x_i$ free. In an incomplete market (more states than securities, here only securities 0 and 1 quoted), how is a new security priced without creating arbitrage?
  1. With a unique price, as in a complete market
  2. At its expected payoff under the real-world probabilities
  3. It cannot be priced at all
  4. With an interval: min and max discounted payoff over consistent measures
Explanation. In the case study the call with strike 12 has interval $[1.4615, 2.0308]$; any price outside it allows an arbitrage.
orl-f-134 · level facile · notes
One-period market: security $i$ costs $s^0_i$ today and pays $s^1_{ij}$ tomorrow in state $j$; security 0 is risk-free (costs 1, pays $R$). Arbitrage LP: $\min\sum_i s^0_ix_i$ subject to $\sum_i s^1_{ij}x_i \ge 0$ for every state $j$, positions $x_i$ free. With three states and consistent prices, the dual returns the risk-neutral measure $q = (0.296;\ 0.704;\ 0)$. What does $q_3 = 0$ signal?
  1. An error in the price data
  2. Degeneracy: state 3 is not priced, a type B arbitrage on it remains
  3. That state 3 cannot occur
  4. A type A arbitrage
Explanation. The fundamental theorem requires $q_j > 0$ for every state to exclude type B arbitrage as well.
orl-f-135 · level facile · notes
One-period market: security $i$ costs $s^0_i$ today and pays $s^1_{ij}$ tomorrow in state $j$; security 0 is risk-free (costs 1, pays $R$). Arbitrage LP: $\min\sum_i s^0_ix_i$ subject to $\sum_i s^1_{ij}x_i \ge 0$ for every state $j$, positions $x_i$ free. Why is the optimal value of this LP always $\le 0$?
  1. The null portfolio is feasible and costs $0$
  2. Because all the prices $s^0_i$ are positive
  3. Because the risk-free return $R$ is at least $1$
  4. Because the positions $x_i$ are free
Explanation. A minimum cannot exceed the value of a feasible solution; a negative value is an immediate cash inflow.
orl-f-136 · level teorema · notes
One-period market: security $i$ costs $s^0_i$ today and pays $s^1_{ij}$ tomorrow in state $j$; security 0 is risk-free (costs 1, pays $R$). Arbitrage LP: $\min\sum_i s^0_ix_i$ subject to $\sum_i s^1_{ij}x_i \ge 0$ for every state $j$, positions $x_i$ free. A risk-neutral measure is a vector $q \ge 0$ with $\sum_j q_j = 1$ and $s^0_i = \frac1R\sum_j q_js^1_{ij}$ for all $i$. Which statement is the fundamental theorem of asset pricing?
  1. No arbitrage if and only if a risk-neutral measure with $q_j \ge 0$ exists
  2. No arbitrage if and only if the risk-neutral measure is unique
  3. No arbitrage of type A or B if and only if one exists with $q_j > 0$ for all $j$
  4. No arbitrage if and only if prices are expected payoffs under real probabilities
Explanation. Strict positivity is needed to exclude type B arbitrage; uniqueness characterizes complete markets, not the absence of arbitrage.
orl-f-137 · level ipotesi · notes
One-period market: security $i$ costs $s^0_i$ today and pays $s^1_{ij}$ tomorrow in state $j$; security 0 is risk-free (costs 1, pays $R$). Arbitrage LP: $\min\sum_i s^0_ix_i$ subject to $\sum_i s^1_{ij}x_i \ge 0$ for every state $j$, positions $x_i$ free. Which results prove that, with no type A and no type B arbitrage, a dual solution (pricing measure) with $p_j > 0$ for every state exists?
  1. Weak duality only
  2. The KKT conditions of a convex QP
  3. The uniqueness of the optimal portfolio
  4. Strong duality (optimum $0$ $\Rightarrow$ dual feasible) and complementary slackness
Explanation. No type A: the primal has optimum $0$, so the dual is feasible. No type B: all state constraints are active in every optimum, and complementary slackness yields strictly positive duals.
orl-f-138 · level ipotesi · notes
One-period market: security $i$ costs $s^0_i$ today and pays $s^1_{ij}$ tomorrow in state $j$; security 0 is risk-free (costs 1, pays $R$). Arbitrage LP: $\min\sum_i s^0_ix_i$ subject to $\sum_i s^1_{ij}x_i \ge 0$ for every state $j$, positions $x_i$ free. Conversely, assume a risk-neutral measure $q > 0$ exists, with $s^0_i = \frac1R\sum_j q_js^1_{ij}$. Which computation shows that there is no arbitrage?
  1. The primal LP is unbounded, so its value is $-\infty$ and no strategy is profitable
  2. Payoffs $\ge 0$ give cost $\frac1R\sum_j q_j\bigl(\sum_i s^1_{ij}x_i\bigr) \ge 0$, $> 0$ if one payoff is positive
  3. The risk-free security costs $1$ and pays $R$, so every portfolio has cost equal to its payoff
  4. The dual objective is zero, and by weak duality every portfolio has cost exactly $0$
Explanation. Substituting the pricing relation in the cost: with $q > 0$ no strategy can collect cash (type A) or gain for free (type B).

orl-f-232 · level facile · notes
One-period market: security $i$ costs $s^0_i$ today and pays $s^1_{ij}$ tomorrow in state $j$; security 0 is risk-free (costs 1, pays $R$). Arbitrage LP: $\min\sum_i s^0_ix_i$ subject to $\sum_i s^1_{ij}x_i \ge 0$ for every state $j$, positions $x_i$ free. Why are the positions $x_i$ free variables in this LP?
  1. Because prices can be negative
  2. Because the number of states is finite
  3. Because the dual requires equality constraints
  4. Because each security can be bought ($x_i > 0$) or sold short ($x_i < 0$)
Explanation. Free positions model buying and short selling; by the duality rules each free variable gives an equality in the dual, the pricing relation $\sum_j s^1_{ij}p_j = s^0_i$.
orl-f-374 · level facile · notes
What is an **arbitrage**, in the lab chapter on arbitrage and pricing?
  1. A risky strategy with a high expected return
  2. A strategy that copies the market index
  3. Money from nothing, with no risk of loss
  4. A loan at the risk-free rate
Explanation. It either cashes in today with no risk of losing tomorrow, or costs nothing today and can only gain tomorrow; in a well-functioning market such opportunities cannot last.

  3. Because markets have no risk-free security
  4. Because prices are fixed by regulators
Explanation. An arbitrage can be scaled at will, so it would be exploited until prices become consistent: trading desks look for inconsistencies, risk managers validate listed prices, new securities are priced consistently.

One-period market: security $i$ costs $s^0_i$ today and pays $s^1_{ij}$ tomorrow in state $j$; security 0 is risk-free (costs 1, pays $R = 1 + r$). Arbitrage LP: $\min\sum_i s^0_ix_i$ subject to $\sum_i s^1_{ij}x_i \ge 0$ for every state $j$, positions $x_i$ free. With an arbitrage of type A the LP is unbounded. How do the notes obtain a finite certificate, a concrete strategy?
  1. By bounding each position, $|x_i| \le 1$
  2. By adding the normalization $\sum_i s^0_ix_i \ge -1$

  4. By removing the risk-free security
Explanation. The constraints are homogeneous, so the strategy can be scaled at will; requiring that today's income be at most 1 makes the optimum $-1$ and the solution a concrete strategy.
orl-f-379 · level teorema · notes
One-period market: security $i$ costs $s^0_i$ today and pays $s^1_{ij}$ tomorrow in state $j$; security 0 is risk-free (costs 1, pays $R = 1 + r$). Arbitrage LP: $\min\sum_i s^0_ix_i$ subject to $\sum_i s^1_{ij}x_i \ge 0$ for every state $j$, positions $x_i$ free. The dual has a variable $p_j \ge 0$ per state, constraints $\sum_j s^1_{ij}p_j = s^0_i$ and objective $\max\sum_j 0\cdot p_j$. What follows from the zero objective?
  1. The dual is always unbounded
  2. The dual has a unique solution
  3. The primal is always infeasible
  4. Every feasible solution is optimal
Explanation. All right-hand sides of the primal are zero, so the dual objective is identically zero: any $p \ge 0$ that prices the securities is an optimal dual solution, and its existence is what matters.
orl-f-380 · level facile · notes
One-period market: security $i$ costs $s^0_i$ today and pays $s^1_{ij}$ tomorrow in state $j$; security 0 is risk-free (costs 1, pays $R = 1 + r$). Arbitrage LP: $\min\sum_i s^0_ix_i$ subject to $\sum_i s^1_{ij}x_i \ge 0$ for every state $j$, positions $x_i$ free. Three-state market of the lab ($R = 1.04$): payoffs of security 1 $(10, 15, 13)$, of security 2 $(30, 15, 25)$. Prices today $s^0_1 = 6$, $s^0_2 = 20$; the strategy $x = (-27, 1, 1)$ costs $-1$ today and pays $(11.92;\ 1.92;\ 9.92)$. What happens to the LP without normalization?
  1. It has optimum $-1$, the income
  2. It is infeasible
  3. It is unbounded
  4. It has optimum $0$: no arbitrage
Explanation. The constraints are homogeneous: multiplying the strategy by any $t > 0$ cashes in $t$ today with nonnegative payoffs, so the cost has no lower bound (arbitrage of type A).

  1. Cost today and payoff in each state
  2. Its expected return under the real probabilities
  3. Its variance across the three states
  4. Only its cost today
Explanation. Type A arbitrage: negative cost today and nonnegative payoff in every state, here $-27\cdot 1.04 + 10 + 30 = 11.92$, $1.92$ and $9.92$; no probabilities are involved.

  2. Short selling would be forbidden
  3. The model would become nonlinear
  4. The duals would no longer be available
Explanation. The default lower bound is 0: the positions could not be negative, so strategies that sell short (such as borrowing at the risk-free rate) would be excluded and many arbitrages not detected.

Consistent prices $(1;\ 13;\ 18.6923)$: the duals of the state constraints are $p^* = (0.2846;\ 0.6769;\ 0)$. What is the risk-neutral measure $q$?

  2. $q = (1/3;\ 1/3;\ 1/3)$

  2. A complete market
  3. A payoff of the call that is never zero
  4. Real probabilities known in advance
