One buys one unit of each risky security (spending 26) and borrows $B$ at the risk-free rate ($x_0 = -B$). What is the largest $B$ for which the payoffs stay $\ge 0$ in every state?
  1. $B = 30$
  2. $B = 38.46$
  3. $B = 27$
  4. $B = 28.85$
Explanation. Payoff in state $j$: $s^1_{1j} + s^1_{2j} - 1.04B \ge 0$, i.e. $B \le \min(40, 30, 38)/1.04 = 30/1.04 = 28.85$; the worst state binds. Today one cashes in up to $28.85 - 26 = 2.85$ with no risk.

Part: Optimization and machine learning (67)

16. Support Vector Machine (35)

orl-f-139 · level facile · notes
SVM: observations $\mathbf x_i$ with labels $y_i \in \{-1, +1\}$, hyperplane $\mathbf w'\mathbf x + b = 0$. Hard margin: $\min\tfrac12\sum_j w_j^2$ subject to $y_i(\mathbf w'\mathbf x_i + b) \ge 1$. Why does this maximize the margin?
  1. The distance between the hyperplanes $\mathbf w'\mathbf x + b = \pm 1$ is $2/\|\mathbf w\|$
  2. The distance between the hyperplanes $\mathbf w'\mathbf x + b = \pm 1$ is $\|\mathbf w\|$
  3. Because the intercept $b$ is a free variable
  4. Because the labels are $\pm 1$ and cancel out
Explanation. A smaller $\|\mathbf w\|$ means a wider margin; the constraints keep each point on its side, at least one unit away in the scale of $\mathbf w$.
orl-f-140 · level facile · notes
SVM: observations $\mathbf x_i$ with labels $y_i \in \{-1, +1\}$, hyperplane $\mathbf w'\mathbf x + b = 0$. Soft margin: $\min\tfrac12\|\mathbf w\|^2 + C\sum_i\xi_i$ subject to $y_i(\mathbf w'\mathbf x_i + b) \ge 1 - \xi_i$, $\xi_i \ge 0$. What does $\tilde\xi_i > 1$ mean for observation $i$?
  1. It lies exactly on the margin
  2. It is a support vector with $\alpha_i = 0$
  3. It is on the wrong side of the hyperplane
  4. It is correctly classified beyond the margin
Explanation. $0 < \xi_i \le 1$ is a margin violation on the correct side; $\xi_i > 1$ puts the point on the wrong side of the hyperplane (misclassified).
orl-f-141 · level facile · notes
SVM: observations $\mathbf x_i$ with labels $y_i \in \{-1, +1\}$, hyperplane $\mathbf w'\mathbf x + b = 0$. Soft margin: $\min\tfrac12\|\mathbf w\|^2 + C\sum_i\xi_i$ subject to $y_i(\mathbf w'\mathbf x_i + b) \ge 1 - \xi_i$, $\xi_i \ge 0$. What is the effect of a small $C$?
  1. A narrow margin and zero training errors
  2. A wide margin, accepting many points inside it
  3. An infeasible model
  4. No effect on the width of the margin
Explanation. $C$ is the price of violations: a small $C$ buys a wide margin, a large $C$ chases every training point. It must be chosen on validation data.
orl-f-142 · level facile · notes
SVM: observations $\mathbf x_i$ with labels $y_i \in \{-1, +1\}$, hyperplane $\mathbf w'\mathbf x + b = 0$. Soft margin: $\min\tfrac12\|\mathbf w\|^2 + C\sum_i\xi_i$ subject to $y_i(\mathbf w'\mathbf x_i + b) \ge 1 - \xi_i$, $\xi_i \ge 0$. Its dual has multipliers $0 \le \alpha_i \le C$. What does $0 < \tilde\alpha_i < C$ say about observation $i$?
  1. It lies strictly beyond the margin
  2. It is misclassified
  3. It does not influence the hyperplane
  4. It lies exactly on the margin, and $b$ is recovered from it
Explanation. KKT complementarity: $\alpha_i = 0$ beyond the margin, $0 < \alpha_i < C$ on the margin ($\xi_i = 0$), $\alpha_i = C$ pays a slack $\xi_i \ge 0$.
orl-f-143 · level facile · notes
SVM: observations $\mathbf x_i$ with labels $y_i \in \{-1, +1\}$, hyperplane $\mathbf w'\mathbf x + b = 0$. Soft margin: $\min\tfrac12\|\mathbf w\|^2 + C\sum_i\xi_i$ subject to $y_i(\mathbf w'\mathbf x_i + b) \ge 1 - \xi_i$, $\xi_i \ge 0$. Its dual has multipliers $0 \le \alpha_i \le C$. Which feature of the dual opens the door to kernels $K(\mathbf x_i, \mathbf x_j)$?
  1. It depends on the data only through the inner products $\mathbf x_i'\mathbf x_j$
  2. It has the bound constraints $\alpha_i \le C$
  3. It is a maximization problem
  4. It has a single equality constraint
Explanation. Replacing $\mathbf x_i'\mathbf x_j$ with $K(\mathbf x_i, \mathbf x_j)$ classifies in a transformed space without building it.
orl-f-144 · level facile · notes
SVM: observations $\mathbf x_i$ with labels $y_i \in \{-1, +1\}$, hyperplane $\mathbf w'\mathbf x + b = 0$. Soft margin: $\min\tfrac12\|\mathbf w\|^2 + C\sum_i\xi_i$ subject to $y_i(\mathbf w'\mathbf x_i + b) \ge 1 - \xi_i$, $\xi_i \ge 0$. Its dual has multipliers $0 \le \alpha_i \le C$. With the RBF kernel $K(\mathbf x_i, \mathbf x_j) = e^{-\gamma\|\mathbf x_i - \mathbf x_j\|^2}$ and $\gamma = 5$, the boundary breaks into islands and 61 of the 90 points are support vectors. What is happening?
  1. The training QP has become non-convex
  2. The model is too simple (underfitting)
  3. Overfitting: the model memorizes the training set
  4. The kernel is not a valid kernel
Explanation. Training remains a convex QP: convexity and ability to generalize are different properties. $C$ and $\gamma$ are chosen together by cross-validation.
orl-f-145 · level facile · notes
SVM: observations $\mathbf x_i$ with labels $y_i \in \{-1, +1\}$, hyperplane $\mathbf w'\mathbf x + b = 0$. Soft margin: $\min\tfrac12\|\mathbf w\|^2 + C\sum_i\xi_i$ subject to $y_i(\mathbf w'\mathbf x_i + b) \ge 1 - \xi_i$, $\xi_i \ge 0$. With unbalanced classes (few defaulters among loan applicants) the slacks of the two classes get different costs $C_-$ and $C_+$. How should they be chosen?
  1. Proportional to the class frequencies
  2. To reflect the costs of the two wrong decisions
  3. Always equal to each other
  4. By maximizing the accuracy on the test set
Explanation. The costs should mirror the decisions (a loan to a defaulter versus a good customer turned down): with a $10\times$ cost on defaulters none escapes, at the price of a few more false alarms.
orl-f-146 · level teorema · notes
SVM: observations $\mathbf x_i$ with labels $y_i \in \{-1, +1\}$, hyperplane $\mathbf w'\mathbf x + b = 0$. Soft margin: $\min\tfrac12\|\mathbf w\|^2 + C\sum_i\xi_i$ subject to $y_i(\mathbf w'\mathbf x_i + b) \ge 1 - \xi_i$, $\xi_i \ge 0$. Its dual has multipliers $0 \le \alpha_i \le C$. Which primal-dual relation holds at the optimum?
  1. $\tilde{\mathbf w} = \sum_i \tilde\alpha_i\,\mathbf x_i$
  2. $\tilde{\mathbf w} = \sum_i \tilde\xi_i\,y_i\,\mathbf x_i$
  3. $\tilde{\mathbf w} = \sum_i \tilde\alpha_i\,y_i\,\mathbf x_i$
  4. $\tilde{\mathbf w} = C\sum_i y_i\mathbf x_i$
Explanation. Only the points with $\tilde\alpha_i > 0$ (support vectors) enter $\tilde{\mathbf w}$. The points with $\tilde\alpha_i = 0$ could disappear without changing anything: in the case study 7 support vectors out of 90.
orl-f-147 · level ipotesi · notes
SVM: observations $\mathbf x_i$ with labels $y_i \in \{-1, +1\}$, hyperplane $\mathbf w'\mathbf x + b = 0$. Soft margin: $\min\tfrac12\|\mathbf w\|^2 + C\sum_i\xi_i$ subject to $y_i(\mathbf w'\mathbf x_i + b) \ge 1 - \xi_i$, $\xi_i \ge 0$. Its dual has multipliers $0 \le \alpha_i \le C$. The inner products $\mathbf x_i'\mathbf x_j$ of the dual are replaced by a kernel $K(\mathbf x_i, \mathbf x_j)$. Under which condition does training remain a convex QP?
  1. $\gamma < 1$ in the RBF kernel
  2. The two classes are balanced
  3. $C$ is small
  4. The kernel matrix $(K(\mathbf x_i, \mathbf x_j))$ is positive semidefinite
Explanation. With a positive semidefinite kernel matrix the dual objective is concave; the boundary in the original space can be arbitrarily curved.
orl-f-148 · level ipotesi · notes
SVM: observations $\mathbf x_i$ with labels $y_i \in \{-1, +1\}$, hyperplane $\mathbf w'\mathbf x + b = 0$. Soft margin: $\min\tfrac12\|\mathbf w\|^2 + C\sum_i\xi_i$ subject to $y_i(\mathbf w'\mathbf x_i + b) \ge 1 - \xi_i$, $\xi_i \ge 0$. Its dual has multipliers $0 \le \alpha_i \le C$. From which condition does the dual constraint $\sum_i \alpha_i y_i = 0$ come?
  1. From the bound $\alpha_i \le C$
  2. From stationarity of the Lagrangian in the free intercept $b$
  3. From the non-negativity of the slacks $\xi_i$
  4. From the classes being balanced
Explanation. $\partial L/\partial b = -\sum_i\alpha_iy_i = 0$; stationarity in $\mathbf w$ gives $\mathbf w = \sum_i\alpha_iy_i\mathbf x_i$ and in $\xi_i$ the bound $\alpha_i \le C$.
orl-f-233 · level facile · notes
SVM: observations $\mathbf x_i$ with labels $y_i \in \{-1, +1\}$, hyperplane $\mathbf w'\mathbf x + b = 0$. Hard margin: $\min\tfrac12\|\mathbf w\|^2$ subject to $y_i(\mathbf w'\mathbf x_i + b) \ge 1$ for every $i$. What happens when the two classes overlap (no line separates them)?
  1. The optimum has $\mathbf w = \mathbf 0$
  2. The model becomes unbounded
  3. The model is infeasible: slacks are needed (soft margin)
  4. Gurobi returns the best separating line anyway
Explanation. No hyperplane satisfies all the constraints, so the QP has no feasible solution; the soft margin adds slacks $\xi_i \ge 0$ paid in the objective.
orl-f-234 · level facile · notes
Support Vector Regression: $\min\tfrac12\|\mathbf w\|^2 + C\sum_i(\xi_i + \xi_i^*)$ with $|y_i - (\mathbf w'\mathbf x_i + b)| \le \varepsilon + \xi_i^{(*)}$, i.e. errors within a tube of width $\varepsilon$ are free. Which observations determine the fitted line?
  1. Only those on or outside the tube
  2. All the observations equally
  3. Only those strictly inside the tube
  4. Only the observation with the largest $y_i$
Explanation. Points strictly inside the tube have null multipliers, like the points beyond the margin in the SVM; in the case study 14 points out of 40 lie outside the tube.
orl-f-298 · level facile · notes
In the lab chapter on Support Vector Machines, the labels $y_i \in \{-1, +1\}$ of the training observations (e.g. reliable / defaulting client) are:
  1. binary decision variables of the QP
  2. continuous variables between $-1$ and $+1$
  3. observed data, not variables
  4. multipliers of the classification constraints
Explanation. The labels are observed data; the variables (coefficients $w_j$, intercept $b$, slacks $\xi_i$, multipliers $\alpha_i$) are all continuous, so the SVM stays within the continuous models of the lab.
orl-f-299 · level facile · notes
The lab trains its Support Vector Machines without any machine-learning library: every classifier is a QP written and solved with Gurobi. Why?
  1. To understand what a classifier actually optimizes
  2. Because libraries cannot handle the kernel
  3. Because Gurobi is faster than any ML library
  4. Because libraries give only local optima
Explanation. The point of the chapter is to see the SVM as an optimization problem (a convex QP with its dual and multipliers), not as a black box; speed or library limits are not the reason.
orl-f-300 · level facile · notes
A Support Vector Machine is a binary classifier obtained by solving a QP. Which managerial question is a classification problem suited to it?
  1. How many loaves will be sold tomorrow?
  2. Which price maximizes the profit of a concert?
  3. How much should be produced in each month?
  4. Will this client repay the loan or default?
Explanation. Credit risk, churn, predictive maintenance, quality control and fraud are the applications of the notes: each observation gets one of two labels. Predicting a quantity is regression, choosing prices or production plans is optimization.
orl-f-301 · level facile · notes
The lab chapter on Support Vector Machines says it «closes the circle» of the course. In what sense?
  1. It introduces integer variables for the first time
  2. Convex QPs and duality drive a classifier
  3. It replaces Gurobi with a machine-learning library
  4. It shows that optimization is not needed in machine learning
Explanation. Convex QPs, duality and multipliers, used for production, logistics and finance, are the engine of the SVM: the support vectors are read through complementary slackness, as shadow prices were.
orl-f-302 · level media · notes
SVM for binary classification: observations $\mathbf x_i \in \mathbb R^p$ with labels $y_i \in \{-1, +1\}$, hyperplane $\mathbf w'\mathbf x + b = 0$ ($\mathbf w$, $b$ free). Hard margin: $\min\ \tfrac12\|\mathbf w\|^2$ subject to $y_i(\mathbf w'\mathbf x_i + b) \ge 1$. Two observations: $\mathbf x_1 = (0; 0)$ with $y_1 = -1$ and $\mathbf x_2 = (4; 0)$ with $y_2 = +1$. What are $\tilde{\mathbf w}$, $\tilde b$ and the margin $2/\|\tilde{\mathbf w}\|$?
  1. $\mathbf w = (1; 0)$, $b = -1$, margin $2$
  2. $\mathbf w = (0.5; 0)$, $b = -1$, margin $4$
  3. $\mathbf w = (0.25; 0)$, $b = -0.5$, margin $8$
  4. $\mathbf w = (0.5; 0.5)$, $b = -1$, margin $2.83$
Explanation. Constraints: $-b \ge 1$ and $4w_1 + b \ge 1$; minimizing $w_1^2 + w_2^2$ gives $w_2 = 0$ and both constraints active: $b = -1$, $w_1 = 0.5$. The hyperplane $x_1 = 2$ is the bisector of the segment, and the margin $2/0.5 = 4$ equals the distance between the two points.
orl-f-303 · level facile · notes
SVM for binary classification: observations $\mathbf x_i \in \mathbb R^p$ with labels $y_i \in \{-1, +1\}$, hyperplane $\mathbf w'\mathbf x + b = 0$ ($\mathbf w$, $b$ free). Hard margin with $n$ observations and $p$ features: $\min\ \tfrac12\sum_j w_j^2$ subject to $y_i(\sum_j w_jx_{ij} + b) \ge 1$. How many variables and constraints does the QP have?
  1. $n$ variables and $p + 1$ constraints
  2. $p$ variables and $n + 1$ constraints
  3. $n + p$ variables and $n$ constraints
  4. $p + 1$ variables and $n$ constraints
Explanation. The variables are the $p$ coefficients $w_j$ and the intercept $b$, all free; there is one classification constraint per observation. The slacks $\xi_i$ (another $n$ variables) appear only in the soft margin.
orl-f-304 · level ipotesi · notes
SVM for binary classification: observations $\mathbf x_i \in \mathbb R^p$ with labels $y_i \in \{-1, +1\}$, hyperplane $\mathbf w'\mathbf x + b = 0$ ($\mathbf w$, $b$ free). Hard margin: $\min\ \tfrac12\|\mathbf w\|^2$ subject to $y_i(\mathbf w'\mathbf x_i + b) \ge 1$ for every $i$. Under which hypothesis on the data does this QP have an optimal solution?
  1. The two classes are linearly separable
  2. The two classes have the same number of points
  3. The features are standardized
  4. There are more observations than features
Explanation. If some hyperplane separates the classes, scaling it gives a feasible $(\mathbf w, b)$; the objective is convex and bounded below by $0$, so an optimum exists. Without separability the feasible set is empty: this is why the soft margin is needed.
orl-f-305 · level facile · notes
SVM for binary classification: observations $\mathbf x_i \in \mathbb R^p$ with labels $y_i \in \{-1, +1\}$, hyperplane $\mathbf w'\mathbf x + b = 0$ ($\mathbf w$, $b$ free). Soft margin: $\min\ \tfrac12\|\mathbf w\|^2 + C\sum_i\xi_i$ subject to $y_i(\mathbf w'\mathbf x_i + b) \ge 1 - \xi_i$, $\xi_i \ge 0$. At the optimum an observation has $0 < \tilde\xi_i < 1$. Where is it?
  1. Exactly on its margin line
  2. On the wrong side of the hyperplane
  3. On the right side, but inside the margin
  4. Far beyond its margin line
Explanation. $y_i(\mathbf w'\mathbf x_i + b) = 1 - \tilde\xi_i$ lies strictly between $0$ and $1$: the point is correctly classified but violates the margin. With $\tilde\xi_i > 1$ it would be misclassified, with $\tilde\xi_i = 0$ on or beyond the margin.
orl-f-306 · level teorema · notes
SVM for binary classification: observations $\mathbf x_i \in \mathbb R^p$ with labels $y_i \in \{-1, +1\}$, hyperplane $\mathbf w'\mathbf x + b = 0$ ($\mathbf w$, $b$ free). Soft margin: $\min\ \tfrac12\|\mathbf w\|^2 + C\sum_i\xi_i$ subject to $y_i(\mathbf w'\mathbf x_i + b) \ge 1 - \xi_i$, $\xi_i \ge 0$. For fixed $(\mathbf w, b)$, what is the optimal value of each slack $\xi_i$?
  1. $\xi_i = 1 - y_i(\mathbf w'\mathbf x_i + b)$, whatever its sign
  2. $\xi_i = \max\{0,\ 1 - y_i(\mathbf w'\mathbf x_i + b)\}$
  3. $\xi_i = |\mathbf w'\mathbf x_i + b|$
  4. $\xi_i = \max\{0,\ y_i(\mathbf w'\mathbf x_i + b) - 1\}$
Explanation. $\xi_i$ must satisfy $\xi_i \ge 1 - y_i(\mathbf w'\mathbf x_i + b)$ and $\xi_i \ge 0$, and costs $C > 0$: it takes the smallest admissible value, the hinge loss. So the soft margin minimizes $\tfrac12\|\mathbf w\|^2$ plus $C$ times the total hinge loss.
orl-f-307 · level facile · notes
SVM for binary classification: observations $\mathbf x_i \in \mathbb R^p$ with labels $y_i \in \{-1, +1\}$, hyperplane $\mathbf w'\mathbf x + b = 0$ ($\mathbf w$, $b$ free). Soft margin: $\min\ \tfrac12\|\mathbf w\|^2 + C\sum_i\xi_i$ subject to $y_i(\mathbf w'\mathbf x_i + b) \ge 1 - \xi_i$, $\xi_i \ge 0$. Credit-risk case (90 clients, 2 indicators): $C = 0.05$ gives margin $2.42$ with 2 errors and 22 points inside the margin; $C = 20$ gives margin $0.36$, 0 errors, 3 points inside. Is $C = 20$ the best choice?
  1. Yes: it makes no error on the training set
  2. Yes: a larger $C$ always generalizes better
  3. No: the smallest $C$ is always the best
  4. Not necessarily: $C$ is chosen on validation data
Explanation. A large $C$ chases every training point (zero errors, very narrow margin); a small one buys a wide margin. It is the regularization–fit trade-off: $C$ is chosen on validation data, never on the training errors or on the test set.
orl-f-308 · level ipotesi · notes
SVM for binary classification: observations $\mathbf x_i \in \mathbb R^p$ with labels $y_i \in \{-1, +1\}$, hyperplane $\mathbf w'\mathbf x + b = 0$ ($\mathbf w$, $b$ free). Soft margin: $\min\ \tfrac12\|\mathbf w\|^2 + C\sum_i\xi_i$ subject to $y_i(\mathbf w'\mathbf x_i + b) \ge 1 - \xi_i$, $\xi_i \ge 0$. When does the soft margin, for $C$ large enough, return the hard-margin solution ($\min\ \tfrac12\|\mathbf w\|^2$ with $y_i(\mathbf w'\mathbf x_i + b) \ge 1$)?
  1. Always, whatever the data
  2. Never: the two models differ for every $C$
  3. When the classes are linearly separable
  4. Only when there is a single feature, $p = 1$
Explanation. If the classes are separable the hard margin is feasible, and for a large enough $C$ paying any slack costs more than shrinking the margin: all $\xi_i = 0$. With overlapping classes the hard margin is infeasible and the slacks stay positive for every $C$.
orl-f-309 · level facile · notes
SVM for binary classification: observations $\mathbf x_i \in \mathbb R^p$ with labels $y_i \in \{-1, +1\}$, hyperplane $\mathbf w'\mathbf x + b = 0$ ($\mathbf w$, $b$ free). Soft margin: $\min\ \tfrac12\|\mathbf w\|^2 + C\sum_i\xi_i$ subject to $y_i(\mathbf w'\mathbf x_i + b) \ge 1 - \xi_i$, $\xi_i \ge 0$. Its dual: $\max\ \sum_i\alpha_i - \tfrac12\sum_i\sum_j\alpha_i\alpha_jy_iy_j\mathbf x_i'\mathbf x_j$ subject to $\sum_i\alpha_iy_i = 0$, $0 \le \alpha_i \le C$. At the optimum an observation has $\tilde\alpha_i = C$. What does complementary slackness say about it?
  1. $\tilde\xi_i > 0$: inside the margin or misclassified
  2. It lies exactly on the margin line
  3. It lies beyond the margin and does not affect the solution
  4. It must be removed from the training set
Explanation. The KKT conditions organize the points: $\alpha_i = 0$ beyond the margin, $0 < \alpha_i < C$ exactly on it, $\alpha_i = C$ when the constraint pays a slack $\xi_i > 0$ (inside the margin, or on the wrong side if $\xi_i > 1$).
orl-f-310 · level facile · notes
SVM for binary classification: observations $\mathbf x_i \in \mathbb R^p$ with labels $y_i \in \{-1, +1\}$, hyperplane $\mathbf w'\mathbf x + b = 0$ ($\mathbf w$, $b$ free). Soft margin: $\min\ \tfrac12\|\mathbf w\|^2 + C\sum_i\xi_i$ subject to $y_i(\mathbf w'\mathbf x_i + b) \ge 1 - \xi_i$, $\xi_i \ge 0$. Its dual: $\max\ \sum_i\alpha_i - \tfrac12\sum_i\sum_j\alpha_i\alpha_jy_iy_j\mathbf x_i'\mathbf x_j$ subject to $\sum_i\alpha_iy_i = 0$, $0 \le \alpha_i \le C$. Credit-risk case with $C = 1$: 83 of the 90 clients have $\tilde\alpha_i = 0$. What happens to the classifier if those 83 points are removed and the QP is solved again?
  1. It changes completely, both $\mathbf w$ and $b$
  2. It becomes infeasible for the remaining points
  3. It stays the same
  4. Only the intercept $b$ changes
Explanation. $\tilde{\mathbf w} = \sum_i\tilde\alpha_iy_i\mathbf x_i$ uses only the 7 points with $\tilde\alpha_i > 0$, the support vectors; the other 83 are beyond the margin and could disappear without changing the solution.
orl-f-311 · level teorema · notes
SVM for binary classification: observations $\mathbf x_i \in \mathbb R^p$ with labels $y_i \in \{-1, +1\}$, hyperplane $\mathbf w'\mathbf x + b = 0$ ($\mathbf w$, $b$ free). Soft margin: $\min\ \tfrac12\|\mathbf w\|^2 + C\sum_i\xi_i$ subject to $y_i(\mathbf w'\mathbf x_i + b) \ge 1 - \xi_i$, $\xi_i \ge 0$. Its dual: $\max\ \sum_i\alpha_i - \tfrac12\sum_i\sum_j\alpha_i\alpha_jy_iy_j\mathbf x_i'\mathbf x_j$ subject to $\sum_i\alpha_iy_i = 0$, $0 \le \alpha_i \le C$. In the credit-risk case the solver reports dual value $4.7868$ = primal value $4.7868$. Which result guarantees this equality?
  1. Weak duality, which holds for every QP
  2. The kernel trick
  3. The uniqueness of the support vectors
  4. Strong duality for convex QPs
Explanation. The soft margin is a convex QP with linear constraints: the KKT conditions are necessary and sufficient and the dual optimum has the same value as the primal. Weak duality would only give an inequality.
orl-f-312 · level facile · notes
SVM for binary classification: observations $\mathbf x_i \in \mathbb R^p$ with labels $y_i \in \{-1, +1\}$, hyperplane $\mathbf w'\mathbf x + b = 0$ ($\mathbf w$, $b$ free). Soft margin: $\min\ \tfrac12\|\mathbf w\|^2 + C\sum_i\xi_i$ subject to $y_i(\mathbf w'\mathbf x_i + b) \ge 1 - \xi_i$, $\xi_i \ge 0$. Its dual: $\max\ \sum_i\alpha_i - \tfrac12\sum_i\sum_j\alpha_i\alpha_jy_iy_j\mathbf x_i'\mathbf x_j$ subject to $\sum_i\alpha_iy_i = 0$, $0 \le \alpha_i \le C$. With a kernel $K(\mathbf x_i, \mathbf x_j)$ in place of $\mathbf x_i'\mathbf x_j$ (e.g. RBF, $K(\mathbf x, \mathbf z) = e^{-\gamma\|\mathbf x - \mathbf z\|^2}$), how is a new point $\mathbf x$ classified?
  1. $\mathrm{sign}(\mathbf w'\mathbf x + b)$ with $\mathbf w$ computed in the original space
  2. $\mathrm{sign}\bigl(\sum_i\tilde\alpha_iy_iK(\mathbf x_i, \mathbf x) + b\bigr)$
  3. $\mathrm{sign}\bigl(\sum_iK(\mathbf x_i, \mathbf x)\bigr)$
  4. By the label of the nearest support vector
Explanation. The transformed space is never built: the classifier uses only kernel values between the new point and the support vectors ($\tilde\alpha_i > 0$), weighted by $\tilde\alpha_iy_i$, plus the intercept.
orl-f-313 · level facile · notes
SVM for binary classification: observations $\mathbf x_i \in \mathbb R^p$ with labels $y_i \in \{-1, +1\}$, hyperplane $\mathbf w'\mathbf x + b = 0$ ($\mathbf w$, $b$ free). Soft margin: $\min\ \tfrac12\|\mathbf w\|^2 + C\sum_i\xi_i$ subject to $y_i(\mathbf w'\mathbf x_i + b) \ge 1 - \xi_i$, $\xi_i \ge 0$. With the RBF kernel $K(\mathbf x, \mathbf z) = e^{-\gamma\|\mathbf x - \mathbf z\|^2}$ the SVM has two hyperparameters, $C$ and $\gamma$. How do the notes choose them?
  1. $\gamma$ first on the training errors, then $C$
  2. The largest values that give zero training errors
  3. $C$ by duality, $\gamma$ by a rule of thumb
  4. Together, by cross-validation
Explanation. $C$ and $\gamma$ interact (both control how closely the frontier follows the training points), so they are chosen together by cross-validation; zero training errors is the overfitting signal, not a goal.
orl-f-314 · level facile · notes
SVM for binary classification: observations $\mathbf x_i \in \mathbb R^p$ with labels $y_i \in \{-1, +1\}$, hyperplane $\mathbf w'\mathbf x + b = 0$ ($\mathbf w$, $b$ free). Soft margin: $\min\ \tfrac12\|\mathbf w\|^2 + C\sum_i\xi_i$ subject to $y_i(\mathbf w'\mathbf x_i + b) \ge 1 - \xi_i$, $\xi_i \ge 0$. «Ring» case with the RBF kernel: with $\gamma = 5$ the frontier breaks into islands and 61 of 90 points are support vectors, yet the training problem is still a convex QP. What does this show?
  1. Convexity and generalization are different things
  2. The QP is no longer convex for large $\gamma$
  3. The solver has stopped at a local optimum
  4. The RBF kernel is not a valid kernel for $\gamma > 1$
Explanation. The QP is convex for every valid kernel and its optimum is global, but a global optimum of the training problem can still memorize the data: generalization is judged on validation data.
orl-f-315 · level facile · notes
SVM for binary classification: observations $\mathbf x_i \in \mathbb R^p$ with labels $y_i \in \{-1, +1\}$, hyperplane $\mathbf w'\mathbf x + b = 0$ ($\mathbf w$, $b$ free). Soft margin: $\min\ \tfrac12\|\mathbf w\|^2 + C\sum_i\xi_i$ subject to $y_i(\mathbf w'\mathbf x_i + b) \ge 1 - \xi_i$, $\xi_i \ge 0$. The rare class (e.g. defaulting clients, $y_i = +1$) costs more when misclassified. How do the notes change the objective?
  1. They duplicate the rare observations in the data
  2. They raise the margin requirement of the rare class to 2
  3. $\tfrac12\|\mathbf w\|^2 + C_-\sum_{i:y_i = -1}\xi_i + C_+\sum_{i:y_i = +1}\xi_i$
  4. They remove part of the frequent class
Explanation. It is enough to weigh the slacks of the two classes differently, with $C_+ > C_-$ for the class whose errors cost more; the model stays a convex QP.
orl-f-316 · level facile · notes
SVM for binary classification: observations $\mathbf x_i \in \mathbb R^p$ with labels $y_i \in \{-1, +1\}$, hyperplane $\mathbf w'\mathbf x + b = 0$ ($\mathbf w$, $b$ free). Soft margin: $\min\ \tfrac12\|\mathbf w\|^2 + C\sum_i\xi_i$ subject to $y_i(\mathbf w'\mathbf x_i + b) \ge 1 - \xi_i$, $\xi_i \ge 0$. Credit-risk case: with equal costs, precision $1.00$ and recall of the defaulters $0.94$; with a cost 10 times higher on the defaulters' slacks, precision $0.97$ and recall $1.00$. How is the change read?
  1. The frontier moves toward the healthy class
  2. The classifier got worse on every measure
  3. The frontier moves toward the defaulters: fewer false alarms
  4. Nothing changed: the differences are numerical noise
Explanation. Raising the cost of missing a defaulter pushes the frontier into the healthy region: recall reaches 100% and precision drops slightly because some good clients are flagged.
orl-f-317 · level facile · notes
SVM for binary classification: observations $\mathbf x_i \in \mathbb R^p$ with labels $y_i \in \{-1, +1\}$, hyperplane $\mathbf w'\mathbf x + b = 0$ ($\mathbf w$, $b$ free). Soft margin: $\min\ \tfrac12\|\mathbf w\|^2 + C\sum_i\xi_i$ subject to $y_i(\mathbf w'\mathbf x_i + b) \ge 1 - \xi_i$, $\xi_i \ge 0$. In the credit-risk case, the recall of the defaulters is $1.00$. What does it mean?
  1. Every client flagged as defaulter really defaults
  2. The training error is zero
  3. All slacks $\xi_i$ are zero
  4. No actual defaulter is classified as reliable
Explanation. Recall = share of the actual defaulters that the classifier detects; precision = share of the flagged clients that really default. Recall 1 means no defaulter escapes, possibly at the price of false alarms.
orl-f-318 · level facile · notes
Support Vector Regression: $\min\ \tfrac12\|\mathbf w\|^2 + C\sum_i(\xi_i + \xi_i^*)$ with $|y_i - (\mathbf w'\mathbf x_i + b)| \le \varepsilon + \xi_i^{(*)}$, where $\xi_i, \xi_i^* \ge 0$. What is the role of $\varepsilon$?
  1. The weight of the errors in the objective
  2. The half-width of a tube of free errors
  3. The largest admissible slope of the line
  4. The share of points allowed outside the tube
Explanation. Errors within $\pm\varepsilon$ of the estimate are ignored; only the points outside the tube pay, through $\xi_i$ (above) or $\xi_i^*$ (below), with weight $C$.
orl-f-319 · level facile · notes
Support Vector Regression on price–demand data (40 observations): with tube $\varepsilon = 8$, 14 points fall outside the tube. What happens to the number of points outside if $\varepsilon$ is enlarged?
  1. It grows, because the tube is stricter
  2. It stays 14, since it does not depend on $\varepsilon$
  3. It decreases: fewer points pay
  4. It becomes zero for every $\varepsilon > 0$
Explanation. A wider tube contains more points, which are then ignored; the estimate rests on the fewer points left outside (with $\varepsilon = 8$ the line was $-10.86p + 220.32$, against the true $-11p + 220$).
orl-f-320 · level teorema · notes
Support Vector Regression: $\min\ \tfrac12\|\mathbf w\|^2 + C\sum_i(\xi_i + \xi_i^*)$ subject to $y_i - (\mathbf w'\mathbf x_i + b) \le \varepsilon + \xi_i$, $(\mathbf w'\mathbf x_i + b) - y_i \le \varepsilon + \xi_i^*$, $\xi_i, \xi_i^* \ge 0$. Which class of problem is it, and what follows?
  1. A convex QP: every local optimum is global
  2. An LP: its optimum is at a vertex
  3. A nonconvex QP: it needs `NonConvex = 2`
  4. An NLP with functional constraints
Explanation. Convex quadratic objective ($\tfrac12\|\mathbf w\|^2$ plus a linear term) and linear constraints: a convex QP, like the soft-margin SVM, with a certifiable global optimum.

17. Robust and quantile regression (32)

orl-f-149 · level facile · notes
A bakery fits sales against temperature in two ways: least absolute deviations (an LP) and least squares (a QP). The history contains five strike days with very low sales. Which fitted line moves more because of them?
  1. The absolute-deviation (LP) line
  2. The least-squares (QP) line, which pays deviations squared
  3. Neither line moves noticeably
  4. Both lines move by the same amount
Explanation. A deviation of $100$ weighs, squared, as much as ten thousand deviations of one: least squares is dragged down, the LP stays with the normal data.
orl-f-150 · level facile · notes
Quantile regression LP: observations $y_i$ with features $x_{ij}$, coefficients $w_j$ and intercept $b$ free, deviations above $u_i \ge 0$ and below $v_i \ge 0$ with $\sum_j w_jx_{ij} + b + u_i - v_i = y_i$; minimize $\sum_i[\tau u_i + (1 - \tau)v_i]$, $0 < \tau < 1$. With $\tau = 1/2$, what does the objective become?
  1. Half the sum of absolute deviations: median regression
  2. The sum of the squared deviations
  3. The largest absolute deviation
  4. The sum of the deviations with their sign
Explanation. Deviations above and below cost the same, $1/2$ each, and at the optimum $u_i + v_i = |y_i - \text{prediction}|$.
orl-f-151 · level facile · notes
Quantile regression LP: observations $y_i$ with features $x_{ij}$, coefficients $w_j$ and intercept $b$ free, deviations above $u_i \ge 0$ and below $v_i \ge 0$ with $\sum_j w_jx_{ij} + b + u_i - v_i = y_i$; minimize $\sum_i[\tau u_i + (1 - \tau)v_i]$, $0 < \tau < 1$. Let $\tilde\pi_i$ be the optimal dual of the equality constraint of observation $i$. In which interval does it lie?
  1. $[0, 1]$
  2. $[-1, 1]$
  3. $[-(1 - \tau),\ \tau]$
  4. $[-\tau,\ 1 - \tau]$
Explanation. From the dual constraints of $u_i$ and $v_i$: $\tilde\pi_i = \tau$ above the estimate, $-(1 - \tau)$ below, an interior value on the interpolated points.
orl-f-152 · level facile · notes
Quantile regression LP: observations $y_i$ with features $x_{ij}$, coefficients $w_j$ and intercept $b$ free, deviations above $u_i \ge 0$ and below $v_i \ge 0$ with $\sum_j w_jx_{ij} + b + u_i - v_i = y_i$; minimize $\sum_i[\tau u_i + (1 - \tau)v_i]$, $0 < \tau < 1$. With $p$ features, what are the support points of the optimal estimate?
  1. The observations with the largest deviations
  2. The outliers, such as the strike days
  3. All the observations below the estimate
  4. The interpolated observations (usually $p + 1$), with interior duals
Explanation. Moving any other observation, as long as it stays on its side of the estimate, changes nothing: the analogue of the support vectors of the SVM.
orl-f-153 · level facile · notes
Quantile regression LP: observations $y_i$ with features $x_{ij}$, coefficients $w_j$ and intercept $b$ free, deviations above $u_i \ge 0$ and below $v_i \ge 0$ with $\sum_j w_jx_{ij} + b + u_i - v_i = y_i$; minimize $\sum_i[\tau u_i + (1 - \tau)v_i]$, $0 < \tau < 1$. Feature selection adds a budget $\sum_j z_j \le t$ with $z_j \ge |w_j|$. Why must the features be standardized first?
  1. To make the LP feasible for every budget $t$
  2. Otherwise the budget adds coefficients in different units
  3. To make all the coefficients $w_j$ positive
  4. Because Gurobi requires standardized data
Explanation. Without standardization the selection would depend on the units of measurement; after it each coefficient reads as pieces per standard deviation of the feature.
orl-f-154 · level facile · notes
Quantile regression LP: observations $y_i$ with features $x_{ij}$, coefficients $w_j$ and intercept $b$ free, deviations above $u_i \ge 0$ and below $v_i \ge 0$ with $\sum_j w_jx_{ij} + b + u_i - v_i = y_i$; minimize $\sum_i[\tau u_i + (1 - \tau)v_i]$, $0 < \tau < 1$. With the budget $\sum_j |w_j| \le t$ on standardized coefficients, the shadow price of the budget goes from $-19.95$ to $-4.20$ pieces per unit as $t$ grows. How is it read?

  2. As the coefficient of temperature in the fitted model
  3. As the number of selected features
  4. As the error made on the strike days
Explanation. The first units of budget buy temperature, which explains almost everything; the budget to choose is the one beyond which the shadow price becomes negligible.
orl-f-155 · level facile · notes
A bakery orders the critical-fractile quantile of demand (newsvendor rule). For a hot weekend day the quantile of the whole history says $148$ pieces, the quantile regression conditioned on the day says $132$. What is the gain?
  1. None: the two numbers are equivalent

  3. About 16 fewer unsold pieces, with the same costs and rule
  4. A higher critical fractile
Explanation. The quantile rule still holds, applied to the demand conditional on the day, using information already in hand; the gap between median and critical fractile is the safety stock.
orl-f-156 · level teorema · notes
Quantile regression LP: observations $y_i$ with features $x_{ij}$, coefficients $w_j$ and intercept $b$ free, deviations above $u_i \ge 0$ and below $v_i \ge 0$ with $\sum_j w_jx_{ij} + b + u_i - v_i = y_i$; minimize $\sum_i[\tau u_i + (1 - \tau)v_i]$, $0 < \tau < 1$. The dual constraint of the free intercept $b$ is $\sum_i\tilde\pi_i = 0$. What does it express?
  1. That the deviations sum to zero
  2. The quantile property: about a fraction $\tau$ of the data below the estimate
  3. That the estimate passes through the mean of the data
  4. That all the duals are zero
Explanation. With $\tilde\pi_i = \tau$ above, $-(1 - \tau)$ below and interior values on the $p + 1$ support points: $\tau n_+ - (1 - \tau)n_- + \sum_{\text{interp.}}\tilde\pi_i = 0$; with $\tau = 1/2$ half above and half below.
orl-f-157 · level ipotesi · notes
Quantile regression LP: observations $y_i$ with features $x_{ij}$, coefficients $w_j$ and intercept $b$ free, deviations above $u_i \ge 0$ and below $v_i \ge 0$ with $\sum_j w_jx_{ij} + b + u_i - v_i = y_i$; minimize $\sum_i[\tau u_i + (1 - \tau)v_i]$, $0 < \tau < 1$. Why is at least one of $\tilde u_i$, $\tilde v_i$ zero at the optimum?
  1. Because $u_i$ and $v_i$ are binary variables
  2. Because the intercept $b$ is free
  3. Because the features are standardized
  4. $\tau, 1 - \tau > 0$: lowering both by the same amount keeps the constraint, lowers the cost
Explanation. Same positive-part reasoning as in the newsvendor: the split of the deviation into $u_i - v_i$ is tight at the optimum.
orl-f-235 · level facile · notes
Bakery history with five strike days. On the 55 normal days the least-absolute-deviation (LP) line has mean error $13.08$ pieces, the least-squares (QP) line $14.81$; the QP refitted *without* the strikes has intercept $213.58$, from which the LP intercept differs by $3.81$ and the QP one by $11.24$. What is the lesson?
  1. The QP is better because it uses all the data
  2. Both methods need the strikes removed by hand
  3. The strikes do not affect either estimate
  4. The LP finds by itself the line the QP finds only after cleaning
Explanation. Paying each deviation once makes the LP robust to outliers: no one has to recognize and remove the strike days first.
orl-f-236 · level facile · notes
Quantile regression for a bakery: for a hot weekend day the median model ($\tau = 0.5$) predicts $126.3$ pieces and the model at the critical fractile $\tau = 9/13$ predicts $132.1$. What is the safety stock, and how is it obtained?
  1. $5.8$ pieces: the quantile prediction minus the median one
  2. $132.1$ pieces: the whole quantile prediction
  3. $16$ pieces: the gap with the unconditional quantile
  4. $0$: no distribution was assumed
Explanation. The safety stock is computed directly as the gap between the two conditional estimates, with no assumption on the distribution of demand.
orl-f-237 · level facile · notes
Quantile regression LP: observations $y_i$ with features $x_{ij}$, coefficients $w_j$ and intercept $b$ free, deviations above $u_i \ge 0$ and below $v_i \ge 0$ with $\sum_j w_jx_{ij} + b + u_i - v_i = y_i$; minimize $\sum_i[\tau u_i + (1 - \tau)v_i]$, $0 < \tau < 1$. With no features ($p = 0$) only the intercept $b$ is left. What does the LP return as $\tilde b$?
  1. The mean of the observations
  2. An empirical $\tau$-quantile of the observations
  3. The median, whatever $\tau$
  4. The largest observation
Explanation. Raising $b$ pays while the share of observations below it is at most $\tau$: the model *is* the quantile rule; with features it gives a conditional quantile.
orl-f-321 · level facile · notes
The lab chapter on robust and quantile regression starts from the parameters used by the other models (demand curves, costs, scenarios). What is its point?
  1. Those parameters are given and need no discussion
  2. Estimating them is itself an optimization problem
  3. They are always estimated by least squares
  4. They can be estimated only with a machine-learning library
Explanation. Someone must estimate the numbers that feed the models, and the estimate is an optimization problem: with absolute deviations it stays an LP, solved with Gurobi as everything else.
orl-f-322 · level facile · notes
In the lab, a demand forecast is estimated by minimizing the sum of absolute deviations instead of squared ones. Which two advantages do the notes give?
  1. A closed-form solution and a unique optimum
  2. Smaller errors on every observation and faster solves
  3. No need for data and no need for a solver
  4. It stays an LP and anomalous days weigh less
Explanation. Absolute deviations keep the problem linear (two nonnegative parts per deviation) and pay each deviation once, so a few anomalous days cannot pull the line; weighting the two parts asymmetrically aims at a quantile.
orl-f-323 · level facile · notes
Quantile regression weighs deviations above and below the forecast asymmetrically, aiming at a quantile of demand instead of its center. Which lab model needs exactly that number?
  1. The newsvendor, which orders the critical fractile of demand
  2. The Markowitz portfolio, which needs the mean return
  3. The pricing model, which needs the demand elasticity
  4. The M/M/1 queue, which needs the arrival rate
Explanation. The newsvendor orders the quantile $\tau = c_u/(c_u + c_o)$ of demand: quantile regression estimates it conditionally on the features of the day.
orl-f-324 · level facile · notes
The lab uses optimization both in the SVM chapter and in the regression chapter. What is the difference between the two tasks?
  1. The SVM is an LP, while regression is a QP
  2. Both classify observations into two labels
  3. The SVM classifies, regression predicts a quantity
  4. Both predict a continuous quantity
Explanation. The SVM assigns one of two labels (a QP); quantile regression predicts a quantity such as demand, and with absolute deviations it is an LP.
orl-f-325 · level facile · notes
Quantile regression LP: observations $y_i$ ($i = 1, \dots, n$) with features $x_{ij}$ ($j = 1, \dots, p$), free coefficients $w_j$ and intercept $b$, deviations above $u_i \ge 0$ and below $v_i \ge 0$ with $\sum_j w_jx_{ij} + b + u_i - v_i = y_i$; minimize $\sum_i[\tau u_i + (1 - \tau)v_i]$, $0 < \tau < 1$. How many variables and equality constraints does the LP have?
  1. $p$ variables and $n + 1$ constraints
  2. $n + p$ variables and $2n$ constraints
  3. $2n + p + 1$ variables and $n$ constraints
  4. $2n$ variables and $p + 1$ constraints
Explanation. Free variables: the $p$ coefficients and the intercept; nonnegative: $u_i$ and $v_i$ for each observation; one equality constraint per observation defines its deviation.

No features ($p = 0$): seven days of sales $88, 96, 104, 112, 120, 132, 148$ and $\tau = 9/13$ (bakery with $c_u = 9$, $c_o = 4$). What are the optimal intercept $\tilde b$ and the optimal value?
  1. $\tilde b = 120$, value $680/13 \approx 52.31$
  2. $\tilde b = 112$, value $768/13 \approx 59.08$
  3. $\tilde b = 132$, value $704/13 \approx 54.15$
  4. $\tilde b = 114.29$ (the mean), value $\approx 57.14$
Explanation. Raising $b$ saves $\tau$ on each observation above and costs $1 - \tau$ on each one below: it pays while the share below is at most $\tau$. $\tilde b = 120$ (4 below, 2 above, $4/7 \le \tau \le 5/7$); value $\tfrac4{13}(32 + 24 + 16 + 8) + \tfrac9{13}(12 + 28) = 680/13$.

Quantile regression LP: observations $y_i$ ($i = 1, \dots, n$) with features $x_{ij}$ ($j = 1, \dots, p$), free coefficients $w_j$ and intercept $b$, deviations above $u_i \ge 0$ and below $v_i \ge 0$ with $\sum_j w_jx_{ij} + b + u_i - v_i = y_i$; minimize $\sum_i[\tau u_i + (1 - \tau)v_i]$, $0 < \tau < 1$. The model requires $0 < \tau < 1$. What goes wrong with $\tau = 1$ (deviations below the forecast cost nothing)?
  1. The LP becomes infeasible for every data set
  2. The LP becomes a QP with a quadratic term
  3. Nothing: it returns the largest quantile
  4. Any forecast above all the data is optimal
Explanation. With $\tau = 1$ only $u_i$ (data above the forecast) is paid: raising the forecast above every observation makes all $u_i = 0$ at no cost, so the optimum is any such forecast. Both weights must be positive for the absolute-value trick and the quantile property to work.
orl-f-328 · level facile · notes
Bakery history (60 days, 5 strike days with about 20 loaves sold): the least-squares line is pulled down by the strikes, the least-absolute-deviations line is not. Why does one anomalous datum in ten move least squares so much?
  1. Because least squares has more variables
  2. Because the LP removes the outliers before estimating
  3. Because the QP is solved with a looser tolerance
  4. Because it pays each deviation squared
Explanation. The squared objective lets a large deviation dominate; the LP pays each deviation once, so the line stays where the normal data are, without anyone cleaning the history by hand.

In the bakery case study, least squares is written as a QP to compare it with the LP of absolute deviations. How?
  1. $\min\sum_i(u_i + v_i)^2$ with $u_i, v_i \ge 0$ and no constraints
  2. $\min\sum_ir_i^2$, $r_i$ free, same equalities as the LP

  4. $\max\sum_ir_i^2$ with $r_i$ free and no constraints
Explanation. One free residual per observation, defined by the same equality as in the LP, and a quadratic objective: a convex QP solved with the same solver.

With the budget constraint $\sum_jz_j \le t$ ($z_j \ge |w_j|$), the optimal mean absolute deviation as a function of $t$ is decreasing and convex. Which result explains the convexity?

  3. The quantile property of the intercept
  4. Strong duality makes every value function linear
Explanation. For a minimization LP, $\tilde z(t)$ is the maximum of the dual objective over a fixed dual feasible set, linear in $t$: a maximum of linear functions is convex. The slope is the shadow price, which rises from $-19.95$ to $-4.20$.

2. Background: LP, QP, NLP and sensitivity analysis (8)

Example 1 · orl-g-lp-duali-cs · level facile · notes
Consider the LP $\max\; 43x_1 + 73x_2$ subject to $x_1 + 4x_2 \le 47$, $3x_1 + 3x_2 \le 96$, $x_1, x_2 \ge 0$; its dual has one variable $\pi_i \ge 0$ per constraint $i$ (the shadow price of the constraint). The optimal solution is $(\tilde x_1, \tilde x_2) = (27, 5)$, with both constraints active. What are the optimal dual values $(\tilde\pi_1, \tilde\pi_2)$?
  1. $(\tilde\pi_1; \tilde\pi_2) = (18.11; 6.22)$
  2. $(\tilde\pi_1; \tilde\pi_2) = (11; 10)$
  3. $(\tilde\pi_1; \tilde\pi_2) = (10; 11)$
  4. $(\tilde\pi_1; \tilde\pi_2) = (10.75; 10.43)$
Explanation. Both variables are positive, so by complementary slackness both dual constraints are active: $1\pi_1 + 3\pi_2 = 43$ and $4\pi_1 + 3\pi_2 = 73$ (the coefficients are the columns of the primal), hence $\tilde\pi = (10; 11)$. Solving with the rows instead of the columns is the typical mistake.

The optimal solution is $(\tilde x_1, \tilde x_2) = (25, 22)$, with both constraints active. What are the optimal dual values $(\tilde\pi_1, \tilde\pi_2)$?
  1. $(\tilde\pi_1; \tilde\pi_2) = (5; 11)$
  2. $(\tilde\pi_1; \tilde\pi_2) = (13.17; 5.67)$
  3. $(\tilde\pi_1; \tilde\pi_2) = (11; 5)$
  4. $(\tilde\pi_1; \tilde\pi_2) = (8; 9.8)$
Explanation. Both variables are positive, so by complementary slackness both dual constraints are active: $2\pi_1 + 2\pi_2 = 32$ and $1\pi_1 + 4\pi_2 = 49$ (the coefficients are the columns of the primal), hence $\tilde\pi = (5; 11)$. Solving with the rows instead of the columns is the typical mistake.
Example 1 · orl-g-lp-costo-ridotto · level facile · notes
Consider the LP $\max\; 43x_1 + 73x_2$ subject to $x_1 + 4x_2 \le 47$, $3x_1 + 3x_2 \le 96$, $x_1, x_2 \ge 0$; its dual has one variable $\pi_i \ge 0$ per constraint $i$ (the shadow price of the constraint). At the optimum the duals are $(\tilde\pi_1, \tilde\pi_2) = (10, 11)$. We add a variable $x_3 \ge 0$ with objective coefficient $c_3 = 49$ and consumptions $a_{13} = 4$, $a_{23} = 1$. What are its reduced cost $\bar c_3$ ($c_3$ minus the value of the resources it consumes at the shadow prices) and the threshold $c_3^{\max}$, the smallest $c_3$ at which $x_3$ becomes worth using?
  1. $\bar c_3 = 2$, threshold $c_3^{\max} = 51$
  2. $\bar c_3 = -5$, threshold $c_3^{\max} = 54$
  3. $\bar c_3 = 9$, threshold $c_3^{\max} = 40$
  4. $\bar c_3 = -2$, threshold $c_3^{\max} = 51$
Explanation. The resources absorbed by one unit of $x_3$ are worth $4\cdot 10 + 1\cdot 11 = 51$ at the shadow prices, so $\bar c_3 = 49 - 51 = -2$: $x_3$ stays at zero and becomes worth activating only if $c_3$ rises to at least $51$.

Example 1 · orl-g-lp-intervallo-b · level media · notes
Consider the LP $\max\; 43x_1 + 73x_2$ subject to $x_1 + 4x_2 \le 47$, $3x_1 + 3x_2 \le 96$, $x_1, x_2 \ge 0$; its dual has one variable $\pi_i \ge 0$ per constraint $i$ (the shadow price of the constraint). At the optimum both constraints are active and $\tilde x = (27, 5)$, with $\tilde x_1, \tilde x_2 > 0$. Keeping $b_2 = 96$ fixed, for which values of $b_1$ does this basis stay optimal (the validity range of the shadow price $\tilde\pi_1$)?
  1. $b_1 \in [24; 96]$
  2. $b_1 \in [35.25; 141]$
  3. $b_1 \in [32; 128]$
  4. $b_1 \in [32; 47]$
Explanation. With both constraints active, $x(b_1)$ solves $A x = (b_1, 96)$: $x_1 = (3b_1 - 384)/(-9)$ and $x_2 = (96 - 3b_1)/(-9)$. The basis stays optimal (the duals do not change) as long as both are $\ge 0$, that is for $b_1 \in [32, 128]$; Gurobi returns it as `SARHSLow`–`SARHSUp`.

  4. $b_1 \in [34.5; 72]$

Example 1 · orl-g-lp-dualita-debole · level facile · notes
Consider the LP $\max\; 43x_1 + 73x_2$ subject to $x_1 + 4x_2 \le 47$, $3x_1 + 3x_2 \le 96$, $x_1, x_2 \ge 0$; its dual has one variable $\pi_i \ge 0$ per constraint $i$ (the shadow price of the constraint). The point $\bar x = (23, 4)$ is feasible for the primal and $\bar\pi = (11, 11)$ is feasible for the dual $\min\ 47\pi_1 + 96\pi_2$ (dual constraints $\pi_1 + 3\pi_2 \ge 43$, $4\pi_1 + 3\pi_2 \ge 73$). By weak duality, which interval certainly contains the optimal value $\tilde z$ of the primal?
  1. $\tilde z \in [989; 1281]$
  2. $\tilde z \in [1573; 1865]$
  3. $\tilde z \in [1281; 1427]$
  4. $\tilde z \in [1281; 1573]$
Explanation. Every feasible primal point gives a lower bound for a maximum, $43\cdot 23 + 73\cdot 4 = 1281$, and every feasible dual point an upper bound, $47\cdot 11 + 96\cdot 11 = 1573$. So $1281 \le \tilde z \le 1573$ (here $\tilde z = 1526$). Swapping the roles of the two bounds is the typical mistake.

  3. $\tilde z = 1636$
  4. $\tilde z = 1626$
Explanation. The new value $57$ is inside the validity range, so the shadow price is exact: $\tilde z + \tilde\pi_1\,\Delta b_1 = 1526 + 10\cdot(10) = 1626$.
Example 2 · orl-g-lp-perturbazione · level facile · notes
Consider the LP $\max\; 32x_1 + 49x_2$ subject to $2x_1 + x_2 \le 72$, $2x_1 + 4x_2 \le 138$, $x_1, x_2 \ge 0$; its dual has one variable $\pi_i \ge 0$ per constraint $i$ (the shadow price of the constraint). At the optimum $\tilde z = 1878$, the shadow price of constraint 1 is $\tilde\pi_1 = 5$ (and $\tilde\pi_2 = 11$), and the validity range of $b_1$ (where $\tilde\pi_1$ stays exact) is $[34.5; 138]$. What is the new optimal value if $b_1$ changes from $72$ to $83$, all else equal?
  1. $\tilde z = 1933$
  2. $\tilde z = 1883$
  3. $\tilde z = 1823$
  4. $\tilde z = 1999$
Explanation. The new value $83$ is inside the validity range, so the shadow price is exact: $\tilde z + \tilde\pi_1\,\Delta b_1 = 1878 + 5\cdot(11) = 1933$.
Example 1 · orl-g-qp-kkt · level facile · notes
Convex QP: $\min\ 6x_1^2 + x_2^2$ subject to $x_1 + x_2 \ge 20$, $x_1, x_2 \ge 0$. At the optimum the constraint is active, the bounds are not, and stationarity of the Lagrangian $L = f + \lambda(20 - x_1 - x_2)$ holds. What are $\tilde x_1$ and the multiplier $\lambda$?
  1. $\tilde x_1 = 2.86$, $\lambda = 34.29$
  2. $\tilde x_1 = 10$, $\lambda = 120$
  3. $\tilde x_1 = 17.14$, $\lambda = 34.29$
  4. $\tilde x_1 = 2.86$, $\lambda = 17.14$
Explanation. Stationarity: $12x_1 = \lambda$ and $2x_2 = \lambda$, so $x_1 = \lambda/12$, $x_2 = \lambda/2$; the active constraint gives $\lambda = 34.29$, hence $\tilde x_1 = 2.86$, $\tilde x_2 = 17.14$. The variable with the smaller quadratic coefficient takes the larger share; $\lambda$ is the marginal cost of one more unit of $d$.
Example 2 · orl-g-qp-kkt · level facile · notes
Convex QP: $\min\ 3x_1^2 + 6x_2^2$ subject to $x_1 + x_2 \ge 9$, $x_1, x_2 \ge 0$. At the optimum the constraint is active, the bounds are not, and stationarity of the Lagrangian $L = f + \lambda(9 - x_1 - x_2)$ holds. What are $\tilde x_1$ and the multiplier $\lambda$?
  1. $\tilde x_1 = 4.5$, $\lambda = 27$
  2. $\tilde x_1 = 6$, $\lambda = 18$
  3. $\tilde x_1 = 3$, $\lambda = 36$
  4. $\tilde x_1 = 6$, $\lambda = 36$
Explanation. Stationarity: $6x_1 = \lambda$ and $12x_2 = \lambda$, so $x_1 = \lambda/6$, $x_2 = \lambda/12$; the active constraint gives $\lambda = 36$, hence $\tilde x_1 = 6$, $\tilde x_2 = 3$. The variable with the smaller quadratic coefficient takes the larger share; $\lambda$ is the marginal cost of one more unit of $d$.
Example 1 · orl-g-lp-tutti-casi · level complessa · notes
Consider the LP $\min\ -4x_1 + 12x_2 - 12x_3$, subject to (1) $x_1 + x_2 \ge 39$, (2) $x_1 + x_2 - x_3 = 64$, (3) $3x_1 - x_2 \le 92$, $x_1 \ge 0$, $x_2$ free, $x_3 \le 0$. Its optimal solution is $\tilde x = (39, 25, 0)$. Let $\tilde\pi_i = \partial\tilde z/\partial b_i$ be the dual value of constraint $i$ (in a minimum: $\pi_i \ge 0$ for $\ge$, free for $=$, $\le 0$ for $\le$). Using complementary slackness, what are the optimal dual values?
  1. $(\tilde\pi_1; \tilde\pi_2; \tilde\pi_3) = (0; 12; -5.33)$
  2. $(\tilde\pi_1; \tilde\pi_2; \tilde\pi_3) = (0; 2; -6)$
  3. $(\tilde\pi_1; \tilde\pi_2; \tilde\pi_3) = (0; 8; -4)$
  4. $(\tilde\pi_1; \tilde\pi_2; \tilde\pi_3) = (0; -4; 8)$
Explanation. Constraint (1) is slack ($64 > 39$), so $\tilde\pi_1 = 0$. $x_1 > 0$ and $x_2 \ne 0$ make their dual constraints active (columns of the primal): $\pi_2 + 3\pi_3 = -4$ and $\pi_2 - \pi_3 = 12$, so $\tilde\pi_2 = 8$, $\tilde\pi_3 = -4 \le 0$ as required. The dual constraint of $x_3$ need not be active: $x_3$ sits at its bound $0$ with reduced cost $-12 + 8 = -4$. Check: $\sum_i b_i\tilde\pi_i = 144 = \tilde z$.
Example 2 · orl-g-lp-tutti-casi · level complessa · notes
Consider the LP $\min\ 4x_1 + 16x_2 - 12x_3$, subject to (1) $x_1 + x_2 \ge 51$, (2) $x_1 + x_2 - x_3 = 64$, (3) $x_1 - 2x_2 \le -41$, $x_1 \ge 0$, $x_2$ free, $x_3 \le 0$. Its optimal solution is $\tilde x = (29, 35, 0)$. Let $\tilde\pi_i = \partial\tilde z/\partial b_i$ be the dual value of constraint $i$ (in a minimum: $\pi_i \ge 0$ for $\ge$, free for $=$, $\le 0$ for $\le$). Using complementary slackness, what are the optimal dual values?
  1. $(\tilde\pi_1; \tilde\pi_2; \tilde\pi_3) = (0; 8; -4)$
  2. $(\tilde\pi_1; \tilde\pi_2; \tilde\pi_3) = (0; 12; -8)$
  3. $(\tilde\pi_1; \tilde\pi_2; \tilde\pi_3) = (0; -4; 8)$
  4. $(\tilde\pi_1; \tilde\pi_2; \tilde\pi_3) = (0; 8; 4)$
Explanation. Constraint (1) is slack ($64 > 51$), so $\tilde\pi_1 = 0$. $x_1 > 0$ and $x_2 \ne 0$ make their dual constraints active (columns of the primal): $\pi_2 + \pi_3 = 4$ and $\pi_2 - 2\pi_3 = 16$, so $\tilde\pi_2 = 8$, $\tilde\pi_3 = -4 \le 0$ as required. The dual constraint of $x_3$ need not be active: $x_3$ sits at its bound $0$ with reduced cost $-12 + 8 = -4$. Check: $\sum_i b_i\tilde\pi_i = 676 = \tilde z$.
Example 1 · orl-g-kkt-proiezione · level media · notes
Convex QP: $\min\ (x - 4)^2 + (y - 4)^2$ subject to $x + y \le 7$, $x, y \ge 0$; $\lambda \ge 0$ is the KKT multiplier of $x + y \le 7$ (Lagrangian $L = f + \lambda(x + y - 7)$). What are the optimum and $\lambda$?
  1. $(4; 4)$, $\lambda = 0$
  2. $(3.5; 3.5)$, $\lambda = 0.5$
  3. $(3.5; 3.5)$, $\lambda = 1$
  4. $(3.5; 3.5)$, $\lambda = 2$
Explanation. The free minimum $(4, 4)$ violates $x + y \le 7$, so the constraint is active. Stationarity: $2(x - 4) + \lambda = 0$, $2(y - 4) + \lambda = 0$, so $x = 4 - \lambda/2$, $y = 4 - \lambda/2$; with $x + y = 7$: $\lambda = 1$, optimum $(3.5; 3.5)$, both positive (bounds inactive). Convex problem: the KKT point is the global optimum.
Example 2 · orl-g-kkt-proiezione · level media · notes
Convex QP: $\min\ (x - 3)^2 + (y - 6)^2$ subject to $x + y \le 4$, $x, y \ge 0$; $\lambda \ge 0$ is the KKT multiplier of $x + y \le 4$ (Lagrangian $L = f + \lambda(x + y - 4)$). What are the optimum and $\lambda$?
  1. $(3.5; 0.5)$, $\lambda = 5$
  2. $(3; 6)$, $\lambda = 0$
  3. $(0.5; 3.5)$, $\lambda = 2.5$
  4. $(0.5; 3.5)$, $\lambda = 5$
Explanation. The free minimum $(3, 6)$ violates $x + y \le 4$, so the constraint is active. Stationarity: $2(x - 3) + \lambda = 0$, $2(y - 6) + \lambda = 0$, so $x = 3 - \lambda/2$, $y = 6 - \lambda/2$; with $x + y = 4$: $\lambda = 5$, optimum $(0.5; 3.5)$, both positive (bounds inactive). Convex problem: the KKT point is the global optimum.

3. The solver: building, solving, interpreting (5)

Example 1 · orl-g-sv-dimensioni · level facile · notes
A gurobipy production-inventory LP has $n = 4$ products and $m = 8$ months: variables $x_{it} \ge 0$ (units produced) and $s_{it} \ge 0$ (inventory at the end of the month) for every product $i$ and month $t$; one balance constraint $s_{i,t-1} + x_{it} = d_{it} + s_{it}$ per product and month (initial inventory $s_{i0}$ is a datum); one machine-hours constraint $\sum_i a_ix_{it} \le b_t$ per month. What does the line `Optimize a model with ... rows, ... columns` of the Gurobi log report?
  1. $64$ rows, $40$ columns
  2. $40$ rows, $64$ columns
  3. $32$ rows, $64$ columns
  4. $40$ rows, $32$ columns
Explanation. Rows are constraints: $nm = 32$ balances plus $m = 8$ capacities, $40$ in all; columns are variables: $2nm = 64$. Checking these sizes is the first reading of the log.
Example 2 · orl-g-sv-dimensioni · level facile · notes
A gurobipy production-inventory LP has $n = 3$ products and $m = 11$ months: variables $x_{it} \ge 0$ (units produced) and $s_{it} \ge 0$ (inventory at the end of the month) for every product $i$ and month $t$; one balance constraint $s_{i,t-1} + x_{it} = d_{it} + s_{it}$ per product and month (initial inventory $s_{i0}$ is a datum); one machine-hours constraint $\sum_i a_ix_{it} \le b_t$ per month. What does the line `Optimize a model with ... rows, ... columns` of the Gurobi log report?
  1. $44$ rows, $33$ columns
  2. $33$ rows, $66$ columns
  3. $44$ rows, $66$ columns
  4. $66$ rows, $44$ columns
Explanation. Rows are constraints: $nm = 33$ balances plus $m = 11$ capacities, $44$ in all; columns are variables: $2nm = 66$. Checking these sizes is the first reading of the log.
Example 1 · orl-g-sv-rc-soglia · level facile · notes
gurobipy, minimization LP: $\min\ 16x_1 + 17x_2$ subject to $2x_1 + 2x_2 \ge 110$, $x_1, x_2 \ge 0$. At the optimum $x_2 = 0$. What are its reduced cost (attribute `x2.RC`, $c_2$ minus the value of the resources it uses at the shadow prices) and the cost threshold `SAObjLow` below which $x_2$ becomes worth using?
  1. `RC` $= 1$, threshold $16$
  2. `RC` $= 1$, threshold $18$
  3. `RC` $= -1$, threshold $16$
  4. `RC` $= 9$, threshold $8$
Explanation. Only $x_1$ is used, so the shadow price of the constraint is $c_1/a_1 = 8$; $x_2$ uses $2$ units, worth $16$: `RC` $= 17 - 16 = 1 \ge 0$ (a variable at its lower bound in a minimum). It becomes worth using when its cost falls to $16$.
Example 2 · orl-g-sv-rc-soglia · level facile · notes
gurobipy, minimization LP: $\min\ 8x_1 + 16x_2$ subject to $2x_1 + 3x_2 \ge 30$, $x_1, x_2 \ge 0$. At the optimum $x_2 = 0$. What are its reduced cost (attribute `x2.RC`, $c_2$ minus the value of the resources it uses at the shadow prices) and the cost threshold `SAObjLow` below which $x_2$ becomes worth using?
  1. `RC` $= 4$, threshold $12$
  2. `RC` $= -4$, threshold $12$
  3. `RC` $= 4$, threshold $20$
  4. `RC` $= 12$, threshold $4$
Explanation. Only $x_1$ is used, so the shadow price of the constraint is $c_1/a_1 = 4$; $x_2$ uses $3$ units, worth $12$: `RC` $= 16 - 12 = 4 \ge 0$ (a variable at its lower bound in a minimum). It becomes worth using when its cost falls to $12$.
Example 1 · orl-g-sv-pi-capacita · level facile · notes
gurobipy, minimization LP: $\min\ 5x_1 + 11x_2$ subject to demand $x_1 + x_2 \ge 130$ and capacity of the cheap source $x_1 \le 70$, $x \ge 0$. Output: optimal cost $1010$; demand constraint `Pi` $= 11$; capacity constraint `Pi` $= -6$, `SARHS` $= [0, 130]$. What is the optimal cost if the capacity becomes $71$?
  1. $\tilde z = 1004$
  2. $\tilde z = 1015$
  3. $\tilde z = 1016$
  4. $\tilde z = 1021$
Explanation. `Pi` $= \partial\tilde z/\partial b$: a $\le$ capacity in a minimum has `Pi` $\le 0$, each unit saves $6$ (the cheap source replaces the expensive one). The new value is inside `SARHS`, so $1010 + (-6)\cdot(1) = 1004$.
Example 2 · orl-g-sv-pi-capacita · level facile · notes
gurobipy, minimization LP: $\min\ 4x_1 + 12x_2$ subject to demand $x_1 + x_2 \ge 50$ and capacity of the cheap source $x_1 \le 40$, $x \ge 0$. Output: optimal cost $280$; demand constraint `Pi` $= 12$; capacity constraint `Pi` $= -8$, `SARHS` $= [0, 50]$. What is the optimal cost if the capacity becomes $44$?
  1. $\tilde z = 248$
  2. $\tilde z = 328$
  3. $\tilde z = 312$
  4. $\tilde z = 296$
Explanation. `Pi` $= \partial\tilde z/\partial b$: a $\le$ capacity in a minimum has `Pi` $\le 0$, each unit saves $8$ (the cheap source replaces the expensive one). The new value is inside `SARHS`, so $280 + (-8)\cdot(4) = 248$.
Example 1 · orl-g-sv-rhs-costante · level facile · notes
In gurobipy the meter constraint of an EV-charging model is written `constr = m.addConstr(quicksum(x[v,t] for v in V) + base[t] <= C)`, with the constant `base[t] = 39` (base load, kW) and `C = 109` (meter limit). What value does Gurobi store in `constr.RHS`, and which value must be assigned to `constr.RHS` to study a meter limit of $134$ kW?
  1. `constr.RHS` $= 148$, to set $173$
  2. `constr.RHS` $= 109$, to set $134$
  3. `constr.RHS` $= 70$, to set $134$
  4. `constr.RHS` $= 70$, to set $95$
Explanation. Gurobi moves the constant to the right-hand side: `RHS` $= 109 - 39 = 70$. Writing `constr.RHS = 134` would impose $\sum_v x_{vt} \le 134$, another constraint; the correct assignment is $134 - 39 = 95$.
Example 2 · orl-g-sv-rhs-costante · level facile · notes
In gurobipy the meter constraint of an EV-charging model is written `constr = m.addConstr(quicksum(x[v,t] for v in V) + base[t] <= C)`, with the constant `base[t] = 29` (base load, kW) and `C = 119` (meter limit). What value does Gurobi store in `constr.RHS`, and which value must be assigned to `constr.RHS` to study a meter limit of $109$ kW?
  1. `constr.RHS` $= 90$, to set $80$
  2. `constr.RHS` $= 148$, to set $138$
  3. `constr.RHS` $= 90$, to set $109$
  4. `constr.RHS` $= 119$, to set $109$
Explanation. Gurobi moves the constant to the right-hand side: `RHS` $= 119 - 29 = 90$. Writing `constr.RHS = 109` would impose $\sum_v x_{vt} \le 109$, another constraint; the correct assignment is $109 - 29 = 80$.
Example 1 · orl-g-sv-perturbazione-nl · level facile · notes
A gurobipy model maximizes $20\log(1 + 1x)$ subject to $0 \le x \le b$, with the logarithm written through `addGenConstrLog`, so the solver returns no dual for the bound $x \le b$. Solving with $b = 5$ gives $35.8352$; with $b = 5.1$ it gives $36.1658$. What is the perturbation estimate of the multiplier of $x \le b$?
  1. $\approx 33.06$
  2. $\approx 7.17$
  3. $\approx 3.31$
  4. $\approx 0.33$
Explanation. Incremental ratio: $(36.1658 - 35.8352)/0.1 = 3.31$, close to the exact derivative $20\cdot 1/(1 + 1\cdot 5) = 3.33$ (the difference is the curvature). Forgetting to divide by $0.1$ is the typical slip.
Example 2 · orl-g-sv-perturbazione-nl · level facile · notes
A gurobipy model maximizes $50\log(1 + 1x)$ subject to $0 \le x \le b$, with the logarithm written through `addGenConstrLog`, so the solver returns no dual for the bound $x \le b$. Solving with $b = 7$ gives $103.9721$; with $b = 7.1$ it gives $104.5932$. What is the perturbation estimate of the multiplier of $x \le b$?
  1. $\approx 62.11$
  2. $\approx 14.85$
  3. $\approx 0.62$
  4. $\approx 6.21$
Explanation. Incremental ratio: $(104.5932 - 103.9721)/0.1 = 6.21$, close to the exact derivative $50\cdot 1/(1 + 1\cdot 7) = 6.25$ (the difference is the curvature). Forgetting to divide by $0.1$ is the typical slip.

4. Multi-period production and inventory (3)

Example 1 · orl-g-prod-2mesi-costo · level facile · notes
Production-inventory LP, one product, two months: $x_t$ = units produced and $s_t$ = inventory at the end of month $t$, balance $s_{t-1} + x_t = d_t + s_t$ ($s_0 = 0$), capacity $x_t \le b_t$; minimize $\sum_t (c\,x_t + h\,s_t)$. Data: demand $d_1 = 55$, $d_2 = 100$; $b_1 = b_2 = 80$ units; $c = 14$ €/unit, holding cost $h = 1$ €/unit/month. What is the optimal cost of the plan?
  1. 2190 €
  2. 2270 €
  3. 2170 €
  4. 2210 €
Explanation. Month 2 produces at most $80$, so $\tilde s_1 = d_2 - b_2 = 20$ units are produced in month 1 and held for one month: $14\cdot 155 + 1\cdot 20 = 2190$ €.
Example 2 · orl-g-prod-2mesi-costo · level facile · notes
Production-inventory LP, one product, two months: $x_t$ = units produced and $s_t$ = inventory at the end of month $t$, balance $s_{t-1} + x_t = d_t + s_t$ ($s_0 = 0$), capacity $x_t \le b_t$; minimize $\sum_t (c\,x_t + h\,s_t)$. Data: demand $d_1 = 25$, $d_2 = 100$; $b_1 = b_2 = 70$ units; $c = 6$ €/unit, holding cost $h = 2$ €/unit/month. What is the optimal cost of the plan?
  1. 810 €
  2. 750 €
  3. 950 €
  4. 870 €
Explanation. Month 2 produces at most $70$, so $\tilde s_1 = d_2 - b_2 = 30$ units are produced in month 1 and held for one month: $6\cdot 125 + 2\cdot 30 = 810$ €.
Example 1 · orl-g-prod-2mesi-duali · level facile · notes
Production-inventory LP, one product, two months: $x_t$ = units produced and $s_t$ = inventory at the end of month $t$, balance $s_{t-1} + x_t = d_t + s_t$ ($s_0 = 0$), capacity $x_t \le b_t$; minimize $\sum_t (c\,x_t + h\,s_t)$. Data: demand $d_1 = 50$, $d_2 = 100$; $b_1 = b_2 = 80$ units; $c = 14$ €/unit, holding cost $h = 1$ €/unit/month. What are the shadow prices $\tilde\pi_t = \partial\tilde z/\partial b_t$ of the two capacity constraints ($\tilde z$ = optimal cost; Gurobi's sign convention)?
  1. $\tilde\pi_1 = 0$, $\tilde\pi_2 = -1$
  2. $\tilde\pi_1 = -1$, $\tilde\pi_2 = 0$
  3. $\tilde\pi_1 = 0$, $\tilde\pi_2 = 1$
  4. $\tilde\pi_1 = 0$, $\tilde\pi_2 = -14$
Explanation. One more unit of capacity in month 2 lets one unit be produced a month later, saving the holding cost: $\tilde\pi_2 = -h = -1$ (a $\le$ constraint in a minimization has $\tilde\pi \le 0$). Month 1 produces $70 < 80$: its constraint is slack and $\tilde\pi_1 = 0$.

Example 1 · orl-g-prod-3mesi · level complessa · notes
Production-inventory LP, one product, three months: $x_t$ = units produced and $s_t$ = inventory at the end of month $t$, balance $s_{t-1} + x_t = d_t + s_t$ ($s_0 = 0$), capacity $x_t \le b_t$; minimize $z = \sum_t (c\,x_t + h\,s_t)$. Data: $(d_1, d_2, d_3) = (60, 55, 120)$, $(b_1, b_2, b_3) = (80, 80, 90)$, $c = 9$ €/unit, $h = 2$ €/unit/month. What are the optimal cost $\tilde z$ and the optimal inventories $\tilde s_1, \tilde s_2$?
  1. $\tilde z = 2245$ €, $\tilde s_1 = 20$, $\tilde s_2 = 45$
  2. $\tilde z = 2175$ €, $\tilde s_1 = 0$, $\tilde s_2 = 30$
  3. $\tilde z = 2185$ €, $\tilde s_1 = 5$, $\tilde s_2 = 30$
  4. $\tilde z = 2175$ €, $\tilde s_1 = 5$, $\tilde s_2 = 30$
Explanation. With the same $c$ every month it pays to produce as late as possible. Month 3 lacks $120 - 90 = 30$ units: $\tilde s_2 = 30$. Month 2 must cover $55 + 30 = 85 > 80$, so $\tilde s_1 = 5$. Cost $9\cdot 235 + 2\cdot(5 + 30) = 2185$ €.
Example 2 · orl-g-prod-3mesi · level complessa · notes

Data: $(d_1, d_2, d_3) = (50, 100, 70)$, $(b_1, b_2, b_3) = (70, 100, 60)$, $c = 11$ €/unit, $h = 2$ €/unit/month.
