  3. $\tilde z = 2500$ €, $\tilde s_1 = 20$, $\tilde s_2 = 20$
  4. $\tilde z = 2440$ €, $\tilde s_1 = 10$, $\tilde s_2 = 10$
Explanation. With the same $c$ every month it pays to produce as late as possible. Month 3 lacks $70 - 60 = 10$ units: $\tilde s_2 = 10$. Month 2 must cover $100 + 10 = 110 > 100$, so $\tilde s_1 = 10$. Cost $11\cdot 220 + 2\cdot(10 + 10) = 2460$ €.

5. Supply chain with congestion and sustainability (2)

Example 1 · orl-g-sc-due-rotte · level facile · notes
Minimum-cost flow LP of a supply chain: a market demands $D = 140$ units, sent on two routes with flows $x_1, x_2 \ge 0$ and demand constraint $x_1 + x_2 \ge D$. Route 1 costs $c_1 = 4$ €/unit with capacity $x_1 \le 110$; route 2 costs $c_2 = 10$ €/unit with unlimited capacity. What are the optimal cost and the shadow price of the demand constraint (cost of serving one more unit)?
  1. cost 740 €, marginal cost 6 €/unit
  2. cost 740 €, marginal cost 4 €/unit
  3. cost 560 €, marginal cost 4 €/unit
  4. cost 740 €, marginal cost 10 €/unit
Explanation. The cheap route is saturated ($x_1 = 110$) and the rest goes on route 2: $4\cdot 110 + 10\cdot 30 = 740$ €. The next unit can only travel on route 2: the shadow price of the demand is $10$.
Example 2 · orl-g-sc-due-rotte · level facile · notes
Minimum-cost flow LP of a supply chain: a market demands $D = 110$ units, sent on two routes with flows $x_1, x_2 \ge 0$ and demand constraint $x_1 + x_2 \ge D$. Route 1 costs $c_1 = 2$ €/unit with capacity $x_1 \le 60$; route 2 costs $c_2 = 3$ €/unit with unlimited capacity. What are the optimal cost and the shadow price of the demand constraint (cost of serving one more unit)?
  1. cost 270 €, marginal cost 3 €/unit
  2. cost 270 €, marginal cost 1 €/unit
  3. cost 220 €, marginal cost 2 €/unit
  4. cost 270 €, marginal cost 2 €/unit
Explanation. The cheap route is saturated ($x_1 = 60$) and the rest goes on route 2: $2\cdot 60 + 3\cdot 50 = 270$ €. The next unit can only travel on route 2: the shadow price of the demand is $3$.
Example 1 · orl-g-sc-congestione · level media · notes
Supply chain with congestion: $D = 235$ units are split between two routes, $x_1 + x_2 = D$, $x_1, x_2 \ge 0$. Route 1 (unit cost $c_1 = 1$, nominal capacity $u_1 = 140$, congestion parameter $\alpha = 2$) costs $c_1x_1 + \alpha c_1x_1^2/u_1$ €; route 2 costs $c_2 = 6$ €/unit with ample capacity. Minimizing the total cost, what is the optimal split $(\tilde x_1; \tilde x_2)$?
  1. $(\tilde x_1; \tilde x_2) = (140; 95)$
  2. $(\tilde x_1; \tilde x_2) = (117.5; 117.5)$
  3. $(\tilde x_1; \tilde x_2) = (235; 0)$
  4. $(\tilde x_1; \tilde x_2) = (175; 60)$
Explanation. At the optimum the marginal costs are equal: $1 + 2\alpha\,1\,x_1/140 = 6$, so $x_1 = 175$ and $x_2 = 60$. The pure LP would saturate route 1; forgetting the factor 2 of the derivative doubles $x_1$.
Example 2 · orl-g-sc-congestione · level media · notes
Supply chain with congestion: $D = 370$ units are split between two routes, $x_1 + x_2 = D$, $x_1, x_2 \ge 0$. Route 1 (unit cost $c_1 = 5$, nominal capacity $u_1 = 200$, congestion parameter $\alpha = 0.5$) costs $c_1x_1 + \alpha c_1x_1^2/u_1$ €; route 2 costs $c_2 = 13$ €/unit with ample capacity. Minimizing the total cost, what is the optimal split $(\tilde x_1; \tilde x_2)$?
  1. $(\tilde x_1; \tilde x_2) = (185; 185)$
  2. $(\tilde x_1; \tilde x_2) = (200; 170)$
  3. $(\tilde x_1; \tilde x_2) = (320; 50)$
  4. $(\tilde x_1; \tilde x_2) = (370; 0)$
Explanation. At the optimum the marginal costs are equal: $5 + 2\alpha\,5\,x_1/200 = 13$, so $x_1 = 320$ and $x_2 = 50$. The pure LP would saturate route 1; forgetting the factor 2 of the derivative doubles $x_1$.

6. The Markowitz portfolio (4)

Example 1 · orl-g-mk-minvar · level facile · notes
Markowitz model with two uncorrelated assets of volatilities (standard deviations of the return) $\sigma_1 = 30\%$ and $\sigma_2 = 40\%$. The shares $x_1, x_2$ of capital, with $x_1 + x_2 = 1$, minimize the portfolio variance (no return constraint). What are the optimal share $\tilde x_1$ and the portfolio volatility $\sigma_p$ (square root of the variance)?
  1. $\tilde x_1 = 50\%$, $\sigma_p = 25\%$
  2. $\tilde x_1 = 57.1\%$, $\sigma_p = 24.2\%$
  3. $\tilde x_1 = 36\%$, $\sigma_p = 27.8\%$
  4. $\tilde x_1 = 64\%$, $\sigma_p = 24\%$
Explanation. Setting the derivative of $\sigma_1^2 x_1^2 + \sigma_2^2 (1 - x_1)^2$ to zero gives $x_1 = \sigma_2^2/(\sigma_1^2 + \sigma_2^2)$: the weights are inversely proportional to the variances (not to the volatilities). The volatility is below that of both assets.
Example 2 · orl-g-mk-minvar · level facile · notes
Markowitz model with two uncorrelated assets of volatilities (standard deviations of the return) $\sigma_1 = 20\%$ and $\sigma_2 = 40\%$. The shares $x_1, x_2$ of capital, with $x_1 + x_2 = 1$, minimize the portfolio variance (no return constraint). What are the optimal share $\tilde x_1$ and the portfolio volatility $\sigma_p$ (square root of the variance)?
  1. $\tilde x_1 = 50\%$, $\sigma_p = 22.4\%$
  2. $\tilde x_1 = 20\%$, $\sigma_p = 32.2\%$
  3. $\tilde x_1 = 80\%$, $\sigma_p = 17.9\%$
  4. $\tilde x_1 = 66.7\%$, $\sigma_p = 18.9\%$
Explanation. Setting the derivative of $\sigma_1^2 x_1^2 + \sigma_2^2 (1 - x_1)^2$ to zero gives $x_1 = \sigma_2^2/(\sigma_1^2 + \sigma_2^2)$: the weights are inversely proportional to the variances (not to the volatilities). The volatility is below that of both assets.
Example 1 · orl-g-mk-portafoglio · level facile · notes
Markowitz model, portfolio of two assets with shares of capital $x_1 = 0.5$, $x_2 = 0.5$; expected returns $\mu_1 = 5\%$, $\mu_2 = 12\%$; volatilities $\sigma_1 = 0.2$, $\sigma_2 = 0.25$; correlation $\rho = 0.75$, so $q_{ii} = \sigma_i^2$ and $q_{12} = q_{21} = \rho\sigma_1\sigma_2$. What are its expected return and its variance $\sum_{i}\sum_{j} q_{ij} x_i x_j$?
  1. expected return 8.5%, variance 0.0444
  2. expected return 8.5%, variance 0.0513
  3. expected return 8.5%, variance 0.0256
  4. expected return 8.5%, variance 0.035
Explanation. The return is linear, $\mu_1 x_1 + \mu_2 x_2$. The variance has three terms: $x_1^2\sigma_1^2 + x_2^2\sigma_2^2 + 2x_1x_2\rho\sigma_1\sigma_2$; the covariance appears twice ($q_{12}$ and $q_{21}$).
Example 2 · orl-g-mk-portafoglio · level facile · notes
Markowitz model, portfolio of two assets with shares of capital $x_1 = 0.4$, $x_2 = 0.6$; expected returns $\mu_1 = 8\%$, $\mu_2 = 13\%$; volatilities $\sigma_1 = 0.3$, $\sigma_2 = 0.15$; correlation $\rho = -0.5$, so $q_{ii} = \sigma_i^2$ and $q_{12} = q_{21} = \rho\sigma_1\sigma_2$. What are its expected return and its variance $\sum_{i}\sum_{j} q_{ij} x_i x_j$?
  1. expected return 11%, variance 0.0117
  2. expected return 11%, variance 0.0171
  3. expected return 11%, variance 0.0225
  4. expected return 11%, variance 0.0495
Explanation. The return is linear, $\mu_1 x_1 + \mu_2 x_2$. The variance has three terms: $x_1^2\sigma_1^2 + x_2^2\sigma_2^2 + 2x_1x_2\rho\sigma_1\sigma_2$; the covariance appears twice ($q_{12}$ and $q_{21}$).
Example 1 · orl-g-mk-correlati · level media · notes
Markowitz model with two assets of volatilities (standard deviations of the return) $\sigma_1 = 0.3$, $\sigma_2 = 0.4$ and correlation $\rho = -0.5$. The shares $x_1 + x_2 = 1$ minimize the portfolio variance $\sum_i\sum_j q_{ij}x_ix_j$, with covariance $q_{12} = \rho\sigma_1\sigma_2$ (no return constraint; the optimum has $x_1, x_2 > 0$). What are $\tilde x_1$ and the minimum variance $\sigma_p^2$?
  1. $\tilde x_1 = 59.5\%$, $\sigma_p^2 = 0.0292$
  2. $\tilde x_1 = 76.9\%$, $\sigma_p^2 = 0.0405$
  3. $\tilde x_1 = 64\%$, $\sigma_p^2 = 0.03$
  4. $\tilde x_1 = 40.5\%$, $\sigma_p^2 = 0.0424$
Explanation. With correlation $x_1 = (\sigma_2^2 - \rho\sigma_1\sigma_2)/(\sigma_1^2 + \sigma_2^2 - 2\rho\sigma_1\sigma_2) = 0.5946$; the variance is $x_1^2\sigma_1^2 + x_2^2\sigma_2^2 + 2x_1x_2\rho\sigma_1\sigma_2$ at that weight. Ignoring $\rho$ or flipping its sign gives the wrong weight.
Example 2 · orl-g-mk-correlati · level media · notes
Markowitz model with two assets of volatilities (standard deviations of the return) $\sigma_1 = 0.2$, $\sigma_2 = 0.4$ and correlation $\rho = -0.25$. The shares $x_1 + x_2 = 1$ minimize the portfolio variance $\sum_i\sum_j q_{ij}x_ix_j$, with covariance $q_{12} = \rho\sigma_1\sigma_2$ (no return constraint; the optimum has $x_1, x_2 > 0$). What are $\tilde x_1$ and the minimum variance $\sigma_p^2$?
  1. $\tilde x_1 = 25\%$, $\sigma_p^2 = 0.085$
  2. $\tilde x_1 = 87.5\%$, $\sigma_p^2 = 0.0288$
  3. $\tilde x_1 = 80\%$, $\sigma_p^2 = 0.0256$
  4. $\tilde x_1 = 75\%$, $\sigma_p^2 = 0.025$
Explanation. With correlation $x_1 = (\sigma_2^2 - \rho\sigma_1\sigma_2)/(\sigma_1^2 + \sigma_2^2 - 2\rho\sigma_1\sigma_2) = 0.75$; the variance is $x_1^2\sigma_1^2 + x_2^2\sigma_2^2 + 2x_1x_2\rho\sigma_1\sigma_2$ at that weight. Ignoring $\rho$ or flipping its sign gives the wrong weight.
Example 1 · orl-g-mk-due-titoli-r · level complessa · notes
Markowitz with two assets: expected returns $\mu_1 = 16\%$, $\mu_2 = 6\%$; volatilities $\sigma_1 = 27\%$, $\sigma_2 = 14\%$; correlation $\rho = -0.2$. Minimize the portfolio variance $x_1^2\sigma_1^2 + x_2^2\sigma_2^2 + 2x_1x_2\rho\sigma_1\sigma_2$ with $x_1 + x_2 = 1$, $x \ge 0$ and expected return at least $\bar r = 12\%$. What are the optimal share $x_1$ and the portfolio volatility?
  1. $x_1 = 60\%$, volatility $16.05\%$
  2. $x_1 = 60\%$, volatility $21.8\%$
  3. $x_1 = 25.2\%$, volatility $11.29\%$
  4. $x_1 = 60\%$, volatility $17.14\%$
Explanation. Minimum-variance share without the return constraint: $x_1 = (\sigma_2^2 - \rho\sigma_1\sigma_2)/(\sigma_1^2 + \sigma_2^2 - 2\rho\sigma_1\sigma_2) = 25.2\%$, with return $8.52\%$. It is below $\bar r$, so the constraint binds: $x_1\mu_1 + (1 - x_1)\mu_2 = \bar r$ gives $x_1 = 60\%$. Volatility $16.05\%$.
Example 2 · orl-g-mk-due-titoli-r · level complessa · notes
Markowitz with two assets: expected returns $\mu_1 = 14\%$, $\mu_2 = 8\%$; volatilities $\sigma_1 = 19\%$, $\sigma_2 = 8\%$; correlation $\rho = 0$. Minimize the portfolio variance $x_1^2\sigma_1^2 + x_2^2\sigma_2^2 + 2x_1x_2\rho\sigma_1\sigma_2$ with $x_1 + x_2 = 1$, $x \ge 0$ and expected return at least $\bar r = 12\%$. What are the optimal share $x_1$ and the portfolio volatility?
  1. $x_1 = 66.7\%$, volatility $12.94\%$
  2. $x_1 = 66.7\%$, volatility $15.33\%$
  3. $x_1 = 15.1\%$, volatility $7.37\%$
  4. $x_1 = 33.3\%$, volatility $8.28\%$
Explanation. Minimum-variance share without the return constraint: $x_1 = (\sigma_2^2 - \rho\sigma_1\sigma_2)/(\sigma_1^2 + \sigma_2^2 - 2\rho\sigma_1\sigma_2) = 15.1\%$, with return $8.9\%$. It is below $\bar r$, so the constraint binds: $x_1\mu_1 + (1 - x_1)\mu_2 = \bar r$ gives $x_1 = 66.7\%$. Volatility $12.94\%$.

7. Pricing and revenue management (2)

Example 1 · orl-g-pricing-ottimo · level facile · notes
Pricing model: choose the price $p$ and the quantity sold $q$ to maximize the profit $(p - c)\,q$ subject to $q \le d(p) = 1100 - 5p$ (demand) and $q \le k$ (capacity), with marginal cost $c = 66$ € and $k = 405$. What are the optimal price $p^*$ and the optimal profit?
  1. $p^* = 143$ €, profit 29645 €
  2. $p^* = 110$ €, profit 17820 €
  3. $p^* = 139$ €, profit 29565 €
  4. $p^* = 187$ €, profit 19965 €
Explanation. Without capacity $p^\circ = (a/b + c)/2 = 143$ with $q^\circ = 385 \le k$: the capacity constraint is inactive and $p^* = p^\circ$.
Example 2 · orl-g-pricing-ottimo · level facile · notes
Pricing model: choose the price $p$ and the quantity sold $q$ to maximize the profit $(p - c)\,q$ subject to $q \le d(p) = 1080 - 4p$ (demand) and $q \le k$ (capacity), with marginal cost $c = 32$ € and $k = 416$. What are the optimal price $p^*$ and the optimal profit?
  1. $p^* = 166$ €, profit 55744 €
  2. $p^* = 151$ €, profit 49504 €
  3. $p^* = 135$ €, profit 42848 €
  4. $p^* = 150$ €, profit 49088 €
Explanation. Without capacity $p^\circ = (a/b + c)/2 = 151$ with $q^\circ = 476 > k$: capacity bites, and the price that sells exactly $k$ is $p^* = (a - k)/b = 166$.
Example 1 · orl-g-pricing-posto · level media · notes
Pricing model: choose the price $p$ and the quantity sold $q$ to maximize $(p - c)\,q$ subject to $q \le d(p) = 1100 - 5p$ and $q \le k$, with $c = 66$ € and $k = 235$; the capacity constraint is active at the optimum. Let $\Pi(k)$ be the optimal profit as a function of the capacity. What is $d\Pi/dk$, the value of one more unit of capacity?
  1. 126 €
  2. 107 €
  3. 13 €
  4. 60 €
Explanation. With $p^* = (a - k)/b = 173$ the profit is $(p^* - c)\,k$, and $dp^*/dk = -1/b$: $d\Pi/dk = (p^* - c) - k/b = 60$. The full margin $p^* - c$ forgets that, to fill the extra seat, the price falls for everybody.
Example 2 · orl-g-pricing-posto · level media · notes
Pricing model: choose the price $p$ and the quantity sold $q$ to maximize $(p - c)\,q$ subject to $q \le d(p) = 1080 - 4p$ and $q \le k$, with $c = 32$ € and $k = 456$; the capacity constraint is active at the optimum. Let $\Pi(k)$ be the optimal profit as a function of the capacity. What is $d\Pi/dk$, the value of one more unit of capacity?
  1. 10 €
  2. 42 €
  3. -104 €
  4. 124 €
Explanation. With $p^* = (a - k)/b = 156$ the profit is $(p^* - c)\,k$, and $dp^*/dk = -1/b$: $d\Pi/dk = (p^* - c) - k/b = 10$. The full margin $p^* - c$ forgets that, to fill the extra seat, the price falls for everybody.

8. Advertising budget allocation (2)

Example 1 · orl-g-budget-due-canali · level media · notes
Advertising budget: the spends $x_1, x_2 \ge 0$ (thousands of €) on two channels maximize $r_1(x_1) + r_2(x_2)$ with $r_1(x) = 160\log(1 + 0.25x)$, $r_2(x) = 180\log(1 + 0.25x)$ (natural logarithm), subject to $x_1 + x_2 \le b = 20$, no caps. What are the optimal allocation and the multiplier $\lambda$ of the budget constraint (extra response per extra thousand €)?
  1. $\tilde x_1 = 9.2$, $\tilde x_2 = 10.8$, $\lambda = 12.14$
  2. $\tilde x_1 = 10$, $\tilde x_2 = 10$, $\lambda = 11.43$
  3. $\tilde x_1 = 9.4$, $\tilde x_2 = 10.6$, $\lambda = 11.93$
  4. $\tilde x_1 = 20$, $\tilde x_2 = 0$, $\lambda = 6.67$
Explanation. At the optimum the marginal returns are equal: $40/(1 + 0.25x_1) = 45/(1 + 0.25x_2)$ with $x_1 + x_2 = 20$, so $x_1 = 9.2$; $\lambda$ is the common marginal return, $12.14$. Splitting in proportion to $a_i k_i$ ignores the saturation.
Example 2 · orl-g-budget-due-canali · level media · notes
Advertising budget: the spends $x_1, x_2 \ge 0$ (thousands of €) on two channels maximize $r_1(x_1) + r_2(x_2)$ with $r_1(x) = 70\log(1 + 0.2x)$, $r_2(x) = 30\log(1 + 0.5x)$ (natural logarithm), subject to $x_1 + x_2 \le b = 50$, no caps. What are the optimal allocation and the multiplier $\lambda$ of the budget constraint (extra response per extra thousand €)?
  1. $\tilde x_1 = 34.9$, $\tilde x_2 = 15.1$, $\lambda = 1.75$
  2. $\tilde x_1 = 24.1$, $\tilde x_2 = 25.9$, $\lambda = 2.4$
  3. $\tilde x_1 = 25$, $\tilde x_2 = 25$, $\lambda = 2.33$
  4. $\tilde x_1 = 50$, $\tilde x_2 = 0$, $\lambda = 1.27$
Explanation. At the optimum the marginal returns are equal: $14/(1 + 0.2x_1) = 15/(1 + 0.5x_2)$ with $x_1 + x_2 = 50$, so $x_1 = 34.9$; $\lambda$ is the common marginal return, $1.75$. Splitting in proportion to $a_i k_i$ ignores the saturation.
Example 1 · orl-g-budget-tetto · level complessa · notes
Advertising budget: the spends $x_1, x_2 \ge 0$ (thousands of €) maximize $r_1(x_1) + r_2(x_2)$, with $r_1(x) = 160\log(1 + 0.25x)$ and $r_2(x) = 180\log(1 + 0.25x)$ (natural logarithm), subject to $x_1 + x_2 \le b = 30$ (multiplier $\lambda$) and the cap $x_1 \le u_1 = 10$ (multiplier $\mu_1$). KKT: $r_i'(\tilde x_i) = \lambda + \mu_i$, with $\mu_2 = 0$. What are the optimal allocation, $\lambda$ and $\mu_1$?
  1. $\tilde x_1 = 10$, $\tilde x_2 = 20$, $\lambda = 7.5$, $\mu_1 = 3.93$
  2. $\tilde x_1 = 10$, $\tilde x_2 = 20$, $\lambda = 7.5$, $\mu_1 = 11.43$
  3. $\tilde x_1 = 13.88$, $\tilde x_2 = 16.12$, $\lambda = 8.95$, $\mu_1 = 0$
  4. $\tilde x_1 = 10$, $\tilde x_2 = 20$, $\lambda = 11.43$, $\mu_1 = 0$
Explanation. Without the cap the marginal returns would be equal at $x_1 = 13.88 > u_1$: the cap binds, $\tilde x_1 = 10$ and $\tilde x_2 = 20$. Channel 2 is interior, so $\lambda = r_2'(20) = 45/(1 + 0.25\cdot 20) = 7.5$; channel 1 gives $\mu_1 = r_1'(10) - \lambda = 11.43 - 7.5 = 3.93$, the value of one more thousand € of cap.
Example 2 · orl-g-budget-tetto · level complessa · notes
Advertising budget: the spends $x_1, x_2 \ge 0$ (thousands of €) maximize $r_1(x_1) + r_2(x_2)$, with $r_1(x) = 70\log(1 + 0.2x)$ and $r_2(x) = 30\log(1 + 0.5x)$ (natural logarithm), subject to $x_1 + x_2 \le b = 60$ (multiplier $\lambda$) and the cap $x_1 \le u_1 = 34$ (multiplier $\mu_1$). KKT: $r_i'(\tilde x_i) = \lambda + \mu_i$, with $\mu_2 = 0$. What are the optimal allocation, $\lambda$ and $\mu_1$?
  1. $\tilde x_1 = 34$, $\tilde x_2 = 26$, $\lambda = 1.07$, $\mu_1 = 0.72$
  2. $\tilde x_1 = 34$, $\tilde x_2 = 26$, $\lambda = 1.79$, $\mu_1 = 0$
  3. $\tilde x_1 = 41.9$, $\tilde x_2 = 18.1$, $\lambda = 1.49$, $\mu_1 = 0$
  4. $\tilde x_1 = 34$, $\tilde x_2 = 26$, $\lambda = 1.07$, $\mu_1 = 1.79$
Explanation. Without the cap the marginal returns would be equal at $x_1 = 41.9 > u_1$: the cap binds, $\tilde x_1 = 34$ and $\tilde x_2 = 26$. Channel 2 is interior, so $\lambda = r_2'(26) = 15/(1 + 0.5\cdot 26) = 1.07$; channel 1 gives $\mu_1 = r_1'(34) - \lambda = 1.79 - 1.07 = 0.72$, the value of one more thousand € of cap.

9. Continuous location of a service (2)

Example 1 · orl-g-loc-weber-1d · level facile · notes
Location on a straight road: customers at positions $a_i$ with weights $w_i$: km 2 (weight 4), km 12 (weight 5), km 13 (weight 1), km 16 (weight 5), km 18 (weight 1). A facility at position $x$ (km) minimizes the weighted total distance $\sum_i w_i |x - a_i|$ (Weber model on a line). Where is the optimal position $x^*$?
  1. $x^* = 13$ km
  2. $x^* = 10$ km
  3. $x^* = 12$ km
  4. $x^* = 11.2$ km
Explanation. In one dimension Weber is the weighted median: the point that leaves at least half of the total weight ($16/2$) on each side, here km $12$. The centroid ($11.2$) is the optimum of the squared distances, the midpoint of the extremes ($10$) that of the minimax.
Example 2 · orl-g-loc-weber-1d · level facile · notes
Location on a straight road: customers at positions $a_i$ with weights $w_i$: km 4 (weight 1), km 9 (weight 3), km 12 (weight 5). A facility at position $x$ (km) minimizes the weighted total distance $\sum_i w_i |x - a_i|$ (Weber model on a line). Where is the optimal position $x^*$?
  1. $x^* = 10.1$ km
  2. $x^* = 9$ km
  3. $x^* = 12$ km
  4. $x^* = 8$ km
Explanation. In one dimension Weber is the weighted median: the point that leaves at least half of the total weight ($9/2$) on each side, here km $12$. The centroid ($10.1$) is the optimum of the squared distances, the midpoint of the extremes ($8$) that of the minimax.
Example 1 · orl-g-loc-minimax-2d · level media · notes
Minimax location in the plane: three districts at $(6; 6)$, $(8; 9)$, $(1; 8)$ (km). The facility at $(x, y)$ minimizes $z$, the largest Euclidean distance from it to a district (no weights). What are the optimal point $(x^*; y^*)$ and the optimal value $z^*$?
  1. $(x^*; y^*) = (4.5; 8.5)$, $z^* = 3.54$
  2. $(x^*; y^*) = (4.5; 7.5)$, $z^* = 3.81$
  3. $(x^*; y^*) = (5; 7.67)$, $z^* = 4.01$
  4. $(x^*; y^*) = (4.39; 9.24)$, $z^* = 3.61$
Explanation. The optimum is the centre of the smallest circle containing the three points. The triangle is obtuse: the circle having the longest side as diameter already contains the third point, so the centre is the midpoint of that side and $z^*$ is half its length, $3.54$. The circle through all three points would be larger.
Example 2 · orl-g-loc-minimax-2d · level media · notes
Minimax location in the plane: three districts at $(4; 9)$, $(3; 1)$, $(8; 7)$ (km). The facility at $(x, y)$ minimizes $z$, the largest Euclidean distance from it to a district (no weights). What are the optimal point $(x^*; y^*)$ and the optimal value $z^*$?
  1. $(x^*; y^*) = (4.44; 4.88)$, $z^* = 4.14$
  2. $(x^*; y^*) = (5; 5.67)$, $z^* = 5.08$
  3. $(x^*; y^*) = (3.5; 5)$, $z^* = 4.03$
  4. $(x^*; y^*) = (5.5; 5)$, $z^* = 4.72$
Explanation. The optimum is the centre of the smallest circle containing the three points. The triangle is acute: no circle built on one side contains the third point, so the optimum is the circumcentre, equidistant from the three districts, with $z^* = 4.14$. The centroid ignores the extreme districts.

10. Smart charging of electric vehicles (2)

Example 1 · orl-g-ev-un-veicolo · level facile · notes
EV charging LP, one vehicle, two one-hour slots: $x_t$ = energy charged in hour $t$ (kWh), $0 \le x_t \le \bar p = 11$ (charger power, kW), requirement $x_1 + x_2 \ge e = 20$ kWh (efficiency $\eta = 1$); minimize $\pi_1x_1 + \pi_2x_2$ with $\pi_1 = 0.15$, $\pi_2 = 0.25$ €/kWh. What are the minimum cost and the shadow price of the requirement (cost of one more kWh)?
  1. cost 3.9 €, shadow price 0.1 €/kWh
  2. cost 3.9 €, shadow price 0.15 €/kWh
  3. cost 3 €, shadow price 0.15 €/kWh
  4. cost 3.9 €, shadow price 0.25 €/kWh
Explanation. The cheap hour is used at full power ($11$ kWh), the remaining $9$ kWh go into hour 2: $3.9$ €. One more kWh would end up in the expensive hour: the dual is the price of the marginal hour, $0.25$.
Example 2 · orl-g-ev-un-veicolo · level facile · notes
EV charging LP, one vehicle, two one-hour slots: $x_t$ = energy charged in hour $t$ (kWh), $0 \le x_t \le \bar p = 6$ (charger power, kW), requirement $x_1 + x_2 \ge e = 7$ kWh (efficiency $\eta = 1$); minimize $\pi_1x_1 + \pi_2x_2$ with $\pi_1 = 0.12$, $\pi_2 = 0.3$ €/kWh. What are the minimum cost and the shadow price of the requirement (cost of one more kWh)?
  1. cost 1.02 €, shadow price 0.3 €/kWh
  2. cost 1.02 €, shadow price 0.18 €/kWh
  3. cost 0.84 €, shadow price 0.12 €/kWh
  4. cost 1.02 €, shadow price 0.12 €/kWh
Explanation. The cheap hour is used at full power ($6$ kWh), the remaining $1$ kWh go into hour 2: $1.02$ €. One more kWh would end up in the expensive hour: the dual is the price of the marginal hour, $0.3$.
Example 1 · orl-g-ev-tre-ore · level media · notes
EV charging LP, one vehicle plugged in for three one-hour slots: $x_t$ = energy drawn from the grid in hour $t$ (kWh), $0 \le x_t \le \bar p = 9$; requirement $\eta\,(x_1 + x_2 + x_3) \ge e = 18$ kWh, with efficiency $\eta = 0.9$; minimize $\sum_t \pi_t x_t$ with $(\pi_1, \pi_2, \pi_3) = (0.18, 0.25, 0.08)$ €/kWh. What are the minimum cost and the shadow price of the requirement (cost of one more kWh in the battery)?
  1. cost 2.34 €, shadow price 0.18 €/kWh
  2. cost 2.84 €, shadow price 0.25 €/kWh
  3. cost 2.84 €, shadow price 0.278 €/kWh
  4. cost 2.84 €, shadow price 0.089 €/kWh
Explanation. The grid must supply $e/\eta = 20$ kWh, bought in the cheapest hours at full power $\bar p$: cost $2.84$ €. One more kWh in the battery needs $1/\eta$ kWh in the marginal hour (price $0.25$): shadow price $0.25/0.9 = 0.278$ €/kWh. Forgetting $\eta$ buys only $e$ kWh.
Example 2 · orl-g-ev-tre-ore · level media · notes
EV charging LP, one vehicle plugged in for three one-hour slots: $x_t$ = energy drawn from the grid in hour $t$ (kWh), $0 \le x_t \le \bar p = 9$; requirement $\eta\,(x_1 + x_2 + x_3) \ge e = 21.6$ kWh, with efficiency $\eta = 0.9$; minimize $\sum_t \pi_t x_t$ with $(\pi_1, \pi_2, \pi_3) = (0.25, 0.2, 0.3)$ €/kWh. What are the minimum cost and the shadow price of the requirement (cost of one more kWh in the battery)?
  1. cost 5.13 €, shadow price 0.3 €/kWh
  2. cost 5.85 €, shadow price 0.3 €/kWh
  3. cost 5.85 €, shadow price 0.333 €/kWh
  4. cost 5.85 €, shadow price 0.222 €/kWh
Explanation. The grid must supply $e/\eta = 24$ kWh, bought in the cheapest hours at full power $\bar p$: cost $5.85$ €. One more kWh in the battery needs $1/\eta$ kWh in the marginal hour (price $0.3$): shadow price $0.3/0.9 = 0.333$ €/kWh. Forgetting $\eta$ buys only $e$ kWh.

11. Service capacity and waiting times (4)

Example 1 · orl-g-code-ottimo · level facile · notes
Capacity of an M/M/1 queue: arrival rate $\lambda = 25$ customers/hour; the service rate $\mu > \lambda$ (capacity) minimizes $C(\mu) = c\,\mu + h\,\lambda/(\mu - \lambda)$, where $c = 4$ €/h per unit of $\mu$, $h = 4$ € per hour a customer spends in the system and $\lambda/(\mu - \lambda)$ is the mean number of customers in the system. What are $\mu^*$ and $C(\mu^*)$?
  1. $\mu^* = 26$, $C = 204$ €/h
  2. $\mu^* = 50$, $C = 204$ €/h
  3. $\mu^* = 30$, $C = 140$ €/h
  4. $\mu^* = 27.5$, $C = 150$ €/h
Explanation. From $c - h\lambda/(\mu - \lambda)^2 = 0$: $\mu^* = \lambda + \sqrt{h\lambda/c} = 25 + 5 = 30$, with cost $4\cdot 30 + 4\cdot 25/5 = 140$.
Example 2 · orl-g-code-ottimo · level facile · notes
Capacity of an M/M/1 queue: arrival rate $\lambda = 27$ customers/hour; the service rate $\mu > \lambda$ (capacity) minimizes $C(\mu) = c\,\mu + h\,\lambda/(\mu - \lambda)$, where $c = 3$ €/h per unit of $\mu$, $h = 1$ € per hour a customer spends in the system and $\lambda/(\mu - \lambda)$ is the mean number of customers in the system. What are $\mu^*$ and $C(\mu^*)$?
  1. $\mu^* = 36$, $C = 111$ €/h
  2. $\mu^* = 27.58$, $C = 129.5$ €/h
  3. $\mu^* = 28$, $C = 111$ €/h
  4. $\mu^* = 30$, $C = 99$ €/h
Explanation. From $c - h\lambda/(\mu - \lambda)^2 = 0$: $\mu^* = \lambda + \sqrt{h\lambda/c} = 27 + 3 = 30$, with cost $3\cdot 30 + 1\cdot 27/3 = 99$.
Example 1 · orl-g-code-misure · level facile · notes
M/M/1 queue (Poisson arrivals, exponential service, one server) with arrival rate $\lambda = 18$ per hour and service rate $\mu = 24$ per hour. What are the utilization $\rho$, the mean time in the system $w$ and the mean number of customers in the system $l$?
  1. $\rho = 75\%$, $w = 2.5$ min, $l = 0.75$
  2. $\rho = 75\%$, $w = 10$ min, $l = 3$
  3. $\rho = 75\%$, $w = 180$ min, $l = 0.17$
  4. $\rho = 75\%$, $w = 10$ min, $l = 4$
Explanation. $\rho = \lambda/\mu$, $w = 1/(\mu - \lambda)$ hours $= 10$ minutes and, by Little's law, $l = \lambda\, w = \lambda/(\mu - \lambda) = 3$. $1/\mu$ is only the mean service time.
Example 2 · orl-g-code-misure · level facile · notes
M/M/1 queue (Poisson arrivals, exponential service, one server) with arrival rate $\lambda = 13$ per hour and service rate $\mu = 22$ per hour. What are the utilization $\rho$, the mean time in the system $w$ and the mean number of customers in the system $l$?
  1. $\rho = 59.1\%$, $w = 6.7$ min, $l = 2.44$
  2. $\rho = 59.1\%$, $w = 86.7$ min, $l = 0.11$
  3. $\rho = 59.1\%$, $w = 6.7$ min, $l = 1.44$
  4. $\rho = 59.1\%$, $w = 2.7$ min, $l = 0.59$
Explanation. $\rho = \lambda/\mu$, $w = 1/(\mu - \lambda)$ hours $= 6.7$ minutes and, by Little's law, $l = \lambda\, w = \lambda/(\mu - \lambda) = 1.44$. $1/\mu$ is only the mean service time.
Example 1 · orl-g-code-promessa · level media · notes
Capacity of an M/M/1 queue: arrival rate $\lambda = 16$ customers/hour; the service rate $\mu > \lambda$ minimizes $C(\mu) = c\,\mu + h\,\lambda/(\mu - \lambda)$ €/h, with $c = 2$ and $h = 8$; the mean time in the system is $w = 1/(\mu - \lambda)$ hours. Marketing promises $w \le w_{\max} = 6$ minutes. What are the optimal capacity under the promise and its cost $C$?
  1. $\mu = 26$, $C = 64.8$ €/h
  2. $\mu = 26$, $C = 52.8$ €/h
  3. $\mu = 24$, $C = 64$ €/h
  4. $\mu = 22$, $C = 65.33$ €/h
Explanation. Without the promise $\tilde\mu = \lambda + \sqrt{h\lambda/c} = 24$. The promise requires $\mu \ge \lambda + 1/w_{\max} = 16 + 60/6 = 26$ (with $w_{\max}$ in hours), tighter than $\tilde\mu$: since $C$ is convex, the optimum is on the constraint, with $C = 2\cdot 26 + 8\cdot 16\cdot 6/60 = 64.8$ €/h.
Example 2 · orl-g-code-promessa · level media · notes
Capacity of an M/M/1 queue: arrival rate $\lambda = 27$ customers/hour; the service rate $\mu > \lambda$ minimizes $C(\mu) = c\,\mu + h\,\lambda/(\mu - \lambda)$ €/h, with $c = 3$ and $h = 1$; the mean time in the system is $w = 1/(\mu - \lambda)$ hours. Marketing promises $w \le w_{\max} = 2$ minutes. What are the optimal capacity under the promise and its cost $C$?
  1. $\mu = 30$, $C = 99$ €/h
  2. $\mu = 29$, $C = 100.5$ €/h
  3. $\mu = 57$, $C = 171.03$ €/h
  4. $\mu = 57$, $C = 171.9$ €/h
Explanation. Without the promise $\tilde\mu = \lambda + \sqrt{h\lambda/c} = 30$. The promise requires $\mu \ge \lambda + 1/w_{\max} = 27 + 60/2 = 57$ (with $w_{\max}$ in hours), tighter than $\tilde\mu$: since $C$ is convex, the optimum is on the constraint, with $C = 3\cdot 57 + 1\cdot 27\cdot 2/60 = 171.9$ €/h.
Example 1 · orl-g-code-robusto · level complessa · notes
M/M/1 capacity model: choose the capacity $\mu$ to minimize $C(\mu, \lambda) = c\mu + h\lambda/(\mu - \lambda)$, with $c = 4$ € per unit of capacity and $h = 3$ €/h per customer in the system. The arrival rate is uncertain, $\lambda \in [31, 42]$ per hour, nominal value $36.5$. One sizes $\mu$ to minimize the worst-case cost over the interval. What are the robust $\mu$, its worst-case cost and its premium over the nominal optimal cost $C(\tilde\mu(36.5), 36.5)$?
  1. $\mu = 73.5$, worst-case cost $298$, premium $58.6\%$
  2. $\mu = 41.73$, worst-case cost $187.86$, premium $0\%$
  3. $\mu = 47.61$, worst-case cost $200.3$, premium $6.6\%$
  4. $\mu = 47.61$, worst-case cost $212.9$, premium $13.3\%$
Explanation. For fixed $\mu$ the cost grows with $\lambda$, so the worst case is $\lambda = 42$: $\mu_{\rm rob} = 42 + \sqrt{h\cdot 42/c} = 47.61$, worst-case cost $212.9$. The nominal optimum costs $187.86$: the insurance premium is $13.3\%$. Sizing on the nominal $\tilde\mu = 41.73 < 42$ would make the queue unstable at the top of the interval.
Example 2 · orl-g-code-robusto · level complessa · notes
M/M/1 capacity model: choose the capacity $\mu$ to minimize $C(\mu, \lambda) = c\mu + h\lambda/(\mu - \lambda)$, with $c = 2$ € per unit of capacity and $h = 1$ €/h per customer in the system. The arrival rate is uncertain, $\lambda \in [28, 42]$ per hour, nominal value $35$. One sizes $\mu$ to minimize the worst-case cost over the interval. What are the robust $\mu$, its worst-case cost and its premium over the nominal optimal cost $C(\tilde\mu(35), 35)$?
  1. $\mu = 46.58$, worst-case cost $102.33$, premium $18\%$
  2. $\mu = 63$, worst-case cost $128$, premium $47.6\%$
  3. $\mu = 46.58$, worst-case cost $96.19$, premium $10.9\%$
  4. $\mu = 39.18$, worst-case cost $86.73$, premium $0\%$
Explanation. For fixed $\mu$ the cost grows with $\lambda$, so the worst case is $\lambda = 42$: $\mu_{\rm rob} = 42 + \sqrt{h\cdot 42/c} = 46.58$, worst-case cost $102.33$. The nominal optimum costs $86.73$: the insurance premium is $18\%$. Sizing on the nominal $\tilde\mu = 39.18 < 42$ would make the queue unstable at the top of the interval.

12. Data Envelopment Analysis (3)

Example 1 · orl-g-dea-ccr-1x1 · level facile · notes
DEA with five DMUs (decision-making units), one input $x$ and one output $y$. DMU $j$: 1 $(x_{11}, y_{11}) = (8, 7)$; 2 $(x_{12}, y_{12}) = (10, 11)$; 3 $(x_{13}, y_{13}) = (3, 10)$; 4 $(x_{14}, y_{14}) = (8, 8)$; 5 $(x_{15}, y_{15}) = (11, 1)$. The CCR efficiency of DMU $o$ is $h_o = \max\, u\,y_o/(v\,x_o)$ over the weights $u, v \ge 0$ that give every DMU $j$ a ratio $u\,y_j/(v\,x_j) \le 1$. What is the CCR efficiency $\tilde h_{5}$ of DMU 5?
  1. $\tilde h_{5} = 0.083$
  2. $\tilde h_{5} = 0.091$
  3. $\tilde h_{5} = 0.273$
  4. $\tilde h_{5} = 0.027$
Explanation. With one input and one output the weights cancel: the efficiency is $y_o/x_o$ divided by the best ratio, $10/3$ of DMU 3: $(1/11)/(10/3) = 0.027$.
Example 2 · orl-g-dea-ccr-1x1 · level facile · notes
DEA with five DMUs (decision-making units), one input $x$ and one output $y$. DMU $j$: 1 $(x_{11}, y_{11}) = (6, 11)$; 2 $(x_{12}, y_{12}) = (5, 2)$; 3 $(x_{13}, y_{13}) = (6, 11)$; 4 $(x_{14}, y_{14}) = (10, 8)$; 5 $(x_{15}, y_{15}) = (12, 10)$. The CCR efficiency of DMU $o$ is $h_o = \max\, u\,y_o/(v\,x_o)$ over the weights $u, v \ge 0$ that give every DMU $j$ a ratio $u\,y_j/(v\,x_j) \le 1$. What is the CCR efficiency $\tilde h_{4}$ of DMU 4?
  1. $\tilde h_{4} = 0.727$
  2. $\tilde h_{4} = 0.436$
  3. $\tilde h_{4} = 0.5$
  4. $\tilde h_{4} = 0.8$
Explanation. With one input and one output the weights cancel: the efficiency is $y_o/x_o$ divided by the best ratio, $11/6$ of DMU 1: $(8/10)/(11/6) = 0.436$.
Example 1 · orl-g-dea-bcc-1x1 · level media · notes
DEA with five DMUs (decision-making units), one input $x$ and one output $y$. DMU $j$: 1 $(x_{11}, y_{11}) = (4, 4)$; 2 $(x_{12}, y_{12}) = (2, 2)$; 3 $(x_{13}, y_{13}) = (5, 7)$; 4 $(x_{14}, y_{14}) = (3, 6)$; 5 $(x_{15}, y_{15}) = (7, 9)$. The input-oriented BCC efficiency $\tilde\theta^{\rm BCC}$ of DMU $o$ is the smallest $\theta$ such that a convex combination of the DMUs ($\lambda_j \ge 0$, $\sum_j\lambda_j = 1$) produces at least $y_o$ with at most $\theta x_o$; the scale efficiency is $\mathrm{SE} = \tilde\theta^{\rm CCR}/\tilde\theta^{\rm BCC}$. DMU 1 has $\tilde\theta^{\rm CCR} = 0.5$. What are its $\tilde\theta^{\rm BCC}$ and $\mathrm{SE}$?
  1. $\tilde\theta^{\rm BCC} = 0.625$, $\mathrm{SE} = 0.5$
  2. $\tilde\theta^{\rm BCC} = 0.75$, $\mathrm{SE} = 0.667$
  3. $\tilde\theta^{\rm BCC} = 0.625$, $\mathrm{SE} = 0.8$
  4. $\tilde\theta^{\rm BCC} = 0.5$, $\mathrm{SE} = 0.8$
Explanation. The BCC virtual DMU is a convex combination ($\sum_j \lambda_j = 1$) producing at least $y_o = 4$ with the least input: combining two DMUs on either side of $y_o$ gives $\tilde\theta^{\rm BCC} = 0.625$, better than the best single DMU ($0.75$). Then $\mathrm{SE} = \tilde\theta^{\rm CCR}/\tilde\theta^{\rm BCC} = 0.8$.
Example 2 · orl-g-dea-bcc-1x1 · level media · notes
DEA with five DMUs (decision-making units), one input $x$ and one output $y$. DMU $j$: 1 $(x_{11}, y_{11}) = (6, 11)$; 2 $(x_{12}, y_{12}) = (5, 2)$; 3 $(x_{13}, y_{13}) = (6, 11)$; 4 $(x_{14}, y_{14}) = (10, 8)$; 5 $(x_{15}, y_{15}) = (12, 10)$. The input-oriented BCC efficiency $\tilde\theta^{\rm BCC}$ of DMU $o$ is the smallest $\theta$ such that a convex combination of the DMUs ($\lambda_j \ge 0$, $\sum_j\lambda_j = 1$) produces at least $y_o$ with at most $\theta x_o$; the scale efficiency is $\mathrm{SE} = \tilde\theta^{\rm CCR}/\tilde\theta^{\rm BCC}$. DMU 4 has $\tilde\theta^{\rm CCR} = 0.436$. What are its $\tilde\theta^{\rm BCC}$ and $\mathrm{SE}$?
  1. $\tilde\theta^{\rm BCC} = 0.6$, $\mathrm{SE} = 0.727$
  2. $\tilde\theta^{\rm BCC} = 0.567$, $\mathrm{SE} = 0.77$
  3. $\tilde\theta^{\rm BCC} = 0.436$, $\mathrm{SE} = 0.77$
  4. $\tilde\theta^{\rm BCC} = 0.567$, $\mathrm{SE} = 0.436$
Explanation. The BCC virtual DMU is a convex combination ($\sum_j \lambda_j = 1$) producing at least $y_o = 8$ with the least input: combining two DMUs on either side of $y_o$ gives $\tilde\theta^{\rm BCC} = 0.567$, better than the best single DMU ($0.6$). Then $\mathrm{SE} = \tilde\theta^{\rm CCR}/\tilde\theta^{\rm BCC} = 0.77$.
Example 1 · orl-g-dea-ccr-2input · level complessa · notes
DEA with five DMUs (decision-making units), two inputs and one output, data $(x_{1j}, x_{2j}; y_{1j})$: 1: $(24, 4; 2)$, 2: $(18, 16; 2)$, 3: $(4, 16; 2)$, 4: $(30, 36; 3)$, 5: $(30, 30; 3)$. Input-oriented CCR envelopment model for DMU $o$: $\min\theta$ subject to $\sum_j\lambda_jx_{ij} \le \theta x_{io}$ ($i = 1, 2$), $\sum_j\lambda_jy_{1j} \ge y_{1o}$, $\lambda_j \ge 0$. For $o = 4$, what are the efficiency $\tilde\theta$ and the radial input targets $(\tilde\theta x_{1o}; \tilde\theta x_{2o})$?
  1. $\tilde\theta = 0.167$, target $(5; 6)$
  2. $\tilde\theta = 0.341$, target $(10.22; 12.27)$
  3. $\tilde\theta = 0.511$, target $(15.33; 18.4)$
  4. $\tilde\theta = 0.667$, target $(20; 24)$
Explanation. With constant returns divide the inputs by the output: the points $(x_{1j}/y_{1j}, x_{2j}/y_{2j})$ are compared with the same output. The envelopment form looks for the smallest $\theta$ such that a combination of DMUs uses at most $\theta$ times both inputs of DMU 4: here the optimum combines two DMUs, $\tilde\theta = 0.511$ (the best single DMU gives only $0.667$).
Example 2 · orl-g-dea-ccr-2input · level complessa · notes

For $o = 2$, what are the efficiency $\tilde\theta$ and the radial input targets $(\tilde\theta x_{1o}; \tilde\theta x_{2o})$?

Explanation. With constant returns divide the inputs by the output: the points $(x_{1j}/y_{1j}, x_{2j}/y_{2j})$ are compared with the same output. The envelopment form looks for the smallest $\theta$ such that a combination of DMUs uses at most $\theta$ times both inputs of DMU 2: here the optimum combines two DMUs, $\tilde\theta = 0.625$ (the best single DMU gives only $0.727$).

13. The Newsvendor and its variants (5)

Example 1 · orl-g-nv-uniforme · level facile · notes
Newsvendor: a quantity $q$ is ordered before the demand is known; each unit costs $c = 12$ €, sells at $p = 21$ € and, if unsold, is salvaged at $v = 9$ €. Demand is uniform on $[140, 164]$ and $q$ maximizes the expected profit. What are the critical ratio $\alpha^* = \mathbb P(D \le q^*)$ and the optimal quantity $q^*$?
  1. $\alpha^* = 0.75$, $q^* = 158$
  2. $\alpha^* = 0.5$, $q^* = 152$
  3. $\alpha^* = 0.25$, $q^* = 146$
  4. $\alpha^* = 0.6364$, $q^* = 155.3$
Explanation. $c_u = p - c = 9$, $c_o = c - v = 3$, $\alpha^* = c_u/(c_u + c_o) = 9/12$; for the uniform distribution $F^{-1}(\alpha) = 140 + 24\,\alpha$, so $q^* = 158$, not the mean.
Example 2 · orl-g-nv-uniforme · level facile · notes
Newsvendor: a quantity $q$ is ordered before the demand is known; each unit costs $c = 14$ €, sells at $p = 18$ € and, if unsold, is salvaged at $v = 4$ €. Demand is uniform on $[60, 116]$ and $q$ maximizes the expected profit. What are the critical ratio $\alpha^* = \mathbb P(D \le q^*)$ and the optimal quantity $q^*$?
  1. $\alpha^* = 0.2857$, $q^* = 76$
  2. $\alpha^* = 0.7143$, $q^* = 100$
  3. $\alpha^* = 0.5$, $q^* = 88$
  4. $\alpha^* = 0.5625$, $q^* = 91.5$
Explanation. $c_u = p - c = 4$, $c_o = c - v = 10$, $\alpha^* = c_u/(c_u + c_o) = 4/14$; for the uniform distribution $F^{-1}(\alpha) = 60 + 56\,\alpha$, so $q^* = 76$, not the mean.
Example 1 · orl-g-nv-discreto · level media · notes
Newsvendor: a quantity $q$ is ordered before the demand $D$ is known, to minimize the expected cost $\mathbb E[c_o(q - D)^+ + c_u(D - q)^+]$, with underage cost $c_u = 4$ per unit of unmet demand and overage cost $c_o = 12$ per unsold unit. Demand scenarios (probability in brackets): $70$ (0.3), $110$ (0.1), $120$ (0.3), $130$ (0.3). What are the optimal quantity $q^*$ and its expected cost?
  1. $q^* = 130$, expected cost 276
  2. $q^* = 70$, expected cost 148
  3. $q^* = 110$, expected cost 180
  4. $q^* = 120$, expected cost 204
Explanation. $\alpha^* = 4/16 = 0.25$: the optimum is the smallest $q$ with $F(q) \ge \alpha^*$, that is $q^* = 70$, with expected cost $\sum_s \pi_s (c_o (q - d_s)^+ + c_u (d_s - q)^+) = 148$.
Example 2 · orl-g-nv-discreto · level media · notes
Newsvendor: a quantity $q$ is ordered before the demand $D$ is known, to minimize the expected cost $\mathbb E[c_o(q - D)^+ + c_u(D - q)^+]$, with underage cost $c_u = 11$ per unit of unmet demand and overage cost $c_o = 4$ per unsold unit. Demand scenarios (probability in brackets): $60$ (0.4), $100$ (0.3), $110$ (0.1), $130$ (0.1), $140$ (0.1). What are the optimal quantity $q^*$ and its expected cost?
  1. $q^* = 110$, expected cost 147
  2. $q^* = 100$, expected cost 152
  3. $q^* = 60$, expected cost 352
  4. $q^* = 130$, expected cost 167
Explanation. $\alpha^* = 11/15 = 0.7333$: the optimum is the smallest $q$ with $F(q) \ge \alpha^*$, that is $q^* = 110$, with expected cost $\sum_s \pi_s (c_o (q - d_s)^+ + c_u (d_s - q)^+) = 147$.
Example 1 · orl-g-nv-vss · level complessa · notes
Newsvendor: a quantity $q$ is ordered before the demand $D$ is known, to minimize the expected cost $\mathbb E[c_o(q - D)^+ + c_u(D - q)^+]$, with underage cost $c_u = 7$ per unit of unmet demand and overage cost $c_o = 11$ per unsold unit. Demand scenarios (probability in brackets): $130$ (0.4), $140$ (0.2), $160$ (0.2), $190$ (0.1), $200$ (0.1). Compute the optimal quantity $q^*$, its expected cost and the value of the stochastic solution VSS (expected cost of ordering the mean demand minus the cost of $q^*$).
  1. $q^* = 130$, cost 147, VSS $= 147$
  2. $q^* = 140$, cost 149, VSS $= 41.8$
  3. $q^* = 130$, cost 147, VSS $= 190.8$
  4. $q^* = 130$, cost 147, VSS $= 43.8$
Explanation. $\alpha^* = 0.3889$ gives $q^* = 130$ with cost $147$. The mean demand is $151$, whose expected cost is $190.8$: VSS $= 190.8 - 147 = 43.8$.
Example 2 · orl-g-nv-vss · level complessa · notes
Newsvendor: a quantity $q$ is ordered before the demand $D$ is known, to minimize the expected cost $\mathbb E[c_o(q - D)^+ + c_u(D - q)^+]$, with underage cost $c_u = 11$ per unit of unmet demand and overage cost $c_o = 4$ per unsold unit. Demand scenarios (probability in brackets): $60$ (0.4), $100$ (0.3), $110$ (0.1), $130$ (0.1), $140$ (0.1). Compute the optimal quantity $q^*$, its expected cost and the value of the stochastic solution VSS (expected cost of ordering the mean demand minus the cost of $q^*$).
  1. $q^* = 110$, cost 147, VSS $= 45$
  2. $q^* = 110$, cost 147, VSS $= 192$
  3. $q^* = 100$, cost 152, VSS $= 40$
  4. $q^* = 110$, cost 147, VSS $= 147$
Explanation. $\alpha^* = 0.7333$ gives $q^* = 110$ with cost $147$. The mean demand is $92$, whose expected cost is $192$: VSS $= 192 - 147 = 45$.
Example 1 · orl-g-nv-normale · level facile · notes
Newsvendor: a product is bought at $c = 8$ €, sold at $p = 20$ € and each unsold unit is recovered at $v = 4$ €; demand is normal with mean $190$ and standard deviation $10$. The optimal order satisfies $F(\tilde q) = c_u/(c_u + c_o)$ with $c_u = p - c$ and $c_o = c - v$, so $\tilde q = \mu + z\sigma$ with $z = \Phi^{-1}(c_u/(c_u + c_o))$ (standard normal quantiles: $\Phi^{-1}(0.6) = 0.253$, $\Phi^{-1}(0.7) = 0.524$, $\Phi^{-1}(0.75) = 0.674$, $\Phi^{-1}(0.8) = 0.842$, $\Phi^{-1}(0.9) = 1.282$). What is $\tilde q$?
  1. $\tilde q = 196.7$
  2. $\tilde q = 198.4$
  3. $\tilde q = 183.3$
  4. $\tilde q = 190$
Explanation. $c_u = 12$, $c_o = 4$, critical ratio $12/16 = 0.75$, $z = 0.674$: $\tilde q = 190 + 0.674\cdot 10 = 196.7$, above the mean because shortages cost more than leftovers. Swapping $c_u$ and $c_o$ gives $\mu - z\sigma$.
Example 2 · orl-g-nv-normale · level facile · notes
Newsvendor: a product is bought at $c = 8$ €, sold at $p = 22$ € and each unsold unit is recovered at $v = 2$ €; demand is normal with mean $90$ and standard deviation $20$. The optimal order satisfies $F(\tilde q) = c_u/(c_u + c_o)$ with $c_u = p - c$ and $c_o = c - v$, so $\tilde q = \mu + z\sigma$ with $z = \Phi^{-1}(c_u/(c_u + c_o))$ (standard normal quantiles: $\Phi^{-1}(0.6) = 0.253$, $\Phi^{-1}(0.7) = 0.524$, $\Phi^{-1}(0.75) = 0.674$, $\Phi^{-1}(0.8) = 0.842$, $\Phi^{-1}(0.9) = 1.282$). What is $\tilde q$?
  1. $\tilde q = 100.5$
  2. $\tilde q = 79.5$
  3. $\tilde q = 103.5$
  4. $\tilde q = 90$
Explanation. $c_u = 14$, $c_o = 6$, critical ratio $14/20 = 0.7$, $z = 0.524$: $\tilde q = 90 + 0.524\cdot 20 = 100.5$, above the mean because shortages cost more than leftovers. Swapping $c_u$ and $c_o$ gives $\mu - z\sigma$.
Example 1 · orl-g-nv-servizio-uniforme · level media · notes
Newsvendor with uniform demand on $[90, 180]$, $c_u = 6$, $c_o = 9$. The expected cost of an order $q$ in $[90, 180]$ is $C(q) = \frac{c_o(q - 90)^2 + c_u(180 - q)^2}{2\cdot 90}$, minimized at $\tilde q = 90 + \frac{c_u}{c_u + c_o}\cdot 90$. A contract imposes a cycle service level $\mathbb P(D \le q) = 0.9$. What are the required order $q_\beta$ and its extra expected cost $C(q_\beta) - C(\tilde q)$?
  1. $q_\beta = 171$, extra cost $33.75$
  2. $q_\beta = 99$, extra cost $168.75$
  3. $q_\beta = 171$, extra cost $168.75$
  4. $q_\beta = 171$, extra cost $330.75$
Explanation. $q_\beta = 90 + 0.9\cdot 90 = 171$; $\tilde q = 126$; $C(q_\beta) = 330.75$ and $C(\tilde q) = 162$, so the service level costs $168.75$ more per cycle than the economic optimum, whose service level is only $0.4$.
Example 2 · orl-g-nv-servizio-uniforme · level media · notes
Newsvendor with uniform demand on $[70, 180]$, $c_u = 4$, $c_o = 2$. The expected cost of an order $q$ in $[70, 180]$ is $C(q) = \frac{c_o(q - 70)^2 + c_u(180 - q)^2}{2\cdot 110}$, minimized at $\tilde q = 70 + \frac{c_u}{c_u + c_o}\cdot 110$. A contract imposes a cycle service level $\mathbb P(D \le q) = 0.9$. What are the required order $q_\beta$ and its extra expected cost $C(q_\beta) - C(\tilde q)$?
  1. $q_\beta = 169$, extra cost $17.97$
  2. $q_\beta = 169$, extra cost $69.3$
  3. $q_\beta = 81$, extra cost $17.97$
  4. $q_\beta = 169$, extra cost $91.3$
Explanation. $q_\beta = 70 + 0.9\cdot 110 = 169$; $\tilde q = 143.33$; $C(q_\beta) = 91.3$ and $C(\tilde q) = 73.33$, so the service level costs $17.97$ more per cycle than the economic optimum, whose service level is only $0.67$.

14. VaR and CVaR (4)

Example 1 · orl-g-cvar-coda-intera · level facile · notes
Risk measures of a loss $L$ with $k = 10$ equally likely scenarios: $24, 32, 35, 5, 33, 25, 36, 1, 8, 11$. $\mathrm{VaR}_\alpha$ is the smallest loss $\ell$ with $\mathbb P(L \le \ell) \ge \alpha$; $\mathrm{CVaR}_\alpha$ is the mean loss in the worst tail of probability $1 - \alpha$. With $\alpha = 0.7$, what are $\mathrm{VaR}_\alpha$ and $\mathrm{CVaR}_\alpha$?
  1. VaR $= 33$, CVaR $= 34.67$
  2. VaR $= 32$, CVaR $= 34$
  3. VaR $= 32$, CVaR $= 36$
  4. VaR $= 32$, CVaR $= 34.67$
Explanation. Sorting the losses, the distribution function reaches $0.7$ at position 7 in increasing order: VaR $= 32$. The tail of mass $1 - \alpha$ contains exactly the 3 worst losses, whose mean is the CVaR, $34.67$.
Example 2 · orl-g-cvar-coda-intera · level facile · notes
Risk measures of a loss $L$ with $k = 8$ equally likely scenarios: $34, 13, 4, 16, 35, 32, 27, 38$. $\mathrm{VaR}_\alpha$ is the smallest loss $\ell$ with $\mathbb P(L \le \ell) \ge \alpha$; $\mathrm{CVaR}_\alpha$ is the mean loss in the worst tail of probability $1 - \alpha$. With $\alpha = 0.75$, what are $\mathrm{VaR}_\alpha$ and $\mathrm{CVaR}_\alpha$?
  1. VaR $= 34$, CVaR $= 36.5$
  2. VaR $= 34$, CVaR $= 35.67$
  3. VaR $= 35$, CVaR $= 36.5$
  4. VaR $= 34$, CVaR $= 38$
Explanation. Sorting the losses, the distribution function reaches $0.75$ at position 6 in increasing order: VaR $= 34$. The tail of mass $1 - \alpha$ contains exactly the 2 worst losses, whose mean is the CVaR, $36.5$.
Example 1 · orl-g-cvar-a-cavallo · level media · notes
Risk measures of a loss $L$ with six equally likely scenarios: $24, 32, 35, 5, 33, 25$. $\mathrm{VaR}_\alpha$ is the smallest loss $\ell$ with $\mathbb P(L \le \ell) \ge \alpha$, and $\mathrm{CVaR}_\alpha = \mathrm{VaR}_\alpha + \mathbb E[(L - \mathrm{VaR}_\alpha)^+]/(1 - \alpha)$. With $\alpha = 0.75$ (the tail of probability $1 - \alpha$ is not a whole number of scenarios), what are $\mathrm{VaR}_\alpha$ and $\mathrm{CVaR}_\alpha$?
  1. VaR $= 33$, CVaR $= 34$
  2. VaR $= 33$, CVaR $= 34.33$
  3. VaR $= 35$, CVaR $= 34.33$
  4. VaR $= 33$, CVaR $= 35$
Explanation. VaR $= 33$, the first loss at which the distribution function reaches $\alpha$. Then $\mathrm{CVaR} = \mathrm{VaR} + \frac{1}{1-\alpha}\sum_s \pi_s (\ell_s - \mathrm{VaR})^+ = 34.33$: the part of the tail mass that sits on the VaR point is counted at the VaR value.
Example 2 · orl-g-cvar-a-cavallo · level media · notes
Risk measures of a loss $L$ with six equally likely scenarios: $34, 13, 4, 16, 35, 32$. $\mathrm{VaR}_\alpha$ is the smallest loss $\ell$ with $\mathbb P(L \le \ell) \ge \alpha$, and $\mathrm{CVaR}_\alpha = \mathrm{VaR}_\alpha + \mathbb E[(L - \mathrm{VaR}_\alpha)^+]/(1 - \alpha)$. With $\alpha = 0.75$ (the tail of probability $1 - \alpha$ is not a whole number of scenarios), what are $\mathrm{VaR}_\alpha$ and $\mathrm{CVaR}_\alpha$?
  1. VaR $= 34$, CVaR $= 34.67$
  2. VaR $= 34$, CVaR $= 34.5$
  3. VaR $= 35$, CVaR $= 34.67$
  4. VaR $= 34$, CVaR $= 35$
Explanation. VaR $= 34$, the first loss at which the distribution function reaches $\alpha$. Then $\mathrm{CVaR} = \mathrm{VaR} + \frac{1}{1-\alpha}\sum_s \pi_s (\ell_s - \mathrm{VaR})^+ = 34.67$: the part of the tail mass that sits on the VaR point is counted at the VaR value.
Example 1 · orl-g-cvar-pesato · level complessa · notes
Risk measures of a loss $L$ with scenarios (probability in brackets): $26$ (0.3), $20$ (0.3), $9$ (0.3), $3$ (0.05), $27$ (0.05). $\mathrm{VaR}_\alpha$ is the smallest loss $\ell$ with $\mathbb P(L \le \ell) \ge \alpha$; $\mathrm{CVaR}_\alpha$ is the optimal value of the Rockafellar–Uryasev LP $\min_\eta\, \eta + \mathbb E[(L - \eta)^+]/(1 - \alpha)$. With $\alpha = 0.9$, what are $\mathrm{VaR}_\alpha$ and $\mathrm{CVaR}_\alpha$?
  1. VaR $= 26$, CVaR $= 26.14$
  2. VaR $= 26$, CVaR $= 27$
  3. VaR $= 27$, CVaR $= 27$
  4. VaR $= 26$, CVaR $= 26.5$
Explanation. Sorting the losses and accumulating the probabilities, $F$ reaches $0.9$ at $\ell = 26$: VaR $= 26$. Then $\mathrm{CVaR} = 26 + \frac{1}{0.1}\sum_s \pi_s(\ell_s - 26)^+ = 26.5$. The conditional mean beyond the VaR ignores the tail mass sitting on the VaR point.
Example 2 · orl-g-cvar-pesato · level complessa · notes
Risk measures of a loss $L$ with scenarios (probability in brackets): $34$ (0.35), $13$ (0.3), $4$ (0.25), $16$ (0.05), $35$ (0.05). $\mathrm{VaR}_\alpha$ is the smallest loss $\ell$ with $\mathbb P(L \le \ell) \ge \alpha$; $\mathrm{CVaR}_\alpha$ is the optimal value of the Rockafellar–Uryasev LP $\min_\eta\, \eta + \mathbb E[(L - \eta)^+]/(1 - \alpha)$. With $\alpha = 0.85$, what are $\mathrm{VaR}_\alpha$ and $\mathrm{CVaR}_\alpha$?
  1. VaR $= 34$, CVaR $= 34.33$
  2. VaR $= 35$, CVaR $= 35$
  3. VaR $= 34$, CVaR $= 34.13$
  4. VaR $= 34$, CVaR $= 35$
Explanation. Sorting the losses and accumulating the probabilities, $F$ reaches $0.85$ at $\ell = 34$: VaR $= 34$. Then $\mathrm{CVaR} = 34 + \frac{1}{0.15}\sum_s \pi_s(\ell_s - 34)^+ = 34.33$. The conditional mean beyond the VaR ignores the tail mass sitting on the VaR point.
Example 1 · orl-g-cvar-eccessi · level facile · notes
Rockafellar–Uryasev formulation of the CVaR: for a threshold $\eta$, the excesses are $\xi_s = (\ell_s - \eta)^+$ and the function to minimize over $\eta$ is $F(\eta) = \eta + \frac{1}{1-\alpha}\sum_s\pi_s\xi_s$. Five equiprobable scenarios ($\pi_s = 0.2$) with losses $\ell = (18; 18; 24; 26; 4)$, $\alpha = 0.8$. For $\eta = 18$ (not necessarily optimal), what are $\sum_s\xi_s$ and $F(\eta)$?
  1. $\sum_s\xi_s = 14$, value $20.8$
  2. $\sum_s\xi_s = 14$, value $32$
  3. $\sum_s\xi_s = 14$, value $21.5$
  4. $\sum_s\xi_s = 0$, value $18$
Explanation. Only the losses above $18$ count: $\xi = (0; 0; 6; 8; 0)$, sum $14$. Then $F(18) = 18 + \frac{1}{0.2}\cdot 0.2\cdot 14 = 32$. Negative differences are cut at zero; forgetting the factor $1/(1-\alpha)$ is the other typical mistake.
Example 2 · orl-g-cvar-eccessi · level facile · notes
Rockafellar–Uryasev formulation of the CVaR: for a threshold $\eta$, the excesses are $\xi_s = (\ell_s - \eta)^+$ and the function to minimize over $\eta$ is $F(\eta) = \eta + \frac{1}{1-\alpha}\sum_s\pi_s\xi_s$. Five equiprobable scenarios ($\pi_s = 0.2$) with losses $\ell = (12; 26; 10; 3; 12)$, $\alpha = 0.8$. For $\eta = 21$ (not necessarily optimal), what are $\sum_s\xi_s$ and $F(\eta)$?
  1. $\sum_s\xi_s = 5$, value $26$
  2. $\sum_s\xi_s = 5$, value $22$
  3. $\sum_s\xi_s = 5$, value $22.25$
  4. $\sum_s\xi_s = -42$, value $-21$
Explanation. Only the losses above $21$ count: $\xi = (0; 5; 0; 0; 0)$, sum $5$. Then $F(21) = 21 + \frac{1}{0.2}\cdot 0.2\cdot 5 = 26$. Negative differences are cut at zero; forgetting the factor $1/(1-\alpha)$ is the other typical mistake.

15. Arbitrage and pricing (5)

Example 1 · orl-g-arb-neutrali · level facile · notes
One-period market with two states of the world: a risk-free security costs $1$ today and pays $R = 1.1$ in both states; a risky security costs $s^0_1 = 33$ today and pays $58$ in state 1 and $31$ in state 2. Risk-neutral probabilities are $q_1, q_2 \ge 0$ with $q_1 + q_2 = 1$ under which every price equals the expected payoff divided by $R$. What are $q_1$ and $q_2$?
  1. $(q_1; q_2) = (0.6259; 0.3741)$
  2. $(q_1; q_2) = (0.0741; 0.9259)$
  3. $(q_1; q_2) = (0.8037; 0.1963)$
  4. $(q_1; q_2) = (0.1963; 0.8037)$
Explanation. The price is the discounted expected payoff: $s^0_1 = (q_1 \cdot 58 + q_2 \cdot 31)/R$ with $q_1 + q_2 = 1$, so $q_1 = (R s^0_1 - 31)/(58 - 31) = 0.1963$. Forgetting $R$ is the typical mistake.
Example 2 · orl-g-arb-neutrali · level facile · notes
One-period market with two states of the world: a risk-free security costs $1$ today and pays $R = 1.05$ in both states; a risky security costs $s^0_1 = 44$ today and pays $50$ in state 1 and $29$ in state 2. Risk-neutral probabilities are $q_1, q_2 \ge 0$ with $q_1 + q_2 = 1$ under which every price equals the expected payoff divided by $R$. What are $q_1$ and $q_2$?
  1. $(q_1; q_2) = (0.819; 0.181)$
  2. $(q_1; q_2) = (0.924; 0.076)$
  3. $(q_1; q_2) = (0.181; 0.819)$
  4. $(q_1; q_2) = (0.7143; 0.2857)$
Explanation. The price is the discounted expected payoff: $s^0_1 = (q_1 \cdot 50 + q_2 \cdot 29)/R$ with $q_1 + q_2 = 1$, so $q_1 = (R s^0_1 - 29)/(50 - 29) = 0.819$. Forgetting $R$ is the typical mistake.
Example 1 · orl-g-arb-call · level media · notes
One-period market with two states of the world: a risk-free security costs $1$ today and pays $R = 1.1$ in both states; a risky security costs $s^0_1 = 33$ today and pays $58$ in state 1 and $31$ in state 2. Risk-neutral probabilities are $q_1, q_2 \ge 0$ with $q_1 + q_2 = 1$ under which every price equals the expected payoff divided by $R$. A call option with strike $34$ pays tomorrow $\max(s^1_{1j} - 34, 0)$ in state $j$, where $s^1_{1j}$ is the payoff of the risky security. In this complete market, what is the arbitrage-free price of the call today?
  1. 4.28
  2. 1.62
  3. 4.71
  4. 10.91
Explanation. The risk-neutral probabilities are $q_1 = (R s^0_1 - 31)/(58 - 31) = 0.1963$, $q_2 = 1 - q_1$. The call pays $(24, 0)$, so its price is $q_1 \cdot 24/R = 4.28$: discounted expected value under $q$.
Example 2 · orl-g-arb-call · level media · notes
One-period market with two states of the world: a risk-free security costs $1$ today and pays $R = 1.05$ in both states; a risky security costs $s^0_1 = 44$ today and pays $50$ in state 1 and $29$ in state 2. Risk-neutral probabilities are $q_1, q_2 \ge 0$ with $q_1 + q_2 = 1$ under which every price equals the expected payoff divided by $R$. A call option with strike $37$ pays tomorrow $\max(s^1_{1j} - 37, 0)$ in state $j$, where $s^1_{1j}$ is the payoff of the risky security. In this complete market, what is the arbitrage-free price of the call today?
  1. 10.14
  2. 10.65
  3. 8.84
  4. 6.19
Explanation. The risk-neutral probabilities are $q_1 = (R s^0_1 - 29)/(50 - 29) = 0.819$, $q_2 = 1 - q_1$. The call pays $(13, 0)$, so its price is $q_1 \cdot 13/R = 10.14$: discounted expected value under $q$.
Example 1 · orl-g-arb-intervallo · level complessa · notes
One-period market with three states: a risk-free security costs $1$ and pays $R = 1.1$ in every state; a risky security costs $s^0_1 = 14$ and pays $(a_1; a_2; a_3) = (5; 36; 11)$ in the three states. A new security pays $(c_1; c_2; c_3) = (5; 1; 2)$. Its price is consistent (creates no arbitrage) if it equals $\frac1R\sum_j q_j\, c_j$ for risk-neutral probabilities $q_j \ge 0$, $\sum_j q_j = 1$, that also price the two existing securities. What is the interval of consistent prices (its endpoints)?
  1. $[1.82; 3.66]$
  2. $[1.71; 3.49]$
  3. $[1.66; 3.33]$
  4. $[0.91; 4.55]$
Explanation. The consistent measures satisfy $\sum_j q_j = 1$ and $\sum_j q_j a_j = R\,s^0_1 = 15.4$: a segment whose endpoints put mass on two states only, one with payoff below and one above $15.4$. Minimizing and maximizing $\frac1R\sum_j q_jc_j$ (two LPs) over it gives $[1.66, 3.33]$. Forgetting the factor $R$ (in the constraint or in the discount) shifts the interval.
Example 2 · orl-g-arb-intervallo · level complessa · notes
One-period market with three states: a risk-free security costs $1$ and pays $R = 1.05$ in every state; a risky security costs $s^0_1 = 24$ and pays $(a_1; a_2; a_3) = (35; 16; 8)$ in the three states. A new security pays $(c_1; c_2; c_3) = (8; 18; 16)$. Its price is consistent (creates no arbitrage) if it equals $\frac1R\sum_j q_j\, c_j$ for risk-neutral probabilities $q_j \ge 0$, $\sum_j q_j = 1$, that also price the two existing securities. What is the interval of consistent prices (its endpoints)?
  1. $[10.38; 12.53]$
  2. $[10.9; 13.16]$
  3. $[7.62; 17.14]$
  4. $[10.72; 13.13]$
Explanation. The consistent measures satisfy $\sum_j q_j = 1$ and $\sum_j q_j a_j = R\,s^0_1 = 25.2$: a segment whose endpoints put mass on two states only, one with payoff below and one above $25.2$. Minimizing and maximizing $\frac1R\sum_j q_jc_j$ (two LPs) over it gives $[10.38, 12.53]$. Forgetting the factor $R$ (in the constraint or in the discount) shifts the interval.
Example 1 · orl-g-arb-portafoglio · level facile · notes
One-period market with three states: security 0 is risk-free (costs 1 today, pays $R = 1.05$ in every state); security 1 costs $15$ and pays $(10; 10; 6)$; security 2 costs $25$ and pays $(16; 26; 32)$. The portfolio $x = (-28; -1; -1)$ holds $x_i$ units of security $i$ (negative = short). What are its cost today, $\sum_is^0_ix_i$, and its smallest payoff tomorrow over the three states?
  1. cost $-68$, smallest payoff $-67.4$
  2. cost $-68$, smallest payoff $-66$
  3. cost $-69.4$, smallest payoff $-67.4$
  4. cost $-68$, smallest payoff $-55.4$
Explanation. Cost: $-28\cdot 1 + -1\cdot 15 + -1\cdot 25 = -68$. Payoffs: $-28\cdot 1.05 + -1s^1_{1j} + -1s^1_{2j}$ = $-55.4$, $-65.4$, $-67.4$, smallest $-67.4$. A negative cost with every payoff $\ge 0$ would be an arbitrage of type A. The risk-free position pays $R$ per unit, not 1.
Example 2 · orl-g-arb-portafoglio · level facile · notes
One-period market with three states: security 0 is risk-free (costs 1 today, pays $R = 1.04$ in every state); security 1 costs $15$ and pays $(18; 9; 6)$; security 2 costs $38$ and pays $(22; 36; 34)$. The portfolio $x = (-36; 2; 1)$ holds $x_i$ units of security $i$ (negative = short). What are its cost today, $\sum_is^0_ix_i$, and its smallest payoff tomorrow over the three states?
  1. cost $32$, smallest payoff $20.56$
  2. cost $32$, smallest payoff $10$
  3. cost $32$, smallest payoff $8.56$
  4. cost $30.56$, smallest payoff $8.56$
Explanation. Cost: $-36\cdot 1 + 2\cdot 15 + 1\cdot 38 = 32$. Payoffs: $-36\cdot 1.04 + 2s^1_{1j} + 1s^1_{2j}$ = $20.56$, $16.56$, $8.56$, smallest $8.56$. A negative cost with every payoff $\ge 0$ would be an arbitrage of type A. The risk-free position pays $R$ per unit, not 1.
Example 1 · orl-g-arb-prezzo-atteso · level facile · notes
One-period market with three states and risk-free gross return $R = 1.02$. The duals of the arbitrage LP give the risk-neutral probabilities $q = (0.2; 0.1; 0.7)$, and every price today is the discounted expected payoff under $q$: $s^0_i = \frac1R\sum_jq_js^1_{ij}$. A new security pays $(10; 16; 4)$ in the three states. What is its arbitrage-free price today?
  1. $s^0 = 6.27$
  2. $s^0 = 6.53$
  3. $s^0 = 9.8$
  4. $s^0 = 6.4$
Explanation. Expected payoff under $q$: $6.4$; discounted: $6.4/1.02 = 6.27$. Forgetting to discount, or using equal probabilities instead of $q$, are the typical mistakes.
Example 2 · orl-g-arb-prezzo-atteso · level facile · notes
One-period market with three states and risk-free gross return $R = 1.02$. The duals of the arbitrage LP give the risk-neutral probabilities $q = (0.3; 0.5; 0.2)$, and every price today is the discounted expected payoff under $q$: $s^0_i = \frac1R\sum_jq_js^1_{ij}$.
