16. Support Vector Machine (7)

Example 1 · orl-g-svm-due-punti · level facile · notes
Hard-margin SVM: $\min \tfrac12\|\mathbf w\|^2$ subject to $y_i(\mathbf w'\mathbf x_i + b) \ge 1$, with $\mathbf w \in \mathbb R^2$ and $b$ free. Two observations: $\mathbf x_1 = (0; 0)$ with label $y_1 = -1$ and $\mathbf x_2 = (2; 2)$ with label $y_2 = +1$. What are the optimal $\tilde{\mathbf w}$ and the margin $2/\|\tilde{\mathbf w}\|$?
  1. $\mathbf w = (0.25; 0.25)$, margin $5.657$
  2. $\mathbf w = (0.5; 0.5)$, margin $2.828$
  3. $\mathbf w = (2; 2)$, margin $0.707$
  4. $\mathbf w = (0.5; 0.5)$, margin $1.414$
Explanation. Both constraints are active: $b = -1$ and $\mathbf w'\mathbf x_2 + b = 1$; the minimum-norm $\mathbf w$ is parallel to $\mathbf x_2$, $\mathbf w = 2\mathbf x_2/\|\mathbf x_2\|^2$. The margin $2/\|\mathbf w\| = \|\mathbf x_2\| = 2.828$ is the distance between the two points.
Example 2 · orl-g-svm-due-punti · level facile · notes
Hard-margin SVM: $\min \tfrac12\|\mathbf w\|^2$ subject to $y_i(\mathbf w'\mathbf x_i + b) \ge 1$, with $\mathbf w \in \mathbb R^2$ and $b$ free. Two observations: $\mathbf x_1 = (0; 0)$ with label $y_1 = -1$ and $\mathbf x_2 = (1; 4)$ with label $y_2 = +1$. What are the optimal $\tilde{\mathbf w}$ and the margin $2/\|\tilde{\mathbf w}\|$?
  1. $\mathbf w = (0.118; 0.471)$, margin $2.062$
  2. $\mathbf w = (1; 4)$, margin $0.485$
  3. $\mathbf w = (0.118; 0.471)$, margin $4.123$
  4. $\mathbf w = (0.059; 0.235)$, margin $8.246$
Explanation. Both constraints are active: $b = -1$ and $\mathbf w'\mathbf x_2 + b = 1$; the minimum-norm $\mathbf w$ is parallel to $\mathbf x_2$, $\mathbf w = 2\mathbf x_2/\|\mathbf x_2\|^2$. The margin $2/\|\mathbf w\| = \|\mathbf x_2\| = 4.123$ is the distance between the two points.
Example 1 · orl-g-svm-soft · level media · notes
Soft-margin SVM: objective $\tfrac12\|\mathbf w\|^2 + C\sum_i \xi_i$, where for given $\mathbf w, b$ the optimal slacks are $\xi_i = \max\{0,\ 1 - y_i(\mathbf w'\mathbf x_i + b)\}$. Candidate classifier $\mathbf w = (0; -2)$, $b = 0$, with $C = 1$; observations $\mathbf x_1 = (1; -2)$, $y_1 = +1$; $\mathbf x_2 = (-1; 2)$, $y_2 = -1$; $\mathbf x_3 = (-1; -2)$, $y_3 = -1$; $\mathbf x_4 = (0; 1)$, $y_4 = +1$. What are $\sum_i \xi_i$ and the objective value?
  1. $\sum_i \xi_i = 6$, objective $8$
  2. $\sum_i \xi_i = 8$, objective $12$
  3. $\sum_i \xi_i = 8$, objective $10$
  4. $\sum_i \xi_i = 2$, objective $4$
Explanation. Compute $y_i(\mathbf w'\mathbf x_i + b)$ for each observation: the points with value $\ge 1$ are beyond the margin and pay nothing, the others pay $1 - y_i(\mathbf w'\mathbf x_i + b)$. The slacks sum to $8$ and the objective is $\tfrac12\cdot 4 + 1\cdot 8 = 10$. Dropping the $\max$ lets well-classified points subtract.
Example 2 · orl-g-svm-soft · level media · notes
Soft-margin SVM: objective $\tfrac12\|\mathbf w\|^2 + C\sum_i \xi_i$, where for given $\mathbf w, b$ the optimal slacks are $\xi_i = \max\{0,\ 1 - y_i(\mathbf w'\mathbf x_i + b)\}$. Candidate classifier $\mathbf w = (-1; 0)$, $b = 2$, with $C = 2$; observations $\mathbf x_1 = (-2; 1)$, $y_1 = -1$; $\mathbf x_2 = (-2; -2)$, $y_2 = +1$; $\mathbf x_3 = (2; 1)$, $y_3 = -1$; $\mathbf x_4 = (2; -1)$, $y_4 = -1$. What are $\sum_i \xi_i$ and the objective value?
  1. $\sum_i \xi_i = 7$, objective $14.5$
  2. $\sum_i \xi_i = 4$, objective $8.5$
  3. $\sum_i \xi_i = 7$, objective $15$
  4. $\sum_i \xi_i = 5$, objective $10.5$
Explanation. Compute $y_i(\mathbf w'\mathbf x_i + b)$ for each observation: the points with value $\ge 1$ are beyond the margin and pay nothing, the others pay $1 - y_i(\mathbf w'\mathbf x_i + b)$. The slacks sum to $7$ and the objective is $\tfrac12\cdot 1 + 2\cdot 7 = 14.5$. Dropping the $\max$ lets well-classified points subtract.
Example 1 · orl-g-svm-margine · level facile · notes
SVM for binary classification: observations $\mathbf x_i$ with labels $y_i \in \{-1, +1\}$, hyperplane $\mathbf w'\mathbf x + b = 0$. A trained SVM has $\mathbf w = (1; 1)$ and $b = 5$; its margin lines are $\mathbf w'\mathbf x + b = \pm 1$. What are the margin (the distance between the two margin lines) and the distance of the point $\mathbf x_0 = (3; -4)$ from the hyperplane?
  1. margin $1.414$, distance $2.828$
  2. margin $1.414$, distance $4$
  3. margin $0.707$, distance $2.828$
  4. margin $1$, distance $2.828$
Explanation. $\|\mathbf w\| = \sqrt{2} = 1.414$: the margin is $2/\|\mathbf w\| = 1.414$; the distance of $\mathbf x_0$ is $|\mathbf w'\mathbf x_0 + b|/\|\mathbf w\| = 4/1.414 = 2.828$. Forgetting to divide by $\|\mathbf w\|$ is the typical mistake.
Example 2 · orl-g-svm-margine · level facile · notes
SVM for binary classification: observations $\mathbf x_i$ with labels $y_i \in \{-1, +1\}$, hyperplane $\mathbf w'\mathbf x + b = 0$. A trained SVM has $\mathbf w = (-2; 4)$ and $b = -3$; its margin lines are $\mathbf w'\mathbf x + b = \pm 1$. What are the margin (the distance between the two margin lines) and the distance of the point $\mathbf x_0 = (-4; -1)$ from the hyperplane?
  1. margin $0.447$, distance $0.224$
  2. margin $0.224$, distance $0.224$
  3. margin $0.447$, distance $1$
  4. margin $0.1$, distance $0.224$
Explanation. $\|\mathbf w\| = \sqrt{20} = 4.472$: the margin is $2/\|\mathbf w\| = 0.447$; the distance of $\mathbf x_0$ is $|\mathbf w'\mathbf x_0 + b|/\|\mathbf w\| = 1/4.472 = 0.224$. Forgetting to divide by $\|\mathbf w\|$ is the typical mistake.
Example 1 · orl-g-svm-duale-w · level facile · notes
SVM for binary classification: observations $\mathbf x_i$ with labels $y_i \in \{-1, +1\}$, hyperplane $\mathbf w'\mathbf x + b = 0$. In the dual of the soft margin ($\max\ \sum_i\alpha_i - \tfrac12\sum_i\sum_j\alpha_i\alpha_jy_iy_j\mathbf x_i'\mathbf x_j$, $\sum_i\alpha_iy_i = 0$, $0 \le \alpha_i \le C$) only three points have $\tilde\alpha_i > 0$: $\mathbf x_1 = (1; 1)$, $y_1 = +1$, $\tilde\alpha_1 = 1.5$; $\mathbf x_2 = (3; 3)$, $y_2 = +1$, $\tilde\alpha_2 = 1.5$; $\mathbf x_3 = (-3; 3)$, $y_3 = -1$, $\tilde\alpha_3 = 3$. What is the optimal $\tilde{\mathbf w}$ of the primal?
  1. $\tilde{\mathbf w} = (-15; 3)$
  2. $\tilde{\mathbf w} = (-3; 15)$
  3. $\tilde{\mathbf w} = (15; -3)$
  4. $\tilde{\mathbf w} = (6; 6)$
Explanation. Primal–dual relation: $\tilde{\mathbf w} = \sum_i\tilde\alpha_iy_i\mathbf x_i$, a sum over the support vectors only, with the sign of the labels: $1.5\,\mathbf x_1 + 1.5\,\mathbf x_2 - 3\,\mathbf x_3 = (15; -3)$. Forgetting the labels $y_i$ is the typical mistake.
Example 2 · orl-g-svm-duale-w · level facile · notes
SVM for binary classification: observations $\mathbf x_i$ with labels $y_i \in \{-1, +1\}$, hyperplane $\mathbf w'\mathbf x + b = 0$. In the dual of the soft margin ($\max\ \sum_i\alpha_i - \tfrac12\sum_i\sum_j\alpha_i\alpha_jy_iy_j\mathbf x_i'\mathbf x_j$, $\sum_i\alpha_iy_i = 0$, $0 \le \alpha_i \le C$) only three points have $\tilde\alpha_i > 0$: $\mathbf x_1 = (4; 3)$, $y_1 = +1$, $\tilde\alpha_1 = 2$; $\mathbf x_2 = (2; 3)$, $y_2 = +1$, $\tilde\alpha_2 = 2$; $\mathbf x_3 = (3; -2)$, $y_3 = -1$, $\tilde\alpha_3 = 4$. What is the optimal $\tilde{\mathbf w}$ of the primal?
  1. $\tilde{\mathbf w} = (24; 4)$
  2. $\tilde{\mathbf w} = (0; 20)$
  3. $\tilde{\mathbf w} = (12; 12)$
  4. $\tilde{\mathbf w} = (0; -20)$
Explanation. Primal–dual relation: $\tilde{\mathbf w} = \sum_i\tilde\alpha_iy_i\mathbf x_i$, a sum over the support vectors only, with the sign of the labels: $2\,\mathbf x_1 + 2\,\mathbf x_2 - 4\,\mathbf x_3 = (0; 20)$. Forgetting the labels $y_i$ is the typical mistake.
Example 1 · orl-g-svm-rbf-valore · level facile · notes
SVM for binary classification: observations $\mathbf x_i$ with labels $y_i \in \{-1, +1\}$, hyperplane $\mathbf w'\mathbf x + b = 0$. With the RBF (Gaussian) kernel $K(\mathbf x, \mathbf z) = e^{-\gamma\|\mathbf x - \mathbf z\|^2}$ and $\gamma = 0.5$, what is $K(\mathbf x, \mathbf z)$ for $\mathbf x = (4; 2)$ and $\mathbf z = (3; 1)$?
  1. $K = 0.018$
  2. $K = 0.607$
  3. $K = 0.368$
  4. $K = 0.493$
Explanation. $\|\mathbf x - \mathbf z\|^2 = 2$ (the squared distance, not the distance), so $K = e^{-0.5\cdot 2} = 0.368$. Points far apart give $K$ close to 0, coincident points $K = 1$; a larger $\gamma$ makes the kernel more local.
Example 2 · orl-g-svm-rbf-valore · level facile · notes
SVM for binary classification: observations $\mathbf x_i$ with labels $y_i \in \{-1, +1\}$, hyperplane $\mathbf w'\mathbf x + b = 0$. With the RBF (Gaussian) kernel $K(\mathbf x, \mathbf z) = e^{-\gamma\|\mathbf x - \mathbf z\|^2}$ and $\gamma = 0.5$, what is $K(\mathbf x, \mathbf z)$ for $\mathbf x = (4; 3)$ and $\mathbf z = (3; 4)$?
  1. $K = 0.368$
  2. $K = 0.493$
  3. $K = 0.607$
  4. $K = 0.018$
Explanation. $\|\mathbf x - \mathbf z\|^2 = 2$ (the squared distance, not the distance), so $K = e^{-0.5\cdot 2} = 0.368$. Points far apart give $K$ close to 0, coincident points $K = 1$; a larger $\gamma$ makes the kernel more local.
Example 1 · orl-g-svr-tubo · level facile · notes
Support Vector Regression: errors within a tube $|y_i - \hat y_i| \le \varepsilon$ cost nothing, those outside pay $|y_i - \hat y_i| - \varepsilon$ (the slacks $\xi_i$, $\xi_i^*$). The estimated line is $\hat y = -8x + 210$, with $\varepsilon = 7$; the observations $(x_i; y_i)$ are (2; 205), (9; 124), (10; 141), (11; 113), (13; 99). How many points fall outside the tube, and what is their total penalty $\sum_i(\xi_i + \xi_i^*)$?
  1. $4$ points outside, penalty $45$
  2. $1$ points outside, penalty $17$
  3. $1$ points outside, penalty $45$
  4. $4$ points outside, penalty $17$
Explanation. Residuals $y_i - \hat y_i$: $11$, $-14$, $11$, $-9$, $-7$. Those with $|r_i| > 7$ are 4; each pays only the part beyond the tube, $|r_i| - 7$, for a total of $17$. Paying the whole residual gives $45$.
Example 2 · orl-g-svr-tubo · level facile · notes
Support Vector Regression: errors within a tube $|y_i - \hat y_i| \le \varepsilon$ cost nothing, those outside pay $|y_i - \hat y_i| - \varepsilon$ (the slacks $\xi_i$, $\xi_i^*$). The estimated line is $\hat y = -6x + 240$, with $\varepsilon = 4$; the observations $(x_i; y_i)$ are (1; 241), (7; 202), (10; 183), (13; 168), (14; 161). How many points fall outside the tube, and what is their total penalty $\sum_i(\xi_i + \xi_i^*)$?
  1. $2$ points outside, penalty $18$
  2. $3$ points outside, penalty $18$
  3. $2$ points outside, penalty $6$
  4. $3$ points outside, penalty $6$
Explanation. Residuals $y_i - \hat y_i$: $7$, $4$, $3$, $6$, $5$. Those with $|r_i| > 4$ are 3; each pays only the part beyond the tube, $|r_i| - 4$, for a total of $6$. Paying the whole residual gives $18$.
Example 1 · orl-g-svm-duale-b · level media · notes
SVM for binary classification: observations $\mathbf x_i$ with labels $y_i \in \{-1, +1\}$, hyperplane $\mathbf w'\mathbf x + b = 0$. A soft-margin SVM (dual multipliers $0 \le \alpha_i \le C$) gives $\tilde{\mathbf w} = (0; 1)$. The point $\mathbf x_k = (2; 3)$ with label $y_k = -1$ has $0 < \tilde\alpha_k < C$. What are the intercept $\tilde b$ and the score $f(\mathbf x_0) = \tilde{\mathbf w}'\mathbf x_0 + \tilde b$ of $\mathbf x_0 = (2; 1)$?
  1. $\tilde b = -3$, $f(\mathbf x_0) = -2$
  2. $\tilde b = -4$, $f(\mathbf x_0) = -3$
  3. $\tilde b = 4$, $f(\mathbf x_0) = 5$
  4. $\tilde b = -2$, $f(\mathbf x_0) = -1$
Explanation. $0 < \tilde\alpha_k < C$ means $\mathbf x_k$ lies exactly on its margin line: $y_k(\tilde{\mathbf w}'\mathbf x_k + b) = 1$, so $\tilde b = y_k - \tilde{\mathbf w}'\mathbf x_k = -4$ (since $1/y_k = y_k$). Then $f(\mathbf x_0) = -3$: $\mathbf x_0$ is assigned to class $-1$.
Example 2 · orl-g-svm-duale-b · level media · notes
SVM for binary classification: observations $\mathbf x_i$ with labels $y_i \in \{-1, +1\}$, hyperplane $\mathbf w'\mathbf x + b = 0$. A soft-margin SVM (dual multipliers $0 \le \alpha_i \le C$) gives $\tilde{\mathbf w} = (-1; 2)$. The point $\mathbf x_k = (-1; -3)$ with label $y_k = -1$ has $0 < \tilde\alpha_k < C$. What are the intercept $\tilde b$ and the score $f(\mathbf x_0) = \tilde{\mathbf w}'\mathbf x_0 + \tilde b$ of $\mathbf x_0 = (3; 2)$?
  1. $\tilde b = 4$, $f(\mathbf x_0) = 5$
  2. $\tilde b = 5$, $f(\mathbf x_0) = 6$
  3. $\tilde b = -4$, $f(\mathbf x_0) = -3$
  4. $\tilde b = 6$, $f(\mathbf x_0) = 7$
Explanation. $0 < \tilde\alpha_k < C$ means $\mathbf x_k$ lies exactly on its margin line: $y_k(\tilde{\mathbf w}'\mathbf x_k + b) = 1$, so $\tilde b = y_k - \tilde{\mathbf w}'\mathbf x_k = 4$ (since $1/y_k = y_k$). Then $f(\mathbf x_0) = 5$: $\mathbf x_0$ is assigned to class $+1$.

17. Robust and quantile regression (7)

Example 1 · orl-g-reg-perdita · level facile · notes
Quantile regression with no features: an intercept $b$ is fitted to $y = (120; 135; 141; 87; 137)$ by minimizing $\sum_i [\tau u_i + (1 - \tau) v_i]$, where $u_i = (y_i - b)^+$ and $v_i = (b - y_i)^+$ are the deviations above and below $b$. With $\tau = 0.8$, what is the objective value for $b = 121$?
  1. $85$
  2. $38$
  3. $47$
  4. $68$
Explanation. The deviations above ($u_i$, observations larger than $b$) sum to $50$ and are paid $\tau$; those below ($v_i$) sum to $35$ and are paid $1 - \tau$: $0.8\cdot 50 + 0.2\cdot 35 = 47$.
Example 2 · orl-g-reg-perdita · level facile · notes
Quantile regression with no features: an intercept $b$ is fitted to $y = (107; 139; 102; 86; 108)$ by minimizing $\sum_i [\tau u_i + (1 - \tau) v_i]$, where $u_i = (y_i - b)^+$ and $v_i = (b - y_i)^+$ are the deviations above and below $b$. With $\tau = 0.9$, what is the objective value for $b = 130$?
  1. $19.8$
  2. $106.2$
  3. $113.4$
  4. $126$
Explanation. The deviations above ($u_i$, observations larger than $b$) sum to $9$ and are paid $\tau$; those below ($v_i$) sum to $117$ and are paid $1 - \tau$: $0.9\cdot 9 + 0.1\cdot 117 = 19.8$.
Example 1 · orl-g-reg-quantile · level media · notes
Quantile regression with no features: an intercept $b$ is fitted to the $n = 7$ observations $y = (126; 143; 149; 88; 145; 130; 151)$ by minimizing $z(b) = \sum_i [\tau (y_i - b)^+ + (1 - \tau)(b - y_i)^+]$, with $\tau = 0.25$. What are the optimal intercept $\tilde b$ and the optimal value $\tilde z$?
  1. $\tilde b = 143$, $\tilde z = 67.75$
  2. $\tilde b = 130$, $\tilde z = 51.5$
  3. $\tilde b = 126$, $\tilde z = 50.5$
  4. $\tilde b = 88$, $\tilde z = 79$
Explanation. Without features the LP returns the empirical $\tau$-quantile: raising $b$ pays while the share of observations below it is at most $\tau$. Since $\tau n = 1.75$, $\tilde b$ is the observation in position 2 in increasing order, $126$, with $\tilde z = 50.5$.
Example 2 · orl-g-reg-quantile · level media · notes
Quantile regression with no features: an intercept $b$ is fitted to the $n = 6$ observations $y = (148; 105; 87; 112; 149; 144)$ by minimizing $z(b) = \sum_i [\tau (y_i - b)^+ + (1 - \tau)(b - y_i)^+]$, with $\tau = 0.75$. What are the optimal intercept $\tilde b$ and the optimal value $\tilde z$?
  1. $\tilde b = 148$, $\tilde z = 36.75$
  2. $\tilde b = 112$, $\tilde z = 86.75$
  3. $\tilde b = 149$, $\tilde z = 37.25$
  4. $\tilde b = 144$, $\tilde z = 38.75$
Explanation. Without features the LP returns the empirical $\tau$-quantile: raising $b$ pays while the share of observations below it is at most $\tau$. Since $\tau n = 4.5$, $\tilde b$ is the observation in position 5 in increasing order, $148$, with $\tilde z = 36.75$.
Example 1 · orl-g-reg-mediana-media · level facile · notes
Seven days of sales of a bakery, one of them during a strike: $124$, $125$, $137$, $142$, $96$, $139$, $23$. With no features, the LP of least absolute deviations ($\min\sum_i|y_i - b|$, quantile regression with $\tau = 1/2$) and the QP of least squares ($\min\sum_i(y_i - b)^2$) each estimate one number $b$. What are the two estimates?
  1. LP $\tilde b = 112.29$, QP $\tilde b = 125$
  2. LP $\tilde b = 125$, QP $\tilde b = 112.29$
  3. LP $\tilde b = 125$, QP $\tilde b = 127.17$
  4. LP $\tilde b = 131$, QP $\tilde b = 112.29$
Explanation. The LP returns the median, the 4th of the 7 sorted values, $125$; the QP returns the mean, $112.29$, pulled down by the strike day. The median ignores how far the anomalous value is: this is the robustness of the LP.
Example 2 · orl-g-reg-mediana-media · level facile · notes
Seven days of sales of a bakery, one of them during a strike: $113$, $141$, $109$, $95$, $114$, $142$, $26$. With no features, the LP of least absolute deviations ($\min\sum_i|y_i - b|$, quantile regression with $\tau = 1/2$) and the QP of least squares ($\min\sum_i(y_i - b)^2$) each estimate one number $b$. What are the two estimates?
  1. LP $\tilde b = 113$, QP $\tilde b = 105.71$
  2. LP $\tilde b = 105.71$, QP $\tilde b = 113$
  3. LP $\tilde b = 113$, QP $\tilde b = 119$
  4. LP $\tilde b = 113.5$, QP $\tilde b = 105.71$
Explanation. The LP returns the median, the 4th of the 7 sorted values, $113$; the QP returns the mean, $105.71$, pulled down by the strike day. The median ignores how far the anomalous value is: this is the robustness of the LP.
Example 1 · orl-g-reg-duale-interpolato · level media · notes
Quantile regression LP with no features: $\min\sum_i[\tau u_i + (1 - \tau)v_i]$ subject to $b + u_i - v_i = y_i$, $u_i, v_i \ge 0$, $b$ free; $\tilde\pi_i$ is the optimal dual of the constraint of observation $i$ (it equals $\tau$ for observations above $\tilde b$, $-(1 - \tau)$ for those below, and $\sum_i\tilde\pi_i = 0$). Data: $y = (94; 101; 84; 87; 106)$, $\tau = 7/10$. What are $\tilde b$ and the dual $\tilde\pi$ of the interpolated observation ($y_i = \tilde b$)?
  1. $\tilde b = 101$, $\tilde\pi = -0.2$
  2. $\tilde b = 94$, $\tilde\pi = 0.7$
  3. $\tilde b = 101$, $\tilde\pi = 0.2$
  4. $\tilde b = 106$, $\tilde\pi = 0.2$
Explanation. $\tilde b$ is the empirical $\tau$-quantile: with $(k - 1)/n < \tau < k/n$ for $k = 4$ it is the 4th smallest value, $101$, with $3$ observations below and $1$ above. From $\sum_i\tilde\pi_i = 0$: $\tilde\pi = (1 - \tau)\cdot 3 - \tau\cdot 1 = 0.2$, inside $[-(1 - \tau), \tau]$.
Example 2 · orl-g-reg-duale-interpolato · level media · notes
Quantile regression LP with no features: $\min\sum_i[\tau u_i + (1 - \tau)v_i]$ subject to $b + u_i - v_i = y_i$, $u_i, v_i \ge 0$, $b$ free; $\tilde\pi_i$ is the optimal dual of the constraint of observation $i$ (it equals $\tau$ for observations above $\tilde b$, $-(1 - \tau)$ for those below, and $\sum_i\tilde\pi_i = 0$). Data: $y = (105; 87; 112; 149; 144; 133; 154)$, $\tau = 11/14$. What are $\tilde b$ and the dual $\tilde\pi$ of the interpolated observation ($y_i = \tilde b$)?
  1. $\tilde b = 149$, $\tilde\pi = 0.286$
  2. $\tilde b = 154$, $\tilde\pi = 0.286$
  3. $\tilde b = 149$, $\tilde\pi = -0.286$
  4. $\tilde b = 144$, $\tilde\pi = 0.786$
Explanation. $\tilde b$ is the empirical $\tau$-quantile: with $(k - 1)/n < \tau < k/n$ for $k = 6$ it is the 6th smallest value, $149$, with $5$ observations below and $1$ above. From $\sum_i\tilde\pi_i = 0$: $\tilde\pi = (1 - \tau)\cdot 5 - \tau\cdot 1 = 0.286$, inside $[-(1 - \tau), \tau]$.
Example 1 · orl-g-reg-lad-3punti · level complessa · notes
Quantile regression LP with one feature ($p = 1$): $\min\sum_i[\tau u_i + (1 - \tau)v_i]$ subject to $wx_i + b + u_i - v_i = y_i$, $u_i, v_i \ge 0$ ($u_i$: observation above the line, $v_i$: below), $w, b$ free, with $\tau = 0.5$ and three observations $(x_i; y_i)$: (1; 34), (4; 47), (5; 12). An optimal line interpolates $p + 1 = 2$ observations. What are the optimal slope $\tilde w$, intercept $\tilde b$ and optimal value?
  1. slope $4.33$, intercept $29.67$, value $19.67$
  2. slope $-5.5$, intercept $39.5$, value $29.5$
  3. slope $-35$, intercept $187$, value $59$
  4. slope $-5.5$, intercept $39.5$, value $14.75$
Explanation. Three candidate lines, one per pair of points; each leaves one residual $r$ on the third point, which costs $\tau r$ if the point is above ($r > 0$) and $(1 - \tau)|r|$ if below. Values: $19.67$, $14.75$, $59$. The cheapest is the line with slope $-5.5$ and intercept $39.5$.
Example 2 · orl-g-reg-lad-3punti · level complessa · notes
Quantile regression LP with one feature ($p = 1$): $\min\sum_i[\tau u_i + (1 - \tau)v_i]$ subject to $wx_i + b + u_i - v_i = y_i$, $u_i, v_i \ge 0$ ($u_i$: observation above the line, $v_i$: below), $w, b$ free, with $\tau = 0.5$ and three observations $(x_i; y_i)$: (1; 56), (3; 46), (8; 38).   4. slope $-1.6$, intercept $50.8$, value $3.4$

Example 1 · orl-g-reg-obiettivo-retta · level facile · notes
Quantile regression LP with one feature: $\min\sum_i[\tau u_i + (1 - \tau)v_i]$ with $wx_i + b + u_i - v_i = y_i$, $u_i, v_i \ge 0$ ($u_i$: observation above the line, $v_i$: below). For the candidate line $w = 1$, $b = 110$ (not necessarily optimal), $\tau = 0.9$ and the observations $(x_i; y_i)$ (4; 117), (5; 125), (14; 112), (18; 138), what are $\sum_iu_i$, $\sum_iv_i$ and the objective value?
  1. $\sum u_i = 23$, $\sum v_i = 12$, value $13.1$
  2. $\sum u_i = 12$, $\sum v_i = 23$, value $21.9$
  3. $\sum u_i = 23$, $\sum v_i = 12$, value $35$
  4. $\sum u_i = 23$, $\sum v_i = 12$, value $21.9$
Explanation. Residuals $y_i - (wx_i + b)$: $3$, $10$, $-12$, $10$; positive parts sum to $23$ (the $u_i$), negative parts to $12$ (the $v_i$). Value $0.9\cdot 23 + 0.1\cdot 12 = 21.9$; swapping the weights of the two sides is the typical mistake.

Example 1 · orl-g-reg-scorta · level facile · notes
A bakery forecasts daily demand from temperature $t$ (°C), price $p$ (€) and weekend indicator $e \in \{0, 1\}$ with quantile regression. Median model ($\tau = 0.5$): $274 - 3.5\,t - 26\,p + 32\,e$; model at the critical fractile $\tau = 9/13$ (the newsvendor quantile): $285 - 3.7\,t - 26\,p + 36\,e$. For a day with $t = 23$, $p = 3$, $e = 1$, how much should be produced, and what is the safety stock?
  1. produce $147.5$, safety stock $10.4$
  2. produce $157.9$, safety stock $157.9$
  3. produce $168.3$, safety stock $10.4$
  4. produce $157.9$, safety stock $10.4$
Explanation. One produces the forecast at the critical fractile, $157.9$; the median forecast is $147.5$, and their difference, $10.4$, is the safety stock, obtained with no assumption on the distribution of demand.
Example 2 · orl-g-reg-scorta · level facile · notes
A bakery forecasts daily demand from temperature $t$ (°C), price $p$ (€) and weekend indicator $e \in \{0, 1\}$ with quantile regression.   2. produce $155.2$, safety stock $155.2$
  3. produce $155.2$, safety stock $2.4$
  4. produce $157.6$, safety stock $2.4$
Explanation. One produces the forecast at the critical fractile, $155.2$; the median forecast is $152.8$, and their difference, $2.4$, is the safety stock, obtained with no assumption on the distribution of demand.
